 Ancestry in the Margins: Justice,
Genomics, and the Ethics of Human
                    Classification

   Sasha Louise May Henriques
                     Sidney Sussex
                          January 2026




    This thesis is submitted for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy.
This thesis is the result of my own work and includes nothing which is the

1|Page
Abstract

This thesis examines how concepts of race, ethnicity, and ancestry influence
categorisation in genomic research, drawing on an ethnographic study conducted at the
Wellcome Sanger Institute. Positioned within critical race theory and critical realism, it
explores how institutional structures shape both ethical challenges and opportunities for
achieving genomic equity. Grounded in the researcher’s positionality as a Black genetic
counsellor, the study critically interrogates how ideas of human difference are
constructed, managed, and operationalised within scientific spaces, and how social
justice might be more deeply embedded within genomic research practices.

The research investigates the persistence of racialised ideas despite formal scientific
rejections of race as a biological category.

Findings reveal that "data diversity" initiatives, while framed as mechanisms for
improving patient outcomes, also confer material, reputational, and economic benefits to
institutions. Scientists often navigate competing demands, balancing the pursuit of
technical excellence, ethical imperatives, and the institutional structures that shape
research priorities. Boundary work to produce genetic ancestry datasets enables both
the reproduction of historical inequities and the creation of new forms of exclusion
through standardised group-making practices.

Through analysis of interviews, publications, and informal aspects of campus life, the
thesis shows that racial ideas are often reintroduced at key stages in the research
pipeline, from participant recruitment to dataset construction and clinical application,
usually without explicit reflection on their ethical or political implications.

This study argues that achieving equitable genomic outcomes and benefits for all
populations cannot be accomplished solely through data diversification. Institutions must
recognise the power and autonomy they possess to shape research practices and
engage honestly with their own interests and limitations.


2|Page
By reconfiguring data diversity efforts to focus not only on hopes for patient outcomes
but also on structural accountability, transparency, and justice, this work seeks to make
the social systems that shape genomic knowledge visible. It invites genomic institutions
to go beyond inclusion as a symbolic act and move toward a transformative model of
genomic equity.

The thesis makes two contributions: it demonstrates how inequities in genomic science
are produced through institutional and methodological practice, and it proposes a
framework for embedding justice into genomic research from the outset.

1 Introduction

I grew up in Newham, a working-class borough of East London and one of the most
ethnically diverse places in the United Kingdom (Regional Ethnic Diversity, 2022).
Cultural, racial and ethnic diversity formed the landscape of my early life. It shaped how I
understood belonging, community, and the social conditions that determine whose
identities are affirmed and whose are marginalised. As I moved into adulthood, I
encountered environments where racial and cultural differences were interpreted in ways
unfamiliar to me. What had been normal in Newham became, in other settings, a source
of exclusion or misrecognition. These shifts sharpened my commitment to understanding
how racialisation operates, and to challenging the forms of inequity that follow.

My professional training as a genetic counsellor deepened these concerns. Within
clinical genetics, my presence as a Black woman was unusual, both among colleagues
and among the patients we served. The genomic data that informed clinical practice
reflected similar absences. Over time, four questions came to animate my work. Where
is the science needed to support Black and other minoritised communities? Why does
the genetic counselling workforce lack ethnic diversity? What does science need to do to
serve all communities fairly? And what questions could we ask of culturally diverse
communities to support their healthcare? These questions form the basis of the inquiry
presented in this thesis.

This study was conducted at the Wellcome Sanger Institute (Sanger/ The Institute)
(3.8.1), a globally influential centre for large-scale genomic research. The Institute was
one of the central sequencing centres in the Human Genome Project (HGP) and played
a decisive role in making the project possible at scale. Sanger’s role in generating
population-scale genomic datasets and its long-standing involvement in shaping
scientific approaches to human variation make it a particularly significant site for
examining how population descriptors, scientific responsibility, and notions of equity are
negotiated in practice. The Institute provided both the organisational setting for this
research and an intellectual environment in which questions about “diversity”, “inclusion”,
and scientific good practice were already under active discussion.


1|Page
Within a British context, discussions of race in science are often overshadowed by
narratives shaped in the United States (US). Although entangled with global forces, race
in Britain has its own distinctive formation rooted in empire, migration, statecraft, and
resistance. Much of the scholarship on population categories, genetics, and racial
inequalities draws from the American experience. A British perspective, therefore, offers
an opportunity to examine how racial categories are taken up, resisted, and naturalised
in scientific practice outside the dominant US frame.




1.1 Defining Race, Ethnicity, and Ancestry for this thesis


The terms race, ethnicity, and ancestry (REA) require clarity given their conceptual
instability. I have drawn together and will be using definitions of these concepts as
summarised in Table 1. For context, race refers to social categories organised by
perceived physical characteristics and geographic identity (Delgado & Stefancic, 1998).
Ethnicity describes cultural and perceived geographic origins connected through shared
practices, language, or history (Birney et al., 2021). Genetic ancestry refers to lineage
inferred through genomic markers. It is distinct from race or ethnicity, although often
correlated in ways that can mislead interpretation (Dauda et al., 2023; A. Lewis et al.,
2022). Genetic similarity refers to the measurable degree of overlap in genetic variants
between individuals or groups. It reflects statistical resemblance in the genome rather
than social or cultural identity. Genetic similarity does not imply discrete biological
boundaries (Fujimura & Rajagopalan, 2011; National Academies of Sciences, 2023).

All these terms create socially meaningful categories, but only genetic similarity is
biologically measurable. These terms are therefore used critically throughout this thesis,
recognising both their social importance and their potential to entrench health inequities
if used without reflexivity. Labels used to denote race, ethnicity, and ancestry groups are
capitalised and italicised when not used in a personal context, either by the author or
within quoted identity descriptors. This formatting reflects the treatment of these terms
as socially and politically constructed categories rather than neutral descriptors. The
conventions adopted in this thesis recognise that such labels carry historical, colonial,
and juridical significance, and that their use is shaped by power relations, institutional
practices, and political struggle. An exception is made for ‘whiteness’, which is rendered


2|Page
in lowercase and without italics to reflect its operation as an unmarked and normative
position of power rather than as a discrete identity category. Where terms appear in
quoted material, original capitalisation and formatting are retained to preserve the
positionality and intent of the source.

Although diversity and inclusion can encompass a wide range of individual and group
attributes, given the focus of this thesis, unless otherwise qualified, diversity and
inclusion here are generally framed through the lens of race, ethnicity, and ancestry.

1.2 Positionality and the Epistemic Commitments of Research

Early in my doctoral research, I met a senior figure in British medical genetics,
introduced to me as someone “sympathetic” to questions of inclusion. When I described
my project, he warned that positioning “social justice” in genomics was provocative. I
explained why that provocation was essential to this type of enquiry. His final words
stayed with me: “You are the right person to do this work.”

I did not hear encouragement. I heard permission. A White senior scientist, shaped by
his own history and institutional authority, had taken it upon himself to affirm my
legitimacy. The encounter made visible how questions of justice and, in particular, social
justice in genomics are not only epistemic but relational, shaped by the hierarchies that
govern who may speak, and with what authority. This experience also revealed a
broader truth: within many scientific spaces, the moral boundaries of genomics are
guarded by those who presume neutrality, even as they reproduce the conditions that
make neutrality impossible.

One other conversation brought this into sharp focus. During a Zoom call with one of the
United Kingdom’s first sickle cell pioneers, I mentioned my forthcoming fellowship. She
immediately referenced the 2019 accusations that Sanger had commercialised African
genomic data without consent (Stokstad, 2019). Although Sanger denied wrongdoing
and formal investigations cleared them of any breach, the incident reignited longstanding
concerns about extractive research and consent. Such controversies expose the fragility
of institutional trust and highlight how promises of open science can obscure uneven
distributions of power.

These moments resonate with my own professional encounters with pioneering Black
women whose achievements sit alongside deep awareness of the exclusions still
present within British science. My mother often remarks that we continue to live in the



4|Page
era of firsts. Being a “first Black woman” in a scientific domain brings both visibility and a
burden of representation. It is a reminder that our inclusion is still conditional.

I come to this work as a dark-skinned, British-born woman of Jamaican descent, shaped
by diasporic histories of slavery, economics, and colonialism. These forces influence not
only my own identity but the scientific structures that shape genomic research today.

The research is situated within the broader institutional climate surrounding data
diversity and the need to expand genomic research involving historically
underrepresented populations. During the period in which this thesis was being written,
Sanger also published a report outlining principles for equitable international research
collaboration (Nicholson et al., 2024) . While that document articulates an institutional
commitment to equity through guidance on data sharing, capacity building, and
partnership, it operates at the level of policy and best practice.

The conditions under which the research was funded and conducted are set out in
Appendix G. The fellowship was established through a joint initiative between Sanger
and Wellcome Connecting Science, with academic oversight provided by the University
of Cambridge.




1.3 Race, Class and Power in Britain: A Necessary Context

Understanding race in Britain requires attending to the historical and political conditions
that shaped its emergence. The emergent dynamics matter for genomics because racial
classification in science reflects and reinforces broader political hierarchies. From the
early 1950s, Caribbean, African, South Asian, and Irish migrants arrived in large
numbers into a society structured by imperial hierarchies. Britain’s post-war need for
labour coexisted with entrenched racial attitudes, producing xenophobic responses not
only to colonial subjects but also to Irish migrants, who were portrayed as poor or
undesirable (Gilroy, 2013; Walter, 2002). These dynamics reveal how whiteness in the
UK has never been uniform. White Irish and Traveller communities were frequently
racialised and subjected to discrimination, surveillance, and hostility (Bhopal, 2018;
Garner, 2003; Judd, 2009). Antisemitism, with deep historical roots, resurfaces during
periods of socio-economic instability (Tolan, 2023). Race in Britain, therefore, operates
as a hierarchy that is both fluid and internally stratified.

5|Page
The political struggles of the 1960s and 70s, led by African, Caribbean, South Asian,
Jewish, and Irish communities, responded to institutional racism, economic injustice, and
state violence (Bunce & Field, 2014; Hall, 2021; Stephenson, 2014; Waters, 2019).
During this period, political blackness emerged as a strategic alliance across these
groups. It was rooted in collective resistance and solidarity, rather than phenotype alone
(Gilroy, 2013; Virdee, 2014).

Yet, race is also shaped through class. Racial minoritisation is often conflated with
poverty or lower socio-economic position, reinforcing Stuart Hall’s argument that race
functions as a key organising principle of inequality. From the 1990s onwards, various
coalitions of political blackness declined as policy and public discourse shifted towards
recognising ethnic difference. Categories such as BAME, Brown, or People of Colour,
many influenced by American racial terminology, became prominent (Starr, 2023).
Although presented as inclusive, they lacked the political force of earlier movements and
often reduced racial difference to administrative labels (Abbas, 2019; Gill, 2024; Gilroy,
2000). Their limitations are evident in the context of resurgent nationalism. The rise of
the British National Party, the English Defence League, and digitally mobilised groups
like Britain First, along with the mobilisation of racial anxieties during the Brexit
campaign, demonstrate that racial hierarchy persists even as its expression shifts
(Solomos, 2020; Valluvan, 2019; Virdee & McGeever, 2019).

Nationalist narratives shape assumptions about belonging and human value, which
travel into scientific infrastructures, data practices, and decisions about which
populations warrant study or investment.

Racial identities are therefore experienced and understood through layered histories and
can shape generational differences in how identity is articulated. In Britain, for example,
generations who lived through the most intense periods of British racism may resist the
term “Black British” while younger generations often embrace it as a hybrid identity
signalling both belonging and critique. These tensions matter because they reflect how
categories of identity evolve, shape political consciousness, and determine how people
experience the institutions that study and govern them.

Understanding the British racial formation, therefore, sets the stage for examining how
race, ethnicity, and ancestry circulate through genomic science today.


6|Page
1.3.1   Institutional Context: Wellcome Sanger Institute and its Histories of
        Engagement with Race and Ethics


This research takes place at the Wellcome Genome Campus, home to international
leaders in genomic science, including Sanger and Wellcome Connecting Science
(WCS), which also funded my PhD fellowship. With nearly three thousand staff, the site
is one of the largest genomic research hubs in the world. It is known for its role in the
HGP and for pioneering large-scale data infrastructures. Yet the institution has also
faced public scrutiny regarding race, data, and ethics.

There is a dearth of UK data on these issues. Though not part of the formal literature
review, guided by a science historian, we both reviewed 62 genomics papers (Appendix
F) published by Sanger towards the end of the HGP (years 2001 – 2003) to gain an
overview of how concepts of race, ethnicity, and ancestry and the collection of human
population data formed part of the historical background of Sanger.

Engagement with ethics varied. Many studies relied on legacy samples, often collected
under earlier ethical regimes or from tissue banks whose origins were not discussed
(Braybrook et al., 2001; Bryan et al., 2002; Howell, 2022; Y. Liu et al., 2002). References
to consent were sparse and procedural (Gibbons et al., 2003; Lehmann et al., 2002).
The datasets overwhelmingly reflected European populations, drawn from cancer
genetics clinics and other longstanding sources such as the Breast Cancer Linkage
Consortium (Thompson et al., 2002), The Utah Centre d′Etudes du Polymorphisme
Humain (CEPH/Utah) pedigrees (Deloukas & Bentley, 2004), the North Cumbria
Community Genetics Project (Meijers-Heijboer et al., 2002)and Icelandic cohorts,
valued for their homogeneity.

In the end, papers hardly reflected on these descriptors. Instead, racialised terms
appeared as routine labels for population stratification: “Caucasian”, “Ashkenazi Jewish”,
“Welsh”, “Estonian” (Antoniou et al., 2002; Bevan et al., 2001; Dawson et al., 2002).
Their use was inconsistent and lacked ethical justification, confirming Reardon's (2009,
2017) observation that race in genomics is unaccounted for yet indispensable.




7|Page
Populations marginal to Britain’s demographic reality were central to its scientific
knowledge. Others appeared only at the margins. This form of silence became an ethical
story, indicating which forms of diversity carried scientific legitimacy and which did not.

1.3.2   Aims and Approach


This thesis unfolds as a cumulative inquiry into how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are
produced, negotiated, and stabilised within contemporary genomic science, and how
questions of justice emerge from these processes.

This thesis seeks to:

    1. Examine how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are conceptualised, operationalised,
        and contested within genomics.
    2. Analyse the institutional, methodological, and structural conditions under which
        these categories are maintained or challenged.
    3. Explore the broader ethical and political implications of classification in genomic
        science.
    4. Develop a framework through which social justice can be embedded into
        genomic research practice

It moves from conceptual and historical grounding through close empirical analysis of
everyday scientific practice to a constructive intervention that seeks to reshape how
genomic research is conducted. The thesis begins by situating the study within
interdisciplinary debates on race, human difference, and responsibility in science.
Chapter 2 traces how population categories have been historically produced and
institutionalised across genetics, medicine, and state practice, and how these legacies
continue to shape contemporary genomics. Drawing on scholarship from science and
technology studies, bioethics, sociology, and critical race theory, it establishes the
conceptual terrain for the thesis and identifies a key gap, namely the absence of UK-
based empirical work examining how genomic researchers navigate race and ancestry
in practice.

The methodological approach is then set out in Chapter 3, which explains how the
research was designed, conducted, and analysed. This chapter provides the interpretive
context for the empirical chapters that follow, detailing the analytic strategy, ethical


8|Page
considerations, and limitations of the study. I discuss how my approach is grounded in
Critical Race Theory, Critical Realism, and ethnography. These frameworks enable
analysis of both empirical practice and the underlying structures that condition it. They
allow this thesis to ask not only how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are constructed in
everyday scientific practice, but also how these constructions can be reimagined in
service of justice.

Attention then turns to how population categories appear in the formal outputs of
genomic science. Chapter 4 analyses publications involving Sanger scientists, showing
how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are constructed through inherited labels, statistical
inference, and harmonisation pressures. It demonstrates how classificatory decisions
are often stabilised upstream, travel across datasets and consortia, and are rarely
revisited in published accounts, while ethical accountability is framed largely in
procedural terms.

Building on this textual analysis, Chapter 5 moves into everyday scientific practice,
examining how power operates in communication, methods, and responsibility within the
Institute. Through interviews and ethnographic engagement, it shows how race is
frequently avoided or displaced. I describe how methodological conventions flatten
human diversity and while responsibility for equity is acknowledged it is yet often
deferred. Crucially, the chapter reveals how authority, hierarchy, and positionality shape
what can be said and done in relation to population categorisation.

Chapters 6 and 7 widen the lens further, situating these practices within broader
institutional and systemic contexts. Chapter 6 examines funding structures,
collaboration, leadership, and institutional commitment, showing how aspirations for
equity are constrained by organisational hierarchies, resource asymmetries, and
historical legacies. Chapter 7 then places Sanger within the wider genomic ecosystem,
mapping how power and autonomy circulate across funders, collaborators, regulators,
industry, and publics, and how ethical ambition is shaped by connection, dependency,
and constraint.

These strands come together in Chapter 8, with the analysis arguing that justice in
genomics is fundamentally a question of power, rather than a technical problem of
missing data alone. Drawing together empirical findings and critical theory, the chapter


9|Page
shows how racialised logics persist through classificatory practices, methodological
standards, and institutional arrangements, while also identifying points of tension,
reflexivity, and possibility.

Developed directly from the study's findings, Chapter 9 presents the JUST DATA
Framework as a justice-oriented model for genomic research. Rather than treating equity
as a retrospective ethical concern, the framework offers a way to embed justice into
research design, data practices, communication, and governance from the outset. In
doing so, it recognises social justice as the process through which equitable genomic
outcomes can be achieved, positioning it as a condition of scientific integrity and a
practical contribution to debates about responsibility, power, and inclusion in genomics

Justice in genomics, this thesis argues, must be designed into science from the start.




10 | P a g e
2 Literature Review

2.1 Background


This literature review explores the evolution of race, ethnicity, and ancestry as
categories in genomic research. It begins by tracing the historical development of racial
thinking in science, emphasising how these concepts originated within systems of
oppression, including slavery, colonialism, and eugenics and gave rise to what is now
recognised as scientific racism. These systems imposed artificial, discrete categories
onto human diversity, which were reinforced through early data practices in genetics and
remain embedded in aspects of contemporary genomic research.

Against this backdrop, it is important to recognise that human genetic variation is
naturally continuous, complex, and multidimensional (Fuentes et al., 2019; Lewontin,
1972; Rosenberg et al., 2002), more akin to a constellation than a set of bounded types
(see Figure 1).




Figure 1: The constellation of human diversity. This multidimensional, complex
constellation with difficult-to-interpret boundaries of genetic similarities represents



11 | P a g e
human population data that is not constrained by time, location or geography. Produced
by the author.

While human genetic variation displays patterns of clustering, the categorical boundaries
imposed upon it are interpretive constructs mapped onto underlying gradients, as the
following discussion will demonstrate. Over time, both conceptual and practical
reductions have simplified this human diversity into rigid classifications. These
reductions, shaped by social, political, and scientific forces, have contributed to the
chronic underrepresentation of African, Indigenous, and some Asian populations in
genomics.

Methods such as mtDNA and Y-DNA tracing (tracking genetic lineages through maternal
and paternal lines) and later autosomal studies, which examine the full genome, offered
increasingly refined means of reconstructing the picture of human ancestry. However,
these approaches were constrained by sampling biases and by restricted access to data
controlled by powerful institutions. Modern genomic research inherits many of these
inequities. Epistemic injustice (Fricker, 2007), understood here as the exclusion of
marginalised groups from shaping what counts as legitimate knowledge, continues to
determine who is studied, whose data is prioritised, and how it is classified. Policies
governing data access, sharing, and harmonisation (using technical solutions to make
heterogeneous data interoperable) frequently privilege powerful institutions and
populations deemed more convenient or accessible. The metaphor is intentional in this
respect: just as constellations are projections we impose upon the stars to create
meaning, so too are categories in genetics interpretive rather than natural. To describe
variation as “complex and multidimensional” is not empty rhetoric but a deliberate refusal
to collapse the many axes of difference into a single scale. While categories can serve
pragmatic functions in medicine or genomics, they should never be mistaken for natural
essences. Rather, they must be understood as heuristic tools that carry both scientific
utility and social consequence, demanding that we hold them with care and
transparency.

Drawing on interdisciplinary scholarship from genetics, sociology, and bioethics, this
chapter interrogates the assumptions underpinning population descriptors. It examines
the methods used to address both the statistical and ethical challenges of describing
human population data and ancestral diversity in genomic research. Race, ethnicity, and


12 | P a g e
ancestry can be associated with health and disease in complex ways (Graves Jr, 2015),
and tailored methodologies are needed to monitor and critique research on race and
genetics and their impact on health (Ford & Harawa, 2010; Frank, 2007). Hood (2022)
and Shim et al., (2014) describe how gene-environment interaction research can use
racial and other dimensions of difference as essential clues to the causes of complex
diseases.

Through exploring the discourse around these issues, I investigate how the practices of
scientists have been presented as solutions to the ethical problems arising from racial
and ethnic inequity in research, and ask when, if, and how social justice has formed part
of these approaches. The chapter concludes by laying the conceptual groundwork for an
epistemology of social justice in genomic research, an approach then examined in
greater depth in the methodology chapter.




2.2 The History of Race as a Scientific Category


Humans have long described themselves through physical appearance, language,
location, cultural practices, and behaviour, often emphasising how these characteristics
distinguish one group from another. The ways in which these distinctions have been
constructed and reinforced over time reflect a complex relationship between science,
politics, and society. (English Heritage, 2021; Lujan & DiCarlo, 2018).

The concept of race as a way to distinguish people is both enduring and pervasive in its
social effects, yet unstable in its definition. As Smedley and Smedley (2005) state, “from
its inception, race was a folk idea, a culturally invented conception about human
differences” (p. 22). This framing positions race as a historically contingent system of
classification designed to organise people into hierarchies of value. Omi and Winant’s
(2014) theory of racial formation further suggests that race is continually reshaped
through political struggle, media, and institutional practice, rather than emerging from
any fixed biological essence. Hall (2021) situates race as a “floating signifier” whose
meaning is produced through discourse and social relations, not through biology.

These perspectives underscore how the concept of race evolved to justify inequality,
naturalise difference, and stabilise power. Understanding this historical and theoretical

13 | P a g e
foundation has been essential for interrogating through this literature how contemporary
scientific disciplines, including genomics, continue to deploy and reinforce racial
categories in ways that reflect and reproduce these legacies.




2.2.1   Turning Difference into Hierarchy


The persecution of Jewish people in the 14th Century by Spanish Christians is one of
the precursors of modern ideas of distinct racial differences (Fredrickson, 2016). In early
Christianity, conversion was regarded as a pathway to salvation, offering redemption
from sin regardless of origin. However, over time, anti-Judaic sentiment evolved into a
more rigid and exclusionary ideology, which erupted in massacres of forced conversions
across the Kingdoms of Castile and Aragon. By the mid-15th century with the “limpieza
de sangre” laws ("cleanliness of blood") emerged the belief that assimilation across
cultural or ethnic boundaries was biologically impossible, marking a key distinction
between modern racism and the earlier ethnocentrism that it supplanted (Fredrickson,
2016; Hochman, 2019; Steinberg, 2001).

The notion that the unchristian were soulless influenced how European thinkers
interpreted human variation during the era of exploration and empire. (Nimako &
Willemsen, 2011). Portuguese writings from the late 15th century provide some of the
earliest examples of Africans being described as a distinct race, reducing enormous
diversity to a single enslaveable category, “others again were as Black as Ethiops, and
so ugly, both in features and in body, as almost to appear (to those who saw them) the
images of a lower hemisphere.” (Luzong, 2022, The Racist Writings of Gomes Eanes De
Zurara, Para 2) . Such racialised language helped frame chattel slavery as a uniquely
racial system and distinguish it from earlier, non-racialised forms of captivity. Only as a
slave could an African be civilised and Christianised, since before this they were “living
in the perdition of their souls.”(Fuentes, 2018 pg. 18). Christianity was thus used to
further ideas of predetermined superiority and inferiority of groups based on race or
descent (J. K. Carter, 2008; Cox, 2002; Ekechi, 1971; Goetz, 2016).

This Christian and European ideology was achieved through increased political, religious
and social dominance (Harris, 2004; L. Young, 2009). European superiority has hence
been identified as evolving into a racial hierarchy that produced today’s definitions of

14 | P a g e
White supremacy (Bonds & Inwood, 2016; Jackson, 1998; C. W. Mills & Shelby, 2022);
a structural system, rather than simply an ideological position, through which racial
hierarchies are produced and maintained, privileging those constructed as White while
subordinating other groups across social, political, and economic domains.

These emerging ideas about difference and hierarchy laid the groundwork for the
taxonomic systems that would soon formalise race within Western science.




2.2.2   Systemising Human Difference: Western Racial Taxonomies


By the 18th century, the height of the transatlantic slave trade, scientist Carolus
Linnaeus divided the human species into four major subdivisions of Homo sapiens.
Linnaeus described H. americanus and H. africanus. H. europaeus and H. asiaticus
(Linneaes, 1758). As shown in a later translation of his work, these divisions were based
on appearance and included descriptions of behavioural traits (Figure 2):




15 | P a g e
Figure 2: Carolus Linnaeus’ classifications of Homo sapiens. Translated from his
work Systema natura by Turton & Linne, 1802

In Linnaeus’s classification, physical appearance was directly tied to behavioural traits,
constructing a hierarchy that naturalised European superiority and entrenched anti-
Blackness. Homo europaeus (Europeans) were described as “fair, brawny” and
characterised as “gentle, inventive, and governed by laws,” implicitly positioning them as
the standard of civilisation. In stark contrast, Homo africanus (Africans) were defined by
their “black” appearance and attributed with traits such as being “indolent, negligent, and
crafty,” and “governed by caprice,” suggesting moral weakness and irrationality. Homo
asiaticus (Asians) were labelled “haughty” and “covetous,” while Homo americanus
(Native Americans) were described as “choleric” and “regulated by customs,” further
emphasising cultural inferiority.

Classification in this fashion, given Linnaeus was a botanist, validated the idea that
humans, like other species, could be improved by breeding (Chial, 2008; Nimako &


16 | P a g e
Willemsen, 2011). Johan Friedrich Blumenbach later categorised humans into five
categories: American, Malay, Ethiopian, Mongolian, and Caucasian, choosing
Caucasians to represent Europeans after his most beautiful discovery of a skull in
Russia’s Caucasus Mountains. (Blumenbach et al., 1865). It is possible that Blumenbach
had not intended his categories to uphold ideas of racial hierarchy when he stated, “All
national differences in the form and colour of the human body [. . .] run so insensibly, by
so many shades and transitions one into the other, that it is impossible to separate them
by any but very arbitrary limits.” (Blumenbach 1825) p. 35). Yet what endured was a
conceptual image as illustrated by Figure 3 of humanity as divisible into a small number
of discrete populations.




Figure 3. Blumenbach’s five racial categories as discrete populations: American is
represented by (blue), Malay (orange), Ethiopian (green), Mongolian (purple), and
Caucasian (yellow). Caucasian appears larger to represent its position as a site of
human origin. The visual separation reflects the historical classification of human
difference that took hold conceptually rather than any scientific reality. Produced by the
author.

While it is important to acknowledge historical justifications made for Blumenbach for
what is now recognised as a racist ideology, the act of categorisation and the power to
categorise based on ideological beliefs, such as the idea of European origin, must not be
overlooked. As Foucault (1980) suggests, systems of knowledge are inseparable from
the power structures that authorise them, meaning that classification itself becomes a


17 | P a g e
mechanism through which inequality is produced and sustained. This is central to
understanding the motivations and directions that scientific thinking would take in the
years that followed. The formalisation of these classifications not only naturalised
hierarchy but also embedded it within scientific authority, shaping the ideologies that
would define whose knowledge counted.




2.3 Ideologies and Epistemic Injustice in Science


While much of the scientific and intellectual history of the 17th and 18th centuries has
centred on European figures, there were others whose contributions were known and, to
varying degrees, respected by European scientists. For example, Ibn Sina (Avicenna)
and Alhazen (Ibn al-Haytham), though active in earlier centuries, were central figures in
medieval Islamic science and medicine and their works, such as ‘The Canon of Medicine
and Book of Optics’ continued to influence European medical and scientific thought into
the 17th century (Ahmad, 2023; Gutas, 2014). In the 18th century, Anton Wilhelm Amo,
a Ghanaian-born philosopher, earned doctorates in philosophy and law in Germany and
lectured at the universities of Halle and Jena. His treatises engaged directly with
Enlightenment philosophy and were received with respect by some European scholars.
Onesimus, an enslaved African in early 18th-century Boston, introduced the practice of
variolation (a precursor to vaccination) to Cotton Mather, a Puritan minister and member
of the Royal Society. Mather advocated for the technique during the 1721 smallpox
epidemic, citing Onesimus as the source of this life-saving knowledge, albeit reaffirming
beliefs about the simple-minded ways of Africans in his parody of their voice in ‘Angel of
Bethesda’, the medical treatise written after the epidemic (Koo, 2007; Nakayama, 2022).

Further, knowledge systems from India, China, and the Islamic world, such as herbal
medicine, astronomical tables, and algebra, were frequently translated or incorporated
into European science, though often without proper attribution (Hobson, 2004; Saliba,
2007). This pattern reflects what decolonial scholars later describe as the systematic
marginalisation of non-Western epistemologies within dominant scientific narratives
(Maldonado-Torres, 2007; Mignolo, 2011)




18 | P a g e
These few examples set the scene for a broader, often suppressed history of global
intellectual exchange and the resilience of marginalised knowledge systems in shaping
modern science (Grosfoguel, 2007). Racialised individuals’ contributions to science
continued to be marked by exploitation, undervaluation, and the increasing use of their
bodies as sites of experimentation and evidence production (Das & Lowe, 2018). As
Figure 4 shows, knowledge about human populations was generated and classified
unevenly, and against this backdrop, there is a muted constellation of more
understanding than is accessed or valued; like outdated thinking about introns in DNA,
much of it is treated as non-coding ‘junk’.




Figure 4: Distorted world map illustrating the geopolitical dimensions of epistemic
influence. Regions on the map are sized and coloured to reflect their perceived
contribution to knowledge production. Europe appears enlarged, and its influence
extends into other regions through traces of yellow, signifying the historical dominance of
European epistemic frameworks. Produced by the author.

Figures such as Sarah Baartman, a Khoikhoi woman taken from South Africa to Britain
in 1810 at the suggestion of British military surgeon Alexander (often called William)
Dunlop, exemplify how Black bodies were objectified, sexualised, and used to reinforce
White superiority. Dunlop claimed she would be exhibited for the study of her physical
features. Though not legally enslaved, the racial, colonial, and exploitative systems
underpinning slavery were fully at work in the way she was treated: as spectacle, as
scientific “evidence” of racial difference, and as property-like. When her popularity in

19 | P a g e
London declined, she was taken to Paris around 1814, exhibited by an animal trainer,
and subjected to the post-mortem examinations of Georges Cuvier, whose anatomical
investigations and displays perpetuated her objectification even after death (‘The
Significance of Sarah Baartman’, 2016).

Baartman’s treatment was part of broader 19th-century European exhibition culture in
which Black and Indigenous people were routinely displayed in human zoos, theatres,
and scientific demonstrations. These spectacles intersected with the rise of scientific
racism, where pseudo-anthropological studies and anatomical dissections ranked non-
European bodies within a racial hierarchy (Blanchard, 2008; Sadiah Qureshi, 2011). In
medicine, similar exploitation occurred in the work of J. Marion Sims, who in the 1840s
conducted repeated gynaecological surgeries on enslaved Black women without
anaesthesia, justified by racist beliefs about pain tolerance (Spettel & White, 2011). As
with the exhibition of Baartman, Sims’s experiments demonstrate how 19th-century
science leveraged racial hierarchies to legitimise exploitation. Both cases reveal how
scientific authority naturalised inequality under the guise of empirical progress,
embedding racialised exploitation within the foundations of modern disciplines (Saini,
2019).

More than examples of individual cruelty, these practices formed part of a broader
epistemic environment in which the bodies of racialised subjects were treated
simultaneously as objects of study and as sources of evidence for theories of human
difference. They exemplify what Ahmed (2002) describes as the way difference is
produced through the repetition of embodied encounters, how racialised bodies become
both the material on which power acts and the conceptual frame through which
knowledge is organised. In this sense, physical and conceptual racialisation are mutually
constitutive: the scientific gaze both constructs and confirms the racial categories
(Yancy, 2008).

It was within this intellectual and ideological climate that Count Joseph Arthur de
Gobineau, often considered one of the founding figures of modern “scientific” racism,
drew on such ‘knowledge’ to compose his Essay on the Inequality of the Human Races
(Biddiss, 1970). Gobineau argued that human history was driven by racial differences,
claiming that the “White race” was superior in intelligence and warned of civilisation
decline by race mixing.


20 | P a g e
Charles Darwin’s theory of evolution, On the Origin of the Species (Darwin, 1859) was
published within this intellectual environment and, while he did not understand the
genetic basis of inheritance, his focus on variation, selection, and heritability laid
essential groundwork for modern genetics. Importantly, the production of scientific
knowledge continued to rely upon contributions from individuals whom Victorian society
deemed intellectually inferior to European minds. Darwin had learned taxidermy in 1826
from John Edmonstone (Figure 5), a formerly enslaved man from British Guiana, while
studying in Edinburgh (Desmond & Moore, 2009; Freeman, 1978; McNish, 2022).


  ‘Image removed due to copyright restrictions. The original artwork 'In the Workshop
  of Taxidermist' (1948) can be viewed at the State Darwin Museum ‘




Figure 5 Artist’s impression of John Edmonstone teaching Darwin to preserve
birds. By © State Darwin Museum. https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/john-
edmonstone-the-man-who-taught-darwin-taxidermy.html

In a letter to his sister, Darwin wrote: “I am going to learn to stuff birds, from a
blackamoor… he only charges one guinea… for an hour every day for two months”
(Darwin, 1826, as cited in Cain, 2021). Darwin himself opposed slavery and did not
subscribe to polygenism - the idea that races had separate origins. He rejected the
notion that Black people were a different species. Yet despite these progressive
positions for his time, Darwin’s own writings were still embedded in Eurocentric
hierarchies. He frequently used language such as “civilised” and “savage,” and framed
certain non-European populations as living representatives of earlier stages of human
development. Edmonstone’s legacy, like those of many non-European contributors,
remains marginalised, lending scientific authority to racial hierarchies. Thus, even where
Darwin sought to challenge overtly racist doctrines and slavery, his work contributed,
willingly or not, to an intellectual climate that would later enable and legitimise eugenics
(Desmond & Moore's (2009).

In this context, knowledge production was inseparable from systems of domination
(Maldonado-Torres, 2007; Mignolo, 2011), making epistemic injustice not only about


21 | P a g e
who is denied recognition, but also about who is objectified and instrumentalised in the
very processes that confer scientific authority (Ashby & Machin, 2021).

Together, these histories show that race was never a neutral category but a construct
carrying both epistemic and ethical weight. This raises the question of whether such
dynamics created the conditions for race science to emerge as a cornerstone of state
policy across much of the Western world, an idea the following section explores,
particularly in relation to its role as an ideological foundation for eugenic practices that
endured well into the 20th century.




2.3.1   Extending Racial Hierarchy into Policy


Francis Galton, a Victorian polymath known for his work in statistics, psychology and the
early development of heredity studies, emerged as a central figure in formalising eugenic
thought.

Coined by Galton in 1883, eugenics was the idea that breeding should be encouraged
among those with desirable traits and discouraged among those with undesirable traits
(Galton, 1883). While these categories were not exclusively racial, they frequently
intersected with racial hierarchies, with Black, Indigenous, and other marginalised
populations disproportionately targeted and stigmatised as carriers of these undesirable
traits (Crossley, 2022; Kevles, 1995).

Working during a period of strong imperial self-assurance, Galton argued that human
traits, social standing and even moral character were primarily determined by
inheritance. Galton’s promotion of eugenics linked ideas of biological superiority to class
and race, presenting these claims as scientific principles. His influence demonstrated
how the developing field of genetics could be used to justify existing hierarchies and to
reinforce the economic and political power structures of empire.

As Britain expanded its imperial reach across Africa, Asia, and the Caribbean, it
exported and embedded racial hierarchies foundational to the functioning of the empire.
Antisemitism, Islamophobia, and the construction of Blackness as inherently inferior




22 | P a g e
were pivotal in shaping British racial thought, feeding into a larger imperial logic that
presented White, European Christian identity as superior and normative.

The eugenics movement entered European and North American society at a time when
thoughts around superior and inferior races were already embedded (Stepan, 1982;
Turda, 2010).

Although Black scholars such as Kelly Miller, W. Montague Cobb, and W. E. B. Du Bois
actively contested biologistic racism, they advocated a form of “eugenics without racism”
aimed at sociopolitical uplift by the early 20th century. Influential American sociologists
like W. I. Thomas of the Chicago School similarly questioned racial determinism. Failed
attempts to contest racial hierarchy reveal the significant institutional power Eugenic
ideologies retained within science and public policy (Lothrop Stoddard, 1929; Nuriddin,
2021).

These ideas are accepted as reaching their most devastating expression in Nazi
Germany, where eugenics became state policy, used to justify genocide and racial
cleansing, culminating in the Holocaust and World War II (Rutherford, 2021). It was only
in the aftermath of these atrocities that a widespread rejection of race as a valid
biological category began to take hold within mainstream science (Omi & Winant, 2014).
Eugenics was eventually overtly discredited not solely for its scientific inaccuracy, but
because of its devastating ethical implications and association with fascism (Farrall,
1979; Fogarty, 2015).

Eugenics in Britain at its peak was not a narrow pseudoscience but a broad moral
project from which genetics is right to distance itself. Its legacies extend into disability,
mental health, intellectual capacity, and reproductive autonomy, the very terrains in
which clinical genetics (the application of genetics to medical care (Clinical Genetics
Society, 2022) ) came to operate into the 21st century. As clinical genetics evolved,
eugenic practices persisted in subtler but still coercive forms such as using sterilisation
as a way of preventing ‘inferior’ populations from reproducing (Kevles, 1995). In 1996, a
Canadian court found that Leilani Muir had been wrongfully sterilised by the Alberta
government (Wahlsten, 1997). In 2013, it was revealed that African American and Latina
women had been forcibly sterilised in California state prisons (Roth & Ainsworth, 2015).




23 | P a g e
As recently as 2017, a U.S. judge was reprimanded for offering reduced jail sentences in
exchange for sterilisation (Hawkins, 2021).

Darwin’s theory of common ancestry and natural variation was gradually co-opted into
racial classification, repurposed to justify hierarchy, exclusion, and harm (Kevles, 1995).
Scientific ideas, even when not intended to do so, have historically served political
agendas. Questions of transparency, legitimacy, and power must therefore be
continually re-examined as genomic technologies expand (Clarke & Wallgren-
Pettersson, 2019).

Thus, even as figures like geneticist Richard Lewontin challenged the biological basis of
race in the 1970s, arguing that “human racial classification is of no social value,
positively destructive of social and human relations”, (Lewontin, 1972 pp. 381–398),
decades later the structures built on earlier misinterpretations of evolutionary theory
remained socially and institutionally entrenched. In Britain, these global and scientific
currents intersect with class and capitalism, embedding racial ideology within the very
structure of economic and social life.




2.3.2   Race in Medicine and Science



These long-standing constructions of race, identity, and hierarchy set the stage for
examining how race continues to operate within medicine and science. Contemporary
racial disparities in Britain underscore the persistence of structural racism. In this thesis,
structural racism is understood as the normalised operation of social, legal, political, and
institutional structures that systematically advantage some racial groups while
disadvantaging others, thereby reproducing racial inequality across time and context
(Crenshaw, 1988; Jones, 2000; Delgado & Stefancic, 2017). In this context the legacies
of eugenics continue to shadow debates over health, disability, and genetic research.
These disparities are especially apparent in the data obtained through genomic research
(qualitative and quantitative), which go on to inform the delivery of clinical genetics
services. Clinical genetics research has primarily been carried out on populations
described by researchers as ‘White European’ (Bustamante et al., 2011; Morales et al.,
2018; Need & Goldstein, 2009). In addition, treating race as a simple explanatory


24 | P a g e
category rather than a complex one yields research findings insufficient to establish
equitable clinical services (Bonham et al., 2009).

The legacy of colonial medicine continues to shape how racialised bodies are
understood and treated in healthcare systems. Racial bias in UK healthcare has deep
historical roots, evident in areas such as pain management and the delayed recognition
of diseases like sickle cell anaemia in Black patients (All-Party Parliamentary Group on
Sickle Cell and Thalassaemia & Sickle Cell Society, 2021). The COVID-19 pandemic
revealed stark inequities in the practice and materiality of medicine: pulse oximeters,
used to measure oxygen levels, were found to be less accurate on darker skin tones, an
example of how racial bias in technology can have life-threatening consequences
(Sjoding et al., 2020). Similarly, outdated and poorly evidenced clinical guidelines, such
as those adjusting for race in diagnosing and treating hypertension, reflect a flawed
biological understanding of race and the persistence of racial thinking in medical practice
(Gopal, 2020).

In contrast, the increased carrier frequencies identified for Tay Sachs in the Ashkenazi
Jewish population, Cystic Fibrosis in Western Europeans, and Sickle Cell Anaemia in
the African and African diaspora populations (African-Caribbean and African American)
led to targeted genetic screening for individuals from these populations. Although
targeted screening is employed to best meet the health needs of specific populations,
we must exercise caution that such uses of race and ethnicity risk deepening health
disparities by further marginalising minoritised groups (Dobson & Arjunan, 2021; Kahn,
2008). Indeed, there are examples where decisions made using data classification for
targeted health care are lifted, without challenge, into a clinical setting (Gopal, 2020;
Lujan & DiCarlo, 2018).

Precision medicine is presented as the future of genetics and as a means of meeting the
health needs of all patients. As former UK Chief Medical Officer Dame Sally Davies
stated: ”[t]he age of precision medicine is now, and the NHS must act fast to keep its
place at the forefront of global science”’ (Von Radowitz, 2017 Para 7). Recent national
strategies reinforce this view by positioning genomics as central to the future of UK
healthcare. The 2022 Accelerating Genomic Medicine report frames genomics as a tool
for earlier diagnosis, targeted treatment and more equitable access to care (Hill &
Prichard, 2022). The NHS 2025 Ten Year Plan echoes this vision, presenting genomics


25 | P a g e
as integral to prevention, personalisation and the UK’s ambition to lead globally (UK
Government, Department of Health and Social Care, 2025). Together, these documents
articulate a clear belief that genomics should become a routine and equitable part of
NHS practice.

However, the diversity in databases, access to testing and underlying socioeconomic
disparities affecting disease outcomes have all been highlighted as limitations to the
promise of precision medicine and its ability to reduce health disparities (Geneviève et
al., 2020; Madhusoodanan, 2020; Roberts, 2011).

Genetic research, particularly Genome-Wide Association studies (GWAS), have
included data from mostly self-identified but also researcher-ascribed categories of
White European or European (M. C. Mills & Rahal, 2020; Need & Goldstein, 2009). For
GWAS, substantial amounts of data are needed from the populations in which they are
conducted, and the relative size of a population could skew the interpretation.

Populations underrepresented in genomic datasets are more likely to get uninformative
genetic test results (Fraiman & Wojcik, 2021; Kurian et al., 2018; Landry & Rehm, 2018;
Richards et al., 2015). The predictive performance of non-European ancestry-derived
polygenic scores is low or may not even be transferable to European populations (Babb
de Villiers et al., 2020; Curtis, 2018; Martin et al., 2019). Variants may even cause
different phenotypic effects in other populations; disease modifiers can go unexplained,
or stronger associations inferred (Sirugo et al., 2019). In a clinical setting, these
differences result in delayed diagnosis, treatment, and management often in groups with
pre-existing poor healthcare outcomes (C. M. Lewis & Vassos, 2020).

However, clarifying the degree of genetic diversity needed poses a statistical challenge
(Jacob, 2022). For example, due to numerous complex social and environmental factors,
African populations and those of African ancestry exhibit significant yet largely
understudied genetic variation (Mathias et al., 2016). Solutions to manage large datasets
in genomics will need to remain a priority (Balazka & Rodighiero, 2020; Floridi, 2012) not
only to power the interpretative capacity of genomic research but to provide fair
outcomes to all (Babb de Villiers, 2019; Hardcastle et al., 2022).




26 | P a g e
2.3.3 The British Genomic Initiatives and the Racial Politics of Inclusion

The UK has long occupied a leading role in global genetic research, a position reinforced
by its imperial legacy, sustained institutional investment, and powerful scientific
infrastructure. (Claussnitzer et al., 2020). The HGP, was significantly driven by British
institutions such as the Wellcome Trust (Wellcome) and the Wellcome Sanger Centre
(later Institute), which quickly became a central node in the global sequencing network
early on in the development of the project (Reardon, 2017). The Wellcome Sanger
Centre contributed approximately one-third of the human genome's first draft (Wellcome
Sanger Institute, 2020). The language around that human genome was often ‘the book
of humankind,’ a metaphor that suggested neutrality and universality (Bodmer & McKie,
1995; Wellcome Sanger Institute, 2000).

It was often noted (and still is) that humans are 99.9% genetically identical, which at the
time helped popularise the idea that a single reference genome could represent
everyone. U.K. Prime Minister at the time, Sir Tony Blair, echoed this narrative when
announcing the success of the HGP, emphasising that human beings differ by only
0.01% at the DNA level (Schatz & Langmead, 2013). As such, scientists pursued the
idea of a "universal" genome, one meant to stand in for all humanity. As Robert
Waterston, a key figure in the HGP, explained:

“We wanted the human genome... not Joe Blow’s genome. It’s your genome. It’s my
genome. It’s representative of everybody’s genome” (Smart, 2024 para. 20).

In practice, however, the initial reference genome was overwhelmingly based on the
DNA of a single anonymous donor, comprising approximately 70–75% of the sequence,
with limited contributions from a handful of others. After considerable ethical debate over
consent and data use, the human reference genome was stated to be drawn largely
from a single donor, whose DNA was described to have 37% African and 57% European
ancestry and sourced predominantly from one bacterial artificial chromosome library
(Green et al., 2010). The debates and obscurity of identity suggest that, rather than
intentional or critically stated choice, the ideal of a universal genome was, instead, built
on assumptions of scientific neutrality that overlooked structural privilege and the
dominance of White, Western population approaches in genomic sampling. Early HGP
planners, it seems, gave little attention to racial and ethnic diversity or critical questions


27 | P a g e
about representation. By the early 2000s, Sanger’s publications treated race and
ancestry as technical variables rather than social categories (see section 1.3.1).

Outside the UK, efforts such as the Human Genome Diversity Project (HGDP), sought to
capture a broader range of human variation (Cavalli-Sforza, 2005) but faced sustained
ethical criticism for the ways in which it grouped and engaged with Indigenous and
minority communities(Claw et al., 2018), as well as for the potential misuse of findings in
discriminatory ways (M’Charek, 2005; Reardon, 2005; Roberts, 2011). This moment
marked a shift in awareness: scientists began to acknowledge which populations had
been left out, and the ethical necessity of naming, recruiting, and representing them in
genomic research (Bentley et al., 2017; Hindorff et al., 2018; Reardon, 2009).

More recently, genomics in Britain has often advanced with limited scrutiny of the racial
logics embedded in its practices. Projects such as the UK Biobank, which began in 2006
and now holds data from over 500,000 individuals, remain heavily skewed toward
individuals of European ancestry (M. C. Mills & Rahal, 2020). The lack of diversity in
early datasets and unclear strategies for inclusion raised questions about whose
genomes were being prioritised and why (Bentley et al., 2017; Popejoy & Fullerton,
2016).

The 100,000 Genomes Project, launched by Genomics England, was initially heralded
as a transformative initiative linking genomic and health data across the UK population
and performed better on diversity than earlier efforts. Our Future Health similarly aligns
with the global push to broaden participation in genomic research (Cook et al., 2025).
Yet the reliance on broad census categories and limited engagement with social
inequalities and the distribution of genetic diversity make this pursuit far more complex
than simply increasing representation (Corpas, Guio, et al., 2025)

Genomics England's Diverse Data, (2024) initiative aims to address these longstanding
imbalances by actively recruiting underrepresented groups into genomic research. Yet
even these initiatives have not explicitly engaged with the underlying structures that
have historically excluded certain groups from knowledge production.

As Britain continues to invest in genomic science, it must therefore reckon with the
socio-political foundations on which this science rests. Scientific objectivity cannot be
presumed when the categories used to define and sort human populations are

28 | P a g e
themselves products of a racialised history, pointing to the need to interrogate how the
very groups used to describe and capture diversity are communicated and evolve, and
how their use continues to reproduce inequality.




2.4 Language about Human Populations

The growing recognition of bias in human genomic datasets raised complex and
consequential questions about how populations are named, conceptualised, and
labelled, decisions that are central to the production and interpretation of scientific
knowledge (Panofsky & Bliss, 2017). Categorisation, therefore, remains deeply
embedded in research design, data analysis, and interpretation. These categories
reflect, and in turn reinforce, cultural assumptions about human difference (Bliss, 2011;
Lorusso & Boniolo, 2008).

While it is widely accepted that humans share 99.9 per cent of their DNA, this concept
has been shaped by geographical and scientific necessities that emphasise how
patterned differences along broad ancestral lines still matter for understanding human
diversity. Such diversity is often difficult to discern when viewed at a broad scale but
becomes clearer with closer and more detailed examination. Figure 6 provides a visual
illustration of this point.




29 | P a g e
Figure 6. We are 99.9 % the same, but ancestry divides us. This conceptual
illustration shows a gradient of human diversity in which colours blend across regions
rather than forming discrete boundaries. From a distance the pattern appears uniform,
but closer inspection reveals subtle variation, representing how ancestral differences
emerge only with more detailed examination. Produced by the author.

In Byeon et al., 2021 which explored the use of population categories in the American
Journal of Medical Genetics between the years 1949 and 2018 (see figure 7), authors
describe the trends they observed, enabling ‘ongoing reflection as the terminology used
to describe genetic variation continues to evolve’.

Figure 7: Use of population categories in the American Journal of Medical

other population descriptors (A-J) Yearly percentage of 200-token segments (pink) an

‘Caucasian’ (F), ‘European’ (G), ‘Hispanic’ (H), ‘Latina/o/x’ (I), and ‘Negro’ (J) from 1949
