International (CC BY 4.0) License

Figure 7 shows an increase in the term European and a decline in the use of Negro, not
accompanied by a corresponding rise in the use of African or other related terms. This
suggests that Negro and African may have referred to distinct concepts or populations,
which is unlikely. Moreover Negro, historically used in the U.S. to describe African
American populations, appears not to have been simply replaced with African but
instead phased out without a clear successor term for the same group. Possibly
revealing US dominance in science and the discourse of race in science. These multiple
readings are important to note as they bring light to the limitations of quantitative
analysis, which, by design, cannot fully capture the meaning, context, or intent behind
the use of specific terms. These historical mappings of human population labels are
helpful to consider the social and scientific shifts influencing the trends identified in the
data.

For example, the study did not include terms such as Black, White, or Native American,
citing difficulties with statistical significance and the complexity of these categories. This
omission significantly limits its capacity to examine how racism may shape the use of
population descriptors in scientific literature, reflecting the broader challenges of
addressing race and racism directly in this field. This study also reveals late adoption of
terms such as Latina/o/x and Hispanic as a research category, a further example that
highlights how entire groups can be essentially absent from scientific research and
discovery, limiting what we can know or understand about them, including the inequities
they face due to this lack of inclusion (González Burchard et al., 2005; Montoya, 2011;
Schulz et al., 2003).

Overall, the narrow range of terms available to the authors of this paper point to
prevailing assumptions about which groups constitute the “major” categories of human
populations. These assumptions, like the categories themselves, are historically
contingent and subject to change over time. Gibbon, (2016) argues that genomic
researchers are not outside the social construction of race and ethnicity.

While some scientists may argue that trends shown in this paper should move to
removing any discrete, biologically fixed racial groupings (1000 Genomes Project
Consortium et al., 2015; Collins & Mansoura, 2001; Yu et al., 2001; Zhao Zhongming et

32 | P a g e
al., 2000), others argue that including race in scientific studies can highlight inequality
(Bustamante et al., 2011). The continued use of outdated and biologically suspect terms
like “Caucasian”, however raises questions about awareness, privilege, and the scientific
literacy to deal with these questions of inequality (Popejoy, 2021).

How scientists report race, ethnicity, and ancestry becomes additionally important
because such work can be misinterpreted or used to support harmful narratives
(Forzano et al., 2021; Novembre & Barton, 2018). Despite researchers issuing clear
statements against the misuse of genetic findings, distorted interpretations continue to
circulate in public discourse and are taken up by groups seeking to claim biological
differences between races (Carlson, 2022; J. J. Lee et al., 2018; Racimo et al., 2018;
Smart, 2022).

As race and ethnicity descriptors fail as accurate proxies for the biological classification
of human populations (Hunt & Megyesi, 2008b; Kaufman & Cooper, 1995) allelic
variation (differences in the versions of a gene found in a population), which is more
closely tied to geography, can reveal more robust biological associations. However,
flaws in sampling and study design, which all require description of groups may result in
findings still fall short of truly representative findings (Fine et al., 2005; A. Fuentes et al.,
2019). Though population labelling is governed to some extent by a researcher's
individual intent, institutional, disciplinary, and regulatory environments within which
researchers operate have a great influence. (Jacob, 2022; Sankar et al., 2015).

Simply eliminating racial terminology without addressing underlying biases could
backfire; ignoring race entirely may allow structural racism and its effects to remain
unexamined (G. Dawson, 2003; Harden, 2021). Rather than removing racial categories,
many now advocate that a more critical understanding of race and its intersections with
genomic science is necessary if health inequalities are to be addressed (Gurdasani et
al., 2019; Mersha & Abebe, 2015; Tishkoff & Kidd, 2004). Grappling with these
challenges is an ethical imperative for genomics, given the discipline’s aspirations to
improve health for all populations. As Hinterberger, (2012) argues attention should shift
from population labels to the population concept itself, since even “individualised”
genomic medicine depends on defining and acting on populations, however they are
constructed.




33 | P a g e
How groups are constructed in genomic research is perhaps just as ethically
consequential as the labels applied to them, since both processes shape how human
variation is categorised and understood. In genomics, where statistical association is a
primary tool of genetic ancestry inference (A. Lewis et al., 2022), such classificatory
decisions take on amplified significance, shaping both the scientific record and the social
meanings attached to genetic variation.




2.5 Constructing Groups in Genomic Research


Genomic researchers employ statistical methods to discover and contribute to the
knowledge of genes and their function in human populations. While these methods can
identify broad patterns, they are constrained by the diversity and quality of the reference
datasets. When applied uncritically, such analyses can inadvertently reify socially
constructed categories, giving the false impression of discrete population boundaries, or
overemphasise differences while downplaying shared variation (Peterson et al., 2019) .
The need to aggregate population data to create groups for comparison echoes earlier
forms of racial grouping, although approached with greater awareness of the underlying
complexity and sharedness of human populations, as illustrated in Figure 8.




34 | P a g e
Figure 8: Clustering still requires drawing boundaries. Statistical methods reduce
complex genetic ancestry data into clusters of similarity, the 5 colours chosen here reflect
the genetic ancestry labels and their relation to Blumenbach’s earlier discussed 5 racial
groupings. Produced by the author.




2.5.1   Tools for Making Groups


Clustering of individuals depends not on the revelation of fixed, natural divisions but on
the method used, the scale of analysis, and the sampling strategy (Rosenberg et al.,
2002; Serre & Pääbo, 2004). Some have noted that while genetic ancestry in scientific
discourse is perceived as having a stronger biological basis, it reflects only recent
migration patterns (A. Lewis et al., 2022).

While statistical approaches to clustering human genetic data may show some
correlations or associations between population groups, many studies report no
significant variability that neatly distinguishes large populations (Belbin et al., 2018; A.
Lewis et al., 2022). As the size and diversity of datasets increase, the continuous
variation in previously apparent differences in traits such as intelligence, behaviour, or
even physical characteristics increases in visibility.

Principal component analysis (PCA) is a statistical technique that reduces high-
dimensional genetic data into a small number of components that capture the greatest
variance, allowing researchers to visualise broad patterns of similarity among individuals
or groups. In human genetics, PCA is commonly used to summarise genomic structure
and to identify large scale gradients of ancestry (Patterson et al., 2006).

While these types of technique generalise patterns of genetic similarity, they struggle to
capture the continuous and overlapping nature of human ancestry, which rarely aligns
with discrete clusters. PCA’s reliance on researcher decisions about which samples to
include or exclude can introduce artificial boundaries, limiting its ability to represent the
full complexity of human variation (Lawson et al., 2018).

Other tools used to trace ancestry, such as the ancestral recombination graph (ARG) do
not require categorisation by population or contextualisation through geographical or
cultural identity (Speidel et al., 2019). They reveal a fundamental contradiction between

35 | P a g e
scientific insight and scientific practice, however. ARG ancestry estimation still almost
always imposes categories, defaulting to continental labels that limit both the accuracy
and the explanatory power of the resulting data (Lewis et al., 2022). Mathieson & Scally,
(2020) have similarly critiqued the limitations of current approaches, underscoring the
inadequacy of existing models in capturing the true spectrum of human genetic ancestry.




2.5.2    Inconsistent Approaches


The inconsistent and often ad hoc application of population categories further
complicates the picture (Bustamante et al., 2011; Hunt & Megyesi, 2008a; Yudell et al.,
2016).

Popejoy and Fullerton, (2016) identified considerable heterogeneity in how populations
were constructed across genomic projects. Panofsky and Bliss (2017) examine how
geneticists label populations and identify eight classification systems based on racial,
geographic, and ethnic logics. They find that these systems were often blended
inconsistently, with growing use of quasi-racial “continental” terms over time. They
suggest that even inconsistency in categorisation can be interpreted as a form of
individual scientific autonomy. Nonetheless, many scholars support the use of more
clearly defined and transparent categories as a safeguard against misuse and
misinterpretation (Ali-Khan et al., 2011; Caulfield et al., 2009; Yudell et al., 2016).

In large-scale genomic initiatives such as the HapMap and 1000 Genomes projects, both
designed to accelerate variant discovery and data sharing, differences in data filtering
and inconsistent ethnicity categorisation left many variants uncatalogued and rendered
datasets inadequate for certain populations (Buchanan et al., 2012).

The HGDP’s controversial legacy informed the design of the International HapMap
Project, launched in 2002 as a separate initiative but one shaped by the earlier political
fallout. HapMap aimed to improve representation by sampling multiple continental
populations and investing in community engagement. Yet, while it included globally
distributed groups such as the Yoruba, Han Chinese, Europeans, and Japanese, these
categories were often abstracted from their broader historical and social contexts (Adsit-
Morris et al., 2023). In The Postgenomic Condition (2017), Reardon critiques this as


36 | P a g e
“bounded” ethical engagement, addressing concerns about inclusion through targeted
consultation and procedural safeguards, while avoiding the more difficult task of
confronting how power relations shape genomic knowledge, including who defines
population categories, to what ends, and with what consequences.

This bounded approach, together with methodological inconsistencies carried forward
into projects like 1000 Genomes, has left unresolved the core challenge: how to pursue
genomic progress without reproducing historical patterns of exclusion and exploitation.
These legacies continue to inform current debates over diversity in large-scale initiatives
such as the UK Biobank and Genomics England, where questions of who is included,
how, and why remain as politically and ethically charged as ever.




2.5.3   Epistemic Approaches to Sampling


The Human Genome Project’s (HGP) Ethical, Legal, and Social Implications (ELSI)
programme initially treated genomics as an unquestionable public good, focusing largely
on technical concerns such as data privacy, while leaving deeper issues of equity
unexamined (Adsit-Morris et al., 2023; Reardon, 2009).

Jenny Reardon’s Race to the Finish (2005) and later The Postgenomic Condition (2017)
argue that genomics has never been independent of questions of race, identity, and
power. Analysis of the HGDP reveals how even well-intentioned diversity initiatives could
misstep: its plan to sample “isolated indigenous populations” provoked strong protests
and was described by critics as a neo-colonial “vampire project” exploiting marginalised
groups (Reardon, 2005; TallBear, 2013). These controversies exposed the field's
persistent difficulty in addressing the politics of sampling and representation. Genomics,
far from operating within a post-racial (Bonilla-Silva, 2022) landscape in which race is
assumed to have lost its social and political significance, necessarily embeds social
decisions about how human differences are defined and valued. Reardon further argues
that the HGDP framed diversity as something for science to preserve, a stance that held
the authority of genomic institutions while avoiding more disruptive questions about how
knowledge production itself.




37 | P a g e
These examples highlight that sampling strategies are never purely technical; they
emerge from existing epistemic assumptions about who can be studied, who is available
for extraction, and what counts as meaningful human difference.

Moreover, the structures of scientific practice often perpetuate exclusion through
mechanisms such as “helicopter science” where research is conducted in and on
marginalised communities without meaningful collaboration or credit for local scholars
and the erasure of indigenous knowledge systems (Atutornu et al., 2022). These
practices not only reproduce hierarchies in authorship and career advancement but also
undermine the epistemic legitimacy of alternative ways of knowing.

These broader structures intersect with the scientific assumptions researchers bring into
the laboratory long before sampling decisions are made. It has long been clear how
genetics is entwined with understandings of identity and human difference, and that this
is not a question of the ‘consequences’ of research, but of the decisions and
assumptions made long before samples are even collected (Rabinow, 2002)

The ways in which scientists categorise, cluster, and analyse data remain grounded in
cultural assumptions about human difference (Bliss, 2011). Educational experiences
shape these assumptions long before researchers ever enter a laboratory. Donovan
(2017) found that adolescents, though able to articulate race as a social construct,
frequently reverted to biological explanations. Jamieson and Radick (2017) similarly
showed that determinist thinking among university students was shaped by early
curricular exposure to biological essentialism. If racial determinism becomes embedded
at the educational stage, its legacy may influence how researchers pose questions,
design studies, and interpret results. Research in science education suggests that these
epistemic habits are cultivated through curricular framings that often reproduce
hierarchical and Eurocentric models of knowledge (Gandolfi, 2021), while limiting
opportunities for students to interrogate how scientific categories acquire authority
(Allchin, 2020; Waight et al., 2022). The implications of these inherited frameworks may
extend beyond scientific practice itself. As seen in Rabinow's (1996) account of
biosociality, biological classifications can become the basis for new forms of social
identity and collective belonging. In this sense, educationally inherited essentialist
frameworks may shape not only how knowledge is produced, but also the social




38 | P a g e
meanings and identities that emerge around genomic understandings of difference
(Rabinow, 2002).

The discussion so far has focused on race, ethnicity and genetic ancestry as
mechanisms by which inequities are reproduced. However, these are no doubt part of a
broader set of scientific practices; workforce diversity, unequal access to resources, and
extractive research models that together sustain the structural hierarchies within which
science operates.

Who is present within the research workforce shapes what is imagined as scientifically
important and which populations are viewed as accessible, legitimate, or visible in
genomic sampling.

Representation within the research workforce compounds these epistemic concerns.
The underrepresentation of Black, Asian, and Minority Ethnic scientists in the UK is well
documented. Of the 11.3% “non-white” scientists at Sanger just 0.7% identified as Black,
compared to Cambridge’s 3.3% population baseline (S. Ahmed, personal
communication, May 2022), mirroring national disparities (Adedokun et al., 2016;
Bonham & Green, 2021; Higher Education Statistics Agency, 2021). Gibney (2022)
reports that fewer than 1% of UK university professors are Black, and that Black
students remain significantly less likely to progress to postgraduate research compared
to their White peers. These disparities are common in scientific institutions, where
leadership and senior research roles remain overwhelmingly White. Such demographic
imbalances risk narrowing the perspectives that inform research agendas, leading to
genomic science that primarily serves dominant populations (Gibney, 2022).

As Marks, (2012) notes, traditional science often defines validity as a statistical fit
between theory and reality, a narrow epistemic frame that sidelines other forms of insight
(Gandolfi, 2019). Alternative epistemologies could therefore challenge this reductionism.
As Feyerabend, (1981) argued that what makes science distinctive is not its methods,
but its epistemic structure, its ways of claiming and validating knowledge.

The challenge, then, is not simply to diversify participation in science, but to transform its
epistemic foundations. These critiques suggest that sampling practices are not only
technical procedures but reflections of deeper epistemic commitments, institutional
power, and structural inequities.


39 | P a g e
A justice-oriented epistemology in genomics would centre pluralism in values, reflexivity
in method, and accountability in practice. It would confront the dominance of European
datasets and push for nuanced, context-specific descriptors that reflect social realities,
not false biological taxonomies. As Broussard (2023) argues in the case of technology,
injustice in science is often systemic and not incidental. When populations are
mislabelled or omitted entirely, genomic research can reinforce hierarchies under the
guise of neutrality.

The following section explores the question of scientific responsibility, considering what it
might mean for genomic researchers to take seriously their social and moral obligations
in the face of structural injustice.




2.6 Scientific Responsibility


The historical construction of race and the attendant hierarchies discussed in Sections
2.3.2 and 2.3.3 suggest an ethical and epistemic responsibility to critically examine the
inherited structures that continue to shape contemporary scientific practice. This position
aligns with broader calls for greater scientific responsibility and reflexivity (Forssén et al.,
2011) . Douglas & Branch, (2024)argue that the mid-twentieth-century ideal of science
as value-free and detached from social responsibility is no longer tenable. In their view,
the principle of "freedom from responsibility" must give way to one of "freedom with
responsibility", echoing Panofsky & Bliss's, (2017) account of science's self-regulating
authority and prompting a reconsideration of the social contract between science and
society. Similarly, Graves et al., (2022) contend that scientists, as members of society,
have responsibilities that extend beyond advancing disciplinary knowledge to advancing
the broader needs of humankind. Meeting this challenge requires more than technical
expertise. It demands an understanding of the social contexts within which genomic
science operates and a willingness to interrogate how established methodologies,
classificatory systems, and inherited assumptions may undermine, rather than enhance,
equitable outcomes. If population is, as Hinterberger, (2012) argues through a
Foucauldian lens, a problem of power and politics, then responsibility in genomics must
include examining how populations are constituted across scientific, institutional,

40 | P a g e
funding, and ethical infrastructures, not merely how research findings are
communicated.

While many scientific institutions explicitly articulate socially oriented missions (Genome
Research Limited, 2022; The Francis Crick Institute, 2025; The Royal Society, 2025) ,
scientists themselves are often motivated by diverse and sometimes competing
priorities, including career progression, publication metrics, disciplinary norms,
intellectual curiosity, and public benefit (Besley & Nisbet, 2013; Rainie, 2015). As a
result, social responsibility is frequently treated as distinct from the responsible conduct
of research, which remains largely focused on procedural obligations such as producing
reliable results, avoiding misconduct, sharing findings, and mentoring future researchers
(Pimple, 2002; Weed & McKeown, 2003). Yet questions of scientific responsibility
inevitably raise questions of justice. Determining what responsibilities scientists have, to
whom they are owed, and how they should be enacted requires engagement with
broader theories of social justice.

Social justice has been conceptualised through a range of theoretical traditions within
the social sciences. Rawls & Kelly, (2001) theory of justice as fairness provides a
foundational account of distributive justice, focusing on the equitable distribution of
rights, opportunities, and resources through institutions that secure equal liberties, fair
equality of opportunity, and arrangements that benefit the least advantaged. Fraser
(2009) extends this framework by arguing that justice requires not only redistribution but
also recognition and representation, drawing attention to the cultural and political
dimensions of inequality. Honneth(1996) similarly foregrounds recognition as a condition
for social participation and human flourishing, while Young, (1990) critiques what she
terms the "distributive paradigm", arguing that justice must also address structural
processes of oppression, marginalisation, powerlessness, and cultural domination.
Extending these critiques, Mills (2005, 2017) argues that liberal theories of justice often
abstract from the historical realities of colonialism, slavery, and racial domination, such
that ideal theory can "ignore, marginalise, or trivialise the distinctive experience of the
oppressed" (Mills, 2005p.168). Collectively, these approaches shift attention from the
distribution of resources towards the operation of power within institutions, social
structures, and systems of knowledge production.




41 | P a g e
These critiques are particularly relevant to genomic medicine. Advancing social justice
requires more than individual integrity or procedural compliance; it demands
engagement with the structural conditions that enable or constrain equitable outcomes.
Young, (2002, 2011) work is particularly instructive in this regard. She argues that
injustice emerges through both oppression, which constrains self-development, and
domination, which constrains self-determination. Importantly, her social connection
model of responsibility extends responsibility beyond individual culpability to encompass
participation in structural processes that reproduce injustice. From this perspective,
scientific responsibility cannot be understood solely as adherence to ethical procedures
or professional norms. It also entails recognising and addressing the ways in which
scientific practices may contribute to, sustain, or challenge broader structures of
oppression and domination. Genomic research, despite its empirical and ostensibly
objective orientation, is neither insulated from nor external to these processes.
Researchers frequently recognise the tensions inherent in conducting socially contested
research, balancing the potential to advance clinical knowledge against the possibility of
reinforcing harmful narratives or inequalities (Forssén et al., 2011). Consequently, while
many scientists explicitly reject racism and may understand their work as contributing to
social progress, they often struggle to articulate what social justice requires in practice
(Bliss, 2011; Ossorio & Duster, 2005). As Martschenko ( 2022) observes, researchers
frequently express uncertainty about what social responsibility entails and whether it is
possible to prevent the misuse or reinterpretation of scientific findings.

For these reasons, this thesis draws on Critical Race Theory (CRT) as its primary social
justice framework. CRT places race and racism at the centre of analysis rather than
treating them as secondary forms of disadvantage. It understands racism as ordinary,
structural, and historically embedded within social institutions, law, policy, and
knowledge production (Crenshaw, 1988; Delgado & Stefancic, 2017). This orientation is
particularly relevant to genomic medicine, where race is formally rejected as a biological
category yet continues to re-emerge through classificatory practices, population
categories, research infrastructures, and scientific narratives. As an emancipatory
framework, CRT is concerned not only with analysing race and racism but with exposing
and challenging the structures that reproduce racial inequality.

The relationship between science and social responsibility remains politically contested
and only partially defined (Glerup & Horst, 2014). This ambiguity enables researchers to

42 | P a g e
frame their role primarily as producers of knowledge while responsibility for downstream
consequences is displaced onto other actors (Douglas & Branch, 2024; Forssén et al.,
2011). However, scientific responsibility extends beyond the production of accurate
knowledge to encompass accountability for the social consequences of classification.
Within genomic medicine, decisions about how populations are defined, categorised,
and represented are not merely technical choices but interventions with implications for
equity, inclusion, and the distribution of benefits and harms. Scientific responsibility
therefore becomes inseparable from questions of social justice, requiring attention not
only to methodological validity but also to the social, political, and historical
consequences of classification practices and the ways scientific knowledge may
reproduce or challenge existing forms of racial inequality. Building on the concerns about
epistemic formation discussed in Section 2.5.3 (Gandolfi, 2021; Waight et al., 2022), the
remainder of this section examines two interconnected domains of scientific
responsibility: science's role in shaping public understandings of human difference, and
the pursuit of data justice and adequate representation as conditions for equitable
genomic futures (Panofsky & Bliss, 2017).




2.6.1   Contribution to Public Understandings of Human Difference


Science has long shaped how societies understand human difference, from its historical
role in codifying race as biological to its influence on contemporary ideas about identity
and belonging. These narratives circulate far beyond laboratories; they appear in
classrooms, policy debates, public health messaging, and popular media, becoming part
of the everyday “common sense” through which people interpret human variation (El-
Haj, 2007; Nelson, 2016; Panofsky, 2014; Wailoo et al., 2012). Yet the authority of
scientific narratives can obscure the political choices they contain. TallBear (2013), for
example, shows how genomic framings of indigeneity echo older colonial logics, while
Nelson, (2008, 2016) illustrates how Black communities negotiate and reinterpret
genetics, revealing science as socially and culturally embedded rather than neutral.

How scientists conceptualise human difference often diverges from how the public or
research participants understand it. Researchers may rely on broad labels such as
European or South Asian for analytical convenience, whereas individuals hold far more

43 | P a g e
nuanced or fluid identities (Ridgeway et al., 2019; Schwartz-Marín & Wade, 2015;
Weller, 2010). Debates persist over the reliability of self-reported ancestry: some
highlight inconsistency as a limitation (Shim et al., 2014), while others argue that paired
with genomic data, self-identification offers important insight into social and
environmental influences on health (Lorusso & Bacchini, 2015; Mersha & Abebe, 2015).
Genetic similarity does not map neatly onto social identity, yet both shape how human
variation is understood.




Figure 9. How the public views ancestry. The discrete and largely continental
groupings reflect how public understandings often lack access to the discontinuous and
overlapping patterns seen in genomic data. Produced by the author.

For some public, fixed and racialised ideas of human groups persist, reinforced in part
by direct-to-consumer ancestry testing, which presents identity as pie chart percentages
rather than as the complex, continuous patterns recognised by population genetics
(Nelson, 2018; Phelan et al., 2014; Roberts, 2011). Public perceptions, though, with
lived experience of fluid groups, then treat ancestry as discrete and largely continental,
as illustrated in Figure 9. Categories circulate between scientific and public spheres,
creating feedback loops that shape both interpretation and the incentives of genomic
research (Panofsky & Bliss, 2017).




44 | P a g e
Outram et al. (2018) show that many people, particularly those they describe as non-
White and lower socioeconomic groups in the United States, continue to view race as
relevant to explaining social inequality, suggesting a broader scepticism toward claims
that society is “post-racial.” Similarly, Schwartz-Marín & Wade (2015) demonstrate how
ideas of nation, race, and belonging shape public interpretations of genetic ancestry,
revealing persistent tensions between visible physical traits and the abstract claims of
genomic science. At the same time, ancestry testing can highlight the fluidity of identity,
prompting individuals to acknowledge mixed heritage and move away from rigid racial
frameworks (Aspinall, 2018; Atkin et al., 2022).

Together, these dynamics illustrate how scientific practice, public interpretation, and
commercial genomics continually interact to shape understandings of human difference.
Even as genomics rejects the notion of biologically discrete races, the categories used,
the language chosen, and the ways results are communicated can reproduce older
racial logics in new forms. These patterns have real consequences for whose genomes
are studied, whose health needs are prioritised, and whose perspectives shape scientific
knowledge.

If scientific narratives shape how human difference is understood, then the responsibility
extends beyond communication to the practices through which data is gathered, and
people are recruited into research, since these processes crystallise conceptual
assumptions into scientific fact. The next section examines how decisions about
sampling, inclusion, and representation are shaped by historical and structural forces,
and why confronting them is essential to producing equitable genomic science.




2.6.2   Ethical Governance, Consent, and Structural Responsibility


In genomics, the prominence of informed consent as an ethical touchstone reflects
procedural orientation. Consent is a widely recognised and easily communicated
concept, one that can be addressed through clear policies, forms, and institutional
oversight. It offers an appealingly concrete response to ethical concerns, allowing
researchers to demonstrate accountability while leaving deeper structural critiques
untouched.

45 | P a g e
Informed consent rests on the principle that freely given agreement lends moral
legitimacy to actions that might otherwise be unacceptable (Boulton & Parker, 2007).
Within biomedicine, it is widely regarded as the primary mechanism for protecting
individual rights and welfare. Yet, as Manson & O’Neill (2007) argue, informed consent
cannot serve as an ethical panacea. It fails when individuals cannot fully grasp the
information, when transactions are flawed, or when excessive demands for “fully explicit”
consent make it impractical.

For example, numerous ethical concerns have been raised about the handling of genetic
data from marginalised populations, including the documented mistreatment of Uyghur
and other ethnic minorities in China, as well as Roma communities in Europe (Lipphardt
et al., 2021; Normile, 2021). Without situating consent within a wider framework of
justice, relying on it risks offering reassurance without resolving the underlying inequities
in research practice (Manson & O’Neill, 2007).

Informed consent can be procedural and formulaic, but does not have to be and, in fact,
often is not in practice (Boulton & Parker, 2007) . Recognising this requires attention not
only to policy and formal guidelines but also to the moral and epistemic life of
laboratories, research projects, and collaborations. It is precisely this gap between
procedural compliance and the lived moral dimensions of scientific work that Parker
(2012) urges us to examine. Drawing on Kleinman’s critique of bioethics, Parker argues
that conventional frameworks, grounded in abstract principles, often fail to capture the
everyday moral reasoning through which researchers navigate their practices. A key
contribution of Parker’s work to the ethics of genomics is his distinction between the
moral and the ethical. Ethical procedures refer to the formal mechanisms that govern
research, while moral judgment concerns more profound questions of what is right, fair,
or just. As a result, practices such as consent forms or data-governance protocols can
be followed meticulously even when the underlying moral issues remain unexamined. A
study may, for example, classify participants into population groups in line with
institutional guidelines yet give little attention to whether those categories reproduce
social inequalities. Presented as neutral or routine, such categories are historically
contingent, shaped by legacies of racialisation, colonial science, and institutional
standardisation. They become ethical objects only when these inherited norms are made
visible and open to critique.

46 | P a g e
Parker’s framing highlights a risk: harmful or exclusionary practices can persist precisely
because they are embedded in what is considered ordinary or even virtuous within
scientific culture. The history of eugenics, once framed as a moral good, shows how
scientific authority can legitimise unethical action. In contemporary genomics, similar
risks arise when institutional norms and methodological conventions go unexamined.

Social science research has shown that the most consequential ethical issues in
genomics often arise not in formal consent processes, but in the everyday routines such
as were categories are made, applied, and normalised. Although these studies vary
widely, they share a focus on the classificatory habits, institutional norms, and historical
inheritances that procedural ethics cannot reach.

The following section examines these social structures within science, exploring how
racialised thinking can become embedded in routine practice and shape genomic
research and its applications.




2.7 Studying Social Structures in Science


This work builds on a wider Science and Technology Studies (STS), tradition of
laboratory and biomedical ethnography, including studies of scientific practice by
scholars such as Cetina(1999), Latour & WooIgar, (1986), Mol (2002) and Waldby and
Mitchell (2006), which demonstrate how scientific facts, classifications, and forms of
value emerge through social, material, and institutional processes rather than being
simply discovered.

A range of theoretical approaches have been used to examine scientific practice,
including Actor-Network Theory(Latour, 2005), co-productionist approaches (Sheila
Jasanoff, 2004), Foucauldian analyses of knowledge and power (Foucault, 1980; Rose,
2007) and broader social constructivist accounts of scientific knowledge (Cetina, 1999;
Latour & WooIgar, 1986). Each offer valuable insights into the production of scientific
facts and institutions. For this thesis, critical Realism (Bhaskar, 1975) was adopted ( see
3.1 rationale for the theoretical framework). This orientation is particularly important for a
study concerned not only with how genomic classifications are enacted, but also with the


47 | P a g e
broader institutional and structural conditions through which they emerge and acquire
authority.

Within STS a substantial body of ethnographic scholarship has examined how genetic
and genomic knowledge is produced in practice (Martschenko, 2022; M’Charek, 2005;
Rabinow, 2002). Rather than treating scientific categories as neutral reflections of
biological reality, these studies investigate how classifications of population, ancestry,
race, and disease are assembled through everyday laboratory work, institutional
routines, technological infrastructures, and broader social and political contexts.
Together, they provide an important methodological and conceptual foundation for
understanding the social life of genomic knowledge. Their work resonates with Bowker
and Star's (2000) influential argument that classification systems are never merely
technical tools but social and political infrastructures that shape what becomes visible,
measurable, and actionable. This insight is particularly important for studies of
genomics, where categories of ancestry, population, and difference increasingly inform
research, policy, and clinical practice.

Montoya's Making the Mexican Diabetic (2011) exemplifies this approach through
sustained ethnographic engagement across clinics, laboratories, and public health
infrastructures in Mexico and the United States. Following the trajectory of “Mexican-
American” blood samples, Montoya demonstrates how national and racial categories are
produced, accrue authority, and travel across institutional contexts. This work exposes
the limits of procedural fixes, such as consent, when broader governance frameworks
and public health agendas drive the classification process.

Fullwiley (2011) traces how funding priorities, publication norms, and laboratory routines
render race a convenient, if problematic, proxy. She employs multi-sited ethnographic
fieldwork across the United States, France, and Senegal of researchers in sickle cell and
related genetic work. Her work illuminates the gap between declared principles and daily
methods, showing how race persists as a moral routine rather than an object of
sustained ethical interrogation.

The findings of Fujimura and Rajagopalan (2011) underscore that analytic decisions can
proxy Race, sedimenting categories into practice. They combine ethnographic
observation and interviews with scientists conducting GWAS on human variation and
complex disease. They identify efforts to replace racial labels with “genetic ancestry”

48 | P a g e
while detailing the socio-material and technical work and software that underpin these
shifts.

Martschenko, (2022) adopts a different vantage point, using semi-structured interviews
with socio-genomics researchers working on intelligence and educational attainment.
Her analysis foregrounds scientists’ own understandings of “social responsibility,”
revealing tensions between pursuing scientifically and socially contested research, the
perceived potential benefits, and the difficulties of public engagement. Importantly, for
this thesis the study surfaces uncertainty among researchers about what social
responsibility entails in practice, highlighting the absence of shared frameworks for
addressing the deeper structural and historical dimensions of their work.

The studies reviewed here demonstrate the value of ethnographic approaches for
examining the ethical and epistemic life of genomics beyond the limits of procedural
ethics. Ethnographic approaches make visible the tacit norms, institutional pressures,
and everyday routines through which population categories are produced and
normalised. They therefore provide a valuable means of examining how classificatory
practices become embedded within scientific institutions and wider systems of
governance.

Other scholars have pushed this inquiry into adjacent terrains. Reardon, (2009, 2017)
interrogates the institutionalisation of ethics in genomics, particularly through ELSI
programmes, showing how formal governance can contain ethical debate within
procedural boundaries that sidestep colonial histories and the political economy of
genomic knowledge. By insisting on collective rights and relational accountability
TallBear (2013) reframes the ethics of genomics from the standpoint of Indigenous
sovereignty, challenging autonomy-based consent models. M’Charek (2005)
demonstrates how race is not simply discovered within genetic research but materially
enacted through laboratory practices, technologies, databases, and interpretive
decisions. Her work is particularly influential in showing how racial categories persist not
because they are biologically self-evident but because they are continually assembled
through scientific practice.

Collectively, this body of work has been highly successful in demonstrating how genomic
classifications are produced and stabilised. By tracing the social and material life of
categories, these studies make visible the points at which technical problem-solving and

49 | P a g e
ethical judgement intersect. While many of these studies address questions of inequality
and categorisation, relatively few engage in sustained dialogue between Critical Race
Theory and ethnographic analyses of contemporary genomic practice, particularly within
the context of UK genomic medicine.

Several theoretical traditions have examined the production and consequences of racial
categories, including racial formation theory (Omi & Winant, 2014) and postcolonial
theory (Haddour, 2019; Partner & Foot, 2013) offer important insights into the historical
production of racial categories and the enduring legacies of colonialism. Racial formation
theory is particularly valuable for understanding how institutions participate in the
creation and transformation of racial meanings over time. Critical Race Theory was
selected because it provides a more explicit framework for examining the relationship
between racialisation, structural racism, and institutional power (Delgado & Stefancic,
2017; Rollock & Gillborn, 2011). As discussed in Section 3.3, this orientation is
especially relevant to genomic science, where contemporary classificatory practices
remain connected to longer histories of racial categorisation while raising questions of
accountability, responsibility, and reparative justice.

Like Fullwiley( 2011), M’Charek (2005), Montoya, (2011)) and (Fujimura and
Rajagopalan, (2011) this thesis examines how categories of human difference are
produced and negotiated within scientific practice. However, it extends this literature by
focusing on contemporary genomic medicine in the UK, a context shaped by distinctive
histories, institutions, and commitments to diversity in genomic research. By bringing
STS scholarship on classification into dialogue with Critical Race Theory, the thesis
examines genomic categorisation not only as a technical and epistemic practice but also
as a site through which broader questions of inequality, accountability, and justice are
negotiated.




2.8 Gaps in Literature


There is a growing body of literature examining the use of race and ethnicity as proxies
for genetic ancestry in genomic research. However, much of it lacks critical engagement
with how these concepts are operationalised across different institutional and cultural


50 | P a g e
contexts. Notable contributions such as guidance from the National Heart, Lung, and
Blood Institute’s Trans-Omics for Precision Medicine (TOPMed) programme (Khan et al.,
2022) and recommendations for improving inclusivity in pre-clinical research by Zaaijer &
Capes-Davis (2021) offer pragmatic interventions. Yet these often assume universal
applicability and overlook the socio-political conditions required for their implementation.
Sustaining such recommendations requires sustained effort and coordinated
collaboration, yet scholarship on the practical challenges of building meaningful
partnerships in a global genomic research environment marked by entrenched power
asymmetries, disciplinary silos, and uneven research infrastructures remains limited.

Although the debate on human population descriptors is recognised as global (Popejoy
et al., 2018) the literature remains dominated by Euro-American perspectives. The
marginal visibility of contributions from scholars and contexts outside North America and
Western Europe reflects a narrow epistemic geography, constraining the field’s capacity
to account for diverse experiences and knowledge systems. This is particularly
significant given the UK’s ambitions to lead in genomic science. Despite this positioning,
there has been relatively little critical reflection on the UK’s colonial legacies and their
continuing influence on how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are conceptualised and used in
genomic research.

The UK’s distinctive history, shaped by colonial taxonomies and postcolonial legacies,
means that, while race and ethnicity are socially constructed, they remain materially
consequential in structuring inclusion, classification, and authority within research.

As Reardon (2017) and Parker (2012) both argue, in different but intersecting ways, the
ethical challenges of genetics are embedded in institutional norms and epistemic
cultures. Building on these perspectives, the literature reveals several critical gaps:

    •   A lack of empirical work on how UK genomic researchers operationalise race and
        ancestry in practice.

    •   Limited engagement with the historical and political contexts that shape these
        practices in the UK.

    •   Few studies that move beyond procedural ethics to examine how moral routines
        in science may reproduce or challenge structural inequities.



51 | P a g e
Discussed in the introduction, race and ethnicity in the UK are not neutral descriptors.
Scholars such as Benjamin (2019); Nelson (2018); Reardon (2017) and TallBear (2013)
urge genomic research to centre racial justice, include scholars of colour and Indigenous
experts, and critically reimagine what scientific responsibility entails. Parker (2012)
reminds us that ethics is not separate from the moral routines of science.

These arguments provide the conceptual and ethical impetus to engage with the gaps
outlined above and, in doing so, establish the foundation for the empirical analysis that
follows, examining how researchers navigate these issues in real-world laboratory and
institutional settings.

.




52 | P a g e
3    Methodology

Recognising race, ethnicity, and genetic ancestry as historically contingent yet materially
consequential categories also requires methods that can make their operations visible
within everyday scientific practice. For me, this is not only an intellectual task but also an
ethical one. As a researcher whose own history is bound up with the legacies of empire,
slavery, and colonial science, I cannot separate my scholarship from the question of
integrity: whose perspectives are centred, whose voices are excluded, and how my own
presence shapes what is seen.

Ethnography provides one way of attending to these dynamics, enabling me to trace
how concepts of race and ancestry are embedded in the moral routines of genomics
while also making my own positionality accountable. Accountability also means opening
yourself up to scrutiny, and for this, public and patient engagement was equally vital,
both as a check on my interpretations and as a resource to ground them in the lived
concerns of those for whom genomics is not abstract but personal. These commitments
shaped the design of this study, from its early scoping through to analysis and
dissemination.

The following chapter, therefore, sets out the project's methodological framework, as
shown in Figure 10. I begin by outlining the theoretical perspectives that underpin this
approach, critical realism and critical race theory, before turning to the pilot work,
research context, data collection, analytic strategies, and ethical practices that structured
the study. In making this shift, I move from establishing why race and ancestry matter in
genomic science to explaining how I investigated their significance in practice.




53 | P a g e
Figure 10. Conceptual flow of ontology, theory, and methods.

The figure provides an overview of how ontological and epistemological assumptions
inform the theoretical frameworks, ethical commitments, and methodological approach
adopted in this study, leading to the selection of ethnographic methods and data
sources. Produced by the author.

54 | P a g e
3.1 Rationale for Theoretical Framework


As discussed in the previous chapter, the use of race, ethnicity, and ancestry categories
is complex, contested, and at times deeply problematic (Birney et al., 2021; Dobson &
Arjunan, 2021; Hardcastle et al., 2022). Some scholars have argued that terms such as
race are so biologically inadequate and socially harmful that they should be abandoned
altogether (Timmer, 2023).

Yet the erasure or replacement of such terms with seemingly less offensive alternatives,
pursued in the name of accuracy, can obscure science’s historical and ongoing role in
producing inequities. Even when unintended, these practices risk reproducing racist
ideologies (Graves et al., 2022; Popejoy, 2021; Saini, 2020). Attempts to ‘do away’ with
the problem swiftly and painlessly may, in fact, limit scientists’ capacity to recognise,
engage with, and address the injustices that can emerge from their work.

This tension highlights the need for approaches that interrogate the social frameworks
underpinning scientific practice and grapple with race’s nuanced and contradictory
operations. Critical Realism (CR) and Critical Race Theory (CRT), which I turn to in the
following chapters, provide such frameworks. Through these perspectives, I outline the
methodological tools and ethical commitments that guide this study, showing how they
make visible the entanglements of science, race, and power while also opening space
for more accountable and just forms of research.




3.2 Critical Realism as a Framework for Race and Ancestry


Critical Realism (CR) provides a way of holding together the tensions between structure,
agency, and context that surfaced in both my early research encounters and the broader
debates from the literature outlined above. Where scientific categories of race and
ancestry risk flattening complexity or erasing social dynamics, CR insists that beneath
what is observed lie real but often unacknowledged structures shaping experience. It
allows attention to the ways individuals exercise agency within these constraints, without
reducing their actions to mere reflections of social forces. In this sense, CR offers a

55 | P a g e
productive foundation for developing methods that seek to make visible the
entanglements of science, race, and power, while also recognising the everyday
negotiations of those most affected.




3.2.1   Ontology, Epistemology, and the Three Domains


Roy Bhaskar's (2013) discussion of realist theory, commonly described as Critical
Realism (CR), he distinguishes between the ‘real’ and the ‘observable’ world. The
observable world is constructed from perspectives and experiences and can be
measured. The ‘real’ world, however, exists independently of human theories,
perceptions, or constructions. What makes this realism critical is its recognition that our
access to reality is always mediated by social and cognitive frameworks; we never
encounter it directly, but only through the categories, concepts, and assumptions
available to us. Critical realists, therefore, argue that while unobservable structures in
the ‘real’ world generate the patterns and events we perceive, the ways we interpret
those patterns are inseparable from the frameworks through which we come to know
them. Categories such as race or ancestry, for example, do not simply reflect reality but
actively shape how it is apprehended. The task of research could be argued to be both
to investigate the structures that underpin observable events and to remain reflexive
about the interpretive lenses through which those structures are made visible.

Critical Realism separates ontology from epistemology, giving each a thorough account.
Ontologically, it asserts that reality exists independently of our perceptions, while
epistemologically it recognises that our knowledge of that reality is always mediated,
partial, and open to revision. In doing so, Critical Realism outlines three ontological
strata (Collier, 1994); see Figure 11 ).




56 | P a g e
                                      Real

                       Total reality: Objects, entities and
                               their mechanisms


                                      Actual

                   Events generated by these mechanisms

                         Both observable unobservable




                                         Empirical

                                 Events observable and
                                 measurable experiences




Figure 11: The vector model of critical realism. Produced by the author. The three

domains of Critical Realism (adapted from (Bhaskar, 2013):


    •   The Real: underlying mechanisms, structures, and entities that exist
        independently of human observation (though often unobservable).

    •   The Actual: events generated by these mechanisms, whether observed or not.

    •   The Empirical: the subset of events experienced or measured through
        observation.




57 | P a g e
3.2.2   Applications and Implications


This stratified ontology allows us to distinguish clearly between what appears, what
happens, and what truly underpins those occurrences. Researchers such as Bhaskar
(2013) and Lawani, (2020) have highlighted CR’s capacity to generate thought-
provoking, insightful research. By acknowledging these layers, Critical Realism supports
a nuanced analysis of how power relations, cultural norms, and material conditions
shape both research practices and outcomes. Importantly, it also offers a framework for
developing interventions that address inequities and foster transformative change, most
notably in fields such as social work and community psychology, where CR has been
explicitly mobilised to guide emancipatory and justice-oriented practice (Botha, 2025; S.
Peter et al., 2025).

While Bhaskar’s framework describes a logical flow from the real to the actual and into
the empirical, the relationship is not unidirectional. Once empirical categories and
measurements are institutionalised, they begin to shape practices, policies, and forms of
knowledge that generate new actual events and, over time, become sedimented into the
real structures of social life. In the context of genomics, categories such as race,
ethnicity, or ancestry are not simply neutral descriptors but empirical tools that direct
research, organise care, and delimit participation. Their use can therefore reproduce
inequalities even as they claim to measure biological reality. For this study, the value of
Critical Realism lies in making visible both the generative mechanisms that underlie
events and the ways in which empirical practices feed back into those mechanisms. It
opens space to ask whether, and how, commitments to social justice might be woven
into empirical work, even in discovery science, so that categories are not only
instruments of measurement but also sites of ethical responsibility.

In what follows, I turn to Critical Race Theory (CRT), which complements this realist
orientation by interrogating how categories such as race and ancestry are themselves
produced, contested, and mobilised within systems of power.




58 | P a g e
3.3 Critical Race Theory


Critical Realism (CR) holds the capacity to suggest strategies for addressing social
problems, and the socio-historical context of this study prevents any claim to neutrality
around race. Critical Race Theory (CRT), therefore, offers the tools to understand and
challenge the social problem of racial inequality. CRT extends this insight by showing
how empirical practices, such as the use of racial or ancestry categories, do not simply
record reality but actively participate in shaping social outcomes, embedding inequity, or
opening possibilities for justice. In this way, CRT offers a framework that links
explanatory depth to the pursuit of social change (Borrell, 2018; Tsosie, 2005).

Critical race theory (CRT) regards race as socially constructed, embedded, and
nuanced. CRT theorists, therefore, seek to understand and challenge discrimination
against persons of colour that has historically served the interests of the dominant White
population (Delgado & Stefancic, 2017; Rollock & Gillborn, 2011).




3.3.1   Origins and Expansion


In the 1980s, a collective of legal scholars of colour began to formalise a body of work
that theorised the systemic and often obscured operations of racism. This framework
redirected attention away from overt individual acts and behaviours, towards the
structural and institutional dimensions through which racism persist (Bell, 1995;
Crenshaw, 1988, 2011).

Since, CRT has expanded to fields of enquiry beyond the American legal system,
addressing the rights of specific marginalised groups through its concern with social
justice . Racial scholars argue that racism influences rates of morbidity, mortality, and
overall well-being (Borrell, 2018; Tsosie, 2005). This expansion has generated distinct
bodies of literature and priorities, but across them CRT retains a set of shared core
principles that emerged from its legal foundations and continue to anchor its application
in new domains.




59 | P a g e
3.3.2   Core Principles of CRT


CRT is often summarised through six interrelated principles (see Figure 12), each
offering a lens for understanding how race and racism operate within social life and
scientific practice.

First, Race is ordinary; embedded in everyday life, this ordinariness, to the point of
invisibility, is part of its power: racism does not only appear in spectacular acts of
prejudice, but is sedimented into institutions, practices, and knowledge systems. As
demonstrated earlier in this thesis, genetics has historically been a key site where ideas
of race were embedded and naturalised, often with enduring effects on racialised and
minoritised people (Fullwiley, 2008; Graves et al., 2022; Rutherford, 2021b). Seeing
racism as ordinary underscores CRT’s insistence that it is systemic rather than
exceptional, requiring methods that can make it visible even in the most routine spaces
of science.

The next CRT pillar race is socially constructed also aligns with, but extends beyond, the
current consensus. Race is understood not as a biological essence but as a product of
social thought, political projects, and relational positioning (Delgado & Stefancic, 2017)
Yet what people mean by “socially constructed” is not uniform: it varies across countries,
cultural and ethnic communities, professional disciplines, and personal experiences
(Popejoy et al., 2020). In genomics this multiplicity is especially consequential. As Birney
et al. (2021) and Hardcastle et al. (2022) argue, the categories deployed by scientists
may appear neutral, but they are shaped by contested histories and carry diverse
interpretations that influence how they are applied and understood.

Interest convergence is another key pillar, which suggests that racism ultimately serves
the interests of a dominant White majority, and that progress toward racial equality tends
to occur only when it also benefits that group (Bell, 1995). In the context of precision
medicine, this principle resonates strongly. Increased data diversity holds not only
potential for more equitable outcomes for racially minoritised and underserved
populations but also brings advancements that benefit the broader population. However,
there are legitimate ethical concerns that such efforts may continue to offer
disproportionate advantages to populations who are already well represented in




60 | P a g e
genomics, such as those of European descent (Bentley et al., 2017; Popejoy & Fullerton,
2016) .

In the pillar of differential racialisation CRT highlights how groups are racialised and
minoritised differently over time, depending on political, cultural, and economic needs.
For instance, behavioural traits have historically been constructed in racialised terms to
justify inequality, while groups such as White Irish in the UK, once minoritised, came to
be absorbed into the White majority through social and political change (Edwards, 2018;
Garner, 2003). Similarly, individuals of “mixed ancestry” may align with a White majority
identity in some contexts and not in others, demonstrating the contingent and shifting
nature of racial stratification (Hickman, 1998). Echoing debates in genomics about the
unstable meanings of ancestry and race.

CRT also insists on the importance of minority voices as a key principle. Perspectives
from minoritised groups, it asserts, bring knowledge of exclusions, silences, and
practices that dominant groups often fail to recognise (Delgado & Stefancic, 2017). This
commitment is both conceptual and methodological, requiring reflexivity about whose
voices are centred and how they are incorporated into analysis. Within this study, it
offers a framework for acknowledging how positionality shapes the interpretation of
genomic practices and institutions.

Finally, intersectionality or anti-essentialism makes clear that no one has a unitary
identity (Crenshaw, 2017). Inequalities arise not only from race but from the intersections
of race with gender, class, sexuality, and other axes of identity. CRT therefore resists
essentialising accounts of race that flatten these complexities. Although critics argue that
intersectionality risks diluting focus or undermining other struggles for justice (Borrell,
2018; Fernander, 2022), CRT explicitly positions itself as a framework capable of holding
multiple dimensions of inequality together. This resonates with scholars in science and
medicine who emphasise the interwoven ways that categories of race, ancestry, and
social difference are mobilised in genomics, shaping both scientific knowledge and its
social consequences (Fullwiley, 2008; Hardcastle et al., 2022; Popejoy, 2021).

This relationship between CRT and genomic practice is illustrated in Figure 12, which
maps the six core principles of Critical Race Theory onto the analytical concerns
developed throughout this thesis.



61 | P a g e
62 | P a g e
                               Critical Race Theory Principles and Their
                                        Application in This Thesis




               Figure 12. Critical Race Theory principles and their application in this
               thesis. This conceptual map shows the six core principles of CRT on a
               single level, with corresponding applications to genomic research placed
               beneath each principle. It illustrates how CRT helps illuminate the
               structural, historical, and epistemic forces that shape population
