               responsibility. Produced by the author.


63 | P a g e
3.3.3   CRT, Realism, and Genomics


Although CRT has been widely applied, one criticism concerns the limited attention it pays to
questions of realism (Anderson, 2021). From a realist perspective, domestic structural racism can be
understood as a manifestation of recurring patterns generated by underlying economic and social
structures (B. Carter, 2002; Nayak, 2006). Similar arguments have been advanced in cultural and
postcolonial scholarship, where Gilroy (2013) demonstrates how race and class are entangled in
sustaining systemic inequalities in Britain. In contrast, more idealist approaches argue that racism is
primarily a function of language, symbols, and psychology (Delgado & Stefancic, 2017). While these
positions are often presented as oppositional, I do not interpret them as mutually exclusive. Rather, I
understand CRT as a set of tools that can illuminate the actual and empirical domains of genomic
research within the broader stratified ontology described by Critical Realism.

Complex social structures therefore underlie the conceptualisation and embodiment of race and racial
ideas, as suggested by CRT, and these can be interrogated through the ontologies of CR. Together,
they emphasise the importance of precise enquiry into the economic, social, and linguistic
constructions of race and ethnicity in genetics across the genomic ecosystem. This study leverages
the strengths of both frameworks: CR provides explanatory depth on the layered structures that shape
genomic research, while CRT highlights the racialised dynamics and inequalities embedded within it.
Used together, they enable an approach that is at once analytical and normative, seeking not only to
describe but also to challenge the injustices that persist in this field (Delgado & Stefancic, 2017).




3.3.4   Toward Methodological Application


In public health, CRT has already been employed to clarify racial biases woven into scientific
knowledge and to inform policy interventions (Ford & Airhihenbuwa, 2018). This orientation underpins
the framework’s capacity to inform this study's research questions, which are themselves oriented
toward equity and accountability. Moreover, CRT’s stance against positivism, combined with CR’s
alternative to both positivism and post-positivism Bhaskar (2013) provides a philosophical foundation
for the methodological approach adopted in this work. In this context, ethnographic inquiry, which I will
discuss in more detail, offers a particularly suitable means of engaging with the practices, categories,
and interactions that constitute race and ancestry in genomics.




64 | P a g e
3.4 Ethnographic Orientation


Ethnography, broadly defined, is the study of people and cultures through sustained engagement in
their natural settings, using observation, participation, and conversation to capture the meanings and
practices that shape social life (Hammersley & Atkinson, 2019). Rather than generating data in
controlled conditions, ethnographers immerse themselves in the everyday activities of a community or
institution and produce what Geertz described as “thick description” (1973, p. 3) of its norms, values,
and dynamics. In contemporary practice, ethnography often combines observation with interviews,
document analysis, and field notes, creating holistic accounts of how people live, work, and interpret
their experiences (Savage, 2000).

While ethnography is often associated with constructivist or interpretivist epistemologies, it can also
be productively situated within a critical realist framework. Critical realism acknowledges the existence
of real social structures and mechanisms that shape experiences, while recognising that these are
always mediated through interpretation (Bhaskar, 2013; Porter, 1993). Ethnography, with its attention
to lived practices and situated meanings, offers a means of accessing the ways such structures are
enacted and reproduced in everyday settings. As Banfield (2019) argues, critical realism provides
ethnographers with a philosophical grounding that balances the search for causal explanations with
sensitivity to context and meaning. In this study, ethnography is used in precisely this way: to
illuminate how broader institutional and social mechanisms around race, ancestry, and genomics are
expressed through the daily practices, discourses, and decisions of researchers.

The method, however, has a contested history. Its origins in anthropology are entangled with
colonialism, where Western researchers often studied non-Western societies in ways that reinforced
hierarchies of knowledge and power (Clifford et al., 1986). Recognising this legacy is important,
particularly in fields marked by inequities.

In this study, ethnography is redeployed to examine a powerful scientific institution rather than an
“othered” population. This shift destabilises ethnography’s colonial genealogy by directing its lens
toward the structures and practices of genomic science itself. In line with calls for “studying up”
(Nader, 1972) the focus here is on elites, institutions, and knowledge producers whose categories and
practices carry significant social consequences.

As discussed in Section 2.7, STS scholars have examined scientific practice through a variety of
analytical traditions, including laboratory studies, Actor-Network Theory, co-productionist approaches,
and biopolitical analyses. While these perspectives inform the broader intellectual context of this
research, the present study adopts an ethnographic approach centred on the everyday practices and

65 | P a g e
interpretations of genomic researchers, reflecting its focus on classification, meaning making, and the
negotiation of human difference in scientific work.




3.4.1   Ethnography in the Study of Genomics



Ethnography is particularly well suited to the study of social structures in action. As Young (2010)
notes, attention to the ways structures are enacted offers a means of apprehending society as a
whole. Ethnography, grounded in participant observation and extended engagement, provides tools to
capture these dynamics in situ, cultivating an in-depth understanding of complex institutional and
cultural practices (Goodson & Vassar, 2011; Reeves et al., 2008; Van Maanen, 2011). Its defining
feature is immersion: the ethnographer gains insight by inhabiting the same spaces, rhythms, and
conversations as participants, enabling comparative judgements about what is particular to a setting
and what may be more widely shared (Flick, 2022; Hammersley & Atkinson, 2019).

Ethnography is not limited to observing behaviour; it also attends to the discursive practices through
which institutions and communities make sense of themselves. Fieldnotes, interviews, and documents
become resources for analysing how categories are articulated, justified, and contested in everyday
talk and formal communication (Emerson et al., 2011; Poole, 1990; Rinaldo & Guhin, 2022). This
attention to discourse is particularly relevant in the genomic sciences, where language is central to
how concepts such as “race,” “ancestry,” or “diversity” are stabilised and gain authority.

Questions of validity and reliability are rightfully questioned in ethnographic work (Hammersley, 2018;
Hegelund, 2005; Mantzoukas, 2012). Sampling often proceeds opportunistically, by availability,
referral, or institutional pathways, and the reader encounters the site through the ethnographer's
interpretive lens. Yet these features do not negate rigour. Small (2009) argues that purposive case
selection, when guided by iterative engagement with data, produces findings that are highly valid for
answering context-specific research questions. Reliability can be enhanced through transparency:
being explicit about positionality, access, and analytic decisions strengthens credibility, while digital
and collaborative practices expand possibilities for documenting and sharing interpretive processes
(Murphy et al., 2021; Savage, 2000). Triangulation further addresses limitations by drawing on
multiple data sources, methods, and settings to cross-check interpretations (Alhojailan, 2012; Jonsen
& Jehn, 2009; Marshall & Rossman, 2014; Reeves et al., 2008). Situated on the Genome Campus,
this study benefited from both data triangulation (through varied sources of institutional knowledge)
and methodological triangulation (qualitative immersion with potential quantitative checks), enhancing
the validity of its conclusions (Flick, 2018, 2022; Fusch et al., 2018; Heale & Forbes, 2013).



66 | P a g e
My positioning as a genetic counsellor reinforced the appropriateness of ethnography in this research.
Genetic counselling practice is grounded in Rogerian principles of empathy, listening, and person-
centred communication (Thorne & Sanders, 2012). These same skills are valuable in ethnographic
encounters, where the goal is to create a space where participants can speak openly about their
experiences, including sensitive and challenging topics such as race, ethnicity, and ancestry. Similar
arguments have been made about the role of nurses and other healthcare workers in ethnographic
research, where clinical communication skills strengthen rapport and data quality (Cruz &
Higginbottom, 2013; Reeves et al., 2013; Savage, 2000). This alignment of professional training with
methodological demands enhanced the credibility of my role as an ethnographer, while also requiring
reflexivity about how my dual positioning, as clinician and researcher, shaped both access and
interpretation.

Semi-structured interviews formed a natural extension of this approach. Like counselling practice,
they balance openness with gentle structure, allowing participants to introduce meanings in their own
terms while ensuring that key areas of inquiry are addressed (Denzin & Lincoln, 2018; Kvale &
Brinkmann, 2009). This format was particularly appropriate for eliciting in-depth reflections on
sensitive and contested concepts such as race, ancestry, and diversity, where rigidity could foreclose
nuance, but entirely unstructured conversations risked producing gaps across accounts. Semi-
structured interviews therefore supported both the depth required for understanding lived and
institutional experiences, and the comparability necessary for analysing patterns across multiple
conversations (Bryman, 2016; Rinaldo & Guhin, 2022) .

Ethnography has also been used productively in related areas of genetics and science. In genetic
counselling, it has illuminated how knowledge and values are co-produced between clinicians and
patients (Cowley, 2016; Outram et al., 2022). Montoya's (2011) and Fullwiley's (2011) work examining
diabetes and sickle cell, respectively, illustrate how racial categories are constructed, applied, and
revised in scientific practice, often producing contradictions and inequities when race is treated as a
discrete descriptor. Their detailed analyses show ethnography’s capacity to uncover the institutional
drivers and cultural assumptions underpinning genomic “knowledge.” More directly relevant, Fujimura
and Rajagopalan (2011) studied biomedical scientists working on human genetic variation and
common complex diseases, tracing how researchers alternately relied on genetic ancestry or racial
categories in their work. Their findings highlight both the methodological ingenuity and the institutional
constraints through which these categories are mobilised, as well as the broader political and social
landscapes that shape them.

In this study, ethnography was well-suited to interrogate the categories, practices, and institutional
cultures of genomics, while the literature review and public engagement strands provided supporting
data and perspectives. Examples of critical realist ethnography demonstrate how the method can sit

67 | P a g e
between constructivist and realist positions, revealing how lived practices reflect underlying social
mechanisms while remaining attentive to meaning and context (Porter, 1993). This capacity to move
between the observable and the structural made ethnography complementary to a critical realist
framework in this project.

At the same time, ethnography has long been a productive site for the development of Critical Race
Theory in education and the social sciences, offering narrative and situated accounts that expose
racial inequality and the limits of dominant epistemologies (L. Parker, 2015; Solorzano & Yosso,
2002). Critical race ethnography demonstrates how lived experience and storytelling can challenge
structures of exclusion while generating new insights into the operations of power.




3.5 Defining and Approaching the Research Question


To clarify the research questions, I undertook two pilot studies. Here, I briefly summarise their
contribution to the main study design. The themes that emerged from this process are central to the
discussions in Chapters 4–7, where they are examined in greater detail.



3.5.1   Pilot Study One


In early campus meetings and scoping discussions, two senior genomic researchers at Sanger were
repeatedly identified as key figures. Both were highly visible in faculty-wide seminars and discussions
on equality, diversity, and equity, and their scientific groups were actively addressing challenges
around diversity in genomic datasets.


Given the depth of interaction with these researchers, I proposed formalising interviews with them as
a pilot study. Approval was granted by the Faculty of Education Research Ethics Committee (REC)
and the Higher Degrees Office of the University of Cambridge. Consent materials were prepared in
collaboration with my supervisor (Appendix A and B).


Two semi-structured interviews were conducted via Zoom to explore the project’s context and
rationale. The interviews sought to incorporate researcher perspectives into the doctoral design, while
also identifying additional scientists who might contribute to the main study. Recordings were
manually transcribed and thematically analysed using a hand-coding approach (Morales et al., 2018).




68 | P a g e
3.5.2   Pilot Study Two


Following Pilot Study One and an initial review of the literature, the rationale for the research
questions became clearer. However, gaps remained, particularly regarding the global genomic
ecosystem and the ways in which human population categories had evolved and been
operationalised in the UK. The more recent history of these debates, especially from the early 1990s
at the Sanger, was largely absent from the literature.


To address this, I conducted a second pilot study to investigate the UK context more directly. This
included an interview with a scientist and clinical geneticist who had worked closely with the Sanger
Institute in the 1990s, informal interviews with two archivists (one attached to the research group of a
pilot one participant, and another working more broadly across the archives), and a conversation with
a science historian specialising in genetics, race, and ethnicity. Data were gathered through audio
recordings, field notes, emails, and meeting minutes. Some of which formed the Introduction and
literature review section of the study.


The decision to pursue ethnography was shaped by my early experiences on the Wellcome Sanger
Campus. As captured in the excerpt from a journal entry below, moving between my clinical role as a
genetic counsellor in London hospitals and the secluded, highly resourced environment of the Sanger
Institute made visible the distance between genomics as a research enterprise and the diverse
publics it seeks to serve.




Journal entry, 10.6.22:



The campus is still supporting hybrid working so I don’t come here many days in the week. The
journey to campus represents a change of state for me. Every stop on the train is a reminder that I am
further away from the noise, colours, and chaos of South London. To reach the campus, I jump on a
shuttle bus and arrive on a sprawling site where space, in contrast to London, seems underutilised,
and chaos isn’t welcome. I am not sure I will ever feel like I am part of this place, but I am fascinated
by it and every conversation I have. I feel uneasy at times. I wonder if something is beneath the still
surface, or perhaps the feeling is my experience of perceiving myself as ‘matter out of place’.




69 | P a g e
The questions that emerged were shaped as much by the insights of scientists, archivists, and
historians as by my perspective as a genetic counsellor and a Black woman entering this space. This
reflexive positioning is consistent with the philosophical frameworks of Critical Realism and Critical
Race Theory introduced earlier. It is therefore necessary to reflect on how position and grounding
informed the design of this study, both methodologically and ethically.




3.6 Study Design


I designed the study as an ethnographic investigation that drew on multiple sources of data to
address the research questions. The primary sources were observations and semi-structured
interviews with genomic researchers across the research contexts. The ethnographic analysis also
incorporated documentary evidence, including photographs, written material, study reports, and
published research articles. In addition, participants willingly shared materials not otherwise publicly
available, such as meeting agendas, draft documents, and conference presentations, which were
treated as research data. The research was conducted in the UK across several geographical
locations. Genomic researcher data was collected primarily at Sanger and the offices of Wellcome
Connecting Science, both located on the Genome Campus where I was based. Additional
observations were carried out at related sites, including the University of Cambridge (Kavli Centre for
Ethics, Science and the Public). For public audience observations and interviews, sites included the
Wellcome Collection in London and the Black Cultural Archives, as well as events at the Sanger in
Hinxton.

Table 2 provides an overview of the study design, illustrating how each method of data collection
related to the research questions.

   71 | P a g e
Alongside ethnographic observation and interviews, I undertook a narrative review of the literature
published by Sanger during the fellowship period. Scientific papers are genomics’ primary mode of
communication, representing what can be publicly acknowledged, formalised, and legitimised through
peer review. This dual focus reflects the critical realist principle that the empirical does not simply
capture reality but is mediated through categories and practices that shape what can be observed.
Papers extend the institutional conversation beyond my immediate access and provide a record of
what is considered acceptable discourse.

The process of acculturation on-site provided both access and constraints. My positionality as a
genetic counsellor familiar with the culture of genomics but simultaneously an outsider critically
examining its structures shaped the kinds of data I could generate. Negotiating access required
sensitivity to institutional hierarchies and an awareness of how my identity and presence influenced
what was disclosed. Problems of access are well-documented in the ethnographic literature
(Hammersley & Atkinson, 2019; Reeves et al., 2013; Savage, 2000), and my experience aligns with
these accounts, including the selective willingness of participants to engage and uneven exposure to
institutional conversations




3.7 Public Engagement as a Parallel Stream


Public engagement was positioned as a necessary parallel stream alongside the ethnographic
approach and was integrated throughout the study. These activities acted as both regulator and
interlocutor, creating intentional spaces in which the project could be challenged on the potential
distributive implications of its findings and ensuring that the research remained grounded in social
justice concerns. Public engagement thus served as a “third commentary,” supplementing the
perspectives of scientists and the literature with those of publics who are often positioned as the
subjects, but not the authors, of genomic discourse.



3.7.1   Position and Grounding


Position and grounding played a crucial role in shaping the design of this study, both as
methodological foundations and as integral aspects of its ethical framework. Relationships with the
public and wider communities, established before and during the project, were equally important,
fostering trust and ensuring accountability to those the study sought to represent.



72 | P a g e
Although CRT emphasises the role of researcher voice and narrative, investigating racism entailed
confronting a phenomenon deeply entangled with my own lived experience, past and present.
Researching race while adapting to the new physical and institutional environment of the Genome
Campus was at times unsettling. As Rattay et al. (2025) introduce, such work can be uncomfortable
for researchers, with implications for both interpretation and well-being. In response, I worked with my
supervisor to implement a package of support, including mentorship from a Black woman scholar
specialising in racial justice in education and counselling from a Black woman therapist.


The societal voice was also present throughout the project. Building on my background in public
engagement, I participated in public-facing events on genetics and ethics, facilitated deliberations with
patient and public groups, and collaborated on community projects throughout the fellowship
(Appendix C). These experiences affirmed the high level of public interest in how genomics might
respond to racial health inequalities.


To mitigate potential bias arising from my positionality and immersion among scientists, I also
engaged with artistic and critical perspectives. Specifically, I collaborated with the artists Larry
Achiampong and David Blandy on the Genetic Automata project, commissioned by the Wellcome
Collection and Wellcome Connecting Science (Blandy & Achiampong, 2022). This series interrogates
race, racism, and ancestry in science through film and public dialogue. During the fellowship, I hosted
an open conversation with the artists, scientists, and staff at the Genome Campus in September
2022, and participated in a panel at the Museums Association Conference in November 2022 on
tackling racism in science. These events overlapped with my ethnographic fieldwork, creating
opportunities for cross-fertilisation between artistic critique, scientific practice, and community
engagement.


My position and grounding thus informed a study design that is critically engaged and ethically
situated. The intersection of professional expertise, personal identity, and community engagement
afforded both insider and outsider perspectives, enabling me to navigate professional and cultural
contexts with reflexivity and sensitivity. In what follows, I build upon these foundations to describe the
design of the main study, explaining how the clarified research questions, philosophical commitments,
and pilot work converged to shape an ethnographic inquiry into genomics research.




73 | P a g e
3.8 Data Collection Site


Wellcome Sanger Institute

The primary site of data collection was the Wellcome Sanger Institute in Hinxton, Cambridgeshire.
Founded by the Wellcome Trust in 1992 and named after biochemist Frederick Sanger, the Institute
gained global recognition for sequencing around one-third of the human genome in the Human
Genome Project, the largest contribution of any single centre (Rogers, 2025). It is now one of the
world’s major hubs for large-scale genomic discovery, combining high-throughput sequencing with
population-scale analysis.

After the Human Genome Project, Sanger expanded into sequencing diverse genomes, including
pathogens, parasites, cancer cells, and model organisms, creating shared resources that shaped
scientific understandings of human variation and disease (Genome Research Limited, 2022;
Wellcome Sanger Institute, 2020). Its research spans six programmes: Cancer, Ageing and Somatic
Mutation; Cellular Genomics; Human Genetics; Parasites and Microbes; Tree of Life; and Generative
and Synthetic Genomics, all supported by extensive sequencing and data infrastructures.

The Genome Campus also hosts the European Bioinformatics Institute (EMBL-EBI), forming an
integrated environment for laboratory science, computational biology and large-scale data
management. As a research setting, Sanger provided a key vantage point for examining how
population descriptors, scientific practice and responsibility intersect, given its reliance on global
genomic datasets and categories that hold both scientific and social significance.

3.8.1   Participants


‘Scientists’

Participants from Sanger included both senior and junior scientists engaged in genomic research,
particularly in human genetics and cell-based studies where considerations of race, ethnicity, and
ancestry arise in relation to datasets.

I conducted primary interviews with six senior researchers, all of whom were heads of research
groups. Heads of group were selected because of their strategic influence over research questions,
funding applications, and publications. Accordingly, the inclusion criteria focused on participants who
could provide insight into the broader genomic ecosystem and research culture at Sanger.

I did not collect demographic characteristics (e.g. ethnicity, age, gender) due to the small sample size
and recruitment approach. However, issues of identity and self-identification were explored as part of


74 | P a g e
the semi-structured interviews with primary participants, in line with the research objectives
concerning race, ethnicity, and ancestry.

I conducted secondary interviews with a further six scientists. In addition, multiple informal
discussions and observations with scientists took place in both individual and group settings.

‘Public’



I selected participants based on their interest in genomics and the ethical issues surrounding race,
ethnicity, and ancestry. Demographic data (e.g. age, gender, ethnicity) were not systematically
collected due to the small sample size and recruitment approach. However, recruitment sites were
chosen specifically for their engagement with Black, Asian, and other groups underrepresented in
genomic research. All participants spoke English, and no communication needs were declared,
although one participant disclosed additional learning needs. The ability to communicate in English
was not set as an exclusion criterion. Although public audiences ranged from 20 to 80 people, four
individuals volunteered to participate in a formal interview across the three sites.

The three sites’ participants were recruited from and were connected to the Genetic Automata film
series, these were:

    •      Black Cultural Archives (BCA): Adult attendees (18+) of Genetic Automata: Black Resistance
           to Scientific Racism, an event that combined an exhibition of the film series with archival
           examples of Black resistance to scientific racism.

    •      Beale’s High School: Young people (15–16 years old) enrolled in Genetic Automata: Project
           Intergen, a creative workshop series that used the films to foster intergenerational conversations
           around science and ethics.

    •      Wellcome Collection: Adult attendees (18+) of Passing Down, a panel discussion linked to the
           Genetic Automata exhibition, featuring the artists Larry Achiampong and David Blandy.

Although public data from these sites did not directly contribute to the thesis's findings, encounters there
shaped my positionality and provided an essential point of critical accountability.




3.8.2      Recruitment


I employed snowballing and opportunistic approaches to recruit scientists. Following the pilot phase, in
which a smaller number of scientists were engaged, participants recommended other colleagues who
met the inclusion criteria and were open to being interviewed. Some of these scientists also extended

75 | P a g e
invitations to their meetings, open days, and laboratory spaces, which provided opportunities for further
interviews and observations. In addition, on-campus events relating to data diversity, ethics in science,
and the Genetic Automata film series offered further opportunities for scientists to self-select into
informal conversations on the research topics.

Young people at Beale’s Academy participated voluntarily in alignment with their school’s core
curriculum. Attendees at the Wellcome Collection gallery event and the Black Cultural Archives also
self-selected to participate. At each public event, opportunities for individual interviews were offered.
Interested participants were contacted directly via email (or, in the case of young people, through a
responsible adult) and interview times were arranged at their convenience. Interviews were offered both
in person and virtually.

3.9 Data Collection


Interviews

All scientists who volunteered for formal interviews were provided with participant information sheets
and consent forms via email (Appendix B1 and B2). Once consent was obtained, between two and
four interview dates were scheduled, spaced at least one month apart. At least one interview was
conducted in the participant’s usual working environment. Initial interviews were semi-structured,
providing a degree of consistency across participants, while subsequent interviews were open and
unstructured, allowing earlier conversations to shape later discussions (Appendix D).

Public participants who volunteered at events were also provided with information sheets and
consent/assent forms (Appendix B4 – B9). Public interviews were conducted within three months of
the exhibition observation, at a time chosen by the participant. These interviews were semi-structured
and designed to explore non-scientists’ attitudes toward the use of population descriptors in
genomics, as well as to reflect on emerging themes from the scientists’ data.

Interviews were conducted either in person or online via Zoom, each lasting 45–60 minutes. In-person
interviews were recorded using the researcher’s iPad; online interviews were recorded via Zoom. In
both cases, transcripts were auto-generated (Zoom or Turboscribe™) and then cross-checked
against the audio file for accuracy. Once verified, all files were downloaded to a password-protected
external drive, and copies were deleted from Zoom, Turboscribe™, and recording devices.

Observations

Consent for observations was sought in writing from the relevant event organiser, team leader, or
faculty management. An invitation to observe was treated as tacit consent, with organisers asked to
remind participants of the presence of an observer.

76 | P a g e
At Sanger, observations took place during team meetings following invitations from heads of
programme, with notification of the observer’s presence always made explicit. These included
listening to routine conversations about the practicalities of research and interactions among
scientists across different career stages. Less specific observations were also made in communal
areas such as dining spaces, the library, and meeting rooms, as well as at open campus-wide
seminars, workshops, and meetings with both internal and external researchers. Online webinars
conducted via Zoom were recorded onto a laptop and saved on a password-protected external drive.

In relation to Beale’s High School’s Genetic Automata: Project Intergen, participation began with
attendance at a workshop. Participants were informed that further observations would take place
during a later visit to display their artworks at the Wellcome Collection, and that the opportunity to
volunteer for individual interviews would be offered during this initial workshop. At the exhibition, a
notice was displayed allowing participants to decline observation. Engagement took the form of
conversations with participants about their artworks and the Genetic Automata display. Recordings
made in public spaces were used for reflection and destroyed following analysis.

At the Black Cultural Archives and Wellcome Collection Passing Down events, notices were displayed
to notify participants of the observation and provide the opportunity to decline. As with other sites,
recordings made in public spaces were used for reflective purposes only and destroyed after analysis.

Field notes

Fieldnotes were an important complement to interviews and observations, providing additional context
and supporting interpretation. They captured details of who was involved (e.g., a junior scientist, but
never names or identifying information), verbatim or semi-verbatim quotations, and non-verbal
aspects of interaction such as setting, tone, and body language. They also noted the tools and
materials used (for example, the categories applied in classifying data). Notes were handwritten and
anonymised either during writing or immediately afterwards.

Fieldnotes were taken in a variety of settings: while sitting in shared spaces at Sanger, during
community events, while moving through exhibitions, and after panel discussions. In these contexts,
they often centred on personal reflections and impressions rather than direct accounts of
conversation. Conversations were never covertly listened to, and no identifying information was
recorded. When photos or recordings of public spaces were taken, these were stored securely and
used only for reflective purposes.

Alongside these fieldnotes, I also kept a reflexive journal throughout much of the research. This
journal recorded thoughts, feelings, and reflections on my own well-being, and became a tool for self-
regulation and accountability. It served as an autoethnographic resource, offering insight into how my
position and perspective shaped the research, and supporting interpretation when analysing
77 | P a g e
interviews and observations some time after the events themselves. In this way, both fieldnotes and
the reflexive journal operated not only as records of data but also as aids to reflexivity and critical
engagement with the research process.

Publications review

To complement the ethnographic and interview data, I undertook a targeted review of institutional
publications. The purpose of this review was to trace how human population categories and related
ethical considerations appeared in the scientific outputs most visible within the Sanger community.

I adopted a discourse-analytic lens to examine how population descriptors are used and discussed
within published genomic papers. This approach treats the publications as ethnographic texts,
attending to what is said, by whom, and what is implied or left unsaid.

The weekly research bulletin provided the basis for the review. Circulated internally across the
Institute, the bulletin compiles recent publications and preprints associated with Sanger and its
partner organisations. As such, it represents more than a record of outputs: it provides a window into
the scientific discourse circulating within the Institute, encompassing both the work that staff produce
and the research judged to be relevant to the community.

This source is particularly valuable because of the position Sanger researchers occupy within global
consortia and collaborations. Staff are frequently one of many contributors to large-scale projects,
working with datasets collected across diverse populations, geographies, and research contexts.

The bulletin therefore captures a wide cross-section of genomic science, extending well beyond a
narrow disciplinary or institutional focus. In this sense, it offered a robust starting point for exploring
how categories of race, ethnicity, and ancestry are invoked within contemporary genomics.

                    -   Sources and Search Process

Papers are identified and compiled by the Sanger Library team, who maintain comprehensive saved
searches across multiple bibliographic databases. The library process for circulation of publications
can be found in Appendix E.

                    -   Selection of Eligible Papers

Between 9 May 2023 and 26 January 2024, the weekly bulletin circulated 920 papers across 22
bulletins. From this, 148 papers were identified as eligible for the review.

Eligibility was determined with reference to the scope of the study and the ethnographic fieldwork.
Specifically, papers were included if they:

    • Related to the work of group leaders and lead scientists I had interviewed.
78 | P a g e
    •   Reported genomic research making use of human population datasets.
    •   Represented clinical, computational, wet-lab, or dry-lab studies.

Exclusions were also clearly defined. Papers from the Tree of Life programme, which focuses on
biodiversity research, were not included, as they did not address human population categories.

A small number of case studies involving very few patients were retained (n), as were a limited set of
papers that addressed patient experience or ethical, legal and social implications (ELSI) in genomics
(n). These subsets, while minor in number, were considered relevant for understanding how human
population categories and ethical issues were articulated in scientific outputs.

By foregrounding publications linked to both my interview participants and human population data,
this selection strategy ensured the review was closely aligned with the study’s broader aim: to
interrogate how categories of race, ethnicity, and ancestry circulate within genomic science and what
ethical implications accompany their use. This approach also follows established practice of using
scientific texts as a site for discourse analysis (Kerr, 2000; Myers, 2022).

                   -     Review Method

Papers judged to be in scope were then entered into an Excel spreadsheet for systematic recording
and analysis.

For each eligible paper, the following fields were completed:

    •   Publication date
    •   Author position (Sanger staff or external)
    •   Area of interest
    •   Datasets or collections used
    •   Source of data
    •   Population categories identified in methods
    •   Population categories identified in conclusions or discussions
    •   Other mentions of population categories (e.g., location or terminology)
    •   Summary of findings and ethical considerations

Where papers referred to race, ethnicity, or ancestry without specifying the labels used,
supplementary material or the direct dataset sources were reviewed to clarify which human population
categories were being referenced.

To minimise omissions, a secondary search was conducted within the eligible papers for common
terms that might indicate relevant categorisations or ethical reflections. These included: population,



79 | P a g e
ethnicity, ancestry, cohort, nationals, individuals, adult, children, men, women, diversity, ethics,
ethical, fairness, diverse, demographics, metadata.

This structured approach enabled the review to do more than catalogue publications: it created a
basis for analysing how population categories and ethical considerations are mobilised within
scientific discourse. In this way, the document review formed part of the ethnographic analysis and
helped address the study’s central questions about the social and ethical framing of genomics.

The interviews, observations, fieldnotes, and publications review provided a multi-layered empirical
base for understanding how population categories and ethical reasoning emerge in genomic practice.
These methods also supplied the material necessary for the subsequent interpretive analysis and
systems-level mapping.




3.10    Analysis


The analytic strategy used in this study brought together a set of interpretive, reflexive, and situated
qualitative approaches that were suited to examining how race, ancestry, and genomic practice
acquire meaning across scientific, institutional, and public settings. Rather than relying on a single
methodological tradition, the analysis was shaped by the demands of each data source and by the
epistemological orientation that underpinned the project. The following analysis outlines the
interpretive procedures used across textual, ethnographic, interview, and organisational materials,
and shows how meaning was generated through immersion, reflexivity, and sustained engagement
with the genomic field.

Coding began with interview transcripts, which were read repeatedly and coded inductively within
Atlas.ti. Initial codes captured recurring discussions of race, ancestry, diversity, inclusion,
responsibility, governance, and classification. Through iterative discussion with supervisors and the
wider research group, these codes were refined and grouped into broader thematic categories. These
emergent themes subsequently informed the analysis of other data sources, including fieldnotes,
institutional publications, and systems mapping materials, allowing patterns identified in one dataset
to be explored, challenged, or elaborated through others. While the coding process remained
inductive, it was also informed by the study's theoretical interests, particularly questions concerning
racialisation, institutional practice, and the production of scientific categories.

The analysis combined formal qualitative techniques with reflexive practices drawn from both
ethnography and genetic counselling. Reflective models such as Gibbs’ reflective cycle (1988) as
cited in Brown (2019) provided a structured way to interrogate non-verbal communication, emotions,

80 | P a g e
and researcher positionality. Reflective techniques from genetic counselling practice were used to
deepen interpretation and maintain critical self-awareness.

All interview transcripts, fieldnotes, documents, and images were managed in Atlas.ti. Interview data
were examined using an emergent thematic analysis approach (Cruz & Higginbottom, 2013) in which
coding developed inductively and was refined through ongoing engagement with the material and
regular discussion with supervisors and advisors (Reeves et al., 2008). Rather than following a
predefined coding manual, patterns were identified through repeated readings and interpretive
attention to how participants framed population categories, addressed equity and inclusion, and
negotiated ethical issues within genomic practice.

Fieldnotes were integral to the analysis. Although not formally coded, they were read reflexively and
revisited throughout the study. They provided contextual detail, captured interactional dynamics, and
situated themes emerging from interviews and publications. Reflexive journals and memos
complemented this process by documenting the researcher’s own positioning and responses,
ensuring transparency in how interpretation was shaped by lived experience within the research
setting.

For the review of institutional publications, eligible papers were scanned for population descriptors
through keyword searches such as population, ethnicity, ancestry, cohort, and diversity. These terms
were traced back into the full text to recover their meaning and contextual use. Where relevant,
additional papers using the same terms were reviewed to identify recurring themes. The process drew
on discourse-analytic sensibilities by attending to the ways scientific texts mobilised categories and
ethical claims Kerr (2000); Myers (2022); Soini and Birkeland (2014).

Although this study does not constitute a full CBPR approach (Israel et al., 2005) it draws on CBPR
principles of recognising participants as knowledge contributors, foregrounding dialogue and voice,
and integrating engagement into the analytic process through the use of intentional public spaces as
described in 3.8.3 and Appendix C.Finally, across interviews, observations, and publications, I
examined references to institutions, partners, and collaborators. This analytic strand sought to map
the social and organisational ecosystem of genomics. Conceptually, this aligns with institutional
ethnography (Smith, 2005) and social network approaches (Crossley, 2010), in which attention to
named actors and organisations provides insight into the structuring of a research environment. This
mapping informed the development of a visual systems representation that traced the connections,
flows, and influences shaping genomic practice. Ethnographic immersion strengthened this process
by grounding the map in observed routines, relationships, and institutional dynamics rather than
abstract categories.



81 | P a g e
Critical Race Theory informed the analysis by sensitising attention to processes of racialisation,
structural inequality, institutional power, and the ways in which seemingly neutral classificatory
practices may reproduce broader social hierarchies. Rather than functioning as a fixed coding
framework, CRT shaped the interpretation of themes and guided questions about how race and
ancestry were understood, justified, and operationalised within genomic practice. Critical Realism
informed the analysis in a different way. It supported movement between empirical observations, such
as interviews, publications, and fieldnotes, and consideration of the wider institutional and historical
mechanisms that might generate those observations. This orientation encouraged interpretation not
only of what participants said and did, but also of the structural conditions through which particular
classificatory practices became possible and acquired authority.

Together, these strategies ensured that the analysis moved across individual experiences, textual
framings, and institutional relationships, while remaining grounded in critical race semiotics(Lawrence
& Hylton, 2022).This analytic movement also informed the structure of Chapters 5-7, which progress
from discourse and meaning-making, to institutional practice, and finally to the wider genomic
ecosystem within which classifications acquire authority and social consequence. Rather than
organising findings solely by data source or thematic code, the chapters were designed to move
across different levels of genomic practice identified through the analysis. Chapter 5 examines the
language, meanings, and classificatory practices evident in institutional publications and researcher
accounts, establishing the discursive environment within which genomic work takes place. Chapter 6
builds on these findings by exploring how questions of equity, responsibility, collaboration, and
institutional commitment are negotiated in practice. Finally, Chapter 7 broadens the analytic lens to
consider the wider genomic ecosystem within which these activities occur, drawing on systems
mapping and ethnographic observation to examine relationships, influence, and institutional power.
This progression reflects a critical realist concern with understanding how observable practices relate
to wider organisational and structural conditions, while also reflecting a CRT-informed interest in how
racialisation and inequality become embedded and reproduced across interconnected institutional
contexts.


3.11    Ethics and Informed Consent


This study was conducted in accordance with the ethical principles outlined by the British Educational
Research Association (BERA, 2018) and received approval from the Faculty of Education Ethics
Committee, University of Cambridge.

My research involved exploring how scientists and publics engage with categories of race, ethnicity,
and ancestry in genomics. These topics are deeply entangled with histories of inequality and
contemporary experiences of exclusion, and I recognised from the outset that questions could be
82 | P a g e
challenging or uncomfortable for participants. Ethical engagement therefore required more than
procedural compliance: it required a commitment to reciprocity, reflexivity, and care.

Informed and Ongoing Consent

Consent was understood not as a single event but as a dynamic, ongoing process. Participants were
provided with clear information about the study, its aims, and their rights, both in writing and verbally,
before any interview or observation. They were reminded that participation was voluntary and that
they could withdraw at any time without consequence. For interviews, consent was confirmed both in
signed forms and again at the start of each recording. For observations, explicit permission was
sought from organisers, and participants were notified of the researcher’s presence through verbal
reminders or notices.

For young people under 18, consent and assent were obtained through a two-step process:
responsible adults (teachers or youth coordinators) facilitated parental consent, and young
participants themselves confirmed their willingness to take part. This approach emphasised agency
as well as protection.

Anonymity and Confidentiality

Protecting confidentiality was particularly important given the relatively small and specialised
community of senior genomic scientists at Sanger, where identities could be inferred even if names
were removed. To mitigate this risk, data were anonymised in transcripts and analysis, and identifying
details (such as names on slides or institutional logos) were redacted before sharing beyond the
supervisory team. However, I was transparent with participants that complete anonymity could not be
guaranteed, and this was explicitly discussed during the consent process.

Participants were also given the option to relinquish anonymity if they wished to be named in outputs
or acknowledged for their contribution. This was important in recognising participants not only as
research subjects but also as potential co-producers of knowledge.

Care and Wellbeing

Given the sensitive nature of the research, attention was paid to participant well-being. I
acknowledged that discussions of race and ancestry in science could provoke discomfort, particularly
for those whose personal or professional identities were implicated. All participants were offered
information about support resources, and I ensured that debriefing and ongoing communication were
possible. Although these offers were not taken up, their availability was an important part of the
ethical stance of the project.

Researcher Reflexivity

83 | P a g e
Finally, ethics in this study extended to me as a researcher. I drew on reflexive practices from genetic
counselling and ethnography, including maintaining a reflective journal and engaging in supervision
and mentoring. These practices provided accountability, supported my well-being, and helped ensure
that ethical principles were enacted not only procedurally but also relationally throughout the project.




3.12    Limitations


Although positionality was intentionally embedded in the methodological approach, it inevitably
shaped interpretation. Bias and prejudice are always present in qualitative research (Hegelund, 2005;
Morse et al., 2002). Iterative conversations with my principal and second PhD supervisors, mentor,
and the wider research group provided space for reflexive discussion about how my race,
background, and experiences influenced the analysis. This reflexive structure, described to
participants in the consent process, was central to ensuring accountability.

The study was also shaped by the scale and focus of the ethnographic fieldwork. Participant
observation, purposive selection, and semi-structured interviews necessarily limited the sample size
in a time-limited project. However, the ethnographic approach provided the depth and contextual
richness that quantitative methods may not capture (Goodson & Vassar, 2011). Focusing on
scientists based at the Wellcome Genome Campus reflected both accessibility and the aim of
producing specific policy-relevant insights within the UK context. This narrow focus limits
generalisability (Savage, 2000) but immersion in the field enabled me to seek out voices beyond
immediate networks, reducing the risk of over-reliance on snowball sampling and allowing alternative
or less prominent perspectives to be included.

A further limitation lies in the scope of the research. By centring on genomic researchers working
primarily with large quantitative datasets, the study does not fully address how population descriptors
are used across all areas of genomics. Nonetheless, the methodological framework developed here is
transferable. Its replication in other institutional or disciplinary contexts could support the development
of more locally responsive frameworks for equity in genomics, as opposed to generic solutions which
have often proved inadequate.

Challenges specific to ethnography were also encountered. Immersion in the research setting
demanded careful negotiation between objectivity and subjectivity, as close engagement risked
influencing interpretation (Hammersley & Atkinson, 2019). Building trust required sustained
investment of time, patience, and cultural sensitivity (Fetterman, 2019). Bringing a critical social
science lens into a genomic science environment also carried risks of being perceived as overly
exposing or insufficiently respectful of disciplinary norms. While there is substantial ELSI (ethical,

84 | P a g e
legal and social implications) work in genomics, it often remains siloed, complicating cross-disciplinary
engagement.

Ethical concerns further shaped the study. Ethnographic work involves recording sensitive aspects of
participants’ professional lives, raising issues of consent, privacy, and context-specific vulnerabilities
(Denzin & Lincoln, 2018). Limited resources, time pressures, and the unpredictability of fieldwork
added to these challenges (Emerson et al., 2011). Together, these factors underscore the resilience
and ethical commitment required to undertake ethnography in scientific settings.

The review of institutional publications also had limitations. While it provided a valuable overview of
how social justice questions intersect with data categorisation in genomics at a major UK institution, it
risked bias towards literature that aligned with my emerging interpretations. Reproducibility was
limited by the context-specific and purposive nature of the review, and the dataset may not be
exhaustive. To mitigate this, I clearly documented the review process, remained transparent about its
intentional focus, and regularly discussed findings with my advisor.

Finally, while data saturation was achieved through a sufficient number of formal interviews,
complemented by extensive informal interviews and over six months of participant observation
(DiStefano & Yang, 2024; Staller, 2021) the study remains bounded by its institutional and temporal
context.




85 | P a g e
4 Race, Ethnicity and Ancestry on the Page: Sanger
    Publications May 2023 Jan 2024


4.1 Introduction

Studies investigating the language around human population descriptors have shown that race and
ethnicity are used inconsistently and in conceptually complex ways in genetics (see section 2.5.2).
This chapter examines how these issues appear in publications involving Sanger scientists, focusing
on papers circulated in the internal weekly research bulletin between May 2023 and January 2024.
Treating these outputs as part of the ethnographic field, the chapter explores how population
descriptors are constructed, justified, and problematised within a UK genomic research environment.

The bulletin collated work across Sanger’s programmes (see section 3.8.1), spanning discovery
science, methods development, and translational applications. Studies range from Harrison et al.,(
2023) who provided an example of clinically oriented research through a randomised trial evaluating
ruxolitinib for polycythaemia vera, while Audain et al. (2023) demonstrated large-scale gene discovery
in rare disease by analysing extensive exome datasets for congenital heart disease. Other studies,
such as Liao et al. (2023) in the Human Pangenome Project and Lotfollahi et al.(2023) on deep-
learning integration of single-cell data, illustrate the Institute’s participation in large international
consortia and its reliance on shared population datasets.

A discourse-analytic lens is used to read these publications as texts, attending to the language
through which categories of race, ethnicity, and ancestry are produced, circulated, and rendered
legitimate. This involves examining what is said about population groups, how often and by whom, as
well as what remains implicit or unsaid. The chapter first analyses how population descriptors are
constructed and harmonised across studies, then traces how these practices embed hierarchies of
race, ancestry, and Europeanness, and finally considers how ethical frameworks in the papers’
address, or fail to address, these dynamics.


4.2 The Construction of Populations


This section examines how human data is categorised across different areas of genomic research,
focusing on how groups are conceptualised, constructed, and labelled within studies involving Sanger
scientists. It considers the purposes these groupings serve, the methods through which they are

86 | P a g e
generated, and the interpretive and ethical challenges they present. In doing so, it explores how
statistical approaches, data sources, and harmonisation practices contribute to the visibility and
reproduction of concepts of race, ethnicity, and ancestry within genomic science.

The purpose of creating groups in genomic research should be restated. Across genomics, where the
goal is to understand genes and their functions, comparison is fundamental. Differences in gene
frequency, structure, or function are typically assessed between defined populations or relative to a
reference dataset, approaches that enable researchers to identify variation and infer biological
meaning (Jobling et al., 2013; Lewontin, 1972).

Population descriptors and descent-oriented classifications are often treated as proxies for shared
genetic origin; for example, the label “European” is used with the implicit assumption of recent
common ancestry. Although this review focuses on race, ethnicity, and genetic ancestry (Table 1),
descent-oriented groups are not always necessary. Even when used, the meaning or salience of any
given population label can shift across time, place, and scientific context (Dauda et al., 2023; Popejoy
et al., 2020).

Conceptual frameworks such as genetic ancestry, genetic similarity, and the socially constructed
categories of race and ethnicity were present, often in overlapping or conflated ways. This overlap
reinforced the need to interrogate not just which terms were used, but how and in what sense they are
being used.

A label on its own did not reveal whether a study is invoking race, ethnicity, or genetic ancestry as its
underlying population construct. For example, the label “White” might be used to indicate a social
category (race), a cultural identity (ethnicity), or a cluster of shared genetic variation (genetic
ancestry). Distinguishing among these uses required close attention to textual and methodological
cues, what was being compared, how similarity was inferred, and which assumptions were made
explicit or left unsaid.




Harmonisation and the Reproduction of Labels



In this section, I consider harmonisation of data and how it can push choices about categories,
especially for population descriptors.

Across the 147 papers in my sample, population grouping was far from routine in either method or
justification. Of the 84 studies that named a dataset or collection, 66 also reported some form of
population categorisation.


87 | P a g e
It was clear that the dimension of genetic ancestry was most often referred to in the reviewed papers
and used across a range of global datasets. Evidence that the group label was used to capture
genetic ancestry was inferred both from definitions of how continental labels are commonly used and
from examples where they are associated with the term “ancestry” or “genetic ancestry” (Qu et al.,
2023; Rasooly et al., 2023; Rhie et al., 2023; Stefanucci et al., 2023). Genetic ancestry was used as a
representation of the DNA inherited from shared ancestors (Kang et al., 2023).

The most common label by far to indicate an ancestry group was the use of continental descriptors;
European, African, East Asian, South Asian, West Asian, Oceanian, and American and sometimes
these were referred to as “major” or “superpopulations” as shown in Figure 13




Figure 13 Illustration representing the geographical location and sample size according to
continental groups of 1000 genome data (Hallast et al., 2023 p. 356). With permission from
Springer Nature.




(Chu et al., 2023; Chundru et al., 2023; Hallast et al., 2023; Henry et al., 2023; Qu et al., 2023; Ravn
et al., 2023; Rhie et al., 2023; Sikkema et al., 2023; Wang et al., 2023).

“Among the 1,008 individuals, 194 had African ancestry originating from the African continent
(excluding African ancestry in the SW USA (ASW) and African Caribbean in Barbados (ACB))”
(Hallast et al., 2023 p.6).

The frequent use of geographic and continental group labels reflects the findings of other studies that
have shown their routine use in genetic studies to demonstrate the idea of genetic ancestry in
population groups (Panofsky & Bliss, 2017).

In several cases, the definitions of categories were only available in supplementary materials, making
the categorisation work effectively invisible in the main text. As a result, superpopulations from


88 | P a g e
resources such as the 1000 Genomes Project were invoked routinely and rarely problematised (Briest
et al., 2023; Hallast et al., 2023; Liao et al., 2023; Meng et al., 2024).

In at least 43 of those papers, the labels appear to have been reproduced directly from the source
dataset, such as the UK Biobank, the Office for National Statistics (ONS), or the 1000 Genomes
Project, rather than constructed or justified within the study itself (Chen et al., 2023; Kentistou et al.,
2023; Wall et al., 2023). Most often, these studies carried categories forward with limited local
rationale.

Wang et al., (2023) defined ancestry groups by relying on the classifications already assigned within
the contributing GWAS cohorts, which typically combined genotype principal component analysis with
cohort-level ancestry labels. They then conducted fixed-effect meta-analyses within these predefined
groups before combining the ancestry-specific results. Figure 14 illustrates how these inherited
categories structure the comparison of risk variants, since the analyses depend on broad continental
groupings rather than clusters generated anew for the study.




Figure 14 A and B Overlap of ancestry-specific prostate cancer risk variants and C Effect size
comparisons across ancestry groups (Wang et al., 2023 p. 2067). With permission from Springer
Nature.




Although the specific labels showed some variation, they recurrently used the same geopolitical labels
to describe populations or datasets. Even when the source datasets or studies were mentioned, the

89 | P a g e
ancestral grouping and labels that may have been part of an original dataset were not always listed
(see Figure 15) (Chung et al., 2023; Lord et al., 2024).




Figure 15 : Image of table of characteristics UK biobank data was used in the study ancestry
groups are not routinely listed. (Chung et al., 2023 p. 6). Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.

Many studies re-use existing population labels due to pressure to harmonise data across projects and
biobanks. For example, Sikkema et al. (2023), in their review ‘An Integrated Cell Atlas of the Lung in
Health and Disease’, align their population descriptors with public datasets to enable comparison
(Figure 16), illustrating how harmonisation can perpetuate inherited categories rather than invite
reconsideration.




90 | P a g e
Figure 16 An overview of the samples included in the Human Lung Cell Atlas (HLCA), donor
metadata and harmonisation. (Sikkema et al., 2023 p. Supplementary table 2). Available under CC
BY 4.0 licence.

More broadly in large collaborations, harmonisation enables data sharing, reproducibility, and
comparability, yet this technical uniformity imposes subtle constraints (Gonçalves & Musen, 2019).

Within these papers, although harmonisation is seldom named, the uniformity of population labels
across projects reflects this dynamic: the drive to maintain shareable data reproduces a narrow
descriptive vocabulary even as analytical methods evolve.

Groups described as sharing genetic ancestry were most often generated through some type of
genetic inference, although detailed methods for defining an ancestry group were not always
included, particularly we can assume because not all studies generated the datasets themselves
(Foreman et al., 2023; Jindal et al., 2023; Karim et al., 2023; J. J.-K. Lee et al., 2023). When labels
were generated or harmonised within a study rather than inherited, the dimensionality and naming
were inconsistent.
91 | P a g e
Groups defined using similar quantitative procedures, such as PCA-based ancestry inference or
genetic similarity thresholds, might be named by geography in one paper (Chundru et al., 2023) by
ethnicity in another, sometimes within the same project (Meng et al., 2024). We also see
superpopulation labels (EUR, AFR, EAS, SAS, AMR) used as if self-explanatory, without parallel
discussion of how these terms were aligned or validated for the specific dataset at hand (Qu et al.,
2023; Ravn et al., 2023; Rhie et al., 2023). Even when alignment appears tidy at the continental
scale, it is seldom tested at finer granularities, sub-continental, diasporic, or admixed identities, where
the correlation between self-report and computation may weaken or change.




4.2.1   Alternative Labelling



Here, I examine the population groups used and, in particular, how word choices signal the
constructed nature of these categories. By comparing terms such as “labelled” and “European-like,” I
show that although authors sometimes gesture towards the provisional character of these groupings,
they continue to rely on inherited continental and subpopulation labels.

Some alternative labels were used to capture ancestry dimensions that may suggest a value held
about the methods for determining the groups. One paper (Molina-Aguilar & Robles-Espinoza, 2023)
referred to groups at one point underneath an image depicting lack of representation of samples from
non-European ancestry in large public repositories as ‘Labelled’ by their non-genetic continental term
(Figure 17).




92 | P a g e
Figure 17 Example of image showing the use of ancestry as a label rather than an essential
quality. (Molina-Aguilar & Robles-Espinoza, 2023 p. 3). Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.

This paper focused primarily on the lack of ancestral diversity in cancer research data rather than on
a specific genomic analysis. The authors may therefore have been intentionally drawing attention to
the limitations of continental labels for representing the full diversity of datasets. However, because
the prefix “labelled” was confined to an image description and not used elsewhere in the text, it reads
less as a consistent terminological shift and more as a commentary on the act of labelling itself. This
choice subtly points to the constructed nature of these groupings. It highlights the decision to assign a
label rather than revealing an inherent truth about biological difference. The wording calls attention to
the scientists’ agency in creating not only the dimensional categories but also the linguistic labels
used to define them.

Interestingly, Sergouniotis et al. (2024), who used UK Biobank data to explore genetic predictors of
retinal morphology, adopted a slightly different linguistic strategy. They appended the suffix “-like” to
ancestry terms, creating expressions such as “European-like ancestries,” “African-like ancestries,”
and “Asian-like populations.” This phrasing suggests a degree of approximation rather than
categorical certainty. For instance, they wrote:

        “After applying standard genetic and OCT quality control filters (Patel, 2016; Currant, 2021),
        we defined a subset of the UK Biobank population that (i) can be considered genetically well-

93 | P a g e
        ixed (i.e. includes participants that were assigned by genotype principal component analysis
        (PCA) to a cluster with subjects of mostly European-like ancestries).” (Sergouniotis et al., 2024
        Pg 5 )

Although the authors did not explicitly justify this terminology, the use of “-like” aligns with the
methodological description of how clusters were defined. It acknowledges that ancestry groups are
not homogenous and that clustering reflects degrees of genetic similarity rather than absolute
categorical boundaries.

This approach implicitly recognises that ancestry categories are analytic constructs, shaped by
computational thresholds rather than discrete biological divisions. Yet, in both Molina-Aguilar &
Robles-Espinoza (2023) and Sergouniotis et al. (2024), the continued use of continental descriptors
reinforces the prevailing convention in genomics: to divide populations along broad, geo-political lines
even while acknowledging their constructedness.

Within these continental ancestry categories, subpopulations were often defined in more granular
terms that incorporated sociocultural, non-genetic, or biological dimensions. Examples include
classifications such as Brahmin, African-American, British Asian, Ashkenazi Jewish, Gambian in West
Africa, and Finnish (Chu et al., 2023; Chundru et al., 2023; DeGorter et al., 2023; Liao et al., 2023;
Rasooly et al., 2023; Thygesen et al., 2023).

However, in most cases, these secondary labels originated from the conventions of the source
datasets, suggesting that researchers rarely departed from inherited classificatory systems when
defining group identities within genomic analyses.




4.2.2   Statistical Approaches


Next, I reflect on how researchers define comparison groups in human genomic studies and how
different classificatory choices shape the kinds of variation that become visible.

Although genetic ancestry is frequently discussed as a dimension, what is often demonstrated
through genetic inference methods is genetic similarity (quantifiable genetic overlap between
individuals, populations, or species (Dauda et al., 2023)). It is conceptually linked to ancestry
because those who share recent ancestry tend to have greater areas of similarity within their
genomes. Although genetic similarity can be measured directly, ancestry can only ever be inferred. It
relies on assumptions about reference populations and historical time frames, meaning it inevitably
reflects the choices researchers make about how to construct a group.



94 | P a g e
Statistical tools are used to cluster complex genomes using averaged measures of similarity,
producing apparent structure within what is, in reality, continuous variation. They rely on quantitative
inference to define ancestry-based groupings (Rouskas et al., 2023) and to find genetic similarity in
pre-labelled samples such as Rasooly et al. (2023) who conduct a large-scale meta-analysis of heart
failure GWAS using control data from individuals of European ancestry.

These tools make invisible variation legible and analysable, but at the cost of reifying fluid genetic
relationships into discrete units for comparison.

Not all datasets in human genetics use descent-oriented groups. Some research instead employs
alternative criteria such as disease phenotype, age, sex, or healthcare utilisation to establish
comparison sets (Ferreira et al., 2023; Ismail et al., 2023). These examples show that such
classifications may be unnecessary and demonstrate approaches that capture variation beyond
ancestry-based framing. In contrast, many studies adopt specific descent-oriented groupings without
explaining their purpose or analytical value, and these group labels often play little role in the results
or conclusions (Jiang et al., 2023; Kentistou et al., 2023).

Methods such as principal component analysis (PCA), ADMIXTURE, and logistic regression models
are employed to infer genetic similarity among individuals (Chundru et al., 2023; Rasooly et al., 2023;
Wall et al., 2023). These methods cluster individuals according to shared allelic patterns, generating
statistical proximities that are often interpreted as indicators of ancestry. PCA, for instance, reduces
genetic variation across loci into a few dimensions, allowing samples to be positioned according to
relative similarity as discussed in section 2.5.1 (Novembre & Barton, 2018). Yet at the same time
these approaches can blur boundaries, producing overlapping clusters that reflect continuous
variation rather than the discrete groups implied by the labels applied to them. As shown by figure 18
the PCA plots of array and RNAseq data using the authors’ selected SNP sets do not show discrete
separation of the broad self-reported ethnicity groups they have chosen to label as clusters. Instead,
individuals assigned the same label are widely dispersed, and groups overlap substantially across the
axes. This pattern underscores the limited correspondence between the inferred genetic structure and
the categorical boundaries implied by the labels.




95 | P a g e
Figure 18 PCA plots of Population structure. Consistency with self-reported ethnicities is claimed
by the overlapping points suggest blurred boundaries. (Fachrul et al. 2023 p. 5). Available under CC
BY 4.0 licence.

ADMIXTURE estimates, for each genome, the proportions derived from different inferred ancestral
components. Researchers use these ancestry profiles to compare groups, examine admixture
histories, and evaluate how self-reported or assigned labels correspond to genetic patterns see
Figure 19) (Lawson et al., 2018).

UMAP visualises these relationships in high-dimensional genetic data into a visual map that
preserves local relationships between individuals. Researchers use these maps to detect fine-scale
substructure and to see how samples with known backgrounds group within larger clusters as, shown
in Figure 20.




96 | P a g e
Figure 19 ADMIXTURE analysis. Labels are self-reported and shows these distinct lines of self-
identity show up in the data by the mixture that they hold. Interestingly “general” which denoted a lack
of specified identity hold prominence both in terms of size and the structure 1. (Wall et al., 2023 p.
3).Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.




Figure 20 UMAP plot showing locations or self-reported group memberships within Pakistan
and South India clusters. Segregations that occur out of clusters expected are said to represent the
metropolitan nature of the recruitment centre (Wall et al., 2023 p.3).Available under CC BY 4.0
licence.



97 | P a g e
When applied to pre-labelled datasets such as the 1000 Genomes or UK Biobank references, these
approaches reproduce the initial labelling conventions. This introduces a circularity in which
computational outputs appear to validate categories that were socially or historically defined rather
than genetically grounded (Dauda et al., 2023). This example illustrates a broader methodological
dependence on legacy datasets that define what counts as an ancestry group. The result is a self-
reinforcing logic in which labels derived from earlier classifications guide new analyses, which in turn
reproduce the same categorical boundaries Chundru et al., 2023 write:

        “We recognise that these genetically inferred ancestry groups do not capture the full genetic
        diversity of human populations. The classifications were based on genetic similarity to
        individuals in the 1000 Genomes and HGDP reference datasets, inferred from PCA.” (Chundru
        et al., 2023 p. 4)

While this makes no explicit difference to the methods they return to it as a limitation of their study.
Despite their findings due to understudied populations and sample sizes their power for gene
discovery is reduced.

This example illustrates a broader methodological dependence on legacy datasets that define what
counts as an ancestry group. The result is a self-reinforcing logic in which labels derived from earlier
classifications guide new analyses, which in turn reproduce the same categorical boundaries. As seen
in studies by Senkin et al., (2023) and Malawsky et al. (2023), the drive for harmonisation across
datasets maintains the appearance of coherence even as the underlying definitions diverge.




4.2.3   Identity, Inference and the Power to Classify



Following the previous discussion of computational approaches to inferring ancestry, this section
turns to how population groups are constructed when researchers combine these inferred categories
with self-reported identities. Rather than revisiting the mechanics of inference, the focus here is on
how self-defined and algorithmically defined labels interact, how each is used in analysis, and how the
authority to classify shifts between participants and data-driven systems.

When primary data are collected directly from participants, self-reported and self-identified categories
serve as the mechanism for defining population groups. These labels, often chosen from predefined
lists, are used to capture ancestry, ethnicity, or other social descriptors (Aung et al., 2023;
Sergouniotis et al., 2024). The terms 'self-reported' and 'self-identified' were used interchangeably in
these papers. Their common usage and comparative lack of critique or limitation saw them both



98 | P a g e
treated as valid indicators of population structure. Often with statements about how they are
comparable with ancestry that was inferred computationally (Fachrul et al., 2023).

As Malawsky et al. (2023) comment shows:

        “A UMAP with 4 PCs identified two large clusters which largely corresponded to individuals
        who self-reported as being of Pakistani or Bangladeshi origin.” (P.e3)

On the one hand, participant identity becomes validation of algorithmic categorisation. Yet, on the
other hand, there is omission of how apparent alignment is driven by the social and historical
structuring of identity, not biological essentialism . Factors such as migration histories, language, and
census regimes contribute to these alignments (A. Lewis et al., 2022).

Sikkema et al., (2023) acknowledge that ethnicity metadata were based on self-reports and
harmonised across datasets, noting that while useful, these measures imperfectly capture the
diversity within human populations. They write:

        “Ethnicity metadata were based on self-reports and harmonized across datasets, which is an
        imperfect approach to representing the diversity of the atlas. SNP-based inference of genetic
        ancestry constitutes a more objective and therefore preferable approach…” (Sikkema et al.,
        2023 p.17)

This perspective situates self-reported ethnicity as both limited and necessary. It acknowledges that
categories are subjective, yet argues they provide meaningful context for genetic association. Wider
scholarship has discussed this interaction between identity and science as dialogic: participants
interpret and reshape their own understandings of race and ancestry through engagement with
genetic studies (Condit et al., 1998; Smart, 2022).

This assumption reflects a persistent tension in genomics: the need to balance participants' own
understandings of identity with researchers’ statistical needs for population stratification. The
combining of self-reported and computationally inferred categories to create composite groupings also
