reflects a pragmatic effort to align social identifiers with genetic data for analytical consistency.

However, such hybridisation can obscure rather than clarify the conceptual basis of population labels.
For example Meng et al. (2024) , in their GWAS study of major depression, describe using PCA
assignments for most individuals to ancestry groups by matching them to reference populations, while
simultaneously relying on self-report for “Hispanic or Latin American” groups.

In studies where genetic inference and self-identification are jointly used, individuals whose
genotypes do not align neatly with reference clusters may be reclassified to fit the closest



99 | P a g e
computationally defined group (McLaren et al., 2023). At times, this misalignment also resulted in
removal (Aung et al., 2023; Meng et al., 2024).

This process demonstrates the inherent instability of ancestry categories. It transforms subjective
social identifiers into data points that can be adjusted or overwritten to maintain statistical coherence.
The analytical precision of computational methods thus absorbs a foundation of socially contingent
and historically inherited categories.

The interplay of methodological necessity, inherited classification systems, and harmonisation
pressures produces a language of population that is both technical and social. In the following
section, the analysis turns to the values embedded in these classifications, exploring how
assumptions about hierarchy, typicality, and whiteness are reproduced through the language and
practices of genomic population science.




4.3 Hierarchies and Power in Genomic Categorisation


In this section, I examine how genomic research papers embed racial hierarchies through routine
labelling and measurement. I show how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are repeatedly conflated in both
language and practice, including cases where “ancestry” substitutes for race as a putatively neutral
proxy. I then trace how Europeanness and whiteness become co-constituted as methodological
baselines, while “diversity” is articulated as what lies outside that norm. Drawing on examples of “non-
European” and related formulations, I reveal how classification choices shape what becomes visible in
analysis and what is rendered invisible in these papers.




4.3.1   Embedded Racial Hierarchies and Conflation of Categories



This section examines how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are routinely conflated in genomic research
linguistically, procedurally, and analytically. I show how “ancestry” then functions as an ethically
acceptable proxy for race, naturalising social categories as biological facts.

This review found several examples of the use of race labels, but with some variation in how they
were applied and understood. Numerous papers used racial terms such as “White” and “Black” to
describe populations (Aung et al., 2023; Jamrozy et al., 2023; Lagou et al., 2023; M. Peter et al.,
2023; Sikkema et al., 2023; Taylor et al., 2024). Fewer studies use the word race itself; however,
when race was used it was operationalised together in data collection. For example, Aung et al.

100 | P a g e
(2023) discuss race and ethnicity together as part of self-reporting as shown in Figure 21 taken from a
table of study cohort characteristics:




Figure 21 Section of table of race and ethnicity as study cohort characteristics. The image
demonstrates how race and ethnicity are treated as, interchangeable demographic variables (Aung et
al., 2023 p. 812). Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.




Participants assigned themselves to these available options. This reinforces researcher-defined
categories, which often appear when self-report is claimed. In providing only predetermined options,
researchers have power over who is included and how identities become part of the study.

This conflation is both conceptual and procedural: it reflects underlying assumptions about what
constitutes meaningful human difference and reproduces them through research design. In this way,
classification practices not only record but also help sustain the analytical blurring that underpins
racial categorisation in science.

In contrast, Qu et al.,( 2023) demonstrates an even more complex conflation between ethnicity and
ancestry, simultaneously describing data as “multi-ethnic,” “trans-ethnic,” and “ancestry-specific.” For
example:

       “Five sets of SNP weights were generated based on the respective LD patterns from the
       following populations, African American (AA), Hispanic American (HAMR), East Asian (ASN),
       Northern European (EUR) and all of the above populations (i.e. trans-ethnic)”(Qu et al., 2023
       p. 3)

       “We show that in the absence of a well-powered trans-ethnic GWAS … trans-ethnic scores
       can be generated from European ancestry GWAS using Bayesian approaches.”(Qu et al.,
       2023 p. 2)

Here, ethnicity and ancestry are used interchangeably to describe genomic datasets, allowing
ancestry to function as a proxy for both race and ethnicity. This reflects a second form of conflation: a

101 | P a g e
linguistic and methodological substitution in which “ancestry” becomes an ethically acceptable stand-
in for social categories that are inaccurate or outdated. Thus, even when race is excluded by design,
its logic remains embedded in the conceptual and analytical framework of population description.

While certain European countries refrain from collecting ethnicity data, invoking GDPR interpretations
or broader commitments to removing racial classifications from public discourse (Simon, 2025), only
one study in this review explicitly advanced this rationale. Here Riba et al.,(2023) state:

       “The information collected in the Patient domain (demographics) encompasses a list of items
       suitable for all countries. We did not include items that cannot be collected in some EU
       member states (e.g., ethnicity, for legal reasons).” (Riba et al., 2023 p. 9).

Given that the collaborations represented in these studies were international, this limitation is
significant. Ethnicity data collection is restricted in France and Germany, where legal and historical
frameworks discourage the categorisation of populations by race or ethnicity ( Simon, 2025). Of the
publications reviewed, France appeared in 22 author affiliations and Germany in 31, suggesting that a
considerable proportion of studies were led or co-authored from countries where such data cannot be
collected. This highlights how national ethical standpoints vary, yet are negotiated largely invisibly
within international research collaborations.

The absence of consensus about when and how ethnicity data are statistically useful also reinforces
conceptual confusion. If banning the collection of ethnicity data is perceived as a strategy to prevent
racial harm, race and ethnicity become conflated in both intent and effect. The assumption that not
naming ethnicity is synonymous with preventing racism reflects an unresolved tension between the
ethical management of difference and the scientific need for demographic accuracy.

Hageman et al.,(2023) illustrate how race and ethnicity are treated as interchangeable, both
conceptually and through their conflation with social determinants. They write:

“Race/ethnicity may also improve risk prediction but was not included in the current study because of
the close correlation between race/ethnicity and social economic determinants” (Hageman et al., 2023
p. 1712).

This phrasing treats “race/ethnicity” as a single unit and positions it as a correlate of socioeconomic
factors. It suggests two perspectives: first, that race and ethnicity are not themselves social factors
but are associated with them, and second, that social determinants are viewed as confounders in risk
prediction. In either case, the choice to exclude these variables shows how conceptual and
procedural uncertainty around race and ethnicity shapes what becomes visible or invisible in analysis.

Most studies listed both ancestry and ethnicity descriptors within their metadata and participant
descriptions for example, “European ancestry,” “African ancestry,” or “Asian ethnicity” without
102 | P a g e
clarifying any conceptual distinction. Throughout, there is an implicit assumption that these terms
have intrinsic, widely understood meanings requiring no further explanation, much like “height” or
“sex” (Aung et al., 2023; Jamrozy et al., 2023; Lagou et al., 2023).

Similarly, Sikkema et al. (2023) explicitly harmonise self-reported racial and ethnic data with genetic
superpopulation categories:

       “Self-reported ethnicity categories were then harmonized with the superpopulation categories
       as follows: Black was categorized as African, White as European, and Latino as admixed
       American…” (Sikkema et al., 2023 p. 1581)

This literal relabelling assumes that race and ethnicity correspond directly to shared genetic ancestry,
effectively translating social identity into biology. The suggestion that ethnicity-specific thresholds
could improve analytical accuracy (Chung et al., 2023) reinforces this conflation, treating social
classification as a proxy for biological difference.

While Sikkema et al. (2023) offer the most literal example, earlier cases demonstrate that this is not
an isolated practice. Across genomic studies, race, ethnicity, and ancestry are used interchangeably
in ways that naturalise social categories as biological facts. Given the evident conflation, blurring, and
inconsistency, this assumption seems to rest on an uncritical confidence in the shared meaning of
these terms.

Some papers even refer to “multi-ancestry” or “ancestry-specific” analyses while simultaneously
categorising data using social or ethnic terms such as “Hispanic/Latino” or “South Asian” (Qu et al.,
2023; Sikkema et al., 2023). This recurrent blending of terms demonstrates that ethnicity and ancestry
become operationally intertwined, suggesting that avoiding racial terminology or substituting it with
ostensibly genetic population descriptors, functions more as a methodological workaround than a
genuine conceptual resolution.

The substitution also reflects assumptions about the legitimacy of ancestry as a concept. While
ancestry can be statistically inferred, such inference presumes that what is being inferred exists as an
underlying, essential reality. Yet individuals do not physically carry the DNA of all their ancestral lines,
and different computational methods infer ancestry in different ways meaning that ancestry is, in
practice, constructed through the application of computational methods. Figure 22 presents an
illustration of the UK Biobank retinal imaging dataset. The image visually depicts how genetic and
optical coherence tomography (OCT) quality filters narrowed the cohort to those deemed genetically
“well-mixed” and with sufficiently high-resolution retinal images.




103 | P a g e
Figure 22 Quality Control. The creation of the cluster of participants with European-like features,
from Sergouniotis et al. (2024) p. 129. Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.




The straight edges of the stacked retinal blocks evoke the straightening performed by the quality
control process itself, where irregular human variation is reshaped to meet the analytical requirements
of the study. Genetic ancestry appears only as a criterion for filtering, not as a dimension of inquiry,
yet it determines whose data become legible enough to be visualised. The image therefore captures
not simply a dataset being refined, but a population being constructed to fit the contours of the
methods.

This belief in the capacity of these methods to reveal something authentic or pre-existing lends them
an aura of scientific and ethical legitimacy, as though statistical power, not in the technical sense, but
as an instrument of scientific authority could uncover the true nature of human difference. It is within
this aura that certain populations become the measure of accuracy and legitimacy.

4.3.2   Admixture and the Problem of Purity



The preceding discussion has shown how the authority of genomic classification continues to
structure language and meaning in science. This same power is manifest in the operation of



104 | P a g e
admixture and the computational tools designed to measure it. They embody the tension between
recognising human interconnectedness and re-inscribing boundaries in the name of analytical rigour.

Across the Sanger-affiliated publications, approaches related to admixture and ancestry inference
were common. Twenty referred conceptually to “mixed” or “admixed” populations, and many
employed computational methods such as ADMIXTURE, STRUCTURE, or comparable ancestry-
deconvolution tools to make an analytic sense of the population structure in such groups. These tools
are standard not only in population genetics and population genomics but also in the genetic
epidemiology of disease, where they are used to adjust for or interpret population stratification.
Admixture-based reasoning has therefore become routine across these domains, providing a basis for
examining both its technical applications and its conceptual implications.

Many of the Sanger-affiliated papers reviewed explicitly addressed cohort diversity, using terms like
mixed, admixed, multi-ethnic, or diverse especially to denote inclusion of non-European populations.
A smaller number of papers used “admixed” or “mixed ancestry” as descriptive categories when
defining sample inclusion, interpreting results, or framing population-level findings. Such instances
show that the computational language of admixture frequently extends into conceptual and
classificatory practice, shaping how diversity, relatedness, and belonging are discussed within the
literature. For example Qu et al. (2023) states:

       “The Brazilian population in the ELSA-Brasil cohort is three-way admixed. We therefore
       compared the transethnic score in this population to each of the three founders: American,
       African and European” (Qu et al., 2023 p. 6)

This phrasing illustrates how individuals and populations are conceived as both mixed and yet still
divisible into constituent ancestral parts. The description of Brazil as “three-way admixed”
simultaneously acknowledges historical interconnection and reinstates a tripartite model of origins. In
such cases, mixture does not dissolve categorical boundaries but reaffirms them: difference is
retained as a measurable proportion. The language of admixture thus enables a form of multiplicity
that remains tethered to discrete ancestral groups, preserving the logic of separation within the very
act of describing combination.

These tensions are also visible in the visual language of admixture. Standard admixture plots present
individuals as composites of predefined ancestral components, turning continuous histories of gene
flow into discretised colour blocks. As Figure 23 illustrates, these images give the appearance of tidy
ancestral partitions even when the underlying data are far more complex.




105 | P a g e
Figure 23 Overview of the genetic structure and global ancestry inference. (A) Countries
represented in the database (B) Principal components analysis (PCA). (C) ADMIXTURE from
Hernández et al. (2020) P. 1044. Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.

In population genomics study admixture was integral to the scientific approach. As an analytic output,
the figure demonstrates how population structure is statistically inferred; as a visual artefact, it shows
how those inferences are stabilised into discrete and legible ancestral components. Another example
was the CCR5Δ32 evolution study, which used local ancestry analysis around the CCR5 locus to infer
a European origin and to interpret its distribution in Latin American and other populations (Ravn et al.,
2023). Inclusion decisions were based on coverage and filtering, but admixture itself was not framed
as a problem to be removed it was central to the evolutionary narrative they sought to construct.

In contrast, despite the frequency with which admixture appears as a descriptive category in GWAS
and medical genomics, relatively few studies treat admixed genetic data as an object of analysis in its
own right. The level of genetic diversity within study samples was frequently treated as a problem to
be managed rather than a feature to be explored (Chen et al., 2023; Chundru et al., 2023; Henry et
al., 2023; Meng et al., 2024). Population structure typically had to be controlled for through principal
component analysis or by stratified analyses within broad ancestry groups, as in the HPRC
pangenome (Liao et al., 2023) and muscle-strength GWAS (Huang et al., 2023).

Heterogeneity, likewise, was at times treated as a technical problem. As in Figure 24, taken from
Fachrul et al. (2023) the PCA plot contains grey points labelled “NA”, representing participants who
were not placed into any predefined ethnicity categories; their muted colouring marks them as present
106 | P a g e
yet unrecognised, included in the visual field but excluded from the labelled groups. The aggregation
of “mixed,” “minority,” or “Other” categories, as in Jamrozy et al. (2023a), also illustrates how those
who do not fit neatly into boundaries, whether racial or ancestry categories, are rendered
methodologically invisible.




Figure 24 The grouping of minority categories and aggregation as “Other” . (Jamrozy et al,
2023 p.5). Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.

Linguistically, Other marks a form of conceptual expulsion: any blurring of categorical boundaries
results in erasure, as individuals lose both ancestral and ethnic identifiers. This means that admixture
is not only a genetic descriptor but also a disciplinary mechanism, it delineates who belongs within the
frame of analysis and who falls outside it.

Significantly, labels such as Admixed American or Mixed American/Latino/Amerindian lacked clear or
consistent definitions (Honigberg et al., 2023; Lord et al., 2024) erasing the complexity of lived
ancestry and to computational manageability. When individuals do not conform to pre-existing
categories, they are either reclassified or removed entirely (Aung et al., 2023; Faquih et al., 2023;
Warrier et al., 2023). As Aung states:

       “We also excluded participants who lacked information on race or ethnicity … those who
       selected ‘do not know’ or ‘prefer not to answer’ were excluded from the study sample.” (Aung
       et al., 2023 p. 809)

This example shows how inability or refusal to align with predefined labels results in exclusion,
reinforcing rigid boundaries reminiscent of early racial taxonomies. At the same time, the exclusion of
admixture is increasingly recognised as a source of inequity, one that limits discovery and
perpetuates bias in genomic resources as Li states:
107 | P a g e
       “There is a strong need for more diverse and deeper human multiomic data, which will
       generate novel insights as a complement to other pre-clinical models for drug discovery and
       development.” (Li et al., 2023)

As in many large GWAS, analysis also restricts genetic relatedness by excluding close relatives,
ensuring that only one individual from each family network contributed to the final dataset (Aung et al.,
2023; Honigberg et al., 2023). While framed as a routine quality control step, relatedness filtering
disproportionately affects populations characterised by dense kinship networks, small community
size, or historically constrained sampling frames. As TallBear (2013) and (Fullwiley (2011) have
shown, such assumptions embed particular social models of population into genomic practice. The
result is that populations may be excluded for being too similar as readily as for being too different.
These exclusions are statistically motivated, but they nonetheless carry social consequences,
particularly for marginalised and underrepresented groups whose demographic structures depart from
this ideal. However, exclusion is not the only available statistical response to relatedness and
population structure. In their GWAS study Meng et al (2024) use GENESIS, a statistical method to
adjust for population structure and relatedness, within the analysis. Researchers, therefore, have
methodological choices available, yet the social consequences or ethics of technical choices were not
discussed in any of the papers reviewed.

Admixture itself\ therefore, appeared in several guises: as a statistical parameter inferred through
modelling (Chundru et al., 2023; Senkin et al., 2023), a confounder to be controlled or eliminated
(McLaren et al., 2023), or a measure of diversity arrayed along a continuum (Sousa da Mota et al.,
2023). In each case, it operated within a logic of containment; an effort to stabilise populations as
internally coherent and externally distinct. When categories such as native, indigenous, or reference
populations were contrasted with admixed ones, the implication was that only certain groups were
sufficiently “pure” or homogeneous to serve as baselines. This framing reproduces a longstanding
classificatory hierarchy, positioning mixture as deviation and purity as scientific norm.

The logic of purity, though often implicit, was made explicit in methodological statements that set
inclusion and exclusion thresholds, where technical criteria served to reinforce moral and historical
assumptions about difference. For example:

       “Principal component analysis (PCA) was performed by EigenStrat software ensuring only
       European ancestry populations were included, and those with less than 80% European
       ancestry were excluded (n = 167)” (Jandu et al., 2023 p. 2)

Subpopulations such as Finnish or Ashkenazi Jewish were often singled out for their homogeneity
(Chu et al., 2023; Toivonen et al., 2024), reinforcing the importance of purity and coherence in
research methodology. This logic is common across genomic studies, where researchers justify

108 | P a g e
restricting analyses to a single ancestry group on the grounds that doing so reduces potential bias
caused by population stratification, even while acknowledging that the resulting findings may not
apply to populations of other ancestral groups. In each case, exclusion is framed as a technical
necessity to avoid bias.

European datasets, due to their sheer size and coverage, dominate reference panels and thereby
define the statistical centre of genetic similarity (Garwany et al., 2023; Julian et al., 2023; Verdier et
al., 2023) and so exclusion due to size was regularly observed. Van der Vossen et al. (2023) note
that participants “of Ghanaian origin were excluded due to the low number of second-generation
participants.” And certain groups, particularly Hispanic/Latino populations, were described as admixed
and consequently excluded for lacking statistical power (Meng et al., 2024).

The pursuit of purity in genomic research rests on a belief in bounded, coherent populations that can
serve as reference points against which others are measured. The technical language of bias
reduction and population stratification conceals a value system that equates homogeneity with validity
and mixture with instability.

In these settings most employ admixture language rhetorically to mark diversity, inclusion, or global
representation while the underlying methodologies seek to minimise its effects. Admixed individuals
are often filtered out, down-weighted, or assigned to the nearest homogeneous cluster, reflecting a
persistent methodological discomfort with hybridity. This asymmetry between the prevalence of
admixture as a conceptual sign of diversity and the paucity of methods designed to model it directly,
reveals how genomics continues to value purity and partition even as it celebrates mixture.

Diversity, however, is not rejected outright. It is embraced when it can be made orderly and
compatible with the classificatory logic of genomics; transformed from a challenge to an attribute that
can be managed, quantified, and contained. Within this framework, admixture is acceptable only
when measured proportionally, not when it exposes the continuum that disrupts categorical order.

The authority of statistical reasoning thus feeds directly into the dominance of whiteness and
Europeanness as the unmarked standards of truth in genomic research, the reference points against
which all other populations are rendered comparable, derivative, or diverse.




4.4 Whiteness and European Dominance


This section examines how whiteness and Europeanness are reproduced as overlapping categories
of legitimacy in genomic research. The first part considers how whiteness is constituted linguistically
and symbolically in genomic discourse. The second examines how European dominance in datasets

109 | P a g e
sustains this logic through methodological practice, shaping both the ethical framing and scientific
authority of diversity itself.




4.4.1   The Authority of Whiteness


The aura of scientific and ethical legitimacy attached to genomic methods, as though statistical power
could uncover the true nature of human difference, extends directly into how populations are
categorised and valued. Within this framework, the use of whiteness in genomic discourse moves
beyond representation into a hierarchical logic in which European identity becomes synonymous with
legitimacy, accuracy, and scientific neutrality.

While categories such as White British or White European appear to describe geographic or ancestry-
based populations, the repeated pairing of racial and geopolitical terms blurs those boundaries and
attaches whiteness to ideas of authentic or “true” European origin (Hanssen et al., 2023; Henry et al.,
2023; Jandu et al., 2023; Lagou et al., 2023; Malawsky et al., 2023; M. Peter et al., 2023). Across the
dataset, geopolitical pairing occurred far more often with White than with Black population labels.
White appeared with national or continental qualifiers ten times, for example White British, White
European, or White American (Aung et al., 2023; Fachrul et al., 2023; Jamrozy et al., 2023; Lagou et
al., 2023; Malawsky et al., 2023; M. Peter et al., 2023; Taylor et al., 2024), while Black was
geopolitically qualified only three times, as in Black British, Black African, or Black Caribbean
(Jamrozy et al., 2023; Sikkema et al., 2023; Taylor et al., 2024). Combinations such as White
European are commonplace, yet terms such as Black European or Asian European are entirely
absent.

The label White was frequently attached to groups defined as having European ancestry (Akbari et
al., 2023; Henry et al., 2023; Jandu et al., 2023; Kentistou et al., 2023; Malawsky et al., 2023; Rasooly
et al., 2023; Reay et al., 2024; Sikkema et al., 2023; Warrier et al., 2023). White British was used as a
specific regional identifier (Jamrozy et al., 2023), yet no examples of White African or White
Caribbean appeared. By contrast, Black British, British Pakistani, and Asian British were used more
often(Aung et al., 2023; Malawsky et al., 2023; M. Peter et al., 2023). This linguistic hierarchy
reinforces whiteness as presumed and therefore unmarked, while racialised identities must be
explicitly named and geographically qualified. Whiteness thus functions as the invisible standard of
comparison against which all other groups are measured (Reardon, 2017; Roberts, 2011).

these naming practices show how whiteness operates as a linguistic norm in genomic classification,
rendering European identity natural, neutral, and authoritative. This symbolic authority is not confined



110 | P a g e
to language alone but is reinforced through the material dominance of European datasets and
reference populations, which is examined in the following section.




4.4.2   European Dominance


European dominance in genomic data mirrors and extends this linguistic hierarchy. It is sustained not
only by historical asymmetries in data collection but by a methodological logic that treats European
ancestry as the scientific baseline. Across the reviewed literature, bias towards European datasets is
routinely framed as a technical condition, while simultaneously functioning as an ethical justification
for the very dominance it describes.

Jandu et al. (2023) make this logic explicit, stating that “studies should also increase recruitment from
genetic ancestry groups other than White Europeans.” The phrasing acknowledges imbalance while
positioning White European data as the default against which all other ancestries are defined. This
framing recurs in references to “European dataset bias” and calls for more “diverse” samples (Ip et
al., 2023; Jamrozy et al., 2023; West et al., 2023). Diversity is invoked without conceptual grounding,
reinforcing White European populations as the implicit standard from which difference is measured.

This dominance is frequently described as both a practical and moral dilemma. Several studies note
their reliance on European populations (Honigberg et al., 2023; Larsson et al., 2023; Qu et al., 2023;
Stefanucci et al., 2023). For example, Akbari et al. (2023) write:



        “Because participants in the INTERVAL cohort are overwhelmingly of European ancestry, we
        have not been able to study genetic variation extraneous to the European ancestry
        population.”(Akbari et al., 2023 p.10)



Such acknowledgements operate as ethical disclosures that paradoxically normalise exclusion,
positioning non-European representation as desirable yet ultimately supplementary.

In other cases, the methodological virtue of whiteness is made explicit. Henry et al., (2023) justify their
reliance on European-only data by stating that:



        “to obtain a more accurate estimate, we used the European ancestry meta-analysis summary
        statistics with pre-computed tagging files derived from 2,000 White British individuals.”



111 | P a g e
Here, European ancestry is equated with quality and control, and whiteness functions as a measure
of methodological purity. A similar logic appears when non-European participants are excluded on the
grounds that they introduce undesirable variability. As Krzak et al. (2023) state:

         “Patients of non-European ancestries were also excluded to reduce confounding,” (p. 20)

Reinforcing the assumption that analytical coherence depends on European homogeneity. Adlam et
al. (2023) likewise justify the absence of non-European data by citing socio-economic limitations in
recruitment, while concluding that greater ancestral diversity would enhance replication power.
Diversity is thus idealised as a theoretical good while operationalised as an analytical risk. Rouskas et
al. (2023) in their analysis of Greek genomic data, note that the GTEx (GTEx Consortium, 2020)
dataset is dominated by individuals of Central and Northern European descent, with only small
numbers of African-American and Asian-American donors . As discussed in sections 1.2 and 3.3.2,
this reflects a stratification within Europe itself, in which a narrower, Northern and US-centred form of
Europeanness functions as the normative reference, positioning other European populations as
peripheral within the category of whiteness.

Together, these rhetorical strategies construct what may be understood as a moral economy of bias.
Underrepresentation is acknowledged as an ethical concern, while homogeneity is framed as a
practical necessity. European datasets retain their status as both scientifically robust and ethically
defensible. Even where diversity is foregrounded (Costanzo et al., 2023; Duckett et al., 2023; T. Liu
et al., 2023). European dominance remains the reference point that validates progress, transforming
bias into evidence of moral awareness rather than a prompt for methodological reform.

DeGorter et al. (2023) exemplify this dynamic in their discussion of African genomic data, starting the
abstract to their paper with:

        “Mapping the functional human genome is often limited to European-descendent population
        samples. To aid in overcoming this limitation…” (p. 2)

The formulation positions Europeanness as the origin of completeness and non-Europeanness as
corrective. Ethical gestures towards inclusion thus remain dependent on the continued centring of
Europe as the point of comparison.

Through these examples, ethical discourse itself becomes a mechanism for reproducing hierarchy. To
acknowledge bias is to reaffirm European authority as the foundation from which correction must
begin. Calls for diversity emerge within, rather than against, the conceptual grammar of whiteness,
ensuring that European, and by extension White, data continue to function as the standard of
scientific truth.



112 | P a g e
4.4.3   Producing Non-Whiteness




This section examines how the concept of non-whiteness is positioned within genomic discourse. If
whiteness and Europeanness operate as unmarked standards of legitimacy, then non-whiteness
appears as their inverse: necessary for scientific completeness, yet always articulated in relation to a
dominant European frame. Inclusion is therefore framed simultaneously as an ethical and scientific
imperative, while remaining tethered to European comparison.

Across the dataset, this positioning is visible in the language used to mark difference. References to
non-European populations appear far more frequently than inverse formulations. The term non-
European occurs 24 times, compared with eight mentions of non-Finnish, five of non-White, and only
three of non-African. By contrast, terms such as non-Asian are entirely absent. This asymmetry
underscores how European datasets function as the normative centre of genomic comparison, while
other populations are named primarily in relation to that norm as categories of exception or diversity.
Whiteness and Europeanness are never marked as deviation, signalling their status as the
unexamined baseline.




Figure 25 explaining the importance of biological variation in skin. (Almet et al., 2023 p.1668).
Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.

113 | P a g e
Figure 25 illustrates how ancestry is naturalised as a biological axis of variation. Taken from a study
aiming to build a consensus Human Skin Cell Atlas (Almet et al., 2023), the figure situates ancestral
origin alongside spatial, temporal, gender, and wound-response factors as an equivalent biological
scale. The authors later argue that a comprehensive atlas requires data from genetically diverse
ancestries, explicitly naming “African, Asian, Hispanic, and Middle Eastern populations” as
underrepresented. While framed as a call for inclusion, this formulation positions these ancestries as
completeness-enhancing additions rather than as foundational to the definition of variation itself,
leaving the normative reference population implied.

A similar logic appears in analytic practice. Duckett et al. (2023), in their study of delayed growth and
puberty, report that excluding participants of “non-European ancestry” “did not change the overall
frequency of LoF MC3R carriers” and conclude that this “overrepresentation is not confounded by
ancestry differences.” (p. 1586). This conclusion is enabled by the overwhelming dominance of
European ancestry within the cohort, leaving insufficient variation to meaningfully test such effects.
Non-European ancestry is treated as a potential source of confounding to be checked and set aside,
while European ancestry remains the unmarked baseline against which stability is assessed.

Related patterns appear in large biobank analyses. Carss et al. (2025) frame diversity as a means of
enhanced characterisation, noting that “within- and cross-ancestry genomic associations” yield novel
insights, while Barclay et al., (2023) acknowledge more directly that UK Biobank participants are not
representative of the UK population Ip et al. (2023), examining lifestyle factors and colorectal cancer
risk, explicitly identify the underrepresentation of non-White participants in UK Biobank, naming “non-
white” as a category of absence. In doing so, whiteness is rendered visible as an ethnic category only
through its absence, reinforcing its implicit dominance even as generalisability is questioned.

The technical consequences of this asymmetry are made visible in Figure 26, taken from Sousa da
Mota et al. (2023):




114 | P a g e
Figure 26 The imputation error rate (NRD) for 42 ancient genomes. (Sousa da Mota et al. 2023
p.4).Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.




The figure depicts non-reference discordance (NRD), an imputation error rate measuring how often
non-reference genotypes are incorrectly predicted across 42 ancient genomes. Genomes of African
ancestry consistently show higher NRD values, reflecting lower imputation accuracy. The authors
explicitly attribute this pattern to bias in reference datasets, which are overwhelmingly European, and
caution that their population labels “Africa”, “Europe”, “Western”, and “Asia” are “vague” and “not fully
descriptive.” Even with this reflexivity, the visualisation positions non-African as a conceptual category
rather than a descriptive one, marking distance from a European reference point that functions as the
standard of accuracy. The figure thus demonstrates how reference bias is not only linguistic but
infrastructural, shaping error, reliability, and analytical confidence.

Together, these patterns construct non-whiteness as difference-as-deficiency. Populations described
as non-European or non-White are rendered ethically necessary yet analytically secondary, valued for
their contribution to diversity rather than for their capacity to redefine the centre of genomic
knowledge. Terms such as underrepresented and understudied frame absence as a moral problem,
while leaving intact the classificatory structure that produces it (DeGorter et al., 2023; Ismail et al.,
2023; McLaren et al., 2023). Efforts to increase diversity are frequently framed in terms of scale rather
than a redistribution of epistemic authority, equating representation with quantity while leaving
underlying categories unchanged (Jain et al., 2023; Kang et al., 2023).

In this way, non-whiteness is incorporated into genomic discourse as a supplement to an already
privileged dataset. Difference is acknowledged, measured, and managed, but rarely allowed to
unsettle the authority of European reference populations. The following section examines how these
linguistic and technical hierarchies are reinforced through the continued circulation of colonial
terminology, historical narratives, and classificatory practices.




4.4.4   Colonialism, Slavery, and Hierarchy



The authority of genomic classification does not only reproduce contemporary hierarchies but also
carries the linguistic and conceptual residues of colonial thought. Terms and categories that once
sustained racial science continue to appear, sometimes uncritically, in the vocabulary of modern
genomics. This section considers how the persistence of this language sustains the conceptual link



115 | P a g e
between racial difference and scientific legitimacy, binding present-day research to older imperial
grammars of knowledge and value.

Several papers use the term Caucasian (Almet et al., 2023; Duckett et al., 2023; van der Vossen et
al., 2023; Yu et al., 2023). Examples are given in text and graphic form as shown by Almet et al.,
(2023) in Figure 27. John et al. (2023), a cancer genomics study investigating spatial heterogeneity in
multiple using multi-region and single-cell sequencing, describes its patient group as “31 Caucasians,”
without clarifying whether this refers to race, ethnicity, or ancestry. Yu et al. (2023) use the term
interchangeably with European, reinforcing a racialised equivalence.




Figure 27. Caucasian as an ancestral origin. (Almet et al., 2023 p.1670). Available under CC BY
4.0 licence.

Caucasian has long been associated with pale skin and European ancestry, particularly in U.S.
contexts where it remains a racial descriptor synonymous with White (Comstock et al., 2023; Morning,
2011; Shamambo & Henry, 2022). Although the term’s nineteenth-century origins in racial
anthropology are well established (see chapter 2), it continues to appear in genomic studies as a
synonym for European ancestry. In this usage, the scientific neutrality of ancestry disguises its
colonial inheritance. To describe participants as Caucasian is to invoke a category invented to
hierarchise human variation while claiming the authority of objective measurement. The term
translates an ideology of race into the language of genetics, allowing a colonial vocabulary of
difference to persist under the guise of biological description.

Although van der Vossen et al.(2023) refer to Turkish participants as having Mediterranean, Middle
Eastern, and Caucasian ancestry, it remains unclear whether Caucasian is used here in the narrow
geographic sense of ancestry linked to the Caucasus region, or as a broader synonym for European-
adjacent or West Eurasian populations. In contemporary genomics, the latter usage is far more

116 | P a g e
common, despite its recognised imprecision (John et al., 2023; Morning, 2011; Yudell et al., 2016).
This ambiguity itself is instructive: it illustrates how a term with explicit origins in racial hierarchy
continues to circulate in scientific discourse under the ostensibly neutral language of ancestry, without
methodological clarification.

Although these examples represent a minority, the uses of Caucasian in John et al. (2023) and Yu et
al. (2023) also demonstrate the persistence of imprecise and racialised terminology that equates
whiteness with European biological coherence ((Popejoy, 2021); as discussed in sections 1.3.1., 2.2.2
and 2.4).

The use of Oriental, like Caucasian, is an explicitly colonial term long rejected as inaccurate and racist
(Yu Hsi Lee, 2016). Colonial language also appears explicitly in (Jamrozy et al., 2023), whose study
on group B streptococcus in pregnant women categorised participants as “Mixed,” “Oriental,” and
“Other”:

        “The remaining women (n = 23, 4%) represented minority groups composed of Mixed,
        Oriental, and other ethnicities, which were grouped here as Other.”

        (Jamrozy et al., 2023, p. 6).

Its appearance, even inadvertently, sustains a classificatory logic in which whiteness is normative and
superior, while identities outside of that classification are residual, peripheral, and indistinct.

Van der Vossen et al. (2023) further reveal how the legacies of slavery and colonialism remain
unevenly invoked. Their study compares ethnic origin groups to explore environmental and
acculturative effects on the gut microbiome, yet slavery is mentioned only in relation to the
Surinamese population:

        “African Surinamese (‘Creoles’ descending from former slaves, mixed Western African and
        Dutch ancestries), South Asian Surinamese (‘Hindustanis’ descending from workers arriving to
        Suriname post-slavery, South-Asian/Indian ancestry).”

        (van der Vossen et al., 2023, p. 3).

By contrast, Moroccan and Turkish participants are described solely through migration and
acculturation.

In Figure 28 visual language mirrors this logic. European, Turkish, and Moroccan identities are
represented as nationally coherent and culturally fluid, while Surinamese identity is divided into
separate ancestral components, African and South Asian. Only some populations are granted
neutrality under the national flag; others become legible only through explicit lineage. The use of flags
collapses nationality, ethnicity, and ancestry into a single national symbolism. In doing so, the image
117 | P a g e
reproduces a logic of classification that relies on social census categories rather than genetic
measures, embedding the social construct of race within genomic representation.




Figure 28. How microbiome shifts correspond with the westernisation of diet and urban
environment. From van der Vossen et al. (2023) p. 2. Available under CC BY 4.0 licence.

This selective historicisation reproduces a racialised asymmetry: some groups are anchored to
colonial histories of enslavement and subjugation, while others are framed as culturally mobile but
historically neutral. Such uneven contextualisation exposes how ethnicity functions as a flexible
category absorbing racialised meanings where colonial legacies are invoked, yet remaining
depoliticised elsewhere.

This asymmetry extends beyond visual rhetoric. In Qu et al. (2023), in the same sentence, they say
European ancestry, while other groups are presented simply “African Americans”, “Hispanics”, and
“Latinos”. In fact, the word ancestry is only used alongside the European group throughout. This
distinction delegitimises some groups by denying them the epistemic status of having an “ancestry,”
while European identity retains scientific depth and continuity. Broader ethnic or racial labels such as
Hispanic, Latin American, or Asian are used interchangeably, flattening their complex geopolitical and
cultural distinctions.


118 | P a g e
The language and visual rhetoric of genomics continue to reproduce colonial hierarchies under the
guise of scientific description. Terms such as Caucasian and Oriental, selective invocations of
slavery, and the conflation of nationality with ancestry sustain a classificatory order in which
whiteness remains the unspoken reference point of biological coherence. The authority of genomic
representation thus rests on boundaries drawn long before the advent of molecular genetics,
translating historical categories of race and empire into contemporary scientific frameworks.

These continuities do not end with terminology. They extend into how diversity, inclusion, and
representation are conceptualised across genomic discourse. The following section examines how
the same colonial logics are rearticulated through the moral language of equity and global
participation, showing how the pursuit of diversity itself often remains problematic.

taken together, these analyses point towards the ethical frameworks through which genomic practices
are governed, justified, and made accountable. Ethical oversight in genomics aims to promote
fairness, equity, and respect for participants, yet the ways in which ethics is defined and enacted often
mirror the same hierarchies that organise scientific reasoning itself. Examining how ethics is
articulated in recent Sanger-affiliated publications reveals how the moral language of inclusion and
diversity becomes institutionalised through procedural norms, while deeper questions of justice,
representation, and historical responsibility remain largely unexamined.




4.5 Ethical Frameworks in Practice


The preceding analysis has shown how colonial hierarchies and classificatory logics persist within
genomic science, shaping both language and methodological authority. This section turns to the
ethical frameworks through which these practices are governed and legitimised. Across the Sanger-
affiliated publications reviewed, ethics is addressed overwhelmingly through procedural mechanisms,
most informed consent, institutional approval, and compliance with established guidelines. While
these frameworks aim to ensure accountability and protect participants, they rarely engage with the
ethical implications of population categorisation, differential representation, or historical inequities. As
a result, the moral language of equity and inclusion is institutionalised mainly through compliance and
documentation, while broader questions of justice, power, and responsibility remain outside the
ethical frame.

In practice, ethics statements focused almost exclusively on informed consent, data protection, and
institutional approval, typically following journal-mandated formats such as the International
Committee of Medical Journal Editors Recommendations (ICMJE, 2025). Ethical compliance was
treated as a matter of procedural sufficiency, with little attention to how population categories are

119 | P a g e
constructed or how exclusion and underrepresentation might constitute ethical issues in their own
right.




4.5.1      Beyond Procedure: The Missing Ethics of Representation



There were limited papers to explore that engaged with ethics as a broader practice of equity and
responsibility, as described in 3.5.1 these papers were not chosen by the staff themselves and so
may not represent a generalised approach. Nonetheless, the campus library aims to capture the
entire publication output across the Institute. Molina-Aguilar and Robles-Espinoza (2023) call for
increased representation of non-European populations in cancer research, while Sharaf et al. (2023)
highlight how linguistic and infrastructural barriers constrain participation in African biodiversity
genomics. Işık et al. 2023 and Samuel, (2023) explore environmental and educational ethics, arguing
for frameworks that integrate sustainability and social responsibility into research design. Others, such
as Bernier et al. (2023) and Welter et al. (2023), focus on data stewardship and FAIR principles,
demonstrating how accountability is framed through technical reproducibility rather than distributive
justice.

Efforts to promote equity within institutions also featured in papers by Bradke et al. 2023 and
Williams et al. (2023), who describe initiatives to build inclusive research cultures and training
programs. At Sanger, postdoctoral fellowships that address the underrepresentation of Black heritage
researchers and the Behavioural Competency Framework illustrate how organisational policies aim to
formalise inclusion. However, these measures remain unevenly implemented and often symbolic.

Engagement with participants and communities, a key theme in ethical practice, also reflects this
asymmetry. Several studies describe meaningful attempts to address sensitive issues through
collaboration (Malawsky et al., 2023; Senkin et al., 2023), while others cite the lack of public or patient
involvement as a limitation (Jindal et al., 2023).

The conflation of race, ethnicity, and ancestry, the dominance of European data, and the legacies of
colonial extraction remain largely outside formal ethical scrutiny, even as they continue to shape the
moral landscape of genomics.

4.5.2      Final Thoughts



Across this analysis, the review of Sanger-affiliated publications has shown how genomic science
continues to grapple with the entanglement of technical practice, historical legacy, and ethical
responsibility. Focusing on Sanger as a place-based site makes it possible to trace how institutional
120 | P a g e
location, people, and published literature intersect, but the patterns identified here are not unique to
Sanger. Rather, they reflect wider dynamics in genomics that were introduced in the literature review,
including the conflation of race, ethnicity, and ancestry, the dominance of European reference
populations, and the persistence of classificatory hierarchies rooted in colonial histories. The pursuit
of objectivity in genomics, therefore, remains inseparable from the social and political contexts in
which knowledge is produced.

Across the publications reviewed, ethical reflection is largely procedural, centred on consent,
governance, and regulatory compliance, while deeper questions of justice, representation, and
historical responsibility are rarely addressed. Although the moral vocabulary of equity and inclusion
has become embedded within genomic research, it is often enacted symbolically rather than
transformatively, reproducing existing hierarchies of legitimacy rather than unsettling them. Taken
together, these findings suggest that contemporary genomics has expanded its ethical language
without fundamentally reconfiguring the structures through which authority, normality, and difference
are defined.

The next chapter follows these patterns of discourse into lived experience. By turning to interviews
with scientists at Sanger, the analysis examines how institutional narratives are interpreted,
negotiated, and sometimes resisted in practice. This shift from published texts to personal accounts
makes visible the human dimension of genomics, showing how ideas of fairness, responsibility, and
diversity are encountered as everyday challenges within scientific work, rather than solely as abstract
ethical commitments.




121 | P a g e
5 Power and the Making of Populations: Scientific Practice and
    Meaning at the Sanger Institute


5.1 Introduction


In this chapter, I examine how power operates within the scientific, institutional and interpersonal life
of Sanger. Drawing on ten months of ethnographic fieldwork that combined in-depth interviews with
scientists (conducted both in person and over Zoom see figure 30 The digital interview), digital and in-
person participant observation, attendance at seminars and meetings and sustained immersion on the
campus (figure 29 auditorium entrance), I explore how authority, hierarchy and institutional identity
shape the ways in which human populations are conceptualised, categorised and managed as data.




Figure 29. Entrance to the Campus Auditorium.
The main venue for Institute away days, town halls and international scientific meetings, illustrating
one of the physical sites of institutional life. Photograph taken by the author, 25 Oct 2023.




122 | P a g e
Figure 30. Digital interview screenshot. A Zoom recording interface, illustrating the use of online
interviews as part of the digital ethnographic dataset. Captured by the author, 3 November 2023.

Power at Sanger is both structural and relational: it is held, felt, exercised, and at times resisted.
Scientists describe moments of both control and constraint, where influence over research direction,
collaboration, and communication is distributed unevenly across positions, disciplines, and global
contexts.

Rather than treating power as a fixed possession, this analysis approaches it as something enacted
through scientific practice. This analysis builds on the understanding of power developed in Chapter
2, where classification, knowledge production, and institutional authority were shown to operate as
mutually constitutive rather than separate domains.

Power appears in the ability to define problems and decide which questions matter; in the authority to
name, group, and communicate difference; and in the institutional privilege that grants access to data,
funding, and legitimacy. At Sanger, positionality, whether as a senior leader, early-career researcher,
or global collaborator, determines not only who can act but what can be known and said.

The following three themes trace how this dynamic unfolds through different aspects of genomic
science.

       ●    Power in Communication examines how authority, hierarchy, and scientific language
            sustain the persistence of racialised ideas, even when scientists believe they have moved
            beyond them.

123 | P a g e
       ●    Power in Methods explores, methodological choices methodological choices and
            research aims becomes a moral and political process that shapes equity in genomic
            outcomes.

       ●    Power Beyond Data (addressed in chapter 6) considers how inclusion, diversity, and
            institutional responsibility are defined and enacted within Sanger’s ecosystem, revealing
            how global influence intersects with local culture.

Across these themes, power is not simply a matter of domination or control but of production: it
determines which truths are made credible, which people and populations are rendered visible, and
how science itself becomes a site where inequality is both recognised and remade.




5.2 Power in Communication: Silence, Avoidance, and the Language of Racial Ideas

This section examines communication as a site of power in genomic science, showing how race
remains a contested and often uncomfortable presence in everyday scientific practice. Among
scientists at Sanger, these tensions were navigated not only through what was said, but through
avoidance, substitution, and silence. Ethnicity frequently functioned as a more acceptable alternative
to race, yet remained an imprecise proxy for genetic ancestry, social identity, or environmental
exposure. Attention to these communicative practices reveals how institutional and disciplinary norms
shape what can be articulated explicitly and what must remain implicit.

Reflecting on the range of labels that emerged during interviews and observational work (Table 4), it
was striking how rarely such terms were clarified or questioned, despite their acknowledged
ambiguity. Labels associated with race and ethnicity were more readily recognised as problematic,
while geographically framed descriptors of ancestry were often treated as self-evident. As shown in
the preceding discourse analysis, shifts in terminology can confer legitimacy on categories that might
otherwise appear contentious. Setting these labels alongside the dimensions they were used to
describe makes visible the overlaps and tensions between race, ethnicity, geography, and genetic
ancestry.




124 | P a g e
Table 4. Labels used by participants to describe population differences across domains

 Dimension            Example of labels used
 Geo-political         African, African American, Asian, Brazilian, Finnish, Lower Middle-
                      income Country/Global North/Global South, Bangladesh, Indian.
                      Iranian, Polish, Western, Japanese
 Race                 African American, Asian, White British/Irish,
 Ethnicity            African American, Asian, Black ancestry, Caucasian, Caribbean.
                      Jewish, Japanese
 Genetic similarity    African/Africa Ancestry, European/ European Ancestry, Japanese
 Health               Underrepresented, Understudied, HDSS,
 demographic
 Genetic Ancestry     Jewish Ancestry, South Asian Ancestry, White European Ancestry


Across interviews, race and ethnicity were frequently used interchangeably and regarded as sensitive
or risky, something to be carefully managed or avoided. Although race was widely acknowledged as a
social construct, it remained present through categories such as ancestry, ethnicity, and location.
Scientists expressed awareness of the historical and ethical concerns surrounding race yet often
framed genetic diversity in ways that sidestepped direct engagement. This avoidance was reinforced
by disciplinary conventions, institutional pressures, and concerns about public interpretation. Race
was thus simultaneously disavowed and reproduced.

Mapping labels and dimensions in this way provides a foundation for the analysis that follows. Three
subthemes are examined in turn: first, how race and ethnicity are ejected or avoided in everyday
interaction; second, how certain terms come to be treated as accurate or acceptable; and finally, how
racial ideas persist despite sustained efforts to distance scientific practice from them. Together, these
subthemes show how race cannot be fully excluded from genomic communication, even when its
explicit invocation is constrained.




5.2.1   Avoidance and Rejection



The first of these themes revealed how race and ethnicity were often understood less as dimensions
of human variation and more as terms that evoke discomfort and unintended associations.


125 | P a g e
Reluctance to engage with race was often expressed through concern about how others might
respond, rather than through an outright denial of its relevance.

Harmful ideas

Even mentioning race was felt to risk legitimising divisive or harmful ideas, reinforcing a tendency to
keep such discussions at a distance. As this participant pondered:

       “Genomics research in that it's extremely powerful, it's liberating, it's empowering, but with it
       comes responsibility and understanding of what the repercussions and consequences could
       be. One of those was how do we report variations and heterogeneity, between individuals
       collectively, and not sort of somehow add fuel to the concept of, you know, racial entities ”

       Group leader, Developmental Biology.

This question captured a central dilemma: the need to describe biological variation and heterogeneity
without invoking the problematic language of race. The description of genomics as “powerful”,
“liberating”, and “empowering” is striking. These terms frame scientific participation as a source of
authority and freedom, positioning science as a space that confers legitimacy in contrast to a less
controlled non-scientific world. The satisfaction expressed here signals an awareness of power, even
as responsibility is emphasised as its ethical counterweight. This concern echoed examples
discussed in 4.3.1, where ethnicity data collection was rejected outright in certain national contexts.
Here, the entanglement extends further: even apparently neutral technical terms were treated as
capable of carrying racial meaning. In this sense, the language of biological difference is never
entirely free from the possibility of being read through race, no matter how carefully it is framed.

       “You know, ….in the past. science is used to solve.. like create differences between people
       that are actually meaningless. You know, in my opinion, race is one of them. Science is [was]
       used to kind of like say, some people are superior, inferior”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

This participant often referred back to historical understandings of race that accompanied their
anxiety. This anxiety also surfaced in public engagements with the history of scientific racism beyond
the research setting (Figure 31). Another scientist also drew on historical frames in their thinking and
judgment, as this participant notes:

       “I mean historically.. very sinister kind of historically. They're very sinister kind of
       categorisations of race, right? Like, I mean, Nazis, and But there what does it? What does it
       mean when someone says that nowadays? I think they are basically referring to skin colour,
       aren't they? That's so coarse and doesn't really mean very much.”

126 | P a g e
        Group Leader, Discovery Science.




Figure 31. Newspaper cutting displayed at the Black Cultural Archives as part of the exhibition
Challenging the Narrative: Black Resistance to Scientific Racism (Appendix C). The cutting
critiques the historical and ongoing harms of scientific approaches to race and the revival of racialised
scientific studies. Photograph taken by the author, 25 January 2024.Permission given by Black
Cultural Archives.

This discomfort often shifted onto ethnicity, particularly when ethnicity appeared as a proxy substitute
for race. For some, ethnicity was experienced as imprecise and vaguely opportunistic:




127 | P a g e
       “[Ethnicity]—that’s a hard one, because it’s a term that I think has sort of become trendy
       nowadays. But also, I don’t really know what it means. It’s because of the bad association with
       race, I think—that’s another one that’s come up that people are using.”

       Group Leader, Discovery Science.

Yet other participants defended ethnicity as biologically meaningful:

       ” Personally, I think there's it's very important to represent different ethnicities in your data sets
       because biology differs a lot. You know, between different ethnicities”

       Postdoctoral Researcher, Discovery Science.

And in some cases, the preference for ethnicity was explicitly enforced through hierarchy, with race
removed from documents through senior authority:

       “I was explicitly discouraged to use the word race. And then my supervisor, basically changed
       everything to ethnicity. And to me, as a non-English speaker, I honestly don't quite understand
       the implication of these race word, but I'm just instructed not to use it”

       Senior scientist, Bioinformatics.

Even where the historical and political contours of the terminology were not fully understood, senior
positions could exercise power to remove race from texts and replace it with a supposedly safer
alternative. What emerged across accounts was a recurring tension between what was meaningful
and what was permissible. Scientists were not only debating terminology; they were negotiating when
and how race or ethnicity could be “welcome” within scientific reasoning, especially in relation to
evidence, justification, and harm. As one Group Leader in Developmental Biology reflected:

       “Is ethnicity needed? How does it interact with other social determinants of health? Is it always
       a surrogate for SES? What is the evidence that this analysis is meaningful—is there any
       evidence? And what’s the evidence that this has actually contributed more harm, directly or
       indirectly, to a particular ethnic group? ’Cause I think that is really needed then that would give
       you more impetus and persuasive evidence of whether this should continue or whether we’re
       just wasting our time.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

Their uncertainty about meaning also extended to questioning practical value, asking:

       “Where has this really been valuable in highlighting something that has made a big difference
       to that ethnic group?”


128 | P a g e
          Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

While race was met with immediate resistance by all, ethnicity remained an uneasy but more
acceptable alternative for some. This created a paradox: race was too politically and socially charged
to engage with directly, yet ethnicity, vague for some and meaningful for others, also proved
inadequate. Together, these ambivalences contributed to avoiding race altogether, while allowing
ethnicity to surface only under certain conditions, especially when it could be framed as scientifically
neutral or methodologically necessary. This unease translated into everyday practices of avoidance,
where discussions of race were redirected, softened, or silenced within the institutional life of the lab.

Race and Gender

In discussions around race and ethnicity, comparisons with gender and sex frequently emerged when
participants were asked why race was seen as problematic. These comparisons often highlighted
perceived similarities such as discrimination, microaggressions, and the historical
underrepresentation of women in research, but they also revealed how gender provided a more
comfortable framework for acknowledging inequality. In doing so, participants could recognise
exclusion while avoiding the deeper discomfort associated with race, and they sometimes reinforced
the idea that discomfort around race and diversity primarily affects racialised and minoritised groups,
rather than implicating institutional structure.

          “let me give you an example and this is what switched it on in my brain like basically if we stop
          tracking say male/female gender pay gap how many people are employed are Male or
          Female, because we just say sex doesn't matter everybody is an individual we are just hiring
          whatever, paying whatever and everyone is the same then you are losing any ability to track
          any inequalities that are attached to those labels that’s the argument to this coarse grain
          analysis.”

          Group Leader, Discovery Science.

This participant had described ethnicity as a “crude” measure, yet when challenged on the
implications of discarding categories entirely, she turned to gender as an analogy. The example
centred on the ability to track inequality, rather than on any inherent scientific value in the category
itself.

          “ Sometimes, you know, you hear something, but because it's so alien to your own experience,
          you cannot even begin to understand it, let alone process it in any way. So, you need to
          almost emotionally advance to be able to recognise it and I think that's even the same for kind
          of like gender issues.”

          Group Leader, Discovery Science.
129 | P a g e
       “Yeah, gender issues, you need to be more emotionally advanced to kind of understand
       gender issues otherwise, you could be, you know, right smack in the middle of a gender
       inequity issue and it will just pass you by.”

       Group Leader, Discovery Science.

The frequency with which sex and gender were invoked suggests that race and ethnicity were often
understood through comparison to gender, both framed as sites of inequity and underrepresentation.
Yet these were the only parallels participants drew; other axes of inequality linked to genetics and
health, such as disability, were notably absent. The prevalence of gender discourse, especially
compared to the brevity with which race was often curtailed, indicates how race remained a more
complex and uncomfortable topic to dwell on.

       “It’s not just around ancestry, you know, we talk about—yanno—historically… Actually, I think
       we used to talk about gender quite a lot, I think, actually, just because scientists don’t like
       using the word sex. Okay? But yeah, yeah, there’s a—there’s a biological sex, so there’s a
       genetic sex, and that’s male or female. And I guess other situations—XY, and what have
       you—but you can be… you could be X… your sex can be XX female, and your gender can be
       male. I mean, that’s entirely fine, just as it’s entirely fine to be ancestrally, erm, you know, to
       identify as… your ancestry genome might be one thing, and then actually, kind of culturally,
       your—this—your ethnicity could be different to your ancestry, is what I’m saying.”

       Group Leader, Discovery Science.

Here again, gender and sex are used as a model to explain ethnicity and ancestry, while race remains
absent from the discussion. The reflection that scientists prefer gender because they “don’t like using
the word sex” echoes earlier tendencies to favour ethnicity over race. In both cases, the more
acceptable term is used to smooth over conceptual and political discomfort, producing a slippage
between accuracy and acceptability.

       “the same with sex and gender ..so basically, now in the publications you shouldn't use
       gender, you should use sex. Because obviously, the implication of different genders right? And
       the sex assigned at birth. So yeah I think here, similar , I'm trying to understand them…the
       implications”

       Postdoctoral Researcher, Discovery Science.

The discomfort was tangible. It surfaced in hesitations, self-corrections, and visible searching for the
‘right’ word, producing moments of insecurity and trepidation around how to speak at all. This unease
extended beyond interviews: during fieldwork, emails relating to this research were repeatedly flagged


130 | P a g e
or blocked because of the use of the word ‘race’, underscoring how the term itself had become
institutionally prohibited.

Power and Prohibition

The widely accepted position that race has no place in genetics, as observed in the broader literature,
was also reflected in participants’ responses. One noticeable way of avoiding engagement with race
was through its prohibition or the perception that it should not be discussed. As a result, key issues
such as racism and the role of race in systemic inequality remained largely unexamined.

        ”I try to avoid using the word race…full. Stop ”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science

I asked why and then after a long pause they remarked:

        “I find it’s a word well which is quite evocative and has a kind of erm it's. It's a word which is
        difficult to define. And I think many people use it [in] different ways….there’s a way in which I
        might want to use the word race that’s not the same as the way somebody who might be
        listening to what I'm saying wants to use race. So I try to stick to ethnicity and ancestry”

        Programme Lead, Discovery science.

As in earlier examples, the authority and institutional power attached to role appeared to shape how
discussions of race could be directed or contained. In a previous case, a younger scientist’s work had
been rewritten by a more senior colleague to remove references to race altogether. Here too, seniority
enables control over what becomes sayable.

        “I feel as scientists we are almost adding to the idea—the notion that there is some biological
        basis to these ethnicities.”

         Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

Despite questioning meaning, this participant acknowledged their participation in that very process of
meaning-making: the biological framing of ethnicities. The statement was delivered with frustration, as
something they felt they should not be contributing to, yet recognised as a recurring outcome of
scientific practice.

Some participants also dismissed the categories as obviously flawed, but that dismissal could itself
function as a way of narrowing discussion:

        “It's just, I think that what they are trying to capture, you know, those ethnicity, or whatever
        boxes blah blah blah, you know, is that they're actually not trying to capture your ethnicity at

131 | P a g e
       all. But it’s with socio-economic kind categorisation, which is a completely different thing. And
       then that gets back to like income, or your parents' income, or your… and then they don't want
       to ask that, you know.”

       Group Leader, Discovery Science.

Like this group leader’s “blah blah blah”, this next scientist’s reflections exemplify a particular
distancing that carried a mix of amusement and dismissal, mocking the notion of categories as if their
redundancy were self-evident. As they spoke, there was a physicality to this rejection: gestures that
seemed to invite complicity. The familiarity of this exchange may also have been shaped by
symmetries in positionality and having encountered one another in institutional spaces concerned with
race and equity. These shared social locations seemed to blur the line between collegial rapport and
epistemic alignment, creating a sense of collusion that both connected and unsettled.

       “Even when you are trying to see the analysis by categories if you plot differences between
       the ethnicities it gives more credence to the idea that there are things that determine or form
       the basis of racial groupings. When you look at it the initial groupings have nothing to do with
       that they just had some daft measurements of pretty superficial anthropomorphic type
       measurements.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

By mocking the historic “daft” measurements used to justify race, the speaker positions themselves as
intellectually above such practices, yet the derision also enables a form of detachment that absolves
them from deeper engagement with how contemporary forms of classification continue to operate.
The laughter and dismissive gestures neutralising the political charge of the topic, transforming
critique into performance. Such distancing is emblematic of what I repeatedly observed: avoidance
that went beyond discomfort, a deliberate narrowing of discussion that restricted its depth and scope.

The avoidance of racial discourse limited the possibility of sustained engagement, diverting
conversation toward broader institutional or logistical concerns such as recruitment, participation, or
global economic disparities. Yet these issues are inseparable from the racialised assumptions
underpinning ideas about data quality and representativeness. As discussed in Section 2.4, such
reluctance to engage aligns with wider critiques of race avoidance in scientific discourse (Fullwiley,
2007; Ossorio & Duster, 2005) and reveals how the presumed neutrality of genetic research can
obscure the structural inequalities that sustain its infrastructures (Roberts, 2011). In this context, ideas
of “accuracy” and “acceptability” emerge as modes through which categorisation can be discussed
without discomfort, framed as technical challenges rather than ethical or political questions. What is
produced through this discourse is not merely methodological caution, but a form of epistemic evasion


132 | P a g e
that sustains the illusion of scientific objectivity while leaving the social and historical dimensions of
classification unexamined.




5.2.2   Accuracy and Acceptance


If race and ethnicity are frequently rejected or avoided, the question that follows is what kinds of
categories are instead treated as accurate, acceptable, and legitimate within genomic practice. This
section examines how terms such as ancestry come to be privileged as scientifically neutral, and how
claims to accuracy function as a way of resolving ethical discomfort without abandoning classification
altogether.

Ancestry as accuracy

Scientists consistently expressed a preference for terms such as ancestry, framing them as more
accurate and scientifically appropriate than race. These terms were described as objective and
semiotically uncomplicated, even though the methods used to produce them, particularly clustering
techniques, rely on subjective and contextual decisions about how difference is constructed.
Geopolitical labels were routinely assigned to genetic clusters, reinforcing the impression that such
groupings possess a real and stable biological existence, despite concurrent acknowledgements of
their constructed nature.

        “So because so I think my understanding for most of these is very pra..practical. It's it's about
        composition of genome differences or the ability to compare but but usually as a very genomic
        sense, and I think a lot of that is is, is detached from social constructs around race or ethnicity.
        It's just, you know, the basis you have in your genome.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

Here, group construction is framed as a practical necessity, grounded in comparison rather than
social meaning. Race and ethnicity are positioned as external to the data itself, while genomic
difference is treated as something that simply exists and can be measured.

        “I don't think we would use race in general. I don't think it's a very accurate formulation. I would
        imagine we would mainly refer to genetic ancestry and the reason for that is that people can
        have different perception of who they identify themselves with in terms of continents,
        nationalities and so on but that isn't as important from a drug discovery perspective as what
        your genetic background is.”

        Senior Scientist, Translational Informatics.

133 | P a g e
Race is rejected here not because it is ethically troubling, but because it is framed as scientifically
imprecise. Identification is characterised as a perception, while ancestry is positioned as a more
reliable inference. This distinction allows ancestry to appear neutral, even though it too is produced
through interpretation and modelling.

The authority to label

Distancing of who can be classified who does the classification becomes more explicit as this scientist
shifts to addressing a hypothetical “you”:

       “So we can infer your genetic ancestry based on the DNA. We can plot it, we can give you
       some kind of coordinates artificially inferred, and we can provide you with a label based on
       seeing where your point is mapping on that plot, with what other populations it’s mapping with,
       right? And it is tricky because also you get a lot of admixture so it's absolutely smearing and
