       blurring in all sorts of directions. So you’re somewhere on the spectrum of that, but that is
       important information, so we would be using genetic ancestry predominantly, and that then
       has a label associated with some kind of population or a larger group.”

       Senior Scientist, Translational Informatics.

The use of “you” raises questions about who is being positioned as the object of classification. While
admixture is acknowledged as complicating inference, the method remains privileged. The data must
fit the analytic framework, and the assignment of a label is treated as both necessary and improving
accuracy relative to race.

       “And so when I think about genetic ancestry, it's about where your kind of genome sits in that
       three dimensional space. That's a very different thing from ethnicity.”

       Group Leader, Discovery Science.

Genetic ancestry is presented as multidimensional and sophisticated, contrasted with ethnicity as
comparatively flat. Notably, this distinction rests on belief rather than on how ancestry is actually
constructed. Although ethnicity is rejected as imprecise, it remains conceptually tethered to ancestry
as a way of explaining difference.

Scepticism towards self-reported ethnicity further reinforced this hierarchy. Self-identification was
frequently portrayed as unreliable, particularly when set against genetically inferred classifications.

       “People may raise concerns when you cluster people like based on their like genotypes…and
       it's not actually reflecting. Well, the self-reported ethnicity is often very inaccurate in terms of
       like how their genetics actually speaks…but for scientific research, of course you don't…. it's


134 | P a g e
       not possible to rely on self-reported ethnicity because that would cause a lot of trouble in the
       analysis.”

       Senior scientist, Bioinformatics.

Here, the scientist positions themselves apart from the public, acknowledging that self-identification
matters socially while insisting it cannot be trusted analytically. Ethnicity is framed as meaningful only
insofar as it can be corrected by genetic data.

Others expressed discomfort with the assumption that ethnic groupings have a biological basis at all:

       “It’s this idea that there is some sort of biological basis by these groupings and they want to
       find out. When I feel that it is probably slightly flawed and there are other ways of looking at
       the data.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

The interviewee’s laughter underscored the perceived absurdity of searching for biological meaning in
ethnic categories, even as such categories continued to structure analysis.

       “I just I just assume that you know, there’s so only much you can trust on self-reported
       ethnicity when you don’t have the genotype data. So the genotype is real with that you can
       infer this ethnicity. But of course I don’t have enough expertise to comment on this.”

       Postdoctoral Scientist, Discovery Science.

Among less senior researchers, uncertainty and deference were more explicit. Genotype was
described as “real”, while ethnicity became something inferred, revised, or corrected. Although
ethnicity was framed as unreliable, it was not discarded entirely. Its value shifted depending on how
and by whom it was produced.

Across seniority levels, hesitancy and expressions of limited expertise revealed a shared uncertainty
about how best to classify human variation. Yet this uncertainty did not disrupt the underlying
assumption that genetic analysis could reveal a more accurate version of identity than self-report.
Ethnicity was treated as having an objective existence that could be accessed through genetic data,
even as participants acknowledged its instability.

Although ethnicity was widely viewed as more acceptable than race, it remained an imprecise and
contested category, particularly when self-reported. The recurring appeal to genetic ancestry reflects
a persistent faith in the objectivity of genomic methods, despite the socially constructed nature of the
populations they produce. Avoiding race, then, does not eliminate its influence. Instead, it is



135 | P a g e
reconfigured through the language of accuracy, inference, and methodological necessity, a
transformation that becomes more explicit in the following section.




5.2.3   Inescapability


As shown in sections 5.2.1 and 5.2.2, scientists at Sanger actively rejected race as an analytic
category, favouring ancestry, ethnicity, or geography as more acceptable alternatives. However, here
I show how these interviews also demonstrate that such substitutions do not remove racial logic from
genomic practice. Instead, race reappears through the dominance of European reference categories,
the moral pressure of classificatory “boxes”, and the embodied language scientists use to situate
themselves within genetic space. In this sense, race is not eliminated but reconfigured.

European ancestry as norm

A central site of this persistence was the dominance of data labelled European ancestry. Interviewees
described this imbalance not simply as a historical legacy, but as actively shaping discovery and
translation. As one participant observed:

        “The vast majority of the data that exists is from predominantly white European populations.
        And that really skews the discovery, drug discovery.”

        Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

Another acknowledged incremental change while emphasising continued concentration:

        “The ancestral diversity of our genome association is improving, but still very, very heavily
        focused on European ancestries.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

In these accounts, European ancestry functioned as both a methodological reference category and a
social descriptor. It was frequently used interchangeably with white, while populations outside Europe
were more often discussed through broader ethnic terms:

        “If an atlas is 70% white or 70% European, what about the rest of the ethnicities?”

        Postdoctoral Researcher, Discovery Science.

This slippage shows how scientific terminology remains entangled with everyday racial language,
even when race is explicitly rejected as an analytic category.


136 | P a g e
Being put in boxes

These tensions were experienced personally. Scientists described frustration with limited or imprecise
options for self-identification:

        “It doesn’t kind of describe it… my dad is [from two countries], my mum is [another country]…
        ancestors were from the British Isles.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

        and

        “The only thing I can choose from those forms is like a white background.”

        Postdoctoral Researcher, Discovery Science.

Comparisons across national contexts further highlighted the instability of these categories:

        “In Malaysia, you are either Indian, Malay, Chinese. Here you have White, European, Black…
        What do those things mean?”

        Group Leader, Developmental Biology

For some, even resisting classification felt morally fraught, although resistance itself is powerful:

        “Am I being obstructive… as a white middle-class person to not go along with the system?”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

Despite recognising the inadequacy of these categories, participants sometimes returned to discrete
racial constructs when describing genetic variation. One scientist described their own genome as
composed of locatable parts:

        “Small segments which are very, very African… but across my entire genome, it’s firmly and
        centrally in the white European, white British cloud.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

The language here is telling. Genomes are imagined as divisible into segments, while European
ancestry is described as central and stable. By contrast, “very, very African” appears peripheral and
exceptional. This way of thinking underpins a methodological logic that permits the exclusion of some
parts and the prioritisation of others, whether for their similarity or, their relative size (section 4.3).
Human data thus becomes a collation of neatly defined ancestral fragments rather than a blend of
multidimensional information shaped by both social and biological histories. This asymmetry recurs in
population labelling, where European ancestry uniquely carries the additional marker of whiteness.
137 | P a g e
Relatedly, one senior scientist described themselves using explicitly racial terminology, “Either white
or Caucasian male, heterosexual”.

The use of Caucasian reflects the persistence of outdated racial language within scientific spaces,
made more consequential by seniority and institutional authority. As shown in Section 4.4.4, the term
carries a long colonial genealogy that continues to be rearticulated in contemporary genomic
discourse under the guise of scientific neutrality.

Demands of Description

The responsibility to describe human difference clearly and accurately sits uneasily alongside
inherited linguistic habits. For early-career researchers in particular, the pressure to follow established
norms, satisfy reviewers, and keep work legible to both scientific and public audiences makes
racialised and ethnic labels difficult to avoid, even when they are critiqued.

Public engagement and language choices therefore become sites where these tensions are most
visible:

           “It is sometimes necessary to have public engagement as part of the research, including the
           choices of words for writing up the manuscript, which is part of the cycle of research, and
           follow-up activities such as results presentation in conferences, Twitter, LinkedIn, interviews
           etc. For these purposes, the choices of words to describe genetic similarity could be different.”

           Senior Scientist, Bioinformatics.

Similarly, another participant described how wider engagement requires shifting language when
communicating with non-specialist audiences:

           “We will describe the populations and their geographical areas. But it will be centred in genetic
           ancestries. When we engage with a wider group, we’ll have to change the language to be
           more accessible.”

           Senior Researcher, Discovery Science.

Language is presented here as something that must be made accessible, positioning public
communication as a translation problem rather than a substantive rethinking of classification. Yet the
emphasis on what remains “centred” also implies stability and hierarchy, and the move to geography
does not escape ancestry-based belonging. In practice, this ‘best-fit’ logic sorts people into categories
that appear objective but remain shaped by social histories of who is assumed to belong where.




138 | P a g e
Vignette - feelings about race outside the field - 11 December 2023

A public participant engaged through parallel public-facing work articulated a markedly different
relationship to race than that expressed by most scientists interviewed. Rather than treating race as
an awkward or risky category to be managed, she framed it as a historically contingent construct
whose harms arise not from its invocation alone, but from the failure to confront the social and political
systems that sustain inequality. While she rejected racial labels for herself, describing herself
foremost as human, this position reflected critical awareness of how race has been mobilised through
science, law, and policy to naturalise hierarchy.

In contrast to scientists who positioned race as scientifically imprecise or publicly dangerous, her
critique was historically expansive and ethically explicit. She situated genomic discourse within longer
trajectories of slavery, colonial extraction, and racial science, arguing that the central problem lies in
separating biological explanation from social conditions such as poverty, discrimination, and power.

This perspective complicates the apparent convergence between public and scientific critiques of
race. While both reject race as a biological essence, they do so from different positions: scientific
discomfort often seeks distance from history, whereas this participant insisted that ethical
responsibility requires sustained engagement with it.




Participants repeatedly described public engagement as a point where these tensions surface most
clearly, requiring shifts in language across audiences. Yet such shifts did not undo the underlying
classificatory logic. Rather, they reinforced distinctions between authoritative scientific description and
its translation for broader publics.

These socio-cultural dimensions of classification were not always clearly understood, particularly
among early-career scientists, who often felt they lacked guidance on what to record, report, or avoid.
This uncertainty left many vulnerable to reproducing outdated racial categories:

       “I don’t know, did you follow the lung cell atlas kind of tweet? So basically, the human cell atlas
       community kind of reconstructed the lung cell atlas, and then there was a subset of donors
       where there was recording of very crude things, like white, black, like very crude. And then
       there was a whole Twitter thing about, like, this is obviously too simplistic, but is it good to
       even record the data or not? .”

139 | P a g e
        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

The next participant linked this directly to the absence of shared norms:

        “I’ve never seen race as a term, which I think is a good thing, it’s not there. We usually see
        ethnicity, and, as I said, the only column I showed you was self-reported ethnicity. But even
        when you publish a paper, there are no guidelines on what should be included, at which terms,
        and yeah, I think it’s still very ambiguous.”

        Postdoctoral Researcher, Discovery Science.

For those who already viewed ethnicity labels as flawed or meaningless, this feeling of an absence of
guidance served only to entrench their use, exposing how critique and compliance can coexist within
the same practice:

        “You submit some work, and the reviewer will ask, did you look at ethnicity? Then you have to
        do it, and it becomes this vicious cycle that is reinforced. And there’s no group of people or
        organisation or learned society that says we need to stop this. It’s not coming from the
        President of the Genetic Society.”

        Group Leader, Genomics.

Taken together, these accounts show how classification becomes less an act of belief than an
infrastructural demand, sustained through habit, hierarchy, and the continual requirement to describe.

Across these accounts, racial ideas persist not as explicit analytic commitments, but as the language
through which authority, precision, and belonging are routinely expressed. Efforts to move beyond
race do not eliminate its effects; instead, race is reconfigured into the vocabularies of ancestry,
population, and data. As authority shape what can be said and known about human difference,
genomic research risks continuing to privilege certain forms of knowledge and certain groups of
people, sustaining long-standing inequities under the guise of neutrality. In this way, genomic
communication continues to reproduce racial hierarchy.



5.3 Power in Methods: Constructing Knowledge and Difference

The making of groups is far from neutral; it is a powerful act that structures both knowledge and equity
within genomic research. Scientific categorisation influences not only the analytical design of research
but also its ethical and social consequences (2.5). This second theme shows that while grouping
individuals is necessary for analytical clarity, it carries significant risks: flattening the complexities of
human diversity, embedding inclusion and exclusion into research practice, raising questions of
accountability, and complicating efforts to achieve genuine data diversity. Underlying these processes
140 | P a g e
are values, biases, and assumptions that shape how knowledge is produced and whose lives it
represents.

5.3.1   Flattening diversity

As discussed throughout this thesis, human populations are profoundly complex, shaped by genetic,
cultural, and environmental variation. While this multidimensionality is widely acknowledged, the ways
diversity is operationalised in genomic research often flatten that complexity. In practice, diversity
must be rendered analytically tractable. This necessary reductionism (Sarkar, 1998) enables
comparison, but it also reshapes what diversity is understood to be.

Participants described ancestry inference as an exercise in approximation rather than precision.
Genomes were situated within multidimensional space, yet ultimately reduced to summary positions
that facilitate analysis rather than reflect lived or biological complexity. As one programme lead
explained:

        “We use tools like principal component analysis to assign a genetic ancestry… you can place
        someone in a multidimensional space and say, this person’s genome is here. Of course, each
        individual will have small sections that look very African or very European, but you don’t
        capture that precisely. What you get is an overall genome ancestry average, which is what we
        often need.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

What is produced here is not a representation of genomic complexity but an operational simplification.
The data are compressed to fit methodological frameworks, rather than methods being designed to
accommodate complexity. Diversity becomes an average, and variation is managed rather than
explored.

Frustration with this narrow framing of diversity was especially pronounced in infectious disease
research, where environmental and social conditions are inseparable from biology:

        “I am frustrated about the definition of diversity… because in truth it just fails. Diversity isn’t
        just ancestry. It’s diet, environment, disease exposure, age, poverty. Suddenly, that feels like
        the real world.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Disease Biology.




141 | P a g e
Here, the laboratory is described as producing a partial and simulated world, constrained by tools that
struggle to accommodate complexity. Reduction becomes not just a methodological necessity but an
epistemic limit.

These pressures extend into data labelling and harmonisation. Sensitivities around ancestry
annotation were widely acknowledged, yet simplification remained unavoidable:

       “There has to be a certain level of simplification and harmonisation so that datasets can speak
       to each other, but you have to make sure you’re not misrepresenting something important.”

       Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

The scientific drive for comparability continues to shape how diversity is operationalised, particularly
through the construction of homogeneity within datasets. One group leader outlines the
methodological rationale:

       “That allows us to do these kinds of homogeneous comparisons between cases and
       controls… we correct for variation within the sampling, and define our case controls from
       within it.”

       Group Leader, Discovery Science.

Homogeneity here is not discovered but produced. While such practices enable statistical precision,
they also obscure the heterogeneity they claim to manage, sustaining the impression of stable and
bounded groups.

Participants were acutely aware of the limitations of continental categories, even as they continued to
rely on them:

       “It’s insane to label Polish and Finnish people under the same umbrella. A geneticist knows
       that. But you still need that branching.”

       Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

This awareness captures a central tension. Scientists recognise that these categories are insufficient,
yet they remain necessary for producing usable data. As Gitelman (2013) argues, the ideal of pure
data is an impossibility. Genomic science depends on simplification and exclusion to function.

As one participant reflected:



142 | P a g e
        “You have to have a comparator. That’s how science works. It’s reductionist.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Disease Biology.

Reduction enables discovery, but it also delimits what can be known. Through practices of averaging,
harmonisation, and comparison, diversity is transformed into manageable difference. In this process,
absence becomes institutionalised. Those least represented in genomic datasets are often those
most exposed to structural inequality, excluded not by accident, but by the very mechanisms that
claim to make human difference scientifically knowable.

5.3.2   Inclusivity and Exclusivity



The creation and application of categories in genomic research affect who is included in or excluded
from studies, shaping data accessibility, analytic comparability, and downstream applicability. Group
labels organise recruitment and participation, but also govern how data are stored, searchable,
shared, and analysed. Participants repeatedly returned to the consequences of categorisation,
particularly how participants experience these divisions, and how “low numbers” become a routine
justification for exclusion.

A Programme Lead described how small ancestry-labelled groups can be treated as a confounder to
be removed, framing this as a historically established solution:

        “There are certain labels that we don’t have in numbers to be able to include… when we were
        doing one of our very first genome-wide association studies… there were some Jewish
        ancestry individuals in the cases, and we didn’t have any Jewish ancestry individuals in the
        controls… so we had to remove the small number of Jewish individuals from the study…”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

They then emphasised that representativeness is negotiated as a matter of “appropriate number”,
rather than a fixed threshold, and positioned newer methods as expanding what is possible, without
removing the underlying constraint:

        “Once you start to get to having… an equivalent number… there are methods you can use…
        there is less need now to say, okay, I’m very tightly controlling genetic ancestry here… but
        still… if you have a very small number of individuals with a very divergent ancestry… you are
        going to run into problems, so you may need to remove them.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.
143 | P a g e
What is striking here is how quickly the analytic problem is located in the data rather than in the
research design or the wider infrastructural conditions that produced the imbalance. “Divergent
ancestry” becomes undesirable, and exclusion is normalised as methodological hygiene. That logic
also helps explain why minority populations remain missing from the kinds of studies that require
large, balanced case-control architectures.

A less senior scientist described the same practice more bluntly, reinforcing how exclusion can
become routine when ancestry-labelled counts are low:

       “Often you have… a few like 10 individuals who are not European. And then basically just
       exclude those people because you just simply don't have that power…”

       Senior Scientist, Bioinformatics.

They gestured towards alternatives, but each requires additional resources, access, or time, for
example, modelling structure, or drawing on external biobank data “to expand your research”. In other
words, inclusion is technically imaginable, but practically costly.

A further example makes the dependence on aligned categories and sample sizes explicit. The same
Programme Lead explained how, when controls include an ancestry-labelled group that cases do not,
the controls are reduced to match:

       “When you get… the UK Biobank data… [it] had more individuals of South Asian ancestry…
       and then we didn’t have any [disease] cases of South Asian ancestry, so we would remove the
       South Asian ancestry individuals from our control set…”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

This shows how interoperability is not just about phenotype harmonisation, but also about the
numerical distribution of people across categories. Categories become the conditions of
comparability, and comparability becomes the condition of inclusion.

Another participant linked these questions to national histories and to different logics for defining who
“counts” in a cohort:

       “Brazilian populations are so mixed for historical reasons… within the study, we are only
       asking that… parents and grandparents were born in the country… we have other studies…
       where… ancestry is self-reported… but… we have a group of scientists who will analyse the
       sequencing data to establish global ancestry and local ancestry.”


144 | P a g e
       Senior Scientist, Global Health.

Two inclusion logics sit side by side here. One is bureaucratic and geopolitical (country of birth across
generations). The other is classificatory and technical (self-report and then genetic inference). Both
can widen a cohort in one sense, while still funnelling participants back into ancestry structures that
determine analytical eligibility. The power lies not only in who is recruited, but in who remains
intelligible to the analysis once “ancestry” is “established”.

Participants also described how metadata labelling and harmonisation reproduce a hierarchy of
epistemic control. As one Group Leader put it, teams inherit the annotations of those who produced
the datasets:

       “We really rely on the data sets that are already out there, and how they are annotated by the
       primary teams… what the most invested people have decided is the right way of calling the
       data… And we adopt this… How do we… harmonise the phenotypic data…? Because the
       phenotypic data could be slightly different… Then you have it in white Europeans.”

       Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

The phrase “most invested people” is doing quiet work here. It points to where infrastructural authority
sits: consortia, centres, and teams able to define taxonomies, set standards, and decide what
becomes interoperable. To adopt their labels is to inherit their assumptions about how populations
and diseases should be delineated. Exclusion therefore occurs well before analysis, embedded in the
upstream politics of annotation.

That politics also surfaced through analogy: the same kinds of classificatory disputes that structure
cell annotation were offered as a model for population grouping:

       “There’s huge community… annotating cells… some people will feel passionate about
       blending some cells together or not. And that's totally the same for humans… we can blend
       some people together… there’s a historical sensitivity. And then there’s also pure data… and
       whether it makes sense to merge people together or not.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

Alongside merging, participants also described how small-population estimates can be subordinated
to larger-population estimates, where “accuracy” is equated with scale:




145 | P a g e
       “We compare… a GWAS from a small population against… a bigger population… [if] no
       evidence of… heterogeneity… we will use the genetic estimate from the big population
       because it comes from a big population. It's more accurate.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science

This sits uneasily with the earlier acknowledgement that small groups are often removed because
power is low. If low power makes the difference hard to detect, “no heterogeneity” may partly be an
artefact of underrepresentation. The move from exclusion to generalisability is therefore not
straightforward.

Some participants did describe attempts to quantify the epistemic cost of exclusion directly:

       “[They are] leading a project whereby you… have different ancestry groups… and then saying,
       if I took one of the data sets out… does that change my conclusion?… you post hoc work
       out… whether having a non-diverse sample affects the conclusions…”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

This reframes diversity as a sensitivity test, making visible how unstable conclusions can become
when representativeness is depleted.

Even proposals to bypass labels by returning to “raw data” were described as institutionally difficult,
distributed, and slow:

       “If we get the raw data… you have to go through data access committee… 20 different
       sources… But it’s not impossible… eventually you will get the data… and then… map… the
       diversity.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

The appeal to “raw” sequence data can sound like a route to neutrality, but it also risks obscuring
what your wider thesis emphasises: data are never raw in any meaningful sense, and access itself is
structured through power, ownership, and institutional hierarchies.

Participants sometimes named the affective discomfort produced by these limits to scientific control:

       “It is scary… we are getting to a different point where the facts are not sufficient, and that is
       scary for a reductionist basic scientist.”



146 | P a g e
        Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

Yet alongside discomfort sat a strong claim that ethnicity has biological importance in practice,
particularly in clinical translation where European-dominant resources limit generalisability:

        “For genetic studies ethnicity has been a very, very important aspect… most of the data are
        generated from European people, and whether they can be generalised… is very
        questionable.”

        Senior Scientist, Bioinformatics.

And, crucially, inclusion was sometimes justified through benefits to the already-dominant groups:

        “If you want to find the potential causal variants… European people… have the same…
        linkage disequilibrium… including people from different ethnicities… helps… identify causal
        variants… [and] also benefit European people themselves.”

        Senior Scientist, Bioinformatics.

Europeanness operates as the unmarked baseline (see section 4.4.3) and how methodological
discomfort with hybridity and structure produces exclusion (see section 4.3.2). Read through that lens,
inclusion is not only an ethical gesture but also an instrumental strategy that can consolidate value for
those already best served by existing infrastructures.

Taken together, these accounts show that inclusion and exclusion are not incidental. They arise at the
intersection of methodological conventions, infrastructural constraints, and epistemic authority. What
looks like a technical limitation (low numbers, harmonisation barriers, access committees) is also a
social and institutional mechanism that determines whose diversity is valued, and whose absence
becomes statistically permissible. This returns us to the ethical stakes of categorisation and to the
moral work done when populations are made comparable by being reduced.




5.3.3   Responsibility and accountability



This section explores how scientists at Sanger negotiated the responsibilities and accountabilities that
come with creating and using human population categories. Across interviews, researchers spoke
about the risks of misrepresentation, the pressures to demonstrate inclusivity and the practical limits
of what they felt able to control. Responsibility appeared dispersed and uneven, often acknowledged

147 | P a g e
yet rarely claimed. What follows are conversations shaped as much by avoidance and uncertainty as
by confidence in scientific method.

Curiosity seemed to be a strong driver for why researchers enter science, often discovery scientists
described their work partly as the pursuit of knowledge for its own sake.

       “ I mean, there is that curiosity and that sort of why, like a child, right, why why why why why,
       so that does have to, that is a kind of driving force for a lot of people I think, and certainly for
       me that has always been really the major reason I go into research and it is just curiosity and
       wanting to know the answers”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

The metaphor of childlike curiosity and innocence is casually assigned to scientists, portraying them
as motivated only by the desire to understand a new world. .

       “Have I always wanted to do this? No, I think for a long time I had no idea what I wanted to do,
       I just kind of was driven by curiosity.”

       Group leader, Translational Informatics

Even where the point being made is that curiosity precedes a fully formed research direction, this
sometimes flippant framing invokes an image of scientific intention that obscures the power and
purpose underpinning research trajectories

       “I think it's naive, you know, no one does anything they're not interested in, they just don't do
       it.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

In one exchange, after I asked what harm might arise from working with under-represented samples,
the scientist leaned back, frowning slightly as though uncertain how to approach the question. I
repeated it, trying to move the conversation beyond the usual moral position that everyone should be
included simply because inclusion is assumed to be ethically self-evident. Given that several
participants had already raised concerns about the sensitivities of using certain language and the risk
of misrepresentation, I had not expected the question to provoke discomfort. She resisted reframing
her stance and instead replied:

       "it's more about the good that we can do I would say it the more positive way is it's less about
       the harm of having a sample that's under-representative and more about the benefit that we
       gain for better representation, yeah? I guess that's how I would put it the obligation, basically."

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.
148 | P a g e
Another scientist described how exclusion is rarely foregrounded during analysis:

          "if you're working with data that you have in your hands, unless you're probably directly
          working with comparing it, I don't think you often think about that its impact will will be limiting,
          right? I think you're just more focused on like, you know oh, it is going to help someone, right?
          You're not thinking who, it's not going to help and what are the reasons for that?"

          Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

The use of inference models enables researchers to work with existing data without directly imposing
labels. Yet, as discussed earlier, summarising findings within frameworks such as genetic ancestry or
ethnic categories still exposes research to misinterpretation. Many participants expressed concern
that their work might be co-opted for harmful purposes, although the wider culture of science does not
consistently encourage deep engagement with these risks.

One senior scientist in his attempts to explain distinctions between sex, gender, ancestry and ethnicity
states:

          "You could be X. Your sex can be XX female. and your gender can be male, I mean, that is
          entirely fine, just as it is entirely fine to be ancestrally erm you know, to identify as, if if
          ancestry genome might be one thing, and then actually, kind of culturally, you this, your
          ethnicity could be different to your ancestry is what I am saying, in much the same way that
          your gender can be different to your sex. …when I was a PhD student and post doc, we would
          kind of use those words interchangeably but now I try, because old habits die hard, I try to use
          them differently and encourage people in my team to use those ancestry and ethnicity sex
          gender to mean very different things. …You can have two questions one to ask their sex and
          one to ask their gender but actually even doing things like that is quite erm it is quite sensitive
          for some people … I am a geneticist so you know kind of confronted with it. 'Yes, but what
          does the genes say' ? Most people, perhaps, are not. They are like, well, my gender is this,
          and that is the most important thing."

          Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

Hearing this, I noticed the uneasy laugh, a small but telling moment. It revealed a tension beneath his
seemingly clear explanation. He used gender and sex as an analogy, which helped him frame
ancestry as similarly flexible, but it also moved the discussion away from the deeper sensitivities
surrounding how ancestry is defined, measured and imposed. The responsibility of creating
categories was present but unspoken. Scientists construct the classifications that participants must
later inhabit, and even the simplest demographic question can feel intrusive or loaded. His
explanation shifted the sensitivity towards the public, while his own position remained grounded in

149 | P a g e
what genetics requires. The laugh exposed the gap between scientific neatness and lived experience
and showed how classification is negotiated, softened and occasionally sidestepped.

A recurring theme in discussions of genomic equity was the idea that increasing diversity in datasets
is the ultimate solution. While this is an admirable goal, it remains challenging given the complexity of
human variation and the structural barriers to collecting comprehensive data. Efforts to improve
diversity often focus on either maximising existing datasets or encouraging participation in
underrepresented groups. These offer practical short-term benefits but cannot fully address the deep
biases within genomics (Fatumo et al., 2022).

One senior scientist described their sense of reassurance about the generalisability of common
variants:

       "So I'm kind of reassured now that we're getting to large enough sample sizes that I can rule
       out for the vast majority of the loci that there are these differences in effect, size, because we'd
       have found them, I think, for some, there are kind of modest differences in effect, size. And I
       think ultimately we'll, you know, because standard errors get smaller. You obviously get more
       power to define tiny differences, we will find the differences, but they will be relatively small.
       And certainly, if you were trying to use these genetic insights to give you genetic findings to
       give you insight into the disease biology, accepting that, there's a kind of very much imperfect
       correlation between an effect size which variants and the relevance of a particular piece of
       disease biology. Then, you know, I still, I still think, if you learn about IBD biology in one
       population, then, based on that, you develop a drug. Then that drug stands a really good
       chance of working."

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

Here, they argued that a drug developed in one population is likely to work widely. This reflects an
expectation that scientific findings should be generalisable. I questioned this confidence given the
simultaneous emphasis on diversity and the insistence that population differences matter. If
differences across groups are minor, why does diversity dominate the discourse?

They later offered an important distinction:

       "Now, I should say. All of this, I just said, applies to common variants that we are getting out of
       GWAS. I am not so confident that the same will be true of rare variants. Rare variants typically
       are younger than more common variants, which means that they are more likely to be
       population specific, because they are more likely to have mutated recently and therefore be
       present in a smaller number of populations. And therefore you know that that problem I was
       talking about earlier about. Oh, I am looking at population A, so I can see the variants, which a

150 | P a g e
       specific population B is more likely to be thing more of a problem for rare variants than it is for
       common variants. So that is an issue, especially because we believe that rare variants, if they
       are associated with disease, are likely to have larger effect sizes. So I think we are at a certain
       point with our understanding around common variants where yes, there are differences erm
       but very likely a discovery in one population, which is kind of like an awesome, exciting ten out
       of ten discovery, is probably a kind of awesome eight or nine out of ten discovery for the other
       population. I do not know how true that would be for rare variants. I think that the jury is still
       out on that one"

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

The metaphor of the jury still being out was illuminating. It acknowledged incomplete evidence and
suspended judgement, revealing how uncertainty becomes normalised in scientific practice. It also
raised the question of who is responsible for deciding when evidence is sufficient. In research,
judgment is dispersed across reviewers, committees, funders, replication efforts and public or
regulatory scrutiny. When the jury is still out, responsibility is unclear, yet the consequences of
uncertainty are felt unevenly, often by populations not captured in datasets.

Researchers acknowledged that data gaps will always exist. Genetic findings apply most reliably to
the populations represented in a study, yet genomics often aspires to universal relevance. As one
infectious disease biologist explained:

       "And so if you really want to understand all aspects of disease, then you have to understand
       all people And so it's not just a fascination with underrepresented populations. It's just an
       acceptance that we're not normal. And then and suddenly, on top of that, you say, well,
       actually, we're really benefitting from this data and other people need to"

       Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

Across interviews, the categorisation of human groups emerged as a contested and ethically charged
process. Scientists drew on concepts of descent, ancestry and ethnicity in ways that often reflected
hierarchies of acceptability. Ethical principles such as maleficence, beneficence, autonomy and
justice appeared intermittently but rarely operated as a coherent framework. Instead, they were
mobilised unevenly and shaped by the immediate practical demands of research. Yet these
conversations also revealed that scientists were not simply carried along by structural norms. Many
demonstrated an active awareness that their own decisions, language choices and methodological
habits played a role in shaping how categories were constructed and used.

Some participants reflected that these discussions expanded their awareness and opened
possibilities within their own practices:

151 | P a g e
       "It's made me rethink how we focus on what type of research we do. And I think we touched
       briefly on the idea of having concepts around social justice at the forefront, and then the
       specific biological areas that would meet that..."

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

This reflection suggested that scientists were not entirely passive within the constraints of genomic
methodology. Instead, they recognised points where they could intervene, however subtly, in research
direction, lab culture or the framing of questions.

By having these conversations intentionally, one participant reflected on the effect they were already
having on them:

       "You're just resurfacing certain concepts by talking to you... one starts to ask themselves: Is
       this something in my research I could do?... Because this could have an impact on my
       research."

        Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

These moments showed that agency was often perceived as quiet and incremental rather than
structural or dramatic. It surfaced in the ability to notice when a concept or ethical concern
reappeared, to adjust a methodological choice, or to steer their group gently toward more reflexive
practices. Scientists described these possibilities as small openings rather than sweeping reforms, yet
they were openings that mattered. They revealed that responsibility was not only distributed and
contested but also something researchers could actively shape in the everyday choices of their work.

However, alongside this sense of individual agency sat a much more sobering account of how
accountability was understood within scientific culture itself. Here, participants shifted from the
possibilities within their own practice to the broader structures that shaped what scientists were
permitted, expected or allowed to do. One participant remarked:

       "Science does not incentivise supervisors positive or negative behaviour. Science has been a
       free for all. Its only now things have changed."

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

I leaned forward (Figure 32) as they continued, aware of my own sympathy for their description.




152 | P a g e
Figure 32. Still from a digital interview during a discussion of institutional accountability and
the cultural norms that can facilitate uncollaborative behaviour. Captured by the author, 30 Oct
23.

       "For a long time science has allowed this anything goes approach as they just wanted people
       to be creative. Even now funders do not really penalise anyone."

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

The interviewee leaned back in her chair, running her fingers repeatedly through her hair as she
reflected on what she understood as ‘bad’ behaviour within the Institute. Her account did not present
this behaviour as overtly malicious, but rather as a by-product of the underlying values that shape
scientific success. She described a culture in which reward structures privilege individual
accomplishment over collective practice, creating conditions that work against sharing, collaboration
and mutual benefit. In her view, these norms form the substrate upon which uncollaborative, or
ethically questionable practices can take root, particularly in the absence of clear mechanisms for
accountability.

Their reflections described a system where accountability has been historically diffuse. Creativity was
prioritised over oversight, and correction often entered only slowly. Many described progressing
through careers without confronting the ethical dimensions of their work until they were compelled to.
Listening to them felt like hearing a sad but familiar story, marked not by individual failings but by
structural dynamics.

These accounts also reinforced an earlier observation: many participants felt they lacked clear
guidance on how to navigate these responsibilities. Only one participant provided the name of any
formal guidance. Expectations associated with ethics review, data access committees, and journals
were instead encountered primarily as requirements to be met to get studies approved or published
and were often experienced as impositions rather than sources of guidance (explored further in
153 | P a g e
Chapter 6). In this sense, the apparent shifting of responsibility suggests less a complete absence of
guidance than a limited engagement with existing resources, shaped by how that guidance is
encountered in practice. In this context, their sense of agency was both real and constrained,
emerging in small decisions precisely because larger frameworks for accountability remained
uncertain.

Across this section, responsibility and accountability emerged as central yet unsettled features of
genomic research. The creation of population categories enables comparison and analysis but also
simplifies diversity, introduces inclusion and exclusion, raises ethical questions about representation
and reinforces structural inequalities. Scientists acknowledged these tensions while often distancing
themselves from their consequences. These methodological practices show how power operates
through research methods, shaping both scientific findings and the meanings assigned to human
difference.

In the next section, I turn to the final operation of power I observed at Sanger: the moments when
conversations moved beyond categories and data, revealing how the institution positions itself in
relation to access, equity and its wider social responsibilities.




154 | P a g e
6    Power Beyond Data: Access, Equity and Institutional
    Responsibility


For many scientists, questions of equity, diversity and inclusion were not simply abstract values but
conditions that shaped what counted as accurate, progressive and impactful research. At the same
time, they described working within systems where access to resources, infrastructure and decision-
making power was unevenly distributed. Power beyond the data therefore appeared in who could set
priorities, who could participate in collaborations and whose needs were taken into account when
designing studies.

This theme focuses on three interconnected subthemes that show how power in genomic science
extends beyond questions of data representation to encompass challenging practical dynamics
governing funding, access, collaboration, and the institutional structures that organise research.

6.1 A Complex Challenge

Diversity in genomic data is perceived as both a challenge and a necessity, as demonstrated
throughout this research. The bias in the data, skewed towards European data, is understood to limit
the applicability of genomic findings and to slow scientific progress. This was discussed in interviews
and more widely across Sanger, as captured in this moment in Figure 33.




155 | P a g e
Figure 33. Image from a Sanger hybrid in-person and virtual discussion fireside chat featuring
senior institute leaders. (Matthew Hurles (R) is Director of the Wellcome Sanger Institute, and Ewan
Birney (L) is the Interim Executive Director of the European Molecular Biology Laboratory (EMBL).
Here, their appearance reflects their institutional roles rather than participation in this research. This
moment in the session highlighted their expressed need for much stronger research cohorts across
Africa, including well-supported birth and adult cohorts and sustained investment in local
infrastructure, and emphasised that such efforts must be led within Africa rather than managed
remotely. The image illustrates the broader institutional context. Captured by the author. 12
December 2023.




6.1.1   Funding

The complexity of achieving more representative data came through strongly in the interviews,
alongside participants emphasising its importance

        “I think people think about it [ancestral bias in datasets] a lot but do not have enough time to
        do anything, or perhaps the word is worry. People worry about it a lot but actually do not have
        the opportunities perhaps. But I do think this is not just a researcher problem, this is a whole
        structural research problem. And so I think even broadening that conversation and highlighting
        the areas and the discussions that people are having is key to solving this. So actually I think
        that is why I was so keen to mention funders. I think they really have a role in this”

        Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

Funding emerged as a significant aspect of these participants' research experience (figure 34). The
complexity of it was often remarked on, as in the moment above, where this group leader was ‘keen’
to emphasise how funding facilitates or constrains efforts to improve diversity in datasets. Another
participant described how funding shapes available resources and influences how studies involving
understudied populations are conducted.

        "There's a lot of infrastructure costs that are needed to support patient recruitment, especially
        if you want to get accurate phenotype data. And oftentimes, in the size of a traditional grant,
        you don't have enough space to fund those elements."

        Programme Lead, Discovery Scientist

This highlights how funding constraints narrow scientific priorities, sidelining recruitment strategies
that would otherwise be methodologically preferable. This challenge was particularly pronounced
when obtaining data from historically underserved and marginalised populations.
156 | P a g e
       Figure 34 Funding display at away day. Photograph showing a screen celebrating a
       research group’s funding, taken during an open day where I was invited to observe one
       participant’s work in greater depth. The image highlights the resources that sustain their
       research and the broader investment shaping work at Sanger. Captured by the author. 4
       October 2023.

While engagement with communities was positioned as an expected element of the research process,
particularly in translational work, this participant highlighted how the absence of targeted funding
produces a structural gap that restricts engagement with underserved communities:

       “All funders are asking now for some kind of public engagement, which is good. But to engage
       with the public in a way that the public will engage back in a meaningful way we need to have
       funds to do that”

       Group leader, Translational Informatics.

Scientists expressed a preference for diverse data to be accessed and processed at its source, and
these conversations were clear in how that also required much more funding, infrastructure and time,
for example:



157 | P a g e
       "I kid you not, it is four times more expensive than it is here… you know, this investment into
       looking at diversifying the datasets is going to just take much longer because you need to
       invest in building blocks to create that environment and infrastructure to be able to generate
       the data."

       Group leader, Translational Informatics.

The lack of funding intersected with broader equity concerns, including collaboration and capacity
building, which were seen as essential for ethical and effective research in global contexts.
Researchers often viewed the pursuit of representative datasets as constrained not by scientific will
but by the financial and institutional priorities that shape what research is possible as discussed here:

       “So, do funders understand, know, in more detail than us, the way we should be behaving,
       and what we should be doing? the answer to that, in my opinion, is no, categorically not”

       Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

And this programme lead, in addition to echoing the well-understood reasoning for appropriate levels
of funding, introduces the need for funding designs that address the complexities of working across
geo-political regions.

       "So if we are now going to go and try and get, you know, samples from other ancestries,
       engage with local clinicians in non-European ancestry populations, quite rightly, they are going
       to need some funding, and traditional fund design ain't used to this."

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

Although this scientist was clear about the limitations of traditional funding models, they did not view
all funding as short-sighted. Instead, they pointed to a shift among some funders, including those
seen as non-traditional and even historically established organisations, towards more intentional
approaches to addressing inequity. One such example was described as follows:

       “A few years back, we were aware of the inequalities, and how much of the studies were
       driven by Europeans for European populations and the Chan Zuckerberg. Initiative launched a
       call to look at different ancestries” Senior Scientist, Global Health.

       This perception of change was echoed by a programme lead, who framed it as part of a
       broader movement among major funders to prioritise diversification in human genetics:

       “I think what I was saying about this movement towards wanting to diversify human genetics. I
       think, actually, some funders like Chan Zuckerberg initiative, for example. And increasingly like
        Wellcome and NIH, you know they are buying into that”
158 | P a g e
       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

However, regardless of whether funders were characterised as traditional or non-traditional, they were
often described as operating within inflexible structures. In reflecting on the additional time required
for research in African and Asian contexts, particularly in comparison with the timelines built into
standard funding cycles, this participant noted:

       “But if you ask a grant funder, ‘Look, we need two years,’ they say, ‘No, no, we're only funding
       12 months.’ You can't deliver a grant in that country on that topic in 12 months. It's impossible
       to do.”

       Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

These comments point to a structural tension between stated commitments to equity and the temporal
constraints imposed by funding regimes. While funders may articulate support for inclusive or global
research agendas, fixed timelines function as a limiting condition that shapes what research can
realistically be undertaken, and where.

This pressure was explicitly linked to questions of power and responsibility, particularly in relation to
well-resourced institutions and their funders:

       “But the science has to happen really quickly…. I feel a little bit like part of the problem of
       Sanger is perhaps Wellcome. Because you know they can. Who holds the power and the
       money. Passivity is an action in itself; by being passive, they have enabled it.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

In focusing on Wellcome as Sanger’s principal funder, participants were clear that Sanger is not
simply acting at a funder’s command. Rather, frustration was directed at what was perceived as
funder passivity. Funders were understood to hold significant power to shape research priorities,
particularly in relation to data diversity, but to be failing to exercise that power in ways that would
enable structural change. This sense of constraint was often expressed through irritation, defiance, or
open critique, even as participants acknowledged the authority funders hold.

       “I'm going to knock the funders again…. I mean, these are my views. So I genuinely don't
       care. I honestly don't. Because I think it's important, really important. I think what you're doing
       is important.”

       Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

This willingness to speak critically reflects a tension between dependence and resistance. Although
funders were widely recognised as powerful actors within the research ecosystem, senior scientists

159 | P a g e
often positioned themselves as ethically justified in challenging funding decisions when they were
seen to limit progress on equity and inclusion.

6.1.2   Social and Ethical Concerns


Across interviews, participants consistently expressed that questions of ethnic, cultural and ancestry
diversity and equity mattered to them, even where they felt under-equipped to address them fully. For
some, this took the form of explicit moral commitment; for others, it was framed as a recognition of the
need for further understanding, guidance, or expertise in navigating the ethical and social dimensions
of genomic research.

        “If we wanted to look at or focus on the populations that have been, or are, in significant
        disparity, we would need to focus the collection sites there, and that’s not what we’re doing.
        I’m also not entirely sure how we would achieve that without someone from the social
        sciences leading recruitment; do you see what I mean?”

        Senior Scientist, Global Health.

They recognised that the limitation lay not only in the funding framework but also in the expertise
needed to engage ethically and meaningfully with communities. Ethics also features in the
considerations raised by the next participant, who states:

        “it's an interesting one. Because in a way. what do you do? If you have a data set that, let's
        say, has been unethically collected. But it's there now, it's extremely valuable. And it's filling in
        the gap that's there, right? And do you deliberately decide not to use it? That's almost like, you
        know, that's even worse.”

        Group leader, Translational Informatics.

Here, ethics is framed not as a single moral rule but as a set of distributed concerns across the
research ecosystem. Ethical value is located in funding priorities, dataset governance, relationships
with communities, and procedural constraints such as consent and access, positioning ethics as both
infrastructural and practical in shaping what research can proceed.

        “For me it is like you don’t tell your children they can do anything and jump on the settee or
        something. You have to set some sort of ground rules. But these ground rules don’t seem to
        exist in science... So where are the rules? By country by institution. Ethical practice is
        governed by who… which policies override each other and which issues are seen as ‘bad’
        enough to be punishable?”

        Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

160 | P a g e
The metaphor of scientists as children in need of boundaries positions responsibility in a notably
passive way, suggesting that ethical conduct in part depends on someone else defining the rules.

However, this regulatory and procedural nature of the research environment wasn’t always seen as fit
for purpose as this group leader expressed below:

       “If you are writing a grant, you cannot engage with the community prior to getting the grant, it
       is unethical. You cannot get the permissions for it. You are constrained by a system that
       enforces what seems to cover all of the bases, but in itself stops you doing what would
       probably be the ideal situation."

       Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

A further tension emerged between the moral imperative of inclusion and the procedural ethics that
shape how, and whether, that inclusion can be realised. As one Programme Lead explained,
ambitions to represent humanity sit uneasily alongside the limits imposed by ethical governance
structures:

       “There was a discussion about whether there should be a piece on definitions, but it's tricky for
       a number of reasons. So one thing is that, on the one hand, there's this desire for the
       [programme] to represent the whole of humanity. On the other hand, when you get samples,
       often there's no record or very little record, depending on how the ethics is structured, of what
       you can record about the sample donor.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

Accessing genetic data that some believed had not already been shaped by inaccurate categorisation
or analysis (raw) was described by some as technically possible but procedurally burdensome. The
same participant outlined how managed-access systems shape everyday research practice:

       “The other way to get information about genetics is from the sequence, but a lot of samples
       are managed access rather than open access and so there's a whole application process that
       very few people go through, frankly, because it just takes time. So they just use the processed
       data rather than the underlying genetic data, because it's such a process to dig into it, such a
       palaver.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

Describing the process as a “palaver” signals how procedural ethics, can come to be experienced as
an obstacle to scientific work. One response to these constraints was to move away from human
tissue altogether, reframing research design to avoid regulatory entanglements:


161 | P a g e
        “And we try and move to cell free, you know, to cell models, and and so on, where there's no,
        none of those constraints. So we think about that. We think about the sample types and we're
        constrained by things like the Human Tissue Act… and the problems that happened at Alder
        Hey and the ethics around storing tissue.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

Here, donors are effectively separated from data, allowing research to proceed with fewer ethical and
bureaucratic obligations.




6.1.3   Open Source and Equal Benefits


Yet, as another senior figure noted, these governance mechanisms do not only protect participants;
they also safeguard datasets themselves as valuable assets:

        “So it's often as protection [restricting access to samples/data], protection of the participants
        themselves, but then there's also protection of assets, having a diverse or unique population
        genetic level datasets of value.”

        Director, Informatics Science.

This framing is revealing. Genetically unique and historically understudied populations are recognised
here as valuable assets. This raises questions about who can access that value and who ultimately
benefits from it. While procedural ethics and data access controls are often experienced by
researchers as hindrances, they can also operate as mechanisms of stewardship. Such processes
create the conditions for retributive benefit, where communities whose data have long been extracted
without return may have greater leverage over how value is generated, shared, and translated into
outcomes.

Sanger’s approach to data sharing was framed as positive and in contrast to the regulatory and
bureaucratic obstacles they often observed elsewhere:

        “Our resources are sort of open source and we have to be able to share that data so it's
        slightly different for us in that we would like to be able to analyse and then make that publicly
        available.”

        Director, Informatics Science.

Open data sharing is a fundamental aspect of the research culture in genomics institutions, notably
Sanger. It helped establish and enforce the Bermuda Principles, which required the open, rapid

162 | P a g e
release of sequence data into the public domain. However, inequities can be exacerbated by open
data access. Researchers in well-resourced settings can more readily navigate open-access
databases, while those in lower-resource environments often lack the necessary infrastructure or
training to engage with these resources fully. This scientist reflected on a campus event that I had
personally helped organise with Wellcome Connecting Science’s education team (Fig. 35 – 38).

       “ I had never thought that open access would actually hinder some parts of scientific
       development. My whole view had been that open science is simply what it is, that you cannot
       do anything different ….actually it is not like that, It has different impacts because of the
       power. It is not a balancing power.”

       Senior Scientist, Global Health.




Figure 35

Figure 36




Figure 37

Figure 38

involved in obtaining and conducting research with diverse data.
