165 | P a g e
This disparity reinforces existing imbalances in scientific knowledge production and participation.

          “it's not everybody who has access to the data and it's not everybody who's enabled because
          the way that the data is actually provided .. it's only for the limited few, who have like high
          computing resources and the ability to code. The vast majority of people don't have access to
          it.”

          Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

When acknowledged, this was framed as a fundamental obstacle to achieving equitable research
outcomes for underserved populations. Access to samples and data from these communities, and
their reciprocal ability to generate meaningful uses and outcomes from that data, remains uneven and
biased.

          “if the benefit of this expensive project is to be equitable then we need to provide the data the
          insights the knowledge outputs in a fair and equitable way in a portal and that's something I
          would say we really need to work on”

          Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

The bias in European datasets, therefore, represents more than a lack of diversity in samples. It
reflects wider inequalities in data access, shaped by the uneven distribution of resources and
technological power. The question of how to make data accessible was raised by this group leader:

          “We need to generate lots of data and we need to provide the data, but how do you provide
          the data so that everybody has equal opportunities and access to it?”

          Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

Several participants recognised this, with one in particular emphasising the need for capacity building
to address this constellation of challenges:

          “We are trying to increase the representativity [sic] of Latin American populations in genetic
          studies we are trying really to build capacity , by training people that is only the very, very
          beginning of trying to have a more equal representation of all populations in genetic studies,
          and how the genetic studies themselves will impact society.”

          Group Leader, Translational Informatics.




166 | P a g e
Despite these challenges, there was optimism. One scientist stated of their work that they “can't be
more excited about this” (Senior Scientist, Translational Informatics), while another described
translatability across ancestries as the “dream”

       Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

This hope extended to imagining a future in which the complexity of obtaining samples would be less
burdensome, although issues of categorisation at this stage remained largely unexamined. There is
an implicit assumption that the ways data are operationalised, and the exclusions and inequities that
follow, are primarily a consequence of insufficient data.

       “Okay the fullness of time we hope that all population everyone's sequence in the world and
       we can we can do well power these irrespective of your ancestry but while we have this
       disparity can we use the [data] in an informed way the data that we have the small amount of
       data that we have in these non-European ancestry groups to bridge that gap.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

Similarly, future access to NHS data was described as “promising” in its potential to:

       “provide, at least for the ancestry diversity within the UK, near full coverage. Obviously, in a
       global sense, you won’t get good coverage, but for all the different groups that are common in
       the UK, you would get good representation.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

When data are sourced elsewhere, the problem seemed to be resolved, framing diversity primarily as
a question of representation. This positions healthcare as an intermediary layer between researchers
and patients, privileging clinical and genetic data while marginalising environmental and social
dimensions.

Across this section, diversity in genomic data emerged as a shared priority that was nevertheless
constrained by funding structures, ethical governance, data access regimes, and uneven global
capacity. Participants consistently framed these barriers as structural rather than individual, producing
a situation in which commitments to equity coexist with practices that reproduce exclusion. The next
section turns to collaboration and teams, examining how scientists attempt to navigate these
constraints through partnerships, capacity building, and working across disciplinary and geopolitical
boundaries.



167 | P a g e
6.2 Teams and Collaboration

This section examines how collaboration operates as a site where diversity, power, and responsibility
are negotiated within genomic science. It shows how teams and consortia are shaped by uneven
resources, infrastructures, and histories, and how these conditions influence whose knowledge is
valued, how trust is built, and what forms of equity are imagined as achievable.




6.2.1   Diversity for Discovery


In their accounts, diversity carried two overlapping meanings: first, as a characteristic of the scientific
workforce shaping team culture, interpersonal dynamics, and decision making, and second, as a
property of the data generated through the Institute’s collaborations and global research networks.
These understandings frequently intersected, with participants suggesting that who is included in
scientific work influences what becomes visible in research, how questions are framed, and which
forms of absence are recognised. This quote discusses this relationship:

        “one cannot think about because you're just sitting in your closed little bubble and somebody
        who's coming just from a different background, like, Oh, you should have looked at, you know,
        X, because obviously X always comes up with Y, and you're like, I never thought about it
        because it never showed up in my little bubble.”

        Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

Participants emphasised the importance of encountering varied ways of thinking and recognised the
value of different personal attributes and experiential backgrounds within research teams. Here, the
metaphor of a “closed little bubble” signals a narrowed and insulated framing of scientific practice.
Cultural experiences were understood as making some issues visible while rendering others less
perceptible, illustrating how different backgrounds can disrupt habitual assumptions and open new
lines of enquiry.

A similar emphasis on collaboration and difference appeared in reflections on interdisciplinarity and
team science:

        “And I also think that the idea that you have this lone genius, eureka moment, is rubbish,
        because you know, what can one person do, build a garden shed? If you want to build a
        skyscraper, you cannot have one person building a skyscraper. So the more ideas you have
        and can bounce about, having a critical mass, the faster you can get things done”

        Group Leader, Developmental Biology.
168 | P a g e
The dismissive tone towards the trope of the solitary eureka moment positions such imaginaries as
“rubbish”, contrasting modest individual achievements with ambitious collective undertakings.
Impressive outcomes were framed as requiring cohesive teams, and diversity within those teams was
frequently linked to progress that benefits society.

       “the collaborative agreement that we have between industry and the academic institutes, …we
       can all come together in and develop things together. …. It is sort of contributing to humanity
       and humankind in supporting everyone.”

       Director, Informatics Science.

How “humanity” benefits from collaborative working also featured in discussions about data diversity.
Participants emphasised that different experiences shape what researchers notice or overlook, and
therefore how absence is identified.

Scientists highlighted the international composition of the Sanger workforce and expressed
confidence in celebrating this aspect of diversity. Comments like this were not infrequent:

       “It’s very international especially at the PhD and post doc level… people come here for training
       from all over the world.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

Workforce diversity was framed as both intrinsically valuable and instrumentally beneficial,
contributing to a richer intellectual environment and more reflexive scientific practice.

       "you often forget what [data] is not there unless you actively think, whereas the person who
       has no access to it is far more likely to see it as you know [missing], I think that's one, the kind
       of like the lived experience and things that you take for granted, I guess.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

Those differently positioned in relation to resources and infrastructures were seen as more likely to
notice the absence. Missingness was framed as something overlooked rather than deliberately
excluded, suggesting that ‘active’ thinking are what these issues require, a different orientation to
standard scientific practice.

This need for international perspectives was also linked to perceived limits of Eurocentric education.
One scientist reflected on how restricted educational exposure could explain, though not fully excuse,
a blind spot around social justice:

       “I have heard comments that almost imply there is a true lack of understanding of the world
        beyond the Western world. Maybe that's because of how education is in this country. You
169 | P a g e
        don't really learn about the countries, you don't learn about the cultures. Even if you do learn
        other languages, it is very much European, Northern European languages. So, there isn't a lot
        of opportunity to really understand. That may be it .. it’s lack of awareness."

        Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

The hesitation in this account suggests an attempt to make sense of limitation without treating it as a
complete justification. Framing blind spots as lack of awareness allows difficult questions to be
softened. Although participants spoke about data diversity in terms of increasing ancestral
representation, ancestry itself remained complex and underexamined. Overlooking one domain could
be presented as a technical omission, while the causal significance of the other was quietly set aside.




6.2.2   Collaboration


In large collaborations and data initiatives, diversity was described as most consequential when it
actively shaped the work rather than simply being present. Within consortia, representation was
framed as particularly important for driving discovery at scale. An example of this is exemplified in
Figure 39.




170 | P a g e
Figure 39. Image from a Sanger blog post celebrating a consortium’s work. A group of scientists
in facemasks look up towards the camera, visually marking the scale and collective spirit of the Atlas
of Variant Effects Alliance. The photograph foregrounds both the diversity of the community involved
and the collaborative labour required to generate knowledge at this
magnitude.https://sangerinstitute.blog/2024/01/16/nine-billion-and-counting-mapping-the-human-
variants-and-their-effects-on-health-and-disease/ 16 Jan 24




However, as this Group Leader explained, such diversity requires deliberate investment and
intentional design:

       “If you want to do it properly, you really need to kind of invest and get the right people to do
       it….You just need a lot of representations, and perspectives of people who are from those
       countries that have been affected by, you know, like they should be part of the panel or part of
       the kind of like group, you know.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

The repeated “you know” signals assumed shared understanding. This participant identified as
notWhite and often spoke explicitly about this, which may have shaped the shared ground they
assumed we held, which didn’t require them to finish their points. They were suggesting that those
who are marginalised, underrepresented or from countries that were exploited were needed in
designing better research. Their view was that intentional representation mattered to them across the
broader ecosystem and that it in fact brought an absent perspective.

Although international teams were widely regarded as necessary for high-quality genomic science,
participants also emphasised that both forms of equity and practical complexities were important. As
discussed here by a group leader explaining a developing consortia:

       “ this is going to be about, imparting expertise and knowledge… identifying what issues and
       organs are relevant… where there is local generation of analysis, or even a funding
       engagement project, which is focused on equal partnership. So talking about how do we work
       as a consortium? What makes an equitable partnership?”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

These reflections on the need for equity are connected with broader concerns in global collaborations,
where disparities in funding and infrastructure acutely shape what research is possible.

       "I can relate to that from our work... Running work in developed countries is so much easier,
       and your return on investment is much faster. ..if you're working in other countries. you will
171 | P a g e
          have to work for two years to figure out how you're going to collect the samples. You know,
          there's no infrastructure. …you don’t have access to the technologies that you have in
          European countries."

          Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

Participants contrasted European and non-European settings through the familiar developed-
underdeveloped divide, emphasising the additional labour required before research could even begin.
Collaboration was as such described as demanding of intention and investment.

          “It’s 10 times cheaper to sequence here [in the UK ]. So what we have is that we have a
          strategy whereby high value, low number sequencing is done in there [in a lower resourced
          country]... Where you need thousands of samples and it is going to take years ... we can
          afford to do here.”

          Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

In describing equitable approaches, practical solutions like this one were given, and participants also
highlighted that unevenness in collaboration was not one that could easily be addressed, as this
group leader described:

          “..by definition you are always keeping one person ahead because people are already ahead
          and pushing forward and everyone else is just trying to catch up and might be 10 years
          behind. In partnerships, people's careers, ages, and capacities are all very different, so it gets
          a bit blurred.”

          Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

This description positions an ecosystem with leadership distributed across and within institutions,
revealing the power dynamics that operate between them. It suggests an acceptance that some gaps
may be too large to ever achieve the equity initially imagined as an aim of these initiatives. Even so,
there remained a sense of hope that, while benefits might be uneven, they would at least be available
to all.

          “….everyone can benefit. Regardless of where you are, you start to get people benefiting
          concurrently.”

          Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

This more utopian framing, however, again sidestepped deeper questions of inequity and the scale of
the disparities involved.



172 | P a g e
6.2.3   Trust


Despite these sidesteps, disparities and unevenness have real-world consequences for the
relationships that can be built between researchers working in vastly different economic and political
contexts. Building and sustaining these relationships as an independent strand of collaboration
emerged as work that researchers were engaged in, either explicitly or implicitly. As this senior
scientist explains:

        “yeah. I don't know if you are aware but Sanger is doing a piece of work on the relationships
        between researchers in Sanger with researchers in low and medium income countries and I
        think that is a step forward”

        Senior Scientist, Global Health.

Where relationships were being built across unequal economic and political contexts, trust became a
central component of collaboration. In these settings, European research, including work led by
Sanger, was itself something to be tested, and this scrutiny shaped how relationships could be
developed and sustained. A reflection from one of my conversations with a Sanger PhD student,
supervised by one of my participants, allowed me to explore the dynamics of trust within these
relationships.




Vignette – Mistrust and Justice – 2 March 2024

It was a bright Friday morning, and I met a bubbly scientist in a café in the centre of Cambridge. They
were a PhD student supervised by one of my participants, who had suggested that I speak with them
about their experiences of working with understudied communities. The suggestion had been made to
help me better understand why these communities might lack trust. This young scientist was open
and enthusiastic about tackling these issues. They expressed frustration and a sense of
disillusionment with how engagement with communities was often carried out. Their own experience
had been something they had pushed for, yet the framework of support around them was not as well
developed as it needed to be. I could immediately see why their supervisor had spoken about them
with such positivity. The mistrust they described was of course rooted in history, yet speaking with this
scientist made clear that current engagement with historically excluded populations remains far from
sufficient to address these injustices. It prompted me to reflect on how historical justice had been

173 | P a g e
positioned in my interviews. Almost all the scientists referred to it as something belonging to the past,
something they were not responsible for, and something that simply created a challenge to be
overcome. How current inequalities continue to reinforce feelings of exploitation and isolation was
rarely, if ever, part of the conversation. In fact, harm, particularly around racial and ethnic categories,
was often framed as the product of ‘outdated’ views. I am not convinced that the observable inequity
supports the idea that such harms are outdated or no longer reproduced through research practices
and the unequal distribution of their benefits.




One scientist’s experience at a meeting with global collaborators revealed how organisational
reputation was understood, particularly in contrast to the benefits that collaboration can bring

       “I actually really learnt about how strong emotions were about this type of work, you know,
       whether people were adequately represented, who was benefiting from it, you know, what was
       the role of those people, the strong views about helicopter science and so on.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

The scientist’s account shows how striking the emotional weight of the discussion was for them. What
they encountered was not a casual critique but a clear articulation of how power operates in global
genomics. The reference to helicopter science underscored this. In the context of a data powerhouse
like Sanger, the term evokes fears that knowledge, samples and value flow in one direction while local
communities gain little meaningful participation or long-term benefit. For this scientist, the openness of
collaborators made visible the depth of these worries and the broader landscape of mistrust that
Sanger becomes associated with, regardless of individual intentions.

This mistrust of European research and researchers extended to the non-science communities they
sought to engage with :

       “it comes back to this idea of community, it's actually suspicion…No one trust any medics and
       … they don't trust us. If you come around saying you want a vaccine, they're gonna say, no,
       we didn't have it because of trust, and it's not just understanding it's lots of things”

       Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

The researcher positions themselves as part of the ‘us’ that communities do not trust. This ‘us’ reflects
a layered distance between scientific and non-scientific worlds, and an identification with medicine as
an inherently benevolent enterprise. Within this framing, healthcare is imagined as something that

174 | P a g e
should be offered and accepted, while community refusal appears as a barrier, revealing a tension
between assumptions of benefit and with the assuredness of “they’re gonna say no”, the legitimacy of
rejection.




6.2.4   Choosing a Team


These interpersonal dynamics were also visible in how research teams were formed and selected.
The process of building collaborations rested not only on formal expertise or institutional alignment,
but on interpersonal relationships.

        “First of all, I met the person who runs it... we spent a few days together just talking about
        ideas... As a result of that, we wrote a grant...”

        Senior Scientist, Global Health.

        Such accounts point to the influence held by senior figures, whose capacity to form teams and
        steer consortia extends beyond data generation. They also highlight how collaboration can
        appear relatively effortless for those with established networks, while relying on time,
        proximity, and the informal labour of relationship-building.

        “The researchers in these countries are truly part of this project... they feel like they have
        ownership..that we’re coming to help, support, and facilitate, but they feel like they are driving
        this project.”

        Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

While leadership here was framed through partnership and facilitation, it was rarely articulated
elsewhere as redress for past extraction; it often felt more like benevolence. As the desire to share
leadership sat alongside an absence of, and at times avoidance of, restitution, collaboration felt
similar to the pursuit of data diversity itself. It tended more towards the imperative to drive discovery
than for justice.

Across these accounts, collaboration emerged as a site where diversity, trust, and power intersect.
While participants expressed strong commitments to equitable partnership, responsibility for enabling
such change was often deferred beyond individual teams and between institutions. The final section
of this chapter, therefore, turns to institutional commitment, examining how responsibility for equity
concerns are taken up, limited, or displaced at the organisational level.




175 | P a g e
6.3 Institutional Commitment
This section examines how responsibility was understood and enacted within the Institute. Drawing on
staff reflections, it considers how power, leadership and institutional culture shaped the capacity to
address questions of equity. These accounts reveal both the limits of individual influence and the
broader structures that govern what becomes possible within the organisation.




6.3.1    Aims and Recognition


Across interviews, responsibility was rarely framed as a neutral or straightforward process. Staff
situated it within the practical realities of working in a hierarchical scientific organisation, where
authority, recognition and the distribution of labour shaped what individuals could influence. The
nature of Sanger’s work and its scientific capacity were central to participants' understanding of the
Institute’s position. Scientific discovery was described as integral to both individual and institutional
identity, and participants articulated a strong sense of epistemic authority, as reflected in the following
comment:

         “One of my principal tasks and goals in life is new scientific insight, regardless of whether
         people think it's useful or not, as long as it's true and it's valid.”

         Group Leader, Discovery Science.

Other participants reinforced this by contrasting academic freedom with the constraints of industry,
using this comparison to underline Sanger’s ability to pursue curiosity-driven research. As one Group
Leader noted:

         “I do not know how industry thinks about it… I am sure that one of the primary drivers is that
         they need to make money… whereas a pure academic environment has the liberty to be far
         more curiosity driven.”

         Group Leader, Translational Informatics.

This freedom, combined with Sanger’s early and unprecedented sequencing capacity, established the
Institute’s global standing from the outset. These advantages provided the momentum for an
organisation that continues to position itself, and be positioned by others, as world-class and
operating at a scale that exceeds most genomic centres worldwide (Fig 40 Wellcome Sanger Institute,
2020).




176 | P a g e
Figure 40: Extract from the Wellcome Sanger Institute ‘About Us’ webpage. Outlining its origins
and achievements while presenting the institute as a dominant actor in genomics and positioning itself
at the forefront of the field’s development. https://www.sanger.ac.uk/about/our-history/ 17 October 25.




The impact of Sanger's sequencing capacity was universally understood as this group leader recalled:



       “The capacity to sequence is a great example of how the power balance changed… we had
       resources… most other places, both in the UK and worldwide, did not have the same
       opportunities. ”

       Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

Given Sanger's international reach, it operates in contexts where trust, reputation, and the legacy of
historical inequalities continue to matter. As seen in 6.2 Sanger's interactions with global partners,
collaborators and participants encountered the wider reputational landscape surrounding Sanger. As
explored in 2.3, Sanger, as part of British and global genomic initiatives, is connected to racialised
hierarchies and exclusion within science that may influence its standing.

The earlier reflections from the Group Leader in Infectious Biology, for instance, hinted at sensitivities
rooted in longer histories of inequality that influence how scientific work is received internationally.
These unspoken histories form part of the backdrop against which Sanger is perceived, especially in
regions with legacies of colonialism and extractive research. Other reflections pointed even more
directly to these concerns, such as the following account, which foregrounded the sensitivities
involved in working in postcolonial contexts



177 | P a g e
        " And you know, one of the sensitivities is like, what's gonna happen to the data? …There’s so
        much sensitivity in that continent of, you know, Europeans coming in, colonising, taking stuff to
        benefit themselves, right? And I am—it’s a really interesting experience for me, because it's
        not part of my history. So I feel like I’m more objective, maybe, to this, but I am very sensitive
        to understanding the sentiments, to make sure that we are not going to do something similar."

        Group leader, Translational Informatics.

Data concerns were understood to be tied to the historical injustices of extraction and exploitation
carried out by European powers. Even though this scientist sees themselves as outside that history,
they recognise its legacy. Interestingly, they also feel they are somehow more objective because they
are neither from the countries that were colonised nor from those that carried out colonisation. It
raises the question of which form of objectivity is being claimed. Is it the objectivity of not feeling the
sensitivities involved, or the objectivity of not having been responsible?

Even if their countries of origin are connected to this legacy of empire, scientists are still unlikely to
have a direct relationship to the gains of colonisation. However, through their association with Sanger,
working in a well-resourced and politically stable setting, all these participants share a privilege
connected to these histories. By raising these points, they attempt to place themselves outside this
privilege or complicity, even while continuing to benefit from their position at Sanger.

One Group Leader reflected on how geopolitical tensions shape the reception of scientific work,
though their comments focused on their research's immediate impact rather than its deeper causes.
These tensions are rooted in global inequalities produced by historical injustices and uneven resource
distribution (Epo et al., 2024):

        “It is a blocker, right, so geopolitical stigmatising of disease and geopolitics, closing borders
        and preventing access is the biggest problem we have.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Biology

This scientist frames the pursuit of science as something that should ideally transcend geopolitical
borders, positioning these barriers as external obstructions to scientific progress. Their account
presents these borders solely as constraints, rather than as boundaries shaped by longer histories
that continue to influence how science is conducted. They noted that even deciding which diseases
become subjects of research is shaped by cultural differences, such as stigma that can influence what
work is considered possible or desirable. Their description of borders and restricted access as the
‘biggest problems’ also shows how keenly they feel the weight of state and organisational power in
their everyday practice. It is the research centre ‘we’, rather than the individual scientist, that becomes



178 | P a g e
constrained and positioned as an outsider. These limits arise not just from personal scientific aims but
from wider political alignments and the geographical position of a UK genomics research centre.

The reputation of the Institute itself also emerged as a concern for some participants, who recognised
how internal experiences feed directly into how Sanger is spoken about beyond its walls. One
Programme Lead in Discovery Science reflected on the frustrations of PhD students who felt
overlooked, an incident I had also heard of from the PhD perspective in student retreat in Bath Figure
41. They noted how such experiences travel with people once they leave and contribute to the
Institute’s external image:

       "… now we only have people who have seen us and lived with us for some time, and then
       there is the Institute's reputation externally as people leave and go on to say things."

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.




Figure 41. Materials from a creative session during the PhD retreat.
The retreat was an informal moments in which PhD students expressed concerns and reflections that
were less visible in formal scientific environments. Photograph taken by the author. 12 Aug 2022.

179 | P a g e
This privilege was often acknowledged, however, with scientists describing Sanger as a place of
considerable resources that enables work and carries influence.

       “It's a little bubble. It's so like almost out of reality in the sense that what we live here in terms
       of access to everything is so different from most parts of, you know, surely Latin America,
       Africa, parts of Asia and so on.”

       Senior Scientist, Global Health.

The participant’s description of Sanger as “a little bubble” signals an awareness of the distance
between the Institute’s well-resourced environment and the material realities of many regions where
its research partners and sample contributors live. Framing everyday life at Sanger as detached from
“reality” highlights how abundance can obscure structural inequalities and dull sensitivity to the
conditions shaping global health research.

Across interviews, several participants used metaphors such as a “bubble” (6.2) or “fishbowl” (7.2.3)
to describe their position, conveying insulation, fragility, and distance from the outside world. While
participants used these images to reflect both privilege and disconnection, my analytic framing of this
position as a delicate dream captures how institutional protection and funding sustain this insulation
while also rendering it precarious. Reliance on a small number of powerful funders reinforces this
fragility, shaping what can be questioned or acted upon and making commitments to inclusion feel
provisional rather than secure. As this quote states:

       “it's also the opportunity that we have at Sanger and partly because of the amazing people
       who are here and the amazing funding that is here, the amazing technologies”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

This insulation matters because Sanger’s position as a UK scientific institution is historically entangled
with uneven global power relations and past injustices. Occupying such a protected space requires
active recognition of these dynamics, particularly if privilege is not to limit responsibility or weaken
commitments to equitable practice.

Although Sanger was seen as a place of technical, financial, and human-resource wealth, for some,
this institutional privilege was accompanied by a sense of responsibility.

       "I have a really unique opportunity to give back in some way, and so I think that's an additional
       motivator for me to feel that. You know, we can make a change, and we can make a change in
       a at the level that's, you know, we can potentially impact the way they will really think about
       drug discovery and treating patients in those countries"

180 | P a g e
       Group leader, Translational Informatics.

The use of the phrase “give back” evokes the language of charitable work, yet it also points to an
unspoken sentiment about the gains that come with being at Sanger, in Europe, and in proximity to
whiteness. These gains persist even when the taking that enabled them is historical rather than
ongoing, and this history forms the backdrop against which such sentiments are expressed as this
group leader experienced:

       “I spent quite a lot of time talking to people because I guess I was not a white person, they
       were more open, yeah, talking about it in a way that was quite insightful.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

It is also notable that this participant positioned themselves outside whiteness in a way similar to the
earlier speaker who saw themselves as separate from European history. In both cases, this
distancing is treated as significant to their insight and access to a social awareness they think others,
who were not White or Western European, may have. This framing distinguishes whiteness from
simply identifying as White and instead casts whiteness and European in terms of what they continue
to represent throughout this thesis: a form of power and dominance embedded in data practices and
institutional presence.

This alignment between whiteness and institutional power was also echoed in material I encountered
at the Genetic Automata exhibition, which pointed to how scientific origin stories, from evolutionary
narratives to anatomical typologies, have been framed through European defaults that continue to
structure global hierarchies of knowledge (Figure 42). These positions bring considerable advantages
to those working within them, yet as these quotes show they can be experienced by underserved
communities as benefits gained at their expense.




181 | P a g e
Figure 42. Photograph from the Genetic Automata exhibition, a public art installation exploring
race, identity and scientific classification. The exhibition visited frequently alongside my fieldwork,
acted as a parallel interpretive space that helped contextualise and reflect on themes emerging from
the ethnography. Photograph taken by the author, 9 Feb 24. Permission given by Wellcome
Collection.

6.3.2   Valuing Social Context


Social and historical awareness was frequently described as something external to the routine
experience of working at Sanger, rather than as a form of expertise embedded within scientific
practice. While many researchers recognised the importance of understanding past harms and
histories , this knowledge was often positioned as optional, personal, or acquired elsewhere, rather
than institutionally cultivated.

        " I think it would have been super important for me to kind of understand historically the
        relationships that did not work in other countries, how much of this helicopter science impact,
        impacted other groups, universities, people I think that would have been quite useful for me. If
        I have had all my life in Sanger, that would have been like transformative because working in
        the UK is already very different"

182 | P a g e
       Senior Scientist, Global Health.

Although this scientist felt that their own experience had made them attuned to the social context of
research in other countries, they recognised that such awareness does not arise automatically from
being at Sanger. In some ways, the environment of the centre the ‘bubble’ can obscure a fuller
understanding of these dynamics, rather than make them more apparent.

This moment sharpens the question of what expertise is needed to work responsibly across such
divides and how scientists understand their role in addressing the political and ethical terrain that
accompanies global data work. As such this participant reflects on the responsibilities and the skills
required to navigate them:

       “I feel as though unless this type of work actually has people with experience, and who can
       comment on that in a more meaningful way, it’s actually very difficult to do. It’s a bit like, you
       know, it’s very easy to see how a cell biologist is not going to be able to undertake genomics
       experiments necessarily. But when it comes to these things, we don’t seem to recognise it as
       actually requiring specialists and experts. You know, you can’t become an overnight
       sociologist.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

This scientist’s reflections were revealing of the hubris and dismissiveness that can surface as others
have recognised when contemplating the place of social and historical understanding within these
disciplines (Adsit-Morris et al., 2023). They treated knowledge, however, as a specialised skill, placing
social expertise on the same level as technical competence, yet the platforming of social science in
settings like Sanger is often not equal, highlighting the gap between what is needed and what is
formally valued. Social science is often seen, as has been shown, useful in its support of scientific
endeavour (recruitment, improved data diversity) rather than in its own right (Keston et al., 2025).

In a later interview, the same group leader drew attention again to how personal attributes, including
having a few "brown" or "black" faces present, are far from sufficient for building trust or addressing
deeper structural concerns. For them, this pointed to a wider issue about how the value of ethics and
social justice is embedded within the organisation, as the reflection below shows:




Vignette – Data diversity on the Agenda – May 2023

The Diversity in Data workshop at Sanger brought together senior staff to consider how diversity,
representativeness, and generalisability should shape the centre’s scientific work. A task of a strategy
given to them by their main funder, the Wellcome Trust. Although the agenda involved concepts
183 | P a g e
deeply entangled with social and historical concerns, the discussion remained firmly technical.
Surrounded by scientific and policy staff, I was the only person in the room with social science
research training, and the absence of that perspective felt pronounced.

This contrast hit me especially sharply because elsewhere in the Institute I had often been invited into
team meetings where researchers actively sought my insight. In those settings, my work was treated
as necessary and overdue, and I was sometimes spoken to as though I held answers or authority.
That sense of welcome made the atmosphere of this senior-led initiative stand out even more.

As my role shifted into that of an ethnographer, I made sure to explain that I was now studying the
organisation rather than contributing to its strategy. Ahead of the workshop, I asked that my position
be communicated and requested permission to take observational notes. Only afterwards did I learn,
indirectly, that a separate email had been sent questioning the governance of my study and denying
that permission. I had not been copied into the exchange.

At the workshop, I introduced myself for transparency and made clear that I would not be collecting
data. Even so, I was questioned about my ethics process in front of the group. The tone felt
corrective, as though I had not understood the basics of research approval. A few weeks later, I was
formally asked to revise and resubmit my protocol for Sanger, despite already having full university
approval. In that moment, my project seemed to be treated not as legitimate research but as
something risky or intrusive.

.




The work on representation continued after this meeting, and a strategy was later published without
involvement from the social science research and dialogue team of which I was part. Despite
repeated claims from interviewees that social science expertise was valued and urgently needed, its
absence from the formal process was striking. This encounter made visible how social science could
be welcomed in everyday conversations, yet marginalised or treated with suspicion in the spaces
where strategic decisions were made.

These senior scientists all saw this historical knowledge of social injustice as important. One group
leader shared their feelings after a conversation with participants at a conference regarding their
strong mistrust of institutions like Sanger due to exploitation and data governance concerns:

       "I think it's not. It's not discussed enough or there's no real awareness of it…every Genome
       Centre probably has had a role in some of these things, because that's how historically it

184 | P a g e
        was…I think it's also not necessarily fair to kind of like put a lens that judges previous things
        with modern expectations and values. What society felt was appropriate changes. But it's
        important to understand that those things happen, ."

        Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

The protection of the institution despite this is clear statement of liability, reintroduces the distance
seen earlier with the participant who positioned themselves outside historical injustice. Here the
‘blame’ is transferred from the institution itself, calling for its judgement to be ‘fair’. Similar arguments
appeared in the 2.2.2 when discussing legacies of scientific racism, where scholars such as
Blumenbach were often excused on the basis that their views were acceptable at the time. Although
the logic of this position is understandable, it nevertheless does little to confront the embedded ideas,
practices and datasets that continue to shape genomic research.

This produced a recurring tension. Participants often described this knowledge as personally
important, yet expressed reluctance to see it as something that should be mandatory or institutionally
provided.

        “Me, personally need to know. Whether it’s necessary? It depends. I mean, it’s not necessary
        to know the historical derivation of those rules, if you like, but to understand often increases
        compliance. Yeah. So I think it’s really about understanding that, making it more likely to think
        and to comply. So it’s not necessary, strictly, but I think it’s certainly desirable that people
        understand the derivation.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

These ruminations were common, and I often held silence, creating space for these almost
contradictory justifications to surface. The image reflects how this pause was also enacted bodily, with
my hand held near my mouth (Figure. 43).




185 | P a g e
Figure 43. Holding silence. Screenshot from a Zoom interview with a Group Leader in Infectious
Biology, taken at the point in the conversation when they discussed the importance of historical
understanding within scientific practice. Captured by the author, 20 Dec 23

A similar dynamic appeared here. The researcher acknowledged the value of historical awareness yet
simultaneously positioned such understanding as beyond what most scientists could reasonably be
expected to acquire. Instead, they suggested commitment to fairness should emerge from
interpersonal decency rather than structured learning or institutional provision:

        “I think it helps a lot. Yeah, you know. On the other hand, I wouldn't you know, we can't all be
        like historians. History is a huge field and I'd like to think that just you know one human being
        meets another human being listens to what that that person has to say so, and then kind of so
        takes it from there”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

In marking what scientists are not expected to be, these accounts expose the historically contingent
construction of scientific identity, and raise the possibility that its boundaries could be drawn
differently.




6.3.3   Boundaries of Responsibility


Within these constrained boundaries, responsibility for engaging with ethics and historical context was
rarely claimed collectively or institutionally, When I did ask who should carry responsibility for
engaging with the historical and social dimensions of scientific practice, participants frequently located
it with less senior researchers. The reasons given tended to centre on the idea that junior staff had

186 | P a g e
more time or that, because they were carrying out the technical work, they were better placed to think
about these issues. Senior leaders were described as being focused on strategy rather than the day-
to-day contexts where questions about history, ethics or representation emerge.

Though these histories sit beneath much of the work, they were absent from any formal training after
the undergraduate level and, even then, appeared inconsistently. Speaking to scientists across the
Campus, it became clear that exposure to these topics was patchy.

        “No, we're not trained for that at all, I would say. We're not trained for that, like we're not
        trained in that. I mean, I wasn't, at least, obviously, I was an undergraduate 30 years ago,
        might have changed now. But it's not in, not in any level of training. Not at any point. Is it part
        of it”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

Although this was a senior scientist, her limited awareness of what early-career researchers
encounter in terms of training on these issues was not far removed from my own experiences
discussing them with PhD peers. Some PhD students who had trained partly overseas described a
different experience. One student, for example, spoke about her time in the United States, where
subjects such as eugenics and scientific racism had featured more explicitly in her scientific
education. These issues surfaced more openly in self-initiated or extracurricular contexts than in the
everyday scientific training offered at Sanger, a pattern illustrated in the following reflection:




Vignette – Scientific Racism in Cambridge – Nov 2022

In November 2022, I received an email from fellow PhD students at the Wellcome Genome Campus
expressing concern about a new academic appointment at the University of Cambridge and a
blogpost he had made. The appointee, Nathan Cofnas, is a philosopher whose work argues that
biological differences between human populations (often discussed in terms of “race”) merit continued
investigation (Echaniz-Furuta, 2022). He contends that restricting research on group differences in
traits like intelligence stifles ‘free inquiry.’ Cofnas has publicly denied being a proponent of racism
(Cofnas, 2022), yet many of his supporters are drawn from circles associated with White nationalist
views (Hood, 2022).

The episode surrounding statements made publicly by Nathan Cofnas placed students in an uneasy
position, caught between their scientific identities and their institutional affiliations. During this period,
several PhD students sought guidance on how to respond, including whether to issue a public letter
as university students and as researchers based at Sanger. The advice they received was cautious.

187 | P a g e
They were discouraged from speaking publicly in connection with Sanger, on the grounds that the
Institute was not formally implicated because Cofnas was not an employee. This position, while
institutionally understandable, left the students feeling unsupported in their frustration and uncertain
about how to voice their concerns. Some turned to the Research and Dialogue team within Wellcome
Connecting Science, where I was based, in search of acknowledgement and direction. Although
Wellcome Connecting Science issued a clear statement addressing scientific racism, no equivalent
statement was released through Sanger’s senior communications channels. The wider context
remained unsettled, as the University of Cambridge continued to grapple with the issue, initially
restricting Cofnas’ supervisory duties and later upholding his fellowship following an internal review,
accompanied by a formal statement issued in October 2025 (University of Cambridge, 2025).




The presence of visible controversies involving institutions with global reach and influence serves as a
reminder of the need for socially responsible genomic research that takes historical context seriously.
Yet within Sanger, the burden of engaging with these difficult historical and social questions
repeatedly fell to early-career researchers, rather than being carried collectively or taken up at the
leadership level. As shown by this participant's response to my suggestion of ELSI training for the
researchers:

       “So yeah, it is a wonderful thing to have, but I do not know how easy it would be to achieve.
       But, you know, it does not need to be faculty, maybe scientists who work on these projects,
       PhD students, technicians, postdocs, you know, would they be interested in having these
       conversations?”

       Senior Scientist, Translation informatics

Just Good Science (Figure 44) was a programme run by the Engagement and Society team at
Wellcome Connecting Science. It provided a space for people across the Campus to explore the
everyday ethics of genomics, focusing on the practical, technical and epistemological questions that
surfaced in routine work but often sat outside formal ethics discussions. By drawing attention to these
day-to-day issues, the project encouraged researchers and other professionals to reflect on the social
and ethical implications of their work. The meetings were open to everyone, and participants could
join as and when they wished, contributing whether they held clear views or were still thinking things
through




188 | P a g e
Figure 44. Blocks used in the Just Good Science initiative, photographed in Hinxton Hall at
Wellcome Sanger Institute. Positioned within a building historically funded through wealth
accumulated by the East India Company, the image underscores how present-day conversations
about ethics and fairness occur within spaces shaped by longer histories of colonial extraction.
Photograph taken by the author, 17 Jan 24.




I attended Just Good Science on several occasions, including facilitating a session with scientists on
the use of race, ethnicity and ancestry in their work. Another session I joined later in my data
collection focused on collaboration between the ‘Global South and Global North’. It centred on how
positive aspects of collaboration with Sanger might be replicated and how to ensure that ‘knowledge
goes back’ to partners in less-well-resourced settings, including capacity building and more equitable
research agendas. These meetings were engaging, honest and, over time, became a kind of
community for me on campus. I often saw other PhD students and postdoctoral researchers there,
many already interested in these conversations, just as senior leaders had predicted. The same faces
reappeared in other venues where ethical and equity concerns emerged, from the Cofnas situation
described earlier to campus discussions on race and equity, where senior leadership presence was
confined to a very small, consistently recurring group. This dynamic was also noted in my interviews:

       “Classic example, a lot of activities are attended by people who are an ethnic minority. But
       then there's never any Senior leadership members who attend. You know how many of those
       activities go… and then it doesn't matter because their absence is not noted. Their absence is
        tolerated”.
189 | P a g e
        Group leader, Developmental Biology.

Those who were willing to engage with questions of equity and ethics were generally junior
researchers, yet their capacity to influence institutional direction was markedly limited. Enthusiasm at
this level has constrained impact without explicit endorsement from senior figures. As one group
leader observed, the ability to effect change is closely tied to positional power:

        "If the people around you do not support it, and if you have not got enough, I think that is the
        most important. Because if you have like a head or a director who says, actually, I do not see
        that as a problem that is not a priority."


        Group Leader, Developmental Biology.


This reflection highlights how responsibility for these issues is frequently placed on those with the
least power to act, while meaningful change requires endorsement from those who set priorities and
determine what counts as important within the organisation.

A similar family metaphor emerged in another account, but here with a different emphasis on
institutional responsibility:

        “The environments in which we raise scientists are quite important... I think we can also raise
        them in an environment where actually how you do science, how we talk about science, is as
        important as the science that you do.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

In this framing, leadership is cast as a parental role within the Institute, with responsibility for shaping
the immediate environments in which scientists are “raised” and for embedding the values that guide
their day-to-day practice. While this reflection concerns the responsibilities of individual leaders, the
following section shifts the focus to how such responsibilities are understood and distributed more
broadly across the organisation.

These reflections raise broader questions about leadership: where authority is concentrated, how
responsibility is assigned and whether those in senior positions take up the ethical commitments that
staff identify as necessary. Scientists described leadership as a form of positional power that sets
agendas, distributes resources and determines who is included in ethical or institutional
conversations.

        "I think it would be kind of naive to think the conversation [data diversity] that takes place in a
        room with, all the members of a research team, takes place in a senior leadership team. And


190 | P a g e
       there's far more weight and decision making that happens as you go higher up. And you know
       [their own] topics then become higher priority ”

       Group leader, Translational Informatics.

One Group Leader in Developmental Biology challenged what they perceived as a widespread belief
that diversifying leadership is sufficient to shift institutional culture. They made this point through a
comparison with government:

       “What is the progress if non-white people are in leadership roles but have views and
       implement policies that are exactly like what a white person would, or even worse the far
       extremes, [not] naming names. But if you look at some government leaders, do you know what
       I mean?”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

This assumption echoes other simplified narratives of equity in genomics, such as the idea that
increasing the diversity of datasets will, by itself, produce more equitable scientific outcomes. Here
whiteness is again framed as structurally tied to the oppression of non-White groups, even when
leaders are not at the “far extremes”. This challenges any assumption that diverse leadership
naturally disrupts entrenched cultures, simultaneously drawing my attention to the longstanding
absence of non-White voices within Sanger’s senior leadership teams.

Notably, this comparison to government and political life was not unique. Both this participant and
others drew on political analogies when describing Sanger, suggesting that they viewed the Institute’s
structure, distribution of authority and exercise of power as resembling a political system as much as
a scientific one. When I asked this participant directly about the responsibility of social justice in
leadership, they remarked:

       "That's a hard question, does it always become a responsibility? I mean I've seen people get
       to the privilege position? And they just abuse it right? I mean, you think about politicians. You
       would wanna think that a lot of them are motivated and driven by the fact, they want to serve
       their community."

       Group leader, Translational Informatics.

This participant, while also using political analogy to talk about power and privilege, reflected on how
these positions must be used cautiously. They noted that privilege itself can become problematic,
distracting from the ‘good’ intentions individuals may bring to leadership roles and complicating their
efforts to act responsibly.



191 | P a g e
Leaders were often perceived as distant from the everyday contexts in which ethical tensions emerge,
and demographic diversity alone was seen as insufficient to shift entrenched institutional norms.
Comparisons with government and political life underscored this point, suggesting that scientists
understood Sanger not only as a research institute but as an organisation whose internal dynamics
resembled the broader political structures that shape scientific authority, privilege and accountability.

Across this theme, a structural condition was revealed as one shaping scientific practice,
collaboration, and institutional responsibility. Efforts to diversify genomic datasets were constrained
not only by methodological and logistical difficulties but also by the legacies of unequal funding
systems, uneven research infrastructures and the labour required to build trust in contexts where
scientific institutions from Europe have long been associated with extractive practices. These
geopolitical landscapes continue to influence how research centres like Sanger are perceived and
how their work is negotiated. Scientists described the need for a deeper understanding of these
histories and the responsibilities they create, yet such awareness was inconsistently held and
unevenly taken up across the Institute.

These accounts show that power extends far beyond the production of data: it is embedded in
institutional histories, colonial legacies, collaborative infrastructures, and everyday hierarchies. This
sets up the final chapter, which turns outward to the broader genomic ecosystem. By examining how
researchers experience funding structures, capacity building, geopolitical constraint and global-
regional scientific inequalities, the chapter asks how social justice can be meaningfully embedded in
the field. Understanding these wider structures reveals both the barriers and the points of leverage
that shape what scientists at different levels of seniority can do. In this sense, the genomic ecosystem
becomes the terrain through which questions of responsibility, equity and power must be navigated,
and where possibilities for more just scientific practice may emerge.




192 | P a g e
                7 The Genomic Ecosystem of Sanger Scientists

7.1 Introduction


Walking through the Sanger Institute’s landscaped campus for the first time, I was struck not only by
the beauty and calm of its setting, nestled in the Cambridgeshire countryside, but by the subtle ways
in which that very calmness signified something deeper about the Institute’s place in the genomic
ecosystem. As a PhD researcher openly identifying as a Black woman, whose interests extended
beyond the scope of my immediate project to broader questions of inclusion and ethics in science, my
time at Sanger was shaped by a complex set of experiences.

This chapter explores these experiences alongside those of other scientists I encountered at Sanger.
It seeks to reflect critically on the institution’s identity, its purpose, history, and relationships with
societal and industrial actors while also acknowledging how contradictions around inclusion,
openness, and impact emerged throughout the daily life of the campus. Drawing on participant
quotes, informal observations, and personal reflection, I aim to paint a picture of what it means to live,
train, and work in a place that is simultaneously cutting-edge and remote; internationally diverse yet
often culturally homogeneous in its leadership; and committed to open science while enmeshed in the
opaque dynamics of industry collaboration.




7.2 The Wellcome Sanger Institute: Mission, Space, and Institutional Identity

Sanger was established to spearhead the UK’s contribution to the Human Genome Project, a global
scientific endeavour of unprecedented scale. Its founding purpose was focused and specific:

        “It was to deliver the Human Genome Project… their mission was to deliver the Human
        Genome project so consortium… it wasn’t a research institute. It was a centre with 100
        employees that were just staff.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

This quote reflects not only the Institute's functional origins but also the structural intention behind its
early formation.




193 | P a g e
From its beginnings as a technical hub, the Institute expanded its mission, moving from sequencing
into broader realms of genomic science, including the study of gene function, disease association,
and the development of new technologies. Yet, there remains a tension between this expansion and
the clarity of its original mission.

        “Maybe it feels like, what is it that we want to achieve? And how do we want to do it and…
        what are the values?”

        Group Leader, Infectious disease.

These questions continue to haunt the Institute’s evolution. One of Sanger’s unique qualities is its
singularity of focus.

        “Sanger is completely different from other… departments… it’s all about… generating genomic
        resources… for the community and that’s what it is —nothing else“

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

This purity of purpose, shaped by Wellcome's funding priorities, sets it apart from academic
departments, even as its leadership describes it as an academic environment. The language of
“nothing else” is especially revealing; it implies a clarity of mission but also a boundary, a delimitation
of what counts as valuable work and, potentially, who gets to do it.

Sanger’s role as a training centre for global scientists was often praised, particularly among PhD and
postdoctoral researchers (6.2). The focus of purpose made Sanger a focal point in global genomic.

The pride in this global reach is not misplaced: training at Sanger represents a form of scientific
capital that can be exported to other research settings, multiplying its influence. However, this
celebratory narrative sometimes masked inequalities within the workforce. Permanent staff,
particularly in non-research roles, remained predominantly British, and diversity in terms of ethnicity
and class, especially among British staff, was still seen as lagging

        “To be honest, I still think we fall short on supporting people from low-income backgrounds.”

        Senior Scientist, Global Health.

As someone from a minoritised background, I found myself reading between the lines of these
conversations. While the campus was open and serene, its spatial and visual cues did not always
suggest inclusivity.

        “You know there is no other signage on campus other than that about Sanger.”

194 | P a g e
       Staff Scientist, Human Genetics.

Coming from a clinical environment where walls were crowded with health information, recruitment
notices for clinical trials, and posters celebrating staff and community groups, Sanger's minimalist
signage stood out. Its physical environment with looming sculptures, vast reflective domes, and
intricate wooden carvings depicting the evolution of sequencing, reflected the Institute’s ambitions but
also reinforced a sense of institutional singularity. The décor was not just aesthetic but ideological:
speaking to a curated identity of prestige, order, and calm, far removed from the noisy, lived-in feeling
of a hospital or university department.

The campus always felt calm: wide open spaces, manicured grounds, and scientists queuing in an
orderly fashion for food at lunchtimes, engaged in composed conversation before returning to their
labs housed in large, multi-storey buildings. This composed rhythm, while comfortable for many,
added to the impression of being “in a bubble.” (Director, Informatics). In contrast, the Francis Crick
Institute (The Crick), a large biomedical research institute based in London, is nestled within a vibrant
urban neighbourhood and integrates science communication into its very entrance. Sanger’s physical
and symbolic isolation contributed to both its sense of prestige and its detachment from the
messiness of the social world. Like Sanger, the Crick is designed around scale, infrastructure, and
collaboration.

Figure 45 and 46 show Sange and the Crick and their surrounding landscapes.




Figure 45. The Wellcome Genome Campus. https://www.sanger.ac.uk/about/campus-
connections/wellcome-genome-campus/ 19 Dec 25.




195 | P a g e
   ‘Image removed due to copyright restrictions. The original artwork
   'Aerial view of the Francis Crick Institute London can be viewed at
   https://www.jasonhawkes.com/media/1912768b-cf6c-4de6-91c8-
   ef2fd484ff58-aerial-view-of-the-francis-crick-institute-london




Figure 46. Aerial view of the Francis Crick Institute London
https://www.jasonhawkes.com/media/1912768b-cf6c-4de6-91c8-ef2fd484ff58-aerial-view-of-the-
francis-crick-institute-london 19 Dec 25.

This feeling extended to how certain types of work were perceived. My own research often elicited
puzzled responses, unusual work undertaken by the Institute. I became known as the one who ‘does
the ethics PhD’, an identifier that felt peripheral. The ethical and social dimensions of genomic
research, while officially acknowledged, were not uniformly integrated into the Institute’s mainstream
activities.

Still, efforts to engage the public were visible. I participated in outreach days designed to demystify
the campus for school groups and other visitors. These groups were brought in on coaches,
minibuses, and shuttles mirroring the way staff themselves commuted in and moved through Sanger
like a curated exhibit. A one-way glass in one of the labs could slide back to reveal the sterile, white-lit
space within: a room dominated not by bustling scientists, but by quietly humming machines (figure
47). As one staff scientist noted on a tour of the campus, this didn’t quite carry the “wow factor” it
might have in previous years. The spectacle of genomics was being replaced by the quiet automation
of its machinery.




196 | P a g e
Figure 47. The Open Lab at Sanger. Photograph taken by the author. 19 Dec 25.


197 | P a g e
Values around data, in particular, were a key distinguishing feature of genomic research at Sanger.
Data acted as both an interface and a boundary: central to the Institute’s function, yet not shared
evenly or freely. As one researcher explained,

       “The other challenges are...Well, working with pharmaceutical companies, they are not
       academic environments, so they do not share information freely and easily. So there are
       challenges in navigating the space...”

       Senior Scientist, Translational Informatics.

Sanger positioned itself as a leader in open science, but the benefits of open-source models were not
uniformly experienced as one group leader remarked.

       “Open source models tend to serve institutions that already have access and power.”

       Group Leader, Infectious Disease.

In theory, open science promised ethical transparency and democratised access; in practice, it often
reinforced existing inequalities.

Sanger, described by some as an academic department, resembled in many ways the polished
campuses of private genomic firms I had visited elsewhere. A strong sense of identity came through
in discussions but also in anecdotal conversations I had on campus. A campus carefully branded,
from signage to sculpture, with minimal intrusion from outside narratives. This sense of coherence,
while admirable in some ways, also served as a visual and spatial confirmation of privilege of being
shielded from the frictions, inequities, and messiness that define many other scientific and social
spaces.

Belonging, Visibility, and Identity on Campus

The team I was part of, which had a public engagement and ethical remit, was frequently referred to
in vague terms, ‘they do the ethics,’ I often heard when being introduced to someone new, as if our
work were niche or ancillary. And yet, we were involved in the visible performance of openness,
welcoming visitors, narrating the institution’s mission, and offering glimpses behind the glass. These
curated experiences, while well-intentioned, often raised questions about who the science was really
for, and what kind of visibility was being offered.

The Black scientists’ group on campus was in its second generation. It had originally formed as a
network specifically for Black scientists, but according to one computational scientist, amid internal
concerns that the group might be too exclusionary, it became broader, the Race Equality Network.
Even within this expanded framework, many of the members, predominantly African-born scientists,
198 | P a g e
still expressed a need for a space to share experiences of migration, race, and professional life. While
the Race Equality Network remained, and the informal Black scientists’ group emerged, socialising
around WhatsApp, lunches, and potlucks. I was invited to both groups during my time and observed
tensions between the institutional framing of itself and these organic, unofficial affinity spaces. Often,
perspectives that never surfaced during my interviews with senior staff.

For example, I attended a panel discussion on International Women’s Day where a question from the
floor asked about the availability of safe spaces at Sanger. A Black woman scientist spoke candidly
about the limitations of these spaces and how race continued to impact how minoritised scientists
expressed themselves. Later, she shared personal stories of microaggressions, stories that echoed
those I was hearing from others during my fieldwork.

From the outset of my project, it was scientists who were women who engaged with me most
frequently and openly on EDI topics. At Race Equality Network events, they were often the dominant
voices, both in the audience and on the panels. Two senior women were regularly called upon to
speak or chair, a sign of both their leadership and the gendered labour in holding space for equity
work. “The usual suspects,” one Group Leader, (Developmental Biology) remarked , while EDI was
increasingly part of the institutional discourse, its practice was uneven, and power remained
concentrated e leadership, highlighting the limited change in who held authority and made decisions.

As the chapter unfolds, I turn to a series of visualisations to further illustrate the genomic ecosystem
as experienced by the students and researchers at Sanger. These images help capture the
complexity of a system in which ethical considerations are inextricably intertwined with scientific
outputs. They reveal the spaces where values align and where they clash and offer novel ways to
reflect on how scientists can be supported when their work lives within such nuanced ethical terrain.




7.3 Mapping the Genomic Ecosystem: A Sanger Perspective

This section presents visualisations that offer a situated and strategic view of Sanger’s position within
a complex and evolving genomic research ecosystem a

s experience by those within in. Each visual draws on data generated through ethnographic fieldwork,
interviews with researchers, and attendance at relevant public and private genomic events. They
provide a means of making visible the relationships, dependencies, and infrastructures that sustain,
enable, and constrain genomic research at scale.

These visual tools are not intended to function as static depictions or universal frameworks. Rather,
they reflect the contingent, embedded, and interpretive nature of system-mapping itself. They are

199 | P a g e
partial but revealing, offering a lens through which to trace flows of data, funding, influence, and
ethical oversight across the many institutional boundaries that make up this ecosystem. Most
importantly, they help surface the themes of power, autonomy, and alignment that will be explored in
greater depth in the following section, which is part of the complex way that Sanger works.

      “When people move from, you know, doing everything over here to everything over there,
      but actually science is in the middle. It's always that sort of middle ground where you
      work together to achieve a goal. There are some things that definitely you can only do
      there, or you can only do here, practically speaking, and because that's the right thing to
      do. But for most large questions, it's about how you work together, and then it's around,
      as you say, that opportunity for benefit.”

      Group Leader, Discovery Science B.

      “…we are a consortium that works with pharmaceutical companies to work closely with
      them to understand sort of the limitations from their side in the context of translating
      genetics to drug discovery and also from our side…to understand how we can impact the
      decision-making based on the science that we do in here.”

      Senior Scientist, Translational Informatics.

These quotations, taken from interviews with researchers at the Sanger articulate the lived
experience of working within a deeply interdependent ecosystem. They underscore the
Institute’s self-understanding as neither fully autonomous nor embedded in a hierarchy of
external actors, but rather situated in a “middle ground” where value is co-produced through
collaboration, negotiation, and shared purpose.

The ecosystem table (Table 5) sets out the institutional architecture that supports genomic research
as a system. The examples provided are grounded in empirical accounts from scientists, and reflect
the kinds of partnerships, dependencies, and obligations that routinely shape research activity. They
do not aim to be exhaustive, but identify the major categories of organisations such as funders, policy
bodies, healthcare providers, and research infrastructure. Understanding these actors and their roles
is crucial to the further discussions in this chapter.

Table 6 describes the interfaces through which they interact. These interfaces include mechanisms
such as data-sharing agreements, regulatory processes, funding allocations, collaborative platforms,
and translational pathways.

These tables outline the genomic research ecosystem surrounding Sanger. Table 5 identifies the
main actors, clinical, institutional, and infrastructural, while Table 6 illustrates the interfaces that
connect them through data sharing, governance, funding, and collaboration. Viewed jointly, they
reveal a dynamic, interdependent system where roles overlap and boundaries blur. This ecosystem
operates through negotiated relationships that enable coordination, innovation, and accountability.
These tables therefore set the foundation for the next section, which visualises these connections in
motion through a system map of flows, interactions, and relationships across the genomic landscape.




7.3.1   System Map: The Genomic Ecosystem in Motion

The system map (Figure 48) provides a graphical and conceptual representation of the direct and
indirect relationships that define the genomic ecosystem surrounding Sanger. The map uses single
connectors to trace the multidirectional flows of data, funding, expertise, regulation, governance, and
clinical influence, offering a spatial language for understanding how knowledge is co-produced,
translated, and circulated across institutional boundaries. Importantly, these connectors do not all
represent the same kind of relationship: some indicate material or interactional exchange, while
others denote normative and regulatory obligations.

The map was developed as an analytic tool to interpret qualitative data generated through interviews,
participant observation, and documentary analysis. Using iterative coding, I traced how researchers,
clinicians, institutional actors, and governance bodies described their relationships, dependencies,
and points of influence within the genomic ecosystem. The diagram does not claim to reproduce the
system as it exists in a formal or organisational sense. Rather, its structure reflects how participants
themselves articulated the world they work within, including which actors were described as central,
peripheral, constraining, or enabling. The hierarchical layout is therefore inferred from patterns in
participants’ accounts and my ethnographic observations, particularly the ways in which authority,
responsibility, and decision-making were consistently described as flowing through certain institutions
and committees more than others.




206 | P a g e
 Figure 48: Sanger's Ecosystem Map. Analytic map of the genomic
 ecosystem surrounding the Wellcome Sanger Institute, based on
 qualitative coding of interviews and observations. Produced by the
 author.




207 | P a g e
Sanger sits at the core of the map (Figure 49). Although connected with other similar
institutes, these appear just beyond the frame for clarity; in practice, they form part of the
broader cluster of research life surrounding Sanger.




Figure 49

Within this cluster, scientific practice is rooted in the generation, analysis, and interpretation
of genomic data, as discussed in the previous chapter. The map illustrates how these
practices are sustained through networks of collaboration with both public and private
partners.

Communication flows through this system in multiple ways, via publications (both
professional and public, including social media), scientific conferences, training exchanges,
and the mobility of individual researchers between institutions (Figure 50).
Figure 50

This communicative ecosystem, represented by the blue boxes, enables the diffusion not
only of scientific knowledge but also of shared norms, values, and expectations regarding
what constitutes good research.
