       “I mean, not global, but we need to communicate our work to other people in order to
       have it sort of recognised, or to share the information, share the knowledge, share
       the insights. And ultimately, that's what you need to do to get your PhD, to get your
       postdoc, to get your job. Once you've got your faculty position, you need to keep
       doing that to maintain your job.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

Conferences are key sites of value negotiation, where topics are not only shared but also
platformed, deliberately positioned as worthy of attention. Scientists are encouraged to
participate not merely to exchange findings but to advance their careers, as the following
examples show. During one interview, a young scientist knocked on the door to speak with
my interviewee; when the scientist left, and our conversation resumed, the participant
remarked that this interruption perfectly illustrated a point she was coincidentally making,
that effective communication and presentation are essential skills. While another participant
organised regular group meetings, some online and some in an open, light-filled room above



209 | P a g e
the café, where early-career researchers practised presentations and received feedback
from peers and more senior researchers.

Conferences also represented a site of commercial engagement. Private and industry
stakeholders were highly visible, with researchers frequently approached by sales
representatives promoting the latest assays or sequencing technologies. For many discovery
scientists, relationships with industry were more direct and familiar than those with patients
or the wider public. This familiarity carried an implicit understanding of industry’s motivations:

       “When you work with the private sector, they are rarely driven by equity, right? It’s
       mainly for-profit organisations. So I think more often than not it is about that.”

       Senior Scientist, Translational Informatics.

Top-down relationships in the system map, shown by directional arrows, illustrate belonging
and ownership, highlighting how influence is understood to operate across layers of the
ecosystem. The diagram is broadly hierarchical, with governance and funding positioned
above the research environment. Professional bodies provide guidance and policy, but
participants did not describe them as prescriptive or dominant. Instead, they are experienced
as part of the culture of interaction between researchers, clinicians, and industry partners.

       “I think having that senior buy-in has certainly had an influence—it influences the
       funding. It may not be obvious, but the funding comes from government and
       philanthropic sources. Wellcome is philanthropic, UKRI would be government, NIH,
       you know, and so on.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

Governance and regulatory authority are enacted through government departments, legal
frameworks, and professional bodies, indicated by colour yellow in the figure. Despite their
formal authority, these structures often appeared distant from the day-to-day reality of
laboratory work. Scientists perceived their operations as organic, practice-based, and
voluntary, perhaps reflecting the layered nature of accountability rather than an absence of
constraint. Misconduct, for instance, was viewed as something that would need to escalate
to before prompting formal intervention.

Funding occupies a central, intermediary position between the clinical interface and
research. The system depends on these streams to function, whether channelled directly
into research institutes or mediated through public healthcare systems.




210 | P a g e
        “And it's a challenge to get funding. For scientists in Asia, Africa, Latin America, it’s
        much harder than for us here. CZI has made a first step, though there were criticisms
        of those funding mechanisms. Now Wellcome is—I'm actually meeting someone from
        Wellcome on Wednesday—interested in [Africa funding]. So, that will change things.
        The funding landscape is shifting.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

The clinical interface emerges as a vital site of exchange, providing both the biological
material and data that drive genomic research and the route through which outcomes return
to patient care. It thus acts as both an input and an output channel, shaping the ecosystem’s
translational capacity.

        “We'll always go back and check that... often we aren't the consent holder; the
        consent holders are the people we're collaborating with. So that link with the UK and
        international network of [clinicians] is absolutely vital for my team.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

        “We collaborate with clinicians and with bioresources that collect—they enrol patients
        and get samples in. Then we generate the GWAS or exome sequence data on tens
        of thousands of patients, which we ourselves haven’t collected.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science.

Yet this translation is often experienced as slow and indirect, contributing to a sense of
separation between scientific intention (“science for society”) and tangible impact. While
researchers express a strong commitment to public benefit, the path from discovery to
application remains long and fragmented, shaped by multiple layers of policy, infrastructure,
and context. The direct connection between patients and ethics committees in the map
reflects not a relationship of interaction or access, but a relationship of institutional
obligation, in which ethics review bodies are constituted to act in the interests of research
participants, often in their absence.

Ideas, meanwhile, circulate far more freely than outcomes. Conceptual frameworks,
population categories, and scientific narratives move rapidly through the system, while
practical benefits advance more slowly, constrained by procedural complexity. The
ecosystem feels culturally cohesive yet institutionally diverse, bound by shared scientific
norms expressed through publications, public discourse, and networks that bridge
organisations and disciplines.


211 | P a g e
Governance structures are often perceived as sitting apart from daily scientific life Figure 51,
yet their influence is profound. Through legislation, professional codes, and ethics oversight,
they define both the limits and methods of research, exerting subtle but far-reaching control.

       “So, you're caught between funders’ objectives, initiatives, strategy, science, and to
       some extent, personal interests. I think it's naive to believe people do things they're
       not interested in. They just don't.”

       Group Leader, Discovery Science.




       Figure 51

Funding, too, was described as an essential but strategically detached (Figure 51) force
shaping priorities from a distance rather than through everyday engagement.

       “In funding organisations, you have officers on the ground who have some influence
       on calls and programmes, but they need buy-in from their bosses. It's like anything in
       life.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

       “The actual physical location of sequencing becomes a practical solution to delivering
       the grant. We focus on capacity building, sharing knowledge, and working together.
       The funders cared only about where sequencing was done, not the bigger picture.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

The clinical interface, then, acts as both source and sink, providing the biological materials
and data that fuel discovery while absorbing research outputs back into clinical and policy




212 | P a g e
settings. Through this cyclical exchange between laboratory and clinic, the genomic
ecosystem sustains itself, continually translating between knowledge and care.

       “If we do something that severs that collaborative network, then we've got no access
       to patients. We've got no samples. We've got nothing. So we wouldn't want to do
       anything that in any way caused any ethical issues for the consent holders. We
       wouldn't want to do anything for the patients that was unethical with their data.”

       Programme lead, Discovery science B.

Within this interpretive framework, patients appear at the periphery of the diagram (figure 52)
yet remain symbolically elevated through direct normative connections to clinical providers
and ethics boards.




Figure 52

This positioning reflects a recurring duality in the empirical material. Patients were frequently
described as essential to the legitimacy, justification, and ethical grounding of genomic



213 | P a g e
research, yet were rarely represented as active participants in the core workings of research
design, data governance, or classificatory decision making. Their peripheral placement is
therefore not a claim about importance, but an analytic reflection of limited inclusion in
everyday institutional processes. This tension is telling of the epistemological gap identified
across the thesis: those whose data and bodies underpin genomic research are central to its
moral claims, yet marginal to its knowledge-making practices. It is within this gap that
questions of power and justice for this group emerge most sharply, revealing how protection,
representation, and exclusion can coexist within the same governance structures.

These visualisations provide a framework for understanding genomic research not as a
series of isolated endeavours, but as a system of structured interdependence. Sanger does
not merely operate within this ecosystem; it actively shapes and is shaped by it. Funding
flows, policy structures, clinical partnerships, and regulatory bodies all play a role in defining
what research is possible, permissible, and prioritised.

The system is neither fully open nor closed it is porous, contested, and subject to continual
renegotiation. While certain structures (such as governance or funding) exert power from a
distance, others (like the clinical interface or collaborative consortia) are sites of active
participation and co-production. These dynamics set the stage for the next section, which will
explore how themes of power and autonomy emerge from this complex and layered system.




7.4 Acts of Power and Autonomy: Visualising Institutional Influence


This section reflects on how power and autonomy operate across institutional domains at
Sanger, using a series of diagrams to support an ethnographically grounded narrative.
Drawing on in-depth fieldwork, these visuals do not aim to produce definitive or
generalisable claims; rather, they offer an interpretive lens on the relational dynamics of
institutional influence and independence.

The section how capacities such as accessing data, training researchers, and acting
ethically are configured in terms of both the influence the Institute holds (power) and the
degree to which it can act independently (autonomy). It then considers how Sanger
navigates these domains in response to, or in tension with, broader commitments to social
justice.

As the ecosystem analysis showed, interdependence is central: other institutions and actors
hold their own power and autonomy. There are situations where others are directly affected


214 | P a g e
by Sanger’s exercise of power or autonomy, and others where Sanger is shaped by theirs.
From themes raised in the previous chapter, several acts of power and autonomy recurred
across interviews. Their frequency and placement made them stand out as significant, both
for the influence they exert and for how they are themselves influenced especially when read
alongside the connections and authority mapped in the ecosystem.




7.4.1   Power and Autonomy in the Domains of Institutional Activity



This section examines how institutional power and autonomy are configured across Sanger’s
domains of activity, and how these configurations shift in practice. Building on the ecosystem
analysis, I use fieldwork-informed diagrams to offer an interpretive account of how influence
and independence are experienced, negotiated, and constrained. It maps power and
autonomy across eight key types of activity revealed through the system map of Sanger,
showing where authority is concentrated and where discretion is limited.

Across these eight domains, Sanger’s positioning is uneven: high power does not always
coincide with high autonomy, and divergence often signals sites of negotiation, dependency,
or indirect influence. Where the two dimensions align, the Institute appears embedded and
confident; where they part company, agency becomes more contingent and relational.

Domain 1: Funding issues Research (Power: Low | Autonomy: High)

Sanger holds influence over internal funding allocations, yet its external power in this domain
is comparatively limited by the priorities and decisions of major funders. While the Institute
remains a powerful scientific actor and exercises some influence beyond its boundaries, its
capacity to shape the overall funding landscape is weaker relative to the degree of autonomy
it holds over internal resource distribution. From interviews, it was clear that although Sanger
can decide how to allocate resources strategically, these choices operate within structural
dependencies that constrain what can be funded, for how long, and at what scale.

        “We’re very fortunate that the Chan Zuckerberg Initiative is a foundation that does
        seem to have some awareness and invests in that. Because, you know, for science,
        what you need is the backing from the funders.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.




215 | P a g e
       “So what it means is that what we have to—or are funded to—do, or supposed to do,
       and nothing else. That comes from the funding intention from Wellcome, which puts a
       hundred million pounds a year to the head of the director—one person.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

Domain 2: Prioritising Agendas (Power: Medium | Autonomy: Medium)

In this domain, power and autonomy are closely interdependent. Sanger’s ability to prioritise
research issues has grown alongside its technical capacity, scientific standing, and
collaborative networks. Interview accounts emphasised that shifts in institutional focus
emerged through interpersonal decisions and evolving scientific opportunity, rather than
through linear direction from funders.

Sanger neither fully determines research priorities nor simply responds to external agendas.
The medium scores reflect this reciprocal configuration, in which influence is real but
relational, and autonomy is meaningful yet shaped by wider scientific, funding, and policy
environments.

Through publications, conferences, and training, Sanger exerts wide influence on scientific
thinking. I observed that while researchers enjoy freedom to develop and communicate new
ideas, broader intellectual control remains mediated by journals, funders, and the hierarchies
of global science. Training emerged as a key vector of influence: a subtle, ongoing
transmission of epistemic norms that shape how genomic science is done worldwide

       “We try to harness the [research] community,,. It’s about helping answer scientific
       questions through our tools and data.”

       Director, Informatics.

While Sanger's reach and impact are a source of pride, assessing them was not
straightforward.

       “Assessing our impact is tricky, but we know we have thousands of users of our
       platform and genetics portal.”

       Director, Informatics.

Domain 3: Data Sharing (Power: High | Autonomy: High)

Sanger’s leadership in genomic data infrastructure gives it strong influence over how
genomic data are generated, curated, and made accessible. From its early role in shaping



216 | P a g e
open data norms, , open sharing has been embedded as an institutional culture rather than
experienced as an external imposition. During my fieldwork, open data was consistently
described as both a moral commitment and a strategic orientation, reflecting an internalised
governance logic rather than compliance with external mandate.

Power and autonomy therefore align in this domain. While Sanger operates within global
data-sharing expectations, it retains significant discretion over how these principles are
enacted in practice, granting it unusual influence over a shared global resource.

       “Sanger is completely different from other institutes or universities… historically it’s
       been all about generating genomic resources—genome projects—for the community,
       and that’s what it is—nothing else.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

       “We used to say it was like working in a fishbowl—clear glass—part of our mandate
       was to make sure some of the benefits of Sanger were accessible to others.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

Domain 4: Access to Patient Samples and Data (Power: Low | Autonomy: High)

Access to patient samples and clinical data depends on collaboration, trust, and ethical
approval, forms of relational rather than formal power. Sanger’s autonomy lies in how it
builds, sustains, and sometimes withdraws from these partnerships, even as external
governance structures limit direct control over access. Researchers were acutely aware of
their dependence on clinical partners, describing these relationships as simultaneously
enabling and constraining.

       “You have to go through the data access committee… the data doesn’t all come from
       one source—it might be twenty different sources. You’ve got to go through twenty
       different data access committees. It’s a faff, but not impossible… eventually, you will
       get the data.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.

Some participants also reflected on the legacy of unethical data practices in global health
research, particularly in African contexts, shaping current caution and procedural oversight.

       “I was shocked by the strong feelings about the way science had been done in
       Africa—people came, did genetic profiling… what they called ‘helicopter science’.”




217 | P a g e
        Senior Scientist, Translational Informatics.

Access, then, is not purely technical but deeply ethical, dependent on legitimacy and
institutional accountability.

Domain 5: Working with collaborators (Power: Medium | Autonomy: High)

Collaboration is central to Sanger’s work. In academic settings, the Institute often leads
through expertise, infrastructure, and prestige.

        “We built an international consortium—started with a London meeting of about a
        hundred scientists, convened a committee, and grew from there.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science

Yet in partnerships with government or industry, its influence is mediated by competing
priorities. From my observations, collaboration at Sanger is both a form of collective
creativity and a terrain of subtle hierarchy.

        “It is a blocker, right, so geopolitical stigmatising of disease and geopolitics, closing
        borders and preventing access is the biggest problem we have.”

        Group Leader, Infectious Biology.

Sanger scientists have considerable discretion in initiating collaborations, particularly in
discovery science where regulatory constraints are lighter. I found this autonomy often relies
on personal initiative and trust networks, especially in global partnerships.

Domain 6: Regulating Ethical Behaviour (Power: Low | Autonomy: Medium)

Ethical practice at Sanger operates within national and international frameworks, but daily
interpretation happens locally. I found that researchers often talk about ethics as culture
rather than rule, something performed through example and mentorship.

        “We teach them [PhDs and Postdocs] to make good figures and write good papers.
        We also create an environment where how you do science—and how we talk about
        science—is as important as the science itself.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

Sanger’s governance systems formalise this to an extent, but the real power lies in shared
norms. Behaving ethically emerges as a set of everyday internal cultural practices, distinct




218 | P a g e
but related to ethical regulation and oversight mechanisms that formally govern those
practices.

Sanger’s compliance systems are well established, but the space for reinterpretation is
limited. I found that while researchers appreciate internal discretion, they also acknowledge
that external frameworks ultimately set the boundaries. Ethical freedom here means finding
room for judgement within established rules.

       “You don’t tell your children they can do anything—like jump on the settee. You have
       to set some sort of ground rules.”

       Group Leader, Developmental Biology.

Ethical behaviour is sustained through trust, mentoring, and social norms rather than rigid
codes. However, autonomy is limited because external regulation and funding priorities
shape what “ethical” means in practice. I observed tension between individual integrity and
institutional constraint, especially when funders’ procedural requirements clashed with local
ethical reasoning.

       “Funding agencies are blind to local requirements. Worst case, they drive malpractice
       by prioritising permissions over proper processes… people do the wrong things due
       to grant constraints.”

       Group Leader, Infectious Disease.

Domain 7: Training the Workforce (Power: High | Autonomy: High)

Training appeared as one of Sanger’s most profound forms of influence. It shapes both skills
and values, embedding a distinctive approach to data, ethics, and collaboration.

       “Training at Sanger looks good on a CV—you can get strong publications and move
       to the next stage of your career.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

Training emerged as both a gift and a projection of institutional identity, a way of exporting
Sanger’s methods and worldview.

       “It’s a place to undertake training, grow a network, and bring those elements
       together—data, function, interactions with patients and a big clinical community.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science A.




219 | P a g e
       “It’s very international at the PhD and postdoc level—people come for training from
       all over the world… there’s now more diversity among British researchers, which is a
       good thing.”

        Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

Domain 8: Ways of Working with Multidimensional Data (Power: High | Autonomy:
Medium)

Sanger’s methodological choices are internally governed. Its power lies in technical mastery;
autonomy is mediated by collective norms and funder expectations. I reflected that limits
here are less about capacity than institutional will, and the willingness to challenge
established paradigms.

       “It’s on institutions, and then on society… institutions need to reflect what they extract
       from wider society, and how they use that to shape the research and training
       environment.”

       Programme Lead, Discovery Science B.

These practices are not simply displays of power or assertions of autonomy. They are
relational, causes and consequences of Sanger’s position within a dense ecosystem of
partners, funders, and publics. Given the recurring themes of power and powerlessness in
this thesis, these acts stand out as the most revealing: moments where values, constraints,
and institutional capacity visibly meet.

As Figure 53 summarises how power and autonomy are distributed across 8 domains of
institutional activity. What emerges is a differentiated landscape: while some domains are
marked by both high power and high autonomy, such as training the workforce or data
sharing, others reveal mismatches between influence and independence.




220 | P a g e
             RELATIONAL UNDERSTANDING OF POWER AND
                AUTONOMY IN KEY GENOMIC RESEACH
             BEHAVIOURS AT WELLCOME SANGER INSTIUTE
                                               Power Score        Autonomy score


 WAYS OF WORKING WITH MULTIDIMENSIONAL DATA                                   3                          2
                          TRAINING THE WORKFORCE                          3                          3
                   REGULATING ETHICAL BEHAVIOUR                   1                          2
                    WORKING WITH COLLABORATORS                        2                          3
             ACCESS TO PATIENT SAMPLES AND DATA               1                          3
                                       DATA SHARING                       3                          3
                              PRIORITISING AGENDAS                        2                          2
                                    FUNDING ISSUES            1                          3




Figure 53: Relational understanding of power and autonomy in genomic research
behaviours at the Wellcome Sanger Institute. Bar diagram showing score for low power
or autonomy is 1, medium scores are 3 and higher scores are 5.

The domain of Funding Research is characterised by relatively low power but high
autonomy. While Sanger cannot directly control the broader funding landscape or dictate its
funders' strategic priorities, it retains flexibility in how those priorities are interpreted and
implemented internally. This autonomy manifests in decisions about which projects to
pursue, how to distribute internal resources, and how to align infrastructural commitments
with broader scientific trends.

The domain of regulating ethical behaviour, meanwhile, holds low power but medium
autonomy, indicating that while ethical practices are sustained through cultural norms,
professional expectations, and collective identity, researchers’ capacity to redefine or act
independently of external ethical frameworks remains limited. In other words, Sanger can to
some extent govern its own conduct but cannot unilaterally determine what counts as ethical
across the broader research environment. Neither does it hold any regulating behaviour over
other organisations passed its collaborative agreements.

Data Sharing emerges as a domain of high power and autonomy, illustrating Sanger’s
leadership in open data initiatives and infrastructural development. A balance of power and
autonomy in prioritising agendas reflect the moderate freedom to innovate and communicate
within and outside of Sanger, their ideas shape broader scientific agendas yet depends on




221 | P a g e
external dynamics such as editorial gatekeeping, institutional hierarchies, and the politics of
academic prestige.

What becomes visible in this comparative view is the unevenness of Sanger’s institutional
positioning. High power is not always matched by high autonomy, and vice versa. Where the
two align, the Institute appears confident and embedded in its operations. Where they
diverge, more precarious forms of institutional agency emerge, spaces where discretion
must be negotiated, and influence is exercised indirectly or through relational means.

These diagrams do not prescribe how power or autonomy ought to function. Instead, they
invite reflection on the multiple ways authority is exercised, independence cultivated, and
collaboration navigated across a scientific institution.

The final section now turns outward: how Sanger scientists understand power and autonomy
in relation to other actors when three key ethical questions are examined. Building on the
experiential, communicative dynamics traced here, the analysis considers using this deep
institutional understanding around categorisation and data.




7.4.2   Power, Autonomy, and Ethical Orientations in the Genomic Ecosystem


By taking the emergence of power and autonomy as key indicators of institutional agency
and the themes raised in Chapters 5 and 6, this section examines how these configurations
shift when the ethical orientation of research changes. It focuses on the questions explored
in this thesis by orienting around three approaches: improving ancestral data diversity,
reforming categorisation practices and positional social justice as a core research area.

Drawing on a the definition of social justice presented earlier by analysing how Sanger
navigates these domains, this section aims to reveal how institutional power and autonomy
are mobilised in response to, or in tension with, broader commitments to social justice.




7.4.2.1 Improving Ancestral Data Diversity


When discussing improving ancestral data, the interviews made clear that researchers felt
held to the power of others for the funding, time, and expertise needed to address data




222 | P a g e
diversity. Government remained a visible enabler, reflecting the political sensitivities of
international collaboration and the need for public-sector anchoring (see section 6.1).

The power they held was relational, shifting with partners and generally increased when they
became part of consortia or initiatives. By contrast, autonomy to join or convene groups was
seen more positively: collaborations were usually described as matters of timing, trust, and
opportunity. Finding the “right people” was organic and often opportunistic rather than
centrally directed (see section 6.2 Choosing a team).

Participation at this scale requires expertise and institutional backing that are unevenly
distributed across global genomics. Inclusion, therefore, depends on the redistribution of
power and privilege as much as on welcoming language; funders and governance bodies
often sit above consortia in making this real. Projects centred on ancestral diversity were,
unsurprisingly, international in scope, often involving industry alongside academic partners.
Interviewees highlighted a partial narrowing of the distance between sectors when the
shared aim of diversifying data was explicit (See section 6.1 Open sources and equal
benefits).

Crucially, improving data diversity is costly, financially and institutionally. Support is
necessary for infrastructure, governance, and local leadership, particularly in African
contexts. Even where initial sample banking is funded, analytical capacity often lags,
illustrating the gap between aspiration and capacity.

In this landscape, power over diversity efforts is dispersed and negotiated, distributed across
a web of funders, governments, and research institutions. Autonomy, meanwhile, resides in
the capacity of scientists and organisations to convene, align, and persist within systems that
are, by design, uneven, demanding collaboration, compromise, and strategic adaptation
rather than unilateral action.




7.4.2.2 Reforming Categorisation Practices

Sanger scientists’ accounts describe an increase in institutional power, reflecting their
technical authority and influence in shaping genomic standards, but a decrease in autonomy,
given the broader social, ethical, and regulatory constraints surrounding population
classification (see section 6.3).

Labels serve important scientific functions for example, stratifying data, detecting
associations, and ensuring reproducibility but they also operate within a larger ecosystem of



223 | P a g e
expectations (see section5.3). Funders, journals, policymakers, and public audiences all look
to these categories to signify something beyond the science itself. As a result, the act of
categorisation becomes both a technical task and a social negotiation, one that continually
exposes the boundaries of scientific autonomy.

At Sanger, researchers expressed a strong sense of ownership over their methods,
confident in their ability to justify the categories they used. Yet this autonomy did not fully
extend beyond the scientific domain. Within the wider ecosystem, social framings of race,
ethnicity, and ancestry remain deeply embedded in institutional practice. Scientists
recognised the enduring power of society, through policy, regulation, and public discourse
(see section 5.3.3), to shape how these categories are understood, which in turn constrains
how science can be conducted.

Scientific power in categorisation is tempered by ethical constraint, and how autonomy in
method collides with responsibility in meaning-making. This tension provides the bridge to
the next section, which explores how these forces play out when researchers attempt to
implement social justice as a core research aim, not merely as an ethical obligation, but as a
defining orientation for genomic science itself.




7.4.2.3 Positioning Social Justice as a Core Research Aim


The final configuration, brings together the previous analyses of data diversity and
categorisation, turning attention to how values such as equity and fairness are
operationalised within the institutional life. Across interviews, many researchers expressed a
genuine desire for social justice to shape their scientific legacy. Yet, this aspiration was
tempered by a recurring concern: that such commitments risk becoming performative rather
than transformative (see section 6.3).

Crucially, this gap was not framed as deliberate resistance but as a result of structural and
cultural inertia. Scientists recognised that Sanger, like many research organisations,
struggles to translate its aspirational goals into embedded practice.

Rather than cynicism, this was voiced as a call for institutional alignment: to ensure that
values such as equity and inclusion are not simply symbolic but built into decision-making
structures, funding mechanisms, and scientific priorities. The pattern observed in the
power/autonomy maps reinforces this: Sanger’s sense of power remains high, but its
autonomy in pursuing social justice appears lower than in other domains. Despite this,


224 | P a g e
researchers still perceive themselves as culturally influential, able to shape thinking and
practices beyond their own walls, even when internal change feels constrained.

Ethical formation and research culture are intertwined: social justice is not only a research
goal but a pedagogical one. It is enacted through mentorship, training, and the subtle
transmission of values within teams (see section 6.1 Social and ethical concerns). This
observation situates social justice as structurally contingent, dependent on how
collaboration, funding, and accountability are designed.

While Sanger retains significant autonomy compared to commercial entities (6.3), it operates
within economic and strategic hierarchies that shape what can be prioritised. For example,
Sanger’s centralised funding model, and where significant financial authority is vested in a
single director.

At the same time, several interviews illustrated how institutional power and autonomy can be
harnessed to drive reform and collaboration. The building of international scientific consortia,
initiated and coordinated from within Sanger, is key to its operations, and one they believe
can be embedded with equity at its core (1.2.5, 6.2). Such collaboration can demonstrate
how Sanger’s prestige and infrastructure can amplify global capacity-building. This form of
power, mobilised through cooperation rather than control, embodies a different expression of
autonomy: one grounded in credibility, reputation, and the ability to convene others.




7.5 Final Thoughts

This chapter has shown that power within the genomic ecosystem is relational and diffuse,
circulating through expertise, networks, and legitimacy rather than through simple hierarchy.
Autonomy, in turn, is made and remade through practice, through the everyday acts of
negotiation, discretion, and alignment that enable research to continue. These processes
reveal not only how institutions act, but how they are themselves shaped by the moral,
political, and scientific environments they inhabit.

The discussion that follows builds on these insights to consider what they reveal about
responsibility, justice, and the redistribution of power in contemporary genomics. It turns
from the description of institutional life to a critical interpretation of how structures of authority
might be reconfigured, asking how an institution such as Sanger can align its considerable
influence with its ethical ambitions, embedding social justice not as a peripheral ideal but as
a central principle of scientific practice.



225 | P a g e
226 | P a g e
8    Discussion: Power as the Foundation of Justice in
    Genomics


8.1 Introduction


In this chapter, using the institutional practices and settings at Wellcome Sanger Institute, I
bring together a final analysis of how race, ethnicity and ancestry (REA) are conceptualised
and operationalised within contemporary genomic research. I argue that questions of justice
in genomics are fundamentally questions about power. They concern who can define
populations, who is rendered visible in data, whose knowledge is treated as credible, and
whose interests are ultimately served by how genomic science is organised.

In my analysis of the literature and practice, the hope for more equitable research has
increasingly been premised on the need for better and more diverse data. Responses have
often focused on quantitative measures of diversity, carrying an assumption that more data
will produce better outcomes. Yet, as I have shown throughout this thesis, the persistent
discourse of the underrepresentation of the world’s most genetically diverse populations is
not merely a technical problem of missing data. Rather, it reflects deeper and uneven
operations of power in how populations are categorised, measured, and understood. The
widening gap between European and non-European samples is tied to classificatory
practices that reproduce racialised hierarchies, to methodological decisions that favour
simplicity over complexity, and to institutional structures that reward particular kinds of
evidence and expertise.

My ethnographic engagement revealed that diversity initiatives at Sanger, although
prominent in institutional rhetoric, often leave intact the underlying epistemic and structural
conditions that produced inequity in the first place. Scientists expressed a desire to improve
ancestral representation and to move away from race as a biological category, yet their
practices remained tethered to inherited labels, Eurocentric reference datasets and funding
models that prioritise statistically efficient designs over justice-oriented ones. Drawing on
Critical Race Theory, the analysis traced how race operates as a social construct that is
institutionally embedded and frequently made invisible through the language of ancestry and
genetic diversity. Critical Realism provided a way to distinguish between the empirical




227 | P a g e
expressions of these dynamics, the regularities they produce, and the deeper mechanisms
that sustain them.

The findings demonstrate that avoiding racial terminology in genomics does not eliminate
racialised thinking. It tends instead to shift it into less visible forms, for example, through the
use of continental ancestry categories, geopolitical labels, and notions of genetic similarity
that are presented as neutral. Through CRT and CR, my analysis reveals that these notions
are in fact structured by colonial histories and the normativity of whiteness. Genetic ancestry
is often promoted as a scientifically robust alternative to race, yet its deployment through
broad and homogenising labels often echoes past inequities. With these approaches,
technical fixes are offered, but standards such as thresholds for variant frequency or
definitions of adequate sample size are not only statistical choices. They are decisions
shaped by who is included in reference datasets and who is absent from them.

This chapter is framed within an epistemology of social justice that understands a reliance
on objectivity and neutrality as insufficient safeguards against the reproduction of racialised
hierarchies.

In the sections that follow, I synthesise the findings of this thesis through three interrelated
modes of power: conceptual, methodological, and structural. I begin by examining
conceptual power, showing how race and ethnicity persist through classificatory practices
that shape meaning, visibility, and legitimacy in genomic science. I then turn to
methodological power, analysing how analytic standards, data infrastructures, and research
designs concentrate authority and constrain ethical possibilities. Lastly, structural power
situates these practices within institutional, funding, and regulatory arrangements that
organise which kinds of genomics are possible. Throughout the chapter, I draw on the lived
experiences of scientists and my own positionality to illuminate how these forms of power
are encountered, negotiated, and sometimes resisted in practice.

However, as justice in genomics requires active engagement with the histories, power
relations and social structures that shape knowledge production, I call for a fundamental re-
examination of how populations are defined, analysed and represented. I argue that
questions relating to social justice are not supplementary ethical questions but central
scientific problems. I therefore conclude the chapter by considering how power might be
reconfigured to pursue justice in genomics and introduce the JUST DATA framework.




8.2 Conceptual Power



228 | P a g e
Conceptual power of science operates through the categories, descriptors, and interpretive
logics used to describe human variation. It is exercised in what becomes thinkable and
sayable about difference, and in how race endures in genomic practice even when formally
rejected, risking sustaining long-standing inequities for racialised and underserved groups
under the guise of neutrality.

In the sections that follow, I trace how race is reintroduced through classificatory routines,
managed through discomfort and avoidance, and made visible through lived experience and
the ethical tensions that accompany categorisation.




8.2.1   Reintroducing Race in Genomic Practice


Ethnographic fieldwork conducted at Sanger illustrates how race and ethnicity are subtly yet
persistently reintroduced across multiple stages of the genomic research process, revealing
the contours of a deeply embedded system that requires both critical scrutiny and intentional
disruption.

The classification and labelling of population groups within genomic studies is far from
standardised. Methodologies for determining groupings vary, and the labels applied, whether
based on geographic origin, ethnicity, or race, are inconsistently used. When researchers do
not establish labels themselves, they often adopt those used by external datasets such as
the UK Biobank, 23andMe, the 1000 Genomes Project, the Office for National Statistics, or
NHS records (Aung et al., 2023; Malawsky et al., 2023; Thygesen et al., 2023). This
inconsistency underscores the complexities and contradictions inherent in defining race and
ethnicity within genomic research.

Against this background, categories persist not because they offer empirical insight into
human genetic variation, but because they serve socio-political functions. In the British
context, race is a product of intersecting histories of empire, migration, institutionalisation,
and resistance (chapter 1.2). These interwoven histories are often not considered in
genomic research in the UK which is essential if we are to dismantle the structures that
perpetuate racial inequality today.

Science has at times positioned itself as capable of distilling and interpreting the meaning of
racial categories that have been socially constructed (Lala & Feldman, 2024; Smedley &
Smedley, 2005; Stuurman, 2000). Despite consequential questions of the place of racial
constructs in genomics, they sometimes continue through genuine curiosity, and at other
times through institutional arrogance. The International HapMap Project offers a clear


229 | P a g e
example of how race can become embedded in the architecture of genomic research, even
when not explicitly invoked. As Ossorio and Duster, (2005) warned,

“the [hapmap] Project could have pernicious effects merely by studying genetic variation in
human subject groups that evoke racial categories, regardless of the intentions of the
researchers” (Ossorio & Duster, 2000 p. 119).

The contemporary elusiveness of race is sometimes taken as evidence of its conceptual
instability, a rationale used to justify its removal from the scientific domain and its relegation
to the social (Rutherford, 2020). As chapter 2.3.1 outlines, the attempted displacement of
race as a biological concept has a long history within genetics (Timmer, 2023). From
Lewontin, (1972) to Rutherford, (2020), a broad scientific consensus affirms that race has no
biological basis. Studies of human genetic variation have consistently undermined the idea
of discrete racial categories, rejecting such classifications as scientifically unsound (1000
Genomes Project Consortium et al., 2015; Collins, 2004; Venter J. Craig et al., 2001; Yudell
et al., 2016)

Yet the very instability of race as a biological concept, and the long history of attempts to
distance science from it, have not eradicated its presence. Instead, these attempts have
produced a conceptual terrain marked by ambiguity and ambivalence . It is within this terrain
that discomfort, avoidance, and selective erasure emerge, dynamics that shape how
scientists talk about race, how they classify populations, and how they navigate the political
and ethical stakes of their work. In fact ethnographic work by Fujimura & Rajagopalan,
(2011) illustrates how genomic scientists have navigated these tensions, often engaging with
racial categories while attempting to distance themselves from their social and historical
weight.




8.2.2     Discomfort and Avoidance


Through this research, race was regularly cast as a social problem to be managed rather
than a construct continually reproduced through research practices, categorisations, and
data infrastructures. Conversations with scientists were often complex and contradictory. At
times, I struggled to accept their inability, or perhaps their reluctance, to acknowledge the
limitations of their frameworks. Yet I found myself extending to many of them a degree of
empathy they did not always extend to themselves, not least because it was clear that they
were negotiating ambiguities they had not been trained to confront.




230 | P a g e
Public engagement work was conducted alongside these interviews. Participation in public-
facing events on genetics and ethics, facilitating deliberations with patient and public groups,
and collaborating with community projects was a crucial counterpoint (Appendix C). It helped
stabilise the critical distance necessary for analysis while revealing how closely the
uncertainties voiced by scientists mirrored those circulating in wider society. Although
scientists often positioned themselves as in service to the public, the questions they
struggled with about race and identity exposed how deeply embedded these classifications
are in everyday life (chapter 5.2.3).

Across the interviews and as captured in Chapter 5.2: Power in communication, discussions
of race were frequently avoided or tightly circumscribed by institutional unease. There was a
strong sense that even broaching the topic risked legitimising divisive or regressive
ideologies. This discomfort reflected a broader academic tendency to sidestep or suppress
racial discourse entirely.

This evasion was not confined to interpersonal dynamics. It was also evident in institutional
practices. Some participants described receiving direct instructions to remove the word
“race” from their work, even when they themselves were uncertain about its relevance or
meaning.

Critical Race Theory identifies this reluctance as colour-blindness, a professed ignorance of
race that, in practice, functions as a method of upholding systemic racism (Bonilla-Silva,
2022; Leonardo, 2009). The scientific erasure of race in language, while retaining its
operational function through ancestry and genetic proxies, thus represents less a break from
historical forms of racialisation than a strategic reconfiguration of them.

Despite these attempts to avoid the term, the power of race as a lived social construct
continually challenged such evasions. As the next sections illustrate, neither scientists nor
the public nor I, as a researcher embedded in these interactions, could fully escape the force
of racialised experience. This confrontation with lived reality not only exposes the limits of
avoidance but also underscores why engaging critically with race remains essential to any
meaningful analysis of genomic practice.




8.2.3   Lived Experience as Legitimate Analytic Frames


Though I did not expect geneticists to offer the moral clarity on issues of race and identity
that eludes wider society, it became clear that the power of racial concepts was both deep
and inescapable within the scientific space. Lived experience provides an essential


231 | P a g e
counterpoint to scientific abstraction. The meanings and consequences of racial
classification are not only conceptual matters within genomics but are lived, negotiated and
felt by those who inhabit scientific spaces. My fieldwork, therefore, required attending not
only to what scientists said about race, but to how race shaped the everyday conditions in
which they and I operated.

My own positionality was never peripheral; it shaped the questions I was able to ask, the
responses I received and the meanings I drew from the silences and deflections that arose.
There was, it seemed, an emotional texture to the work because of this. I was not merely
observing dynamics of racialisation; I was implicated in them.

Racialisation was experienced unevenly among scientists, shaped by nationality and migrant
status. While some peers only encountered racialisation after coming to the UK, my
upbringing as a second-generation immigrant meant that racialised identity was already an
established feature of my personal and professional life.

These tension-laden perspectives reinforced the necessity of holding academic analysis
alongside the lived realities of race in the UK. Racial categories therefore continue to
permeate social life, and scientific discourse neither escapes nor resolves this. Instead, it
reflects and refracts these wider forces, much like patterns observed in genetic data mirror
the social and historical conditions that shape them.

My research underscored the importance of embedding minoritised perspectives within
genomic science not as symbolic gestures of inclusion, but as intellectual and ethical
contributions that challenge, unsettle and broaden the conceptual frameworks through which
the field understands human difference.

Lived experience of myself and others reveals that race is not an abstract concept operating
elsewhere, but a force that actively shapes scientific encounters, assumptions and
interpretive habits. Recognising this is crucial for interpreting how the logics of race
materialise in the categories and population descriptors used within genomic research.




8.2.4   Persistence of Racial Logics in Genomic Categories


The empirical material revealed that racial logics did not simply linger in genomics; they were
continually regenerated through everyday classificatory routines, publication practices and
inherited data structures. What follows traces how these mechanisms operated in practice,
showing how race persisted.



232 | P a g e
As section 2.2.1 discusses religion, and Christianity in particular, played a central role in
shaping racial ideologies. Despite considerable diversity across colonised populations,
Europeans constructed a racial hierarchy in which they positioned themselves as the apex of
civilisation. This worldview was normalised through political power, missionary activity, and
assumed intellectual authority (J. K. Carter, 2008; Cox, 2002; Ekechi, 1971; Goetz, 2016;
Harris, 2004; L. Young, 2009). It is within this ideological lineage that the ethical validity of
data collected from such historically embedded power imbalances is questioned.

An analysis of the digital discourse in journal publications circulated on campus during the
period of this study revealed a persistent entanglement of racial and ethnic labels within
genomic publications. These categories were frequently used as non-genetic descriptors for
population groups. As Chapter 4 highlighted, the use of familiar racial terminology, such as
Black and White, was widespread. While some researchers employed these terms to
indicate ethnicity, others acknowledged their racial connotations. White was frequently
associated with individuals of European descent, often labelled as White British or similar
designations tied to geographical origin.

Ethnicity was frequently seen as connected to, and therefore problematised with, race, not
necessarily as a dimension of human variation but as a term that evokes discomfort and
unintended associations. This suggests that while these categories are analytically
convenient, they are also deeply fraught, laden with connotations that undermine their
scientific neutrality.

The rejection of race as a scientifically valid category was juxtaposed with the largely
uncontested use of concepts such as genetic ancestry and genetic similarity. While these
are defined as dimensions distinct in scale, resolution, and analytical purpose from the ideas
of race and often ethnicity, their deployment through broad continental groupings
undermined this distinction.

The endurance of geo-political labels to describe genetic ancestry and similarity is not
necessarily because they are epistemologically sound, but because they offer
methodological expediency, an expediency that comes at the expense of both ethical
integrity and epistemic robustness. As a result, race persists not always in language or as a
coherent biological reality but as a constellation of practices, assumptions, and expectations
embedded within the architecture of genomic research. As shown in the earlier ethnographic
literature reviewed even when scientists avoid explicit racial terminology, the distinction
between race and ancestry frequently collapses in practice (see section 2.7 and 5.5.4).
These analyses of precision medicine projects show how these “slippages” are embedded in




233 | P a g e
data structures and analytic routines, revealing the depth at which racial logics persist.
(Bentz et al., 2024; Gutin, 2019).

Scholars such as Leonardo, (2009) and Bonilla-Silva, (2022) argue that whiteness operates
as an invisible yet normative force in knowledge production, dictating the standards for what
is considered valid, credible, or even perceptible within academic and scientific contexts. In
genomic science, this normativity is encoded in the tools, categories, and methodological
priorities that dominate the field. They are presented as innocuous means of grouping
populations, yet they resonate uncomfortably to me with historical legacies of empire,
eugenics, and scientific racism.

At Sanger conversations, especially those regarding data quality, statistical power, and
publication potential, were frequently anchored in the use of European reference data. This
data was sometimes considered standard or “clean,” and at other times “dominant” and
“well-powered”, operating as a silent benchmark, reinforcing the racialised norms embedded
in genomic methodologies. To interrogate these practices is not to disparage science, but to
strengthen it. Exposing how whiteness and power operate through scientific classification
enables us to reimagine a genomics rooted in reflexivity, justice, and epistemic humility; one
that actively questions the boundaries it draws, rather than reifying them

Researchers frequently negotiated this tension in both formal and informal discourses. On
one hand, there was an uncritical comfort with the use of continental ancestry groupings as
descriptors of human populations. At the same time, some participants expressed frustration
with narrow conceptions of diversity. Notably, however, this viewpoint often emphasised that
non-genetic determinants of health, such as environmental factors, were omitted from the
current discourse on diversity. Criticisms of concepts of diversity did not address how even
existing models of genetic variation themselves perpetuate reductive understandings of
human difference.

Even where scholars such as Rebbeck et al. (2022) argue that “inferred ancestry must be
distinguished from race” (p. 890), acknowledging that the latter is historically tied to social
hierarchies of power and privilege, they frequently stop short of proposing truly alternative
frameworks for representing genetic diversity. Similarly, there was little discussion of
improved methodologies for studying human variation that move beyond existing
categorisation, falling short of calls from Lewis et al. (2022) to “use concepts and tools that
acknowledge that ancestry is continuous ” (p. 251).

These patterns, as revealed through my ethnographic and discursive analysis, show how
genomic categories continued to draw upon and reproduce racial logics even when framed
by participants as scientific, neutral, or purely methodological. What my findings expose are


234 | P a g e
not isolated slips but systemic tendencies. They raise difficult questions about responsibility,
the limits of scientific neutrality, and the ethical vocabularies researchers mobilise to account
for their decisions.

8.2.5   Ethics, responsibility and communication


A recurring pattern across this research was that the language of ‘ethics’ appeared
frequently in institutional and interpersonal discourse, yet its practical application was
uneven, contingent, and often overshadowed by methodological and structural demands.

Beauchamp and Childress’s principles of biomedical ethics (Beauchamp and Childress,
2019) are intended to function collectively as a safeguard for ethical practice. In the context
of this study, concerns about maleficence centred on the potential harm caused by
misclassification or reductionist groupings. In contrast, beneficence was often invoked to
justify categorisation as a means of discovering group-specific insights or improving clinical
outcomes. Autonomy appeared largely in symbolic terms; participants were sometimes led to
believe they had influence over their classifications, even though, in practice, groupings were
typically predefined by researchers. The principle of justice, concerned with equitable
access, representation, and outcomes, was frequently cited but seldom operationalised in
any substantive way. Emanuel et al., (2000) Outline seven requirements for evaluating the
ethics of clinical research, two of which are particularly justice-oriented: fair subject selection
and a favourable balance between risks, benefits, and the distribution of research gains.
These justice-oriented principles require researchers to avoid exploiting vulnerable groups,
to ensure that the burdens and benefits of participation are equitably distributed, and to
justify inclusion on scientific rather than structural grounds. In genomic research, however,
these ideals are often balanced by pragmatic priorities such as statistical power, data
availability, and institutional expectations.

These dynamics resonate with Reardon’s analysis of how genomics and ethics intersect,
where normative commitments to justice and accountability are continually shaped and
constrained by the organisational and epistemic structures of the field (Reardon, 2009,
2017). My findings extend this critique by showing how ethical principles do not operate as
stable or abstract ideals. Instead, they are negotiated, diluted, or sidelined in the routine
classificatory and analytical work of genomic practice. In this sense, the postgenomic
condition that Reardon describes is lived as much as theorised, manifest in the technical
practices that subtly reproduce racial logics while claiming to move beyond them. It is at this
point that my ethnographic material diverges from a description of the condition itself,
illustrating how such ethical tensions materialise within everyday scientific practice.



235 | P a g e
In practice, bioethical and clinical research principles were routinely invoked in institutional
rhetoric; they were refracted through the pragmatic imperatives of genomic research:
statistical power, data standardisation, and publishing pressures. Rather than providing a
coherent ethical architecture, they often operated unevenly and tactically, aligning with
institutional priorities and output-driven goals. This pattern, evident across my fieldwork,
demonstrates how ethical decision-making becomes treated as contingent yet quickly
overridden by the methodological and structural demands of contemporary genomics.

How scientists describe race, ancestry, and ethnicity matters, not only to research
participants but also to public interpretation. Interviews for this study revealed that
researchers’ discomfort with race is often framed around fear of public backlash rather than
critical engagement with its scientific use (chapter 5.3.3). Such anxieties, while
understandable, also point to a deeper institutional avoidance. Race remains both
conceptually fraught and politically charged. Yet ignoring it altogether does not neutralise its
impact; in fact, it may inadvertently entrench existing inequities by allowing them to go
unchallenged (Bonilla-Silva, 2022; Gutin, 2019; Leonardo, 2009).

Some formal guidance exists for the use of race, ethnicity, and ancestry in research (Khan et
al., 2022; National Academies of Sciences, 2023). Khan et al. (2022) In their guidance,
underscore the far-reaching consequences of these terminological choices not only for the
internal validity of research, but also for how findings are communicated to clinicians,
policymakers, and the public. They caution that without clear, context-sensitive definitions
and frameworks for interpreting population descriptors, genomic science can unintentionally
perpetuate misunderstanding and inequity. It has also been suggested that more precise,
critically informed terminology could mitigate the risk of misinterpretation and prevent the
repurposing of genomic findings in ways that reinforce health inequalities (Panofsky & Bliss,
2017; Yudell et al., 2016). Further, as discussed in Section 5.3.3 and Chapter 6,
engagement with existing guidance and resources was often limited by ignorance or
unfamiliarity, as well as by the pressures of getting science done and a sense that such work
fell outside participants’ perceived roles (Samuel, 2024).

My engagement, however, highlights that addressing the justification of choices in
communication requires more than careful word choice; it demands sustained scrutiny of
how racialised categories are constructed, by whom, and with what authority. Such
interrogation exposes not only the ethical obligations attached to naming but also the
unequal power to define, delimit, and normalise population categories, a dynamic that
becomes even more pronounced in the methodological architectures examined in the
following section.



236 | P a g e
8.3 Methodological Power


Population categories make comparison and analysis possible, but they also simplify
diversity, produce inclusion and exclusion, and carry ethical consequences for
representation. While scientists often acknowledged these tensions, responsibility for their
effects was frequently displaced. For marginalised populations in particular, these
methodological practices show how power operates through research methods, shaping
both scientific outcomes and the meanings attached to human difference.

As section 5.3.2 explores, scientific classification influences the equity of genomic research
outcomes. This influence operates not only through direct outputs such as disease
associations or pharmacogenomic insights, but also through less visible processes including
the formation of inclusion and exclusion criteria, the ethical tensions surrounding data
diversity, and the prioritisation of analytical clarity over social complexity.

Despite increasing recognition of the need for diversity, European populations remain
disproportionately overrepresented in genomic research (Bentley et al., 2017; Corpas, et al.,
2025). Problems of mislabelling and limited harmonisation across datasets also endure in
the field (Corpas, et al., 2025), raising concerns about data stewardship and epistemic
responsibility. These structural conditions formed the backdrop against which the
significance of the methodological decisions examined in this study emerged.

The categorisation of human groups in genomic research is a site of methodological
necessity and ethical tension. These processes employed are not neutral; they reflect a
stratified hierarchy of acceptability that intersects with broader systems of power and
privilege.

Scientific classification in this context becomes a form of boundary work. This concept,
introduced by Gieryn (1983), refers to the discursive strategies used by scientists to
delineate what counts as legitimate science and who qualifies as authoritative. Boundary
work maintains intellectual demarcations that separate science from non-science, often in
ways that protect the autonomy and prestige of the scientific field.

Star & Griesemer (1989) notion of boundary objects complements this understanding by
describing categories or tools flexible enough to be interpreted across different social worlds
yet stable enough to retain coherence within scientific institutions. Scientific categories are




237 | P a g e
therefore not neutral descriptors but sites where authority, meaning, and inclusion are
negotiated.

In genomics, boundary work is particularly visible in decisions about how to classify human
populations, whether through ethnicity, genetic ancestry, or genetic similarity (see section
4.2). Scientists often seek to separate scientific classifications from their social and political
histories, even when those classifications draw upon earlier racialised schemes (Schramm &
Rottenburg, 2012)

Framing “race” as socially constructed can be used to bracket off problematic social usages
while leaving genetic population categories intact, a move mirroring the dynamics of
boundary work (Gieryn, 1983; Schramm & Rottenburg, 2012). My own deep reading of
scientific texts, institutional documents, and interview accounts demonstrates how such
rhetorical manoeuvres permeate routine classificatory practice and governance.

Classification here, therefore, reflects boundary work in action (see section 5.3). It stabilises
scientific authority by positioning population groupings as biologically meaningful even when
they align with non-biological usage or politically loaded categories. Genetic ancestry, in
particular, functions as a boundary object: mutable enough to operate across varied settings
yet presented as an objective and apolitical measure (see section 5.2.2).

As Epstein (2007) and Fujimura & Rajagopalan (2011) note, the naming and framing of
population categories in genomics both reflect and reproduce existing racial and social
logics. These decisions are not merely technical; they are acts of epistemic authority. By
choosing which forms of difference to foreground and which to occlude, researchers
construct and legitimise certain groupings while rendering others invisible.

8.3.1   Techniques of Categorisation


A longstanding critique highlights that continental ancestry categories oversimplify genetic
diversity and reproduce racial and colonial assumptions (Ali-Khan et al., 2011; Fujimura &
Rajagopalan, 2011; Tishkoff & Kidd, 2004). These conventions persist within genomic
workflows, reinforcing the historical legacies outlined earlier and shaping the methodological
terrain encountered in my fieldwork.

A clearer picture emerges when examining how these systems operate in practice.
Clustering techniques underpin the scientific conceptualisation of human variation, yet their
outputs are routinely assigned geopolitical labels. As shown in section 4.3, publications
overwhelmingly employ continental categories such as European, African, East Asian, South



238 | P a g e
Asian, West Asian, Oceanian, and American, often described as “major” or “super
populations”. These labels appear statistical but rest on long-standing political and
geographic assumptions.

Techniques such as PCA can inadvertently reproduce ethnic or racial groupings. As
discussed in Chapter 4, PCA is ostensibly used to detect and correct for population
structure, yet in practice, it is also used to infer or assign ancestry. Computational clustering,
therefore, becomes a proxy for categorising samples into groups that map closely onto
socially recognisable labels. Methods framed as neutral corrections thus contribute to the
reification of distinctions that carry deep social meaning.

Although some participants described PCA as “traditional”, suggesting it may eventually be
superseded, it remained central in explicit and implicit discussions of how groups are made
and labelled. Alternative methods do exist, including those that model complex ancestral
dynamics or rely on allelic distributions rather than predefined population labels. Techniques
such as ancestral recombination graphs (ARGs), discussed in section 2.5.2, and newer
approaches aimed at removing “crude” labels were cited as innovations, yet they appeared
only marginally in the everyday discourse analysed in Chapter 4 and were mentioned only
briefly and implicitly across discussions of scientific practice, power and methods analysed
extensively in Chapters 5 and 6. This uneven uptake indicates that while alternatives are
available, the techniques that become routine are shaped by constraints, expectations, and
institutional norms (Dauda et al., 2023). As this research demonstrated, choices are always
made in the context of data shaped by social, political, and historical forces.

For instance, thresholds such as the five per cent minor allele frequency (MAF),
used to define “common” variants, exemplify how seemingly technical decisions can
entrench unequal knowledge production. Largely derived from European samples,
these thresholds have become de facto standards that marginalise variants relevant
to underrepresented groups (Fatumo et al., 2022; Martin et al., 2019). This exclusion
constitutes a form of epistemic injustice, privileging Eurocentric paradigms while
rendering other forms of variation illegible (Popejoy & Fullerton, 2016; Sirugo et al.,
2019).

Against the backdrop of persistent European overrepresentation in genomic datasets, what
is at stake is not simply numerical imbalance but the power relations that shape how
genomic knowledge is produced and legitimised. Dataset composition influences
interpretability, accuracy, and the visibility of different populations in downstream analyses,
reinforcing the methodological asymmetries outlined above.


239 | P a g e
Study recruitment practices further rely on categorical impositions. Predefined labels, which
are not always statistical in origin, determine eligibility and representation (see section 4.2).
Attempts to diversify genomic research hinge on representational inclusion, yet such
inclusion is constrained by the categories through which it is operationalised (see section
5.3.2). Researchers frequently adopt labels provided by earlier teams, thereby inheriting
prior classificatory decisions.

These labels shape what becomes scientifically visible. They influence research questions,
the interpretation of findings, and the translation of genomic research into clinical and public
health contexts (Popejoy & Fullerton, 2016; Sirugo et al., 2019). Structural constraints
deepen these issues. As shown in section 6.1, well-resourced UK cohorts emerged from
sustained investment, whereas comparable infrastructures are often absent in settings with
higher proportions of non-European ancestry populations. Collaborations beyond
established cohorts, therefore, require funding mechanisms not well supported by current
grant schemes.

Scientists are thus working with data already shaped by exclusions. Low representation
reflects not only statistical underpowering but also the exclusionary nature of recruitment
strategies and labelling conventions. As a result, “diverse” datasets may still carry
overlooked biases arising from the omission of individuals who do not fit established
categories.




8.3.2   EDI: Exclusion, Data Practices, and Inequity


The continued use of inherited population and classificatory systems reflect long-standing
structural and epistemic investments. While they may enable statistical analysis, they also
obscure lived realities and reinforce reductive or harmful narratives (Morning, 2011;
Panofsky & Bliss, 2017). Recruitment protocols, dataset composition, and visualisation tools
manifest downstream in recognisable patterns. Variants once classified as pathogenic are
reinterpreted when more diverse reference data become available. Polygenic risk scores
underperform for individuals whose ancestry is poorly represented, and gene discovery
remains concentrated within heavily sequenced populations. What counts as sufficient, rare,
or significant is shaped by who is included in reference datasets and who is absent.
Technical conventions concerning sample size and representativeness are therefore
inseparable from the social and historical conditions structuring genomic evidence.




240 | P a g e
Datasets may appear inclusive while concealing patterned exclusions. Examining
provenance, recruitment strategies, and labelling conventions, along with transparent
reporting of limitations, is essential. In some contexts, degrees of missingness or imposed
homogeneity should be evaluated as indicators of structural exclusion or empirical traces of
marginalisation rather than neutral absences.

In section 6.2, researchers reflected on the assumption that open-source resources
automatically translate into equitable access. Recognition that open data infrastructures can
reproduce inequities unless supported by meaningful engagement, resourcing, and
equitable governance again illustrates how routine and even assumed ethical practices
shape patterns of inclusion and exclusion.

The pursuit of improved scientific accuracy aligns with the ethical imperative to include
underrepresented populations and reveals what I term methodological interest convergence.
Correcting biases becomes necessary not only for equity but also for scientific optimisation.
Gains for marginalised groups thus emerge not in opposition to institutional priorities but
because achieving those priorities requires greater inclusion.

Methodological routines underpinning genomic research are deeply entangled with
classification, representation, and access. These practices stabilise inherited assumptions
about human difference and reproduce longstanding inequities, even when framed as
neutral or technically necessary. Efforts to refine categorisation and broaden representation
reveal how scientific optimisation and equity concerns increasingly converge, yet these gains
occur within infrastructures shaped by asymmetries of power, resources, and authority.

The production of genomic knowledge becomes inseparable from the structural conditions
governing who is included, how categories are defined, and whose perspectives shape the
interpretation of variation.




8.4 Structural Power

This interrogation did not stop at the level of categories and concepts. To understand why
racialised practices persist in genomic science, it was necessary to move from individual
decision-making to the institutional and structural contexts that enable or constrain more
equitable research. Scientific knowledge is not produced in a vacuum; it emerges within an
ecosystem shaped by funding structures, institutional hierarchies, peer review protocols,
publication incentives, and national and global policy agendas. These forces do not simply




241 | P a g e
influence research priorities; they condition which questions are asked, who asks them, and
whose interests are ultimately served.

Critical realism offered a particularly generative framework for understanding this ecosystem,
urging attention not only to what is visible or measurable (the empirical), but also to the
systemic enablers and constraints (the actual and the real) that underlie those empirical
manifestations. Diversity and inclusion were widely acknowledged as important, yet
researchers felt constrained by structural pressures to publish quickly, secure competitive
grants, and adhere to conventional markers of rigour.

As discussed in Chapter 7, and as visualised in the system map (Figure 48), the centre of
genomic research at Sanger comprised scientific practices focused on data generation,
analysis, and interpretation. These were embedded within broader collaborative networks
that included public and private-sector partners. Encircling this core were multiple layers of
structural influence, including academic collaborators, clinical institutions, funding bodies,
regulators, policy makers, and the public. Constraints were therefore actively maintained
through institutional practices around funding, publishing, and ethical oversight that tended
to favour technical efficiency over structural reflexivity.




8.4.1   Infrastructures of Legitimacy: Journals, Descriptors, and Institutional
        Hierarchies

One important dimension of structural power was the continued reliance on Eurocentric
reference datasets, whose exclusionary effects are increasingly documented (Fatumo et al.,
2022). Researchers who sought to expand the representativeness of their studies often
encountered formidable barriers, including restricted access to diverse samples and complex
legal and ethical protocols (see Chapter 6).

The choices scientists made about population descriptors are shaped not only by personal
ideology but also by broader structural forces, including editorial expectations and
disciplinary conventions (A. Lewis et al., 2022; Mauro et al., 2022; Sankar et al., 2015). Khan
et al. (2022) similarly observe that guidance often stops short of reshaping the incentives
that govern day-to-day research practice. Early in my PhD, I engaged directly with these
debates by attending the first public National Academies of Sciences Engineering and
Medicine (NASEM) meeting. In addition, when I later facilitated a workshop on equity in data
with an international group of scientists at Sanger (see section 6:1). A conversation with one
of the NASEM report editors highlighted a central issue: the guidelines being developed



242 | P a g e
were deeply influenced by US-specific classificatory histories, and there was genuine
uncertainty about how well these frameworks would travel beyond a Euro-American context.
This UK-based case highlights how ostensibly global guidance interacts with distinct national
histories of race and governance.

This encounter echoed a point raised in the literature review: while debate about population
descriptors in genomics is global (Popejoy et al., 2018) the literature remains
overwhelmingly Euro-American in scope. Perspectives from outside these regions and from
scholars working within alternative classificatory systems are strikingly underrepresented, a
pattern itself a product of structural exclusion.

Publishing norms emerged as a key site where scientific value, career progression, and
