structural power intersected. Decisions made by journals about what to publish and where
have profound implications for researchers’ trajectories, funding prospects, and institutional
standing. Editorial practices often prioritised methodological standardisation and statistical
robustness, leaving limited space for reflexivity, equity, or critical engagement with race and
power. Initiatives such as the Journal of the American Medical Association (JAMA) editorial
on the reporting of race and ethnicity seek to impose clearer standards, yet also reveal how
inconsistent practices remain across journals (Flanagin et al., 2021).

Journals, as gatekeepers of scientific legitimacy, can either passively reflect existing norms
or work to reshape them, for example by establishing enforceable guidelines, requiring
transparency about population descriptors, and promoting interdisciplinary scholarship that
centres equity and justice (Khan et al., 2022; National Academies of Sciences, 2023). As my
analysis of the genomic ecosystem showed, institutional power and autonomy are not
always aligned, and where they diverge, important tensions emerge in how collaborative
science is negotiated and enacted (see Chapter 7).




8.4.2   Funding and Policy

While funders often espouse values of diversity and inclusion, their stated commitments are
not always matched by infrastructural support. Interviews repeatedly highlighted funding
structures as a central barrier. Scientists described the difficulty of pursuing data diversity
and equity-centred research within grant programmes not designed to support such goals
(see section 6.1). Building large, well-characterised cohorts in the United Kingdom, such as
UK Biobank, has required sustained and substantial investment. Comparable infrastructure
was often absent for non-European ancestry populations or was located in settings with far



243 | P a g e
fewer resources. Efforts to establish collaborations or collect samples from
underrepresented populations thus required funding models that current schemes were
poorly equipped to provide.

Some funders, particularly newer philanthropic actors such as the Chan Zuckerberg
Initiative, were described as rare sources of support for data diversity and inclusion-oriented
agendas, for example through calls explicitly focused on ancestries underrepresented in
genomic research (see section 6.1). Yet such initiatives remained the exception rather than
the norm. Many researchers were motivated by a desire to produce inclusive and ethical
science, but found themselves constrained by funding models and institutional logics that
rewarded other priorities. The disjuncture between personal commitment and structural
constraint reinforced the sense that researchers were navigating an ecosystem shaped more
by inertia than by vision.

Regulatory frameworks added another layer of complexity (see sections 7.2.1 and 7.2.3).
Oversight was exercised through a diffuse assemblage of government bodies, ethics
committees, and professional associations. Researchers often experienced these systems
as informal and distant, rooted in tacit practices rather than clearly articulated mechanisms.
This did not necessarily signal a lack of regulation, but rather the layered and opaque nature
of accountability. Critically, many ethical review processes prioritised issues such as consent
and privacy while sidelining questions of justice (see section 6.1). Although these
dimensions are vital, they do not address the structural and historical inequalities that shape
research participation and vulnerability. Initiatives that centred community engagement,
transparent data governance, and critical pedagogy were present, albeit unevenly. Given its
stature and resources, the Sanger Institute has both the capacity and the responsibility to
lead in this space.

While Sanger cannot directly reshape the global funding environment or dictate funder
priorities, it does retain a degree of institutional flexibility in how those priorities are
interpreted and operationalised. This autonomy is expressed in decisions about which
projects to prioritise, how internal resources are allocated, and how institutional
infrastructures align with broader scientific and societal trajectories (see section 7.2.2). As a
globally respected hub for genomic research, Sanger occupies a uniquely influential position:
it can help redefine what constitutes excellence in genomics, broadening this to include
reflexivity, inclusivity, and accountability.

My research intersected with the Institute’s obligations, driven by funder mandates, to
develop policies on data diversity (see section 1.2.5 and 6.3). Yet I had limited engagement



244 | P a g e
with how institutional culture would need to change for such policies to be effective. Despite
this, my PhD was at times framed as a lever for transformation (Appendix G1 an G2). As a
consequence of my embeddedness in the field, I discovered, sometimes indirectly, that my
work was referenced in internal meetings as part of the Institute's endeavours to improve
data diversity. As my work unfolded, this offered less reassurance than expected, because it
exposed both the weight of expectation placed on the project and the degree to which the
scale and complexity of the issue were underestimated.

I also found myself invited into decision-making spaces as a presumed diversity and equity
expert. Although I brought substantial knowledge from my work as a genetic counsellor and
engaged in equity work in clinical genetics, I did not possess the same depth of technical
expertise as many of the scientists around me. At times, the expectation that I would resolve
these dilemmas felt less like collaboration and more like a subtle deflection of responsibility.
While often couched in respect, these moments suggested a quiet displacement of
discomfort onto my role.

My empathy for colleagues enabled me to recognise this vulnerability. Attending both to their
human limitations and to the structural constraints they navigated opened space to imagine
how social justice might function meaningfully within the genomic ecosystem, not simply as
an external critique but as a practice embedded in its structures. The pursuit of benevolent
outcomes in science is therefore not merely a matter of good intentions. It requires alignment
between individual commitments, institutional infrastructures, and policy frameworks.




8.4.3   Access, Power, and Data Solidarity

As touched on in discussing methodological power practices of data sharing are often
presented as inherently beneficial, yet they are deeply entangled with structural power. At
institutions like Sanger, open-source data sharing remained a foundational principle (Ross-
Hellauer et al., 2020), but inequities in access persisted. Researchers based in well-
resourced institutions were far more likely to possess the computational capacity, training,
and infrastructural support needed to navigate and exploit large genomic datasets. Those
without such resources were effectively excluded from full participation in genomic research
(Bezuidenhout et al., 2017).

As discussed in Chapter 6, scientists observed that only a relatively small group of highly
trained individuals with significant computing power and coding expertise could use large-
scale open data in practice. For many others, especially those working in under-resourced



245 | P a g e
settings, such data remained technically “open” yet materially out of reach. These comments
point to a profound ethical challenge: data access is not only a technical matter but a
question of justice, closely tied to legacies of colonial science, ongoing economic inequality,
and the concentration of intellectual capital in

The ability to collect, store, govern, and openly share genomic data is itself an act of power,
one that remains heavily concentrated in well-funded institutions in the high-income
countries. These institutions not only control technical infrastructures but also shape the
standards, categories, and narratives through which genomic data become legible and
legitimate. In this sense, the global distribution of data does not reflect shared ownership or
democratic participation, but rather the continuation of extractive and centralised models of
knowledge production.

Barbara Prainsack’s concept of data solidarity is particularly relevant here. In the later stages
of this research, a meeting with Prainsack helped to crystallise and extend reflections
already emerging from my fieldwork about how data, benefit, and justice are linked
(Prainsack et al., 2022). Data solidarity shifts attention away from questions solely of data
ownership towards the uses to which data are put and who benefits from those uses.
Applied to genomic diversity, this perspective challenges the assumption that increasing the
diversity of datasets automatically leads to more equitable outcomes. If data from
underrepresented communities are incorporated into global resources without meaningful
benefit returning to those communities, then inclusion risks reproducing familiar extractive
logics.

Ethical practice, viewed through data solidarity, requires more than broadening participation.
It demands using insights gained from data to inform more just methodological and
governance decisions: for instance, developing better classification systems, deliberately
oversampling marginalised populations where appropriate, or choosing not to sample certain
groups where risks or histories of harm make participation ethically fraught.

Thus, the pursuit of data diversity in genomics cannot be reduced to encouraging
researchers to recruit “more diverse” samples or to deposit data in open repositories. It must
confront deeper systemic questions about power, history, and structural inequality that
shape which forms of knowledge are produced, by whom, and for whose benefit (Benjamin,
2019).

True collaboration and participation must be grounded in autonomy and reciprocity rather
than institutional benevolence. If genomic research is to be ethically legitimate, it must



246 | P a g e
recognise and actively challenge the structures of power. Without these transformations,
efforts towards inclusion risk entrenching inequality, reaffirming rather than disrupting
dominant logics of scientific authority.




8.4.4   Power, Positionality, and the Case for a Justice Framework

The preceding sections demonstrated how classificatory practices, methodological choices,
and institutional norms shape the production of genomic knowledge. Yet understanding
these dynamics requires recognising the broader conditions under which scientists work.
Questions of inclusion and justice cannot be examined solely in relation to datasets or
methodological standards. They are also anchored in the relational and organisational
dynamics that structure genomic research. Power circulates through institutions as much as
through algorithms, and positionality influences not only who is present, but whose
perspectives are legitimised, whose concerns are amplified, and whose absences are
normalised. This section, therefore, brings these dynamics together to show why a more
explicit justice framework is required.

Diversity and equity emerged not as discrete themes but as threads running through
classification practices, methodological choices, and institutional structures. Sanger was
frequently described by interviewees as a diverse space. Yet this narrative did not align
straightforwardly with my own experience as a Black researcher, often one of very few Black
people in genomic settings. Diversity appeared concentrated in junior, training, and
administrative roles, with leadership remaining predominantly White and male.

These dynamics were not incidental. They reflected what this research has shown more
broadly: that race and power are not confined to categories within datasets, but are
embedded in institutional culture, collaboration patterns, and leadership structures.
Whiteness operated as an unmarked norm, shaping which questions were prioritised, which
populations were centred, and which ethical concerns were considered legitimate. At the
same time, discourses of diversity and equity in genomics frequently blurred the distinction
between visible diversity in teams and deeper structural change. Data diversity and
workforce diversity were sometimes treated as interchangeable achievements, creating a
false sense of progress even where systemic inequalities in access, authorship, and benefit
sharing persisted.

Questions of power and autonomy were also visible in how scientists navigated classification
and ethics. Researchers had considerable influence over how categories were applied in


247 | P a g e
practice, yet their capacity to disrupt inherited racial or ethnic labels was constrained by
publishing norms, editorial expectations, and ambiguous guidance on population descriptors.
Similarly, while ethical approval and informed consent were routinely invoked, these were
often treated as procedural checkpoints rather than occasions for deeper reflection on
structural inequality, reciprocity, or benefit sharing. In practice, scientists operated within
systems that positioned ethics and diversity-related concerns as adjacent to, rather than
constitutive of, rigorous science.

Power in genomics is relational and layered. It is exercised through institutional hierarchies,
funding models, publishing infrastructures, and everyday interpersonal dynamics, as well as
through classificatory schemes and technical standards. Autonomy is similarly constrained:
researchers may wish to challenge racialised logics, but their efforts are shaped by
incentives that prioritise speed, scale, and conventional markers of impact over reflexivity,
equity, or redistribution.

It is within this context that the need for a more explicit Genomic Social Justice Framework
becomes clear. I therefore introduce the JUST DATA framework as a justice-oriented model
for rethinking the design, interpretation, and governance of genomic research. Building on
Critical Race Theory, decolonial thought, and Critical Realism, the framework places ethics,
power, and participation at the centre of genomic practice. It is intended to complement
existing guidance by offering researchers and institutions concrete ways to embed equity
and accountability throughout the research lifecycle, treating social justice not as a
peripheral ideal but as a core condition of scientific integrity.




248 | P a g e
     9    Operationalising Justice from the Start: The JUST
                         DATA Framework
9.1 Introduction

Tracing the journey of human data, and the ways in which it has been systematically shaped
and narrowed, makes clear that methodological and statistical practices cannot be separated
from the conceptual and structural forces that organise genomic research. The preceding
discussion has shown how racialised categories persist through classificatory conventions,
how methodological norms reproduce Eurocentric defaults, and how institutional structures
shape what counts as rigour, inclusion, and scientific value. These dynamics together
underscore that efforts to diversify genomic datasets will remain limited unless grounded in
an explicit social justice approach.

The JUST DATA framework responds to this need. It offers a practical, justice-oriented
model for embedding equity principles into genomic research, designed to support scientists
in working ethically and rigorously with human population datasets and their production. It
positions justice not as an external consideration, but as integral to how data are assembled,
labelled, analysed, and communicated. The need for such a framework is especially salient
in the UK context, where racial discourse within scientific and public domains is often subtle,
euphemised, or displaced, yet the underlying operations of categorisation, exclusion, and
power within genomics remain comparable to those observed globally. A UK lens reveals a
particular challenge: equity work is frequently interpreted as relevant only where overt racism
is visible, obscuring the structural mechanisms through which racialised assumptions endure
in scientific practice. The JUST DATA framework speaks directly to this gap.

Unlike frameworks such as FAIR (Rocca-Serra et al., 2023) and CARE (Carroll et al., 2020)
or critical interventions that call for the expansion or reorientation of ethics in genomics, such
as Unbounding ELSI (Adsit-Morris et al., 2023), this framework is intentionally situated and
operational. While FAIR and CARE articulate important principles for data stewardship and
governance, and Unbounding ELSI offers a powerful critique of how ethics has been
historically bounded by the priorities of genomics itself, these approaches do not specify how
justice is undermined or reproduced through everyday classificatory, methodological, and
institutional decisions. JUST DATA is designed to intervene at the specific institutional,
methodological, and narrative decision points through which genomic inequities are



249 | P a g e
produced and stabilised in practice, including population categorisation, agenda setting, data
harmonisation, authorship, and communication. Grounded in ethnographic analysis of a UK
genomic institution, the framework translates justice from an abstract aspiration into a set of
actionable commitments that respond to the particular histories, organisational dynamics,
and racialised silences that structure genomic research in context.

Although rooted in critical race theory, the principles of this framework extend beyond
categories of race alone. They apply to any data derived from marginalised or underserved
populations, and to any scientific context in which historical inequality shapes who is
represented, who is excluded, and whose interests are served.

The framework is built around eight interlinked pillars that correspond to the points of
structural vulnerability identified in this thesis. None of these pillars operate in isolation; each
is dependent on the others, and each illustrates how equity in genomics requires
transformation rather than mere representation. The JUST DATA framework therefore
functions as a 360-degree evaluative tool, intended to support both the development of new
actions and the critical assessment of existing research practices. As shown below, each
pillar is underpinned by four foundational actions: reflective, informed, sincere, and
equitable. These actions form the iterative process through which justice-oriented decision-
making must move. They are not linear or prescriptive steps, but cycles of ongoing dialogue
and evaluation, designed to generate action, assess its impact, and refine future efforts.
Crucially, they require that institutions and researchers remain open to critique, responsive
to new insights, and prepared to confront inherited norms.

This is not a checklist. It is a transformative approach to how human data are conceived of,
accessed, analysed, and shared. It recognises that scientific practices are never neutral, and
that achieving justice in genomics demands rethinking how power, knowledge, and ethics
intersect across every stage of the research process. If enacted with intention, the JUST
DATA framework has the potential not only to improve data diversity but to reshape the
values that define excellence within genomic science.




9.2 Architecture of the JUST DATA Framework

The JUST DATA framework builds from the empirical findings presented in this thesis, which
show that inequities in genomics arise not only from methodological decisions or data
limitations, but from the interaction of conceptual norms, institutional logics, and historical




250 | P a g e
power structures. The framework therefore integrates ethical inquiry, technical rigour, and
structural awareness into a single model capable of guiding both analysis and action.

At the centre of the framework sit eight interlinked pillars, each addressing a distinct point of
vulnerability within the genomic research ecosystem: the assumptions that shape scientific
judgement; the structures and incentives that organise research; the narratives through
which genomics communicates risk and difference; the mechanisms through which trust and
accountability are enacted; and the infrastructures that determine who can meaningfully
participate in genomic science. While each pillar responds to a discrete set of challenges,
none operate independently. They function relationally, reflecting the interdependence of
social, ethical, and technical decisions in genomic practice.

These pillars rest upon four foundational actions - Reflective, Informed, Sincere, and
Equitable - which form the iterative cycle through which justice-oriented practice must pass.
These actions constitute the conditions necessary for the pillars to be applied with integrity.
They are not sequential steps, but ongoing commitments to critical awareness, contextual
knowledge, transparent communication, and redistribution of power.

A central premise of the JUST DATA framework is that justice cannot be assessed only at
the level of outcomes. Equity is not something that can be retroactively added to research
outputs; it must be built into the conceptual, methodological, and institutional processes
through which genomic knowledge is formed. For this reason, the framework is positioned at
the beginning of scientific decision-making, not at the end. It functions as a guide for the
foundational choices that shape research design, data generation, categorisation, analysis,
and communication. Power and social justice are therefore not themes that accompany the
framework, but the conditions that underpin it. The alignment of justice and methodology is
what allows genomic research to produce equitable outputs, rather than simply testing equity
after the fact.

The framework is intentionally cyclical rather than linear. Genomic research rarely follows
rigid stages, and neither do the ethical concerns that accompany it. Instead, the JUST DATA
framework offers a structure for revisiting decisions, interrogating assumptions, and refining
practices as projects evolve. It is not a compliance tool or checklist. It is a conceptual anchor
designed to guide researchers and institutions in evaluating the justice implications of their
choices, from project inception to publication, data sharing, and long-term impact.

Figure 58 illustrates this architecture: the eight pillars form the body of the framework, while
the four foundational actions operate as continuous forces shaping how each pillar is



251 | P a g e
enacted. The framework is designed to sit within ongoing institutional processes such as
research design, funding applications, ethics review, analytic decision-making, and
communication strategies. It can be used to initiate new actions or to critically assess
existing ones.




Figure 58: JUST DATA principles that support the four foundational actions of the
framework.

In the sections that follow, each foundational action and each pillar is described in detail. For
every pillar, the discussion is structured around four elements:

   •   Aim - what the pillar aims to address.

   •   Why it matters - the structural, methodological, or conceptual risks if the pillar is
       ignored.

   •   Empirical grounding - how issues identified in this thesis demonstrate the need for
       the pillar.

   •   What it involves - the concrete practices, behaviours, and institutional commitments
       required to enact the pillar in everyday research contexts.

Because power is unequally distributed within genomic institutions, justice requires not only
identifying these asymmetries but actively redistributing decision-making authority, epistemic
legitimacy, and material resources. These components form a framework that seeks not only
to improve representation in genomic datasets, but to transform the cultural, institutional, and
conditions under which genomic knowledge is produced.




252 | P a g e
9.2.1   Foundational Actions of the JUST DATA Framework

The JUST DATA Framework is built on four foundational actions that operate as the
conditions through which justice must be enacted in genomic science: reflective, informed,
sincere, and equitable.

Each foundational action consolidates an empirical strand developed earlier: conceptual
slippage and classificatory persistence (Chapters 4 and 8.2), communicative discomfort and
displaced responsibility (Chapter 5), methodological standards and inherited Eurocentric
defaults (Chapter 6 and 8.3), and institutional incentives and infrastructures (Chapter 7 and
8.4).

These actions precede and shape methodological and analytical decisions. They recognise
that justice cannot be secured by adjusting outputs at the end of a project. Instead, it must
be embedded at the point where scientific judgements are formed, classifications are
chosen, collaborations are negotiated, and data practices are designed. Each action
responds directly to the structural dynamics illuminated in this research, particularly the
operations of power, the persistence of inherited categories, and the institutional tendencies
that narrow the possibilities for ethical and inclusive genomic science.

Crucially, these actions are not linear steps. They form an iterative cycle, guiding
researchers and institutions through ongoing processes of questioning, assessment, and
refinement. They require scientists to remain receptive to critique, open to discomfort, and
committed to addressing the deeper historical and political conditions that shape genomic
research. Together, they create the foundation upon which the eight pillars of the JUST
DATA Framework rest. Without them, the pillars risk becoming technical aspirations rather
than drivers of structural change.

Reflective

Reflective practice involves a sustained engagement with the assumptions that underlie
genomic research. It requires researchers to interrogate the conceptual frameworks,
inherited categories, and normative logics embedded within scientific training and
methodological choices. As demonstrated in this thesis, classificatory practices in genomics
are shaped by historical racial science, geopolitical defaults, and institutional expectations
that elevate clarity and efficiency over complexity and nuance.




253 | P a g e
This action builds directly on my analysis of how classificatory habits and the normativity of
whiteness shape what becomes thinkable in genomic practice, even when race is formally
disavowed. (Chapters 4, sections 5.2 and 8.2.4.).

These pressures can lead scientists to reproduce racialised patterns of categorisation even
when they recognise their limitations.

Reflection is required here as a one-off ethical gesture, but also as a continuous process of
asking how race, power, and inequality shape the scientific imagination. Reflective action
positions justice as a scientific responsibility and demands that researchers continually re-
examine the conceptual scaffolding of their work.

Informed

Informed action requires that research decisions be grounded in both technical expertise and
context-sensitive knowledge. This includes historical awareness, engagement with lived
experiences, and familiarity with the social and political contexts from which data emerge.

To be informed is therefore to recognise that scientific competence alone is insufficient. It
responds to the gaps I observed in researchers’ historical and ethical grounding, where race
was treated as external to science and guidance was often unknown, unused, or
experienced as outside participants’ roles (as discussed in section 5.3.3; 8.2.2; 8.2.5 and
Chapter 6).

It requires integrating insights from social sciences, humanities, and communities affected by
research. This action challenges the epistemic hierarchies that have historically marginalised
non-biological forms of knowledge within genomics and positions interdisciplinary learning as
essential to scientific rigour. It also acknowledges that informed decision-making is
necessary for developing methodological approaches that do not reproduce historical
inequities.

Sincere

Sincere action calls for transparency, accountability, and candour in how scientific decisions
are justified and communicated. Sincere here is an intentional choice chosen as a call to
both genuine and serious attention.

This action is grounded in my findings that ethics and governance were often treated as
procedural checkpoints, while responsibility for the downstream consequences of
classification and communication was frequently displaced (sections 8.2.5, 8.3, 5.2, and 5.3).


254 | P a g e
Such proceduralism can obscure the deeper questions of justice, reciprocity, and structural
inequality that accompany work with human population data.

Sincerity requires scientists and institutions to aim beyond minimal compliance. It includes
acknowledging the limitations of datasets, the exclusions built into recruitment and sampling
strategies, and the historical forces shaping the availability of particular forms of data.
Sincere action is central to building trustworthiness in genomic research, particularly for
communities who have historically been harmed or marginalised by scientific institutions. It
embeds ethical responsibility not as a bureaucratic obligation but as a public commitment to
openness, humility, and accountability.

Equitable

Equitable action is grounded in the principle that justice in genomics demands the
redistribution of power, opportunity, and material resources. It recognises that representation
alone cannot resolve the structural inequalities that shape who participates in genomic
science and who benefits from it. This study revealed inequities particularly for researchers
in countries with lower financial capacity and resources than the UK. It draws on the
structural constraints documented across funding, publishing, and data infrastructures,
where inclusion was pursued within Eurocentric defaults and unequal capacity, limiting who
can lead, interpret, and benefit from genomic research (as analysed in sections 6.1, 6.2;
sections 8.4.1 to 8.4.3 and Chapter 7).

Equitable action requires confronting these asymmetries directly. It includes supporting
collaborations that build capacity rather than extract data, ensuring that communities can
meaningfully influence research agendas, and addressing the institutional hierarchies that
limit participation and leadership for marginalised groups. It demands not only fair
distribution of benefits, but fair distribution of decision-making authority. Equitable action is
essential because it ensures that justice is not treated as an aspirational value but as a
practical commitment embedded in the structures of genomic research itself.

These four foundational actions constitute the ethical grounding of the JUST DATA
Framework. They establish the conditions under which justice can shape scientific practice
from the outset, ensuring that the pillars of the framework are not adopted superficially but
enacted in ways that meaningfully address power, inequity, and the historical legacies that
structure genomic research. They provide the lens through which methodological,
institutional, and communicative decisions must be evaluated, and create the iterative cycle




255 | P a g e
through which research practices can evolve towards greater justice, responsibility, and
inclusivity.




9.2.2   Pillars of the JUST DATA Framework


1. Justice-Centred Habits (J)

Aim

This pillar places justice at the centre of scientific reasoning. It calls for a shift from episodic
ethical reflection to a sustained practice of examining the assumptions that underpin
genomic work, including the racialised logics, normative defaults, and inherited categories
embedded in training and methodology.

Why it matters

Without justice-centred habits, scientific practices risk reproducing the biases, omissions,
and classificatory legacies documented throughout this thesis. Embedding justice into
everyday decision-making ensures that methodological choices are guided by structural
awareness, not convenience or tradition. This pillar establishes the ethical orientation
necessary for all other elements of the framework to function meaningfully

Empirical grounding

    •   Scientists acknowledged tensions around racialised categories but felt constrained
        by institutional norms.

    •   Reflexive engagement appeared informally but was not widely institutionalised. ref

    •   Leadership homogeneity limited opportunities for justice-oriented cultural
        transformation.

What it involves

Justice-centred habits require researchers and institutions to confront the epistemic
conditions in which genomic science operates, to recognise the histories that shape present-
day data practices, and to cultivate cultures where questioning entrenched norms is a
collective responsibility rather than a personal burden. It positions reflexivity not as an




256 | P a g e
optional add-on, but as a fundamental scientific habit that guides how problems are framed,
how populations are conceptualised, and how findings are interpreted.




2. Uncovering Institutional Barriers (U)

Aim

This pillar focuses on identifying the structural and organisational conditions that shape
scientific possibility.

Why it matters

Institutional structures profoundly shape research outcomes. Unless these barriers are made
visible and addressed, justice-oriented intentions will be continually overridden by systemic
constraints. This pillar shifts responsibility from individuals to institutions and underscores
that equity requires structural change, not isolated acts of inclusion.

Empirical grounding

    •   Funding models and publication incentives prioritised speed and novelty over
        reflexivity and equity.

    •   Researchers were structurally tied to inherited datasets and restrictive editorial
        norms.

    •   Power remained centralised in homogenous leadership structures that influenced
        agenda-setting and collaboration.

What it involves

It asks institutions to scrutinise how their funding structures, recruitment patterns, leadership
demographics, authorship norms, and ethical governance processes enable or constrain
justice-oriented research. Uncovering institutional barriers means recognising that inequity
persists not because individuals fail to care, but because systems reward certain forms of
productivity, knowledge, and authority while marginalising others. It requires institutions to
examine how default practices become naturalised, how decision-making power is
concentrated, and how scientific agendas are shaped by forces that are not neutral.




257 | P a g e
3. Storytelling and Power Analysis (S)

Aim

This pillar foregrounds scientific communication and recognises that genomic narratives can
reinforce racialised interpretations or challenge them, depending on how they are crafted.

Why it matters

Narratives shape how genomic research is understood by clinicians, policymakers,
communities, and the public. Unexamined narratives risk reinforcing racialised thinking and
obscuring the social and historical processes that shape genetic variation. This pillar ensures
that communication becomes an ethical practice, grounded in transparency, accuracy, and
accountability.

Empirical grounding

   •   Publications often used racial or ethnic labels without contextual explanation.

   •   Ethical and historical considerations were often omitted from published work.

   •   Simplified or deterministic interpretations were sometimes amplified through
       institutional communication channels.

   What it involves

Genomic research does not simply generate findings; it tells stories about populations, risk,
identity, and human difference. Storytelling and power analysis require scientists to
interrogate the narratives they construct, to be transparent about how categories were
defined, which assumptions informed analytical choices, and what has been omitted or
simplified. It calls for careful attention to language, context, and interpretation, ensuring that
research communication does not naturalise social hierarchies or reproduce inequities




4. Trust, Transparency and Accountability (T)

Aim

This pillar emphasises that trust in genomic research must be actively cultivated through
transparency and accountability rather than assumed as a by-product of neutrality or
technical competence.


258 | P a g e
Why it matters

Without accountability, trust remains fragile and easily undermined, particularly for
communities historically excluded or harmed by genomics. This pillar ensures that
institutions move beyond procedural ethics toward relational responsibility, strengthening the
legitimacy, transparency, and social value of genomic research.

Empirical grounding

   •   Ethical processes are more often treated as procedural obligations rather than
       substantive engagements.

   •   Researchers expressed uncertainty about who held responsibility for justice-oriented
       decisions.

   •   Communities and collaborators had limited visibility within institutional accountability
       structures.

What it involves

It calls for governance structures that extend beyond formal ethics review to include
community oversight, transparent institutional decision-making, and internal mechanisms
that hold researchers responsible for the social implications of their work. Shared
accountability recognises that trust is relational; it is built when institutions acknowledge past
harms, demonstrate openness in their practices, and distribute responsibility across
leadership, researchers, and broader governance systems. This pillar promotes a research
culture where accountability is visible, collective, and grounded in ethical responsibility.




5. Data Innovation (D)

Aim

This pillar focuses on reimagining the technical and statistical foundations of genomic
science to promote equity, accuracy, and inclusivity.

Why it matters

Genomic findings are only as reliable as the categories, samples, and algorithms that shape
them. Without equity-driven innovation, genomic science risks reinforcing the very biases it



259 | P a g e
seeks to correct. This pillar ensures that methodological reform becomes a central pathway
to justice by aligning technical accuracy with ethical responsibility.

Empirical grounding

   •   Existing population descriptors often failed to capture lived identities or demographic
       realities.

   •   Scientists described methodological constraints tied to inherited datasets and
       dominant statistical tools.

   •   Institutions rarely recognised the development of equity-focused methods as
       legitimate scientific work

What it involves

It calls for innovation that does not simply increase representation but interrogates the
conceptual and structural biases embedded in population categorisation, sampling
strategies, and analytical models. Data innovation requires scientists to develop tools that
quantify diversity, detect bias, and challenge inherited assumptions about human variation.
Data integrity involves transparency in how descriptors are selected, how limitations are
understood, and how methodological choices reflect ethical and contextual considerations.
This pillar situates methodological reform as a core scientific responsibility, not an optional
ethical enhancement.




6. Agenda-Setting for All (A)

Aim

This pillar asserts that justice in genomics cannot be achieved if research priorities are
defined solely by funders, institutional leaders, or traditional scientific agendas.

Why it matters

Without inclusive agenda-setting, genomic research risks reinforcing global inequities and
failing to address the needs of those most affected by its outcomes. This pillar supports a
shift toward shared authority, enabling research that is both ethically grounded and
scientifically relevant to diverse populations.




260 | P a g e
Empirical grounding

   •   Community perspectives not visible in agenda-setting processes.

   •   Collaborations across less resourced financial contexts contributes to inequity of
       outcomes.

   •   Researchers described the dominance of funder-driven priorities over equity-oriented
       goals.

What it involves

It calls for structurally embedding the perspectives of communities historically marginalised
or underrepresented in genomic data. Agenda-setting for all requires meaningful
participation from marginalised voices at the earliest stages of project development,
extending through funding applications, ethics review, analysis, and dissemination. It draws
from participatory research traditions and emphasises co-creation of scientific priorities,
ensuring that genomic research addresses real-world needs rather than simply aligning with
existing power structures




7. Training for Transformation (T)

Aim

This pillar recognises that long-term structural change in genomics depends on education
that integrates ethical, historical, and social perspectives alongside technical expertise.

Why it matters

Without comprehensive education, justice remains aspirational rather than actionable. This
pillar ensures that researchers have the tools, knowledge, and institutional support needed
to apply the framework, enabling cultural change that can sustain justice-centred scientific
practice across generations.

Empirical grounding

   •   Training on race, ethics, and classification was inconsistent and or felt absent.

   •   Ethical responsibility was disproportionately placed or taken up by junior or
       marginalised staff.


261 | P a g e
   •   Scientists expressed uncertainty about how to navigate the ethical and social
       dimensions of their methodological choices.

What it involves

Training for transformation calls for embedding critical genomics education across all levels
of scientific work, including leadership, early-career researchers, administrative staff, ethics
boards, and communication teams. It challenges institutions to move beyond optional
workshops or isolated modules and instead cultivate a sustained culture of learning. This
pillar emphasises that scientists must understand the historical legacies of race science, the
ethics of data practices, and the epistemic dimensions of categorisation to engage
meaningfully with justice-oriented research.




8. Accessibility and Equity (A)

Aim

This pillar highlights that equity in genomics requires more than the availability of data; it
requires equitable capacity to use, interpret, and benefit from genomic resources.

Why it matters

Technical openness without equitable capacity reinforces global hierarchies in knowledge
production. This pillar ensures that genomic data systems are designed to support fair
participation and benefit-sharing, preventing the reproduction of extractive or exclusionary
models of scientific practice.




Empirical grounding

   •   Researchers in lower-resourced countries faced structural barriers to meaningful
       data use.

   •   Open data initiatives did not account for disparities in computational capacity or
       training.

   •   Data governance frameworks often reproduce existing inequities by contributing to
       methodological choices because of differences in access.



262 | P a g e
What it involves

Accessibility and equity in data use call for infrastructure, training, computational resources,
and governance structures that support meaningful participation across diverse research
environments. This pillar challenges simplistic assumptions about open access and
emphasises that access must be understood relationally: who can work with the data, who
can act on it, and who remains excluded.




9.2.3   Bringing the Pillars Together: How JUST DATA Operates as a Cycle

The JUST DATA Framework is designed as an integrated, iterative model in which the eight
pillars and four foundational actions work together to shape ethical, methodological, and
institutional decisions in genomic research. Rather than functioning as isolated components,
the pillars form a coherent structure that addresses the conceptual, methodological, and
structural challenges identified throughout this thesis. When viewed alongside the framework
graphic (Figure 59), their interdependence becomes clear. Justice-centred science emerges
not from adopting a single pillar, but from understanding how each reinforces the others
within a continuous cycle of practice.




263 | P a g e
Figure 59 shows the interdependence of pillars and action as a continuous cycle.

At the centre of this model are the four foundational actions: reflective, informed, sincere,
and equitable. These actions operate across every pillar, guiding the choices researchers
and institutions make when framing research questions, selecting categories, designing
methods, communicating findings, and building collaborations. They ensure that the
framework intervenes at the start of the scientific process, shaping the assumptions, habits,
and structural conditions that precede methodological decisions. By repeatedly engaging in
these actions, researchers build the capacity to recognise how power operates in data
production and to embed justice directly into scientific reasoning.

The eight pillars provide the practical architecture for this process. Each addresses a distinct
point of structural vulnerability in the genomic ecosystem, ranging from classification
practices and institutional constraints to communication, collaboration, education, and data
access. Taken together, they address the full lifecycle of genomic research: how data are
conceptualised, generated, categorised, analysed, and circulated. The cycle therefore


264 | P a g e
reflects the iterative nature of scientific work itself. As projects evolve, so too must the ethical
and methodological choices that shape them. The framework provides a means to revisit
decisions, refine assumptions, and identify where justice has been sidelined or constrained.

The graphic illustrates how this cycle loops back on itself. Researchers may enter at any
pillar, but work becomes transformative only when the full cycle is engaged. For example,
innovations in statistical methods (Data Innovation and Integrity) cannot realise their ethical
potential without justice-centred habits, transparency in communication, equitable agenda-
setting, and meaningful access to data. Similarly, efforts to involve marginalised
communities require institutional reform, training for transformation, and an honest analysis
of where power sits within existing systems. The cycle makes it clear that justice in genomics
is not a discrete outcome but an ongoing process requiring coordinated attention across
multiple dimensions of scientific practice.

In sum, the JUST DATA Framework provides a way of thinking and working that embeds
justice into the everyday fabric of genomic research. Bringing the pillars and foundational
actions together into a cyclical model offers a practical route for addressing the inequities
and power dynamics that shape genomic science and for guiding the development of
research practices that are both methodologically robust and socially responsible.


9.3 Limitations and Future Research


This thesis has developed a justice-oriented analysis of genomic research through a UK-
based institutional case study. Its strength lies in the close ethnographic and critical
examination of how race, ethnicity and ancestry are conceptualised, operationalised and
negotiated within a major genomic research institution. However, this situated focus also
creates important limitations. The findings should not be read as universally generalisable to
all genomic institutions, national contexts or research cultures. Rather, the thesis offers an
empirically grounded account of how power, classification and equity operate within a
specific institutional and geopolitical setting.

A central limitation is that the research is UK-focused. The analysis is shaped by the
histories, policy environment, institutional arrangements and racial formations of the United
Kingdom. Race and ethnicity are not stable or universal categories; they are produced
through particular histories of empire, migration, governance and social classification. The
ways in which race is avoided, displaced or reintroduced through ancestry in the UK may
therefore differ from how similar processes unfold in the United States, continental Europe,
Africa, Asia, Latin America or other genomic research settings. The UK focus is justified


265 | P a g e
because it enables detailed examination of a context where race is often made less explicit,
while racialised logics remain embedded in scientific and institutional practice. However,
future research should explore how the JUST DATA framework travels across different
national, regulatory and historical contexts, and whether its principles require adaptation in
settings with different classificatory systems, community relationships and histories of
scientific extraction.

A second limitation is that this thesis is context-specific. The empirical work is grounded in
one institutional environment and in the practices, discourses and structures encountered
there. While the Wellcome Sanger Institute is globally influential and therefore a valuable site
for examining the organisation of genomic science, it cannot represent the full diversity of
genomic research institutions. Other settings may have different funding arrangements,
governance structures, community engagement models, leadership cultures or relationships
with clinical translation. This specificity is not a weakness; it reflects the methodological
commitment of the thesis to understanding how power operates in practice, within concrete
institutional arrangements. Nonetheless, future research could test, extend and refine the
framework through comparative studies across multiple institutions, including clinical
genomics services, biobanks, commercial genomics companies, community-led research
initiatives and research settings outside high-income countries.

A third limitation concerns the thesis’s primary focus on race, ethnicity and ancestry. This
focus was necessary because the research question centred on the persistence of racialised
logics in genomic classification and because race remains a particularly powerful and
historically loaded organising construct within genomics. The thesis therefore deliberately
foregrounds race, racialisation, whiteness and the politics of ancestry. However, this means
that intersectionality and the wider range of human diversity were not examined in equal
depth. Dimensions such as disability, sex, gender, sexuality, class, religion, migration status,
language, geography, age and socioeconomic position also shape participation in research,
access to genomic medicine, experiences of classification and the distribution of benefit and
harm. These dimensions do not operate separately from race but intersect with it in complex
ways. Future research should therefore examine how the JUST DATA framework can be
applied intersectionally, and how its pillars might need to be expanded or adapted to account
for overlapping forms of marginalisation.

Relatedly, the framework has not yet been empirically tested against other measures or
dimensions of diversity. JUST DATA was developed from the findings of this thesis and is
therefore grounded in the empirical realities of race, ethnicity, ancestry and institutional
power in genomics. Its broader relevance lies in its claim that justice must be embedded at


266 | P a g e
the level of research design, categorisation, governance, analysis and communication.
However, future work should examine whether the framework is equally useful when applied
to other forms of exclusion or underrepresentation, such as disability inclusion in genomic
research, gendered patterns of diagnosis, socioeconomic barriers to participation,
geographic inequities in data access, or the exclusion of linguistically minoritised
communities. Such work would help determine whether JUST DATA functions as a general
justice framework for genomics or whether it requires distinct adaptations for different
domains of inequality.

Future research could also evaluate the framework in practice. This might involve piloting
JUST DATA within funding applications, ethics review processes, cohort design, data access
committees, publication guidelines or institutional strategy. Researchers could assess
whether the framework changes the questions asked at the start of projects, improves
transparency around population descriptors, strengthens community accountability, or
supports more equitable collaborations. Longitudinal studies would be particularly valuable
for examining whether the framework influences institutional culture over time, rather than
being adopted superficially as another statement of values. Its usefulness should also be
assessed by those most affected by genomic inequities, including communities whose data
are collected, researchers working in lower-resourced settings, and professionals involved in
translating genomic findings into care.

Although this thesis does not claim to offer a universal solution to all inequities in genomics.
It offers a situated, theoretically informed and empirically grounded framework for
recognising how justice is shaped by power, classification, institutional structures and
methodological choices. Its future value will depend on whether it can be critically adapted,
tested and refined across different contexts and forms of diversity. In this sense, JUST
DATA should be understood not as a finished model, but as a starting point for building
genomic science that is more reflective, accountable and responsive to the full complexity of
human difference.




9.4 Conclusions


This thesis has shown that genomic science is shaped by practices of classification,
methodological norms, and institutional structures that reproduce longstanding patterns of
inequality. The JUST DATA Framework developed here offers a practical way to intervene in
these processes, positioning justice not as an outcome to be checked at the end of research,


267 | P a g e
but as a principle that must guide scientific decisions from their earliest stages. By bringing
together eight pillars and four foundational actions, the framework reframes excellence,
rigour, and responsibility in genomic research as inseparable from social justice.

The implications for genomic practice and policy are significant. Embedding justice into
research design challenges institutions to reconsider how they define merit, allocate
resources, structure collaborations, and engage with the communities whose data they
depend on. It aligns with global movements that call for more responsible and inclusive
science yet attends to the specific histories and power relations that shape genomic
research in the United Kingdom. Institutions such as Sanger are well positioned to adopt
models like JUST DATA, not only to improve the quality and relevance of their work, but to
help lead the field towards a more equitable future.

Justice in genomics cannot be achieved through representation alone. It requires confronting
the structural and epistemic conditions that shape how human difference is defined,
measured, and valued. This thesis argues that genomic science must engage with these
challenges directly, recognising that every methodological choice is also an ethical one. The
promise of genomics lies not only in its ability to map variation, but in its potential to
contribute to a more just scientific and social world. The responsibility now lies with
researchers, institutions, and policy-makers to act with intention, to question inherited
assumptions, and to build systems of knowledge that reflect and respect the full diversity of
human experience.

The contribution of this thesis is therefore twofold: it demonstrates how structural and
methodological forces shape inequities within genomic science in a UK institution, and it
offers a framework through which justice can be operationalised from the outset of scientific
decision-making

Representation can be celebrated while substantive inclusion remains limited, and scientific
authority continues to be shaped by the norms and comfort of whiteness. This reality
underscores why justice in genomics must move beyond representation and toward a
redistribution of power, knowledge, and opportunity. The JUST DATA framework is an
invitation to this work: to reimagine genomic science not only as a technical endeavour, but
as a collective ethical project grounded in responsibility, solidarity, and care.

10.2    Appendix AI
                                 Faculty of Education
                Research Ethics Review Process - Principles



The Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process follows stages and principles already
well embedded within Faculty practices.
The Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process recognizes the diversity of research
carried out in the Faculty. This includes research designs involving, but not limited to,
qualitative research, quantitative research, mixed-methods research, theoretical research,
research conducted on or with existing artefacts (e.g., creative works, literary sources, historical
document analysis, literature reviews, or meta-analyses), secondary data analysis, and others.
The Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process aims to ensure that important yet
ethically risky research can still be conducted, with due consideration for a range of issues.
Underpinning the Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process are five key research
ethics principles: Autonomy; Beneficence; Nonmaleficence; Justice; and Independent Review.
The Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process follows guidelines and best practices
currently outlined in the Ethical Guidelines for Educational Research of the British Educational
Research Association (BERA), and any future updates.




269 | P a g e
                       Research Ethics Review Process


The Faculty’s Research Ethics Review process
The Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process follows the principles outlined above
and consists of three stages.
All three stages are underpinned by the initial completion of this Research Ethics Review Form
and assembly of all accompanying documentation, as detailed in the Submission Checklist on
the last page of the Form.
Stage 1 consists of the submission of the Form and all accompanying documentation to an
independent knowledgeable person of standing, and a discussion with this person. This should
be an individual in the Faculty of Education with relevant research expertise who would be
deemed to be independent of the research being reviewed, that is who would not be deemed to
have a conflict of interest with either the researcher(s) making the submission, or the research
being submitted for review.
This Stage 1 discussion may result in one of two outcomes:
   1. Ethical endorsement for the research granted (potentially after the submission of
      further supporting documentation) if no further ethical concerns are raised; or
   2. Recommendation for Stage 2 review (potentially after the submission of further
      supporting documentation) if minor ethical concerns or clarifications are raised (at the
      discretion of the person undertaking the review.
Stage 2 consists of a discussion of any ethical dimensions of your research as evident from
Stage 1 with the initial independent knowledgeable person and another individual. This
individual should be an expert in either the substantive topic of the research, and/or its
methodological design as relevant to the range of ethical dimensions to be discussed.
This Stage 2 discussion may result in one of two outcomes:
   1. Ethical endorsement for the research granted (potentially after the submission of
      further supporting documentation) if no further ethical concerns are raised; or
   2. Recommendation for Stage 3 review (potentially after the submission of further
      supporting documentation) if unresolved or complex ethical issues remain.
Stage 3 consists of the lead researcher obtaining formal ethical endorsement through the
Faculty of Education’s Standing Panel for Ethics, convened as per Policy R001/2019.
In line with Policy R001/2019, Stage 3 may result in one of three outcomes:
   1. Ethical endorsement for the research granted (potentially after the submission of
      further supporting documentation) if no further ethical concerns are raised; or
   2. Recommendation for the submission to be forwarded to a School or University level
      ethics committee; or
   3. Ethical endorsement for the research not granted. This means that the research cannot
      be undertaken in its submitted form. If a subsequent submission is to be made, full
      reconsideration of the research design and its ethical implications must be carried out in
      advance, and a new form completed and put through all three stages above.


270 | P a g e
After ethical endorsement has been obtained



Once ethical endorsement has been obtained, the form and all accompanying documentation
should be lodged with the appropriate office, by email, as follows:
For undergraduate students, this is the ethics coordinator of the relevant paper.
For graduate students, this is the Graduate Office: graduate@educ.cam.ac.uk. Please also
observe any route-specific arrangements communicated to route students and staff.
For staff, this is the Research Office: Research-Office@educ.cam.ac.uk. Each endorsed ethics
submission lodged with the Research Office will be assigned a number, which will be
communicated to the lead researcher/principal investigator.



In the event of changes to a study that has already received ethical endorsement



On occasion, studies may undergo changes after ethical endorsement has been received.



In this case, the primary investigator of the study (as named in the original ethics submission)
must revise the originally submitted ethics form with updated information, retaining any
information that has remained the same and updating all answers to reflect the new study
parameters. If the change relates to the primary investigator changing, then the new primary
investigator must note this change and revise the originally submitted form.



The revised form should then enter the research ethics review process. In the interest of robust
review, the process should start from the beginning, with Stage 1. There is no guarantee that
ethical endorsement will be given at the same Stage as for the initial submission.



Constituencies for whom The Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process does
not apply



The Faculty of Education Research Ethics Review Process applies to all constituencies in the
Faculty of Education except PGCE students and PPD students, whose courses have specific
arrangements for ethical review and securing ethical endorsement. These processes are aligned
with the above principles but recognize the needs and nature of research carried out in these
programmes.

Recruitment Procedures
If the research involves new data collection from human participants, then describe how potential participants will be identified and
how access to contact information will be obtained. Describe how participants will be recruited. Submit all advertising materials
(posters, emails, letters etc.) with this form. Please limit the response for this question to 200 words (unless there are substantial
ethical issues to discuss).

Scientists will specifically include genomic researchers who encounter race, ethnicity and ancestry through the datasets in their
work. On the genome campus this includes those who use GWAS, are involved in human variation or genetic interpretation of human
data. The pilot study was carried out with scientists from one particular scientific group I have used this to identify further scientists t
fit the profile and these will be approached through email introduction and open seminars about the study on the research campus..
Career stage of scientists: The profile of participants will consist of those more senior researchers and heads of research groups. I
have identified these as the group who have the most influence over large research questions, funding applications and overview of
publications. Within the immersion of scientists however I will be observing and communicating with scientists across career levels of
research groups. Although in depth interviews are not planned with all these individuals I will have substantial contact with them.




                                                                  276
                                                                  /12
Crucial data to address research question three in particular around the ecosystem of genomic research will be accessible through
this method.


The public in this case consists of those engaged with genetics research around race, ethnicity, and ancestry who are attending events
or related to the Genetic Automata exhibition which includes data categorisation around race , ethnicity and genetic ancestry
This will include invited participants from the Sickle Cell and Thalassemia community and from genetic youth forums where race,
ethnicity and ancestry have already been encountered.. A selected sample (two-three) participants at each public event may be asked
to take part in semi-structured interviews. The ages of the participants will vary across this sample to provide access to the data from
a broader range of public views. However, this data will be used to better inform my reflexivity and interpretations rather than a
representative sample of the societal views.



Informed Consent
If the project involves new data collection from human participants, then describe how informed consent will be obtained.
Informed consent is the process where the participants are provided detailed information about the purpose of the research, the risks
and benefits to them as participants, what will be expected of them, and then given the opportunity to agree to participate or not.
Consent documents and scripts must be written in a language and at a level the subjects will understand. Participants should be
given the right to withdraw their consent at any time and for any reason without consequences.
How long will you allow potential participants to decide whether to take part? What arrangements have been made for people who
might not adequately understand verbal explanations or written information given in English, or who have special communication
needs?
The researcher is responsible for minimizing coercion and undue influence. Coercion occurs when there is an overt or implicit threat
of harm presented in order to obtain participation, such as when a subject will lose access to certain services if they decline
participation, when a student will experience reprisal or disapproval from an instructor, or when an employee will experience
reprisal or disapproval from a supervisor. Undue influence can occur when there is an offer of an excessive or inappropriate reward
to secure participation, such as a large cash payment or other gift.
Describe the procedures used for informed consent for this study. If consent is from adult participants, then give details of who will
take consent and how it will be done. If informed consent will be sought from vulnerable groups (e.g., those with learning
difficulties, incarcerated populations) of if the researcher holds power over participants (e.g., classroom teacher, line manager,
historical colonial structures, etc.), then say how you will ensure that consent is voluntary and fully informed.
If you are recruiting children or young adults (aged under 18 years) specify the age-range of participants and describe the
arrangements for seeking informed consent from a person with parental responsibility. If you intend to provide children under 16
with information about the study and seek agreement, outline how this process will vary according to their age and level of
understanding.
If the research design does not involve informed consent (e.g., covert observation of people in public spaces, posts from social media,
online data harvesting, etc.), please provide a clear justification for this decision to deny participant autonomy; you should outline
below how participant beneficence, nonmaleficence and justice will be withheld.

Voluntary informed consent will be obtained from all participants in writing. The consent forms
and information sheets for both parts of the study are provided in Appendix B of the study
protocol.

Consent for interviews will be collected via a consent form that will be distributed with an
information sheet prior to the interview, and discussed at the start of the interview. All research
participants will be told the purpose of the project and asked to consent to audio recording.

Participants will be provided with contact details for Sasha Henriques, Prof Middleton and Prof
Arday. The information and consent process will ensure, as far as possible, that participants
understand the scope of the study. as described below. They will be told that they can
withdraw from the project at any time if they wish to do so.

Consent will be treated as dynamic and continual, as willingness to enter into a reciprocal
relationship and not as equivalent to wanting to participate to any part of the study. Individual
consent to observation will be sought at the beginning of each session, or if circumstances
change (e.g. someone enters the room during the observation).




                                                                 277
                                                                 /12
Possible public exposure of individuals and teams working on sensitive subjects will be paid
attention to and mitigated as much as possible. Data may not be included in published analysis
for example or avoidance of using direct quotes from participants. Where possible, such
decisions will be negotiated in dialogue with participants themselves and colleagues with
experience of conducting research on sensitive topics.

Anonymity and confidentiality

Interviews and transcripts de-identified will be used only in pseudonymised form – both to
ensure they are protected from any harm associated with what they say, and to facilitate
frank and open discussion. Although anonymity is aimed for it cannot entirely be
guaranteed. Genetics is a highlight specific and unique field, failure to maintain anonymity
may also be a source of professional caution for the researcher and participant.

Participants will be informed of the implications at the beginning of the study. To protect
their confidentiality, extra caution will be used at the stage of reporting. Identifying details
of individual researchers will be completely removed from interviews, notes as well as
documentary and photographic evidence, and any information that could possibly be used
to identify the teams will only be included when strictly necessary to convey an import
point. Analysis of data and interviews will be thematic and any mention of the scientific
studies or roles that participants have that could be identifying will be described in terms of
the themes that emerge from the data which will more general and less identifiable. While
the researcher will adhere to these measures, participants themselves they might decide
to relinquish their anonymity (e.g. to be co-authors on research outputs or to respond to
the research)

Describe any discomfort or inconvenience that participants may experience. Include information about procedures that for some
people could be physically stressful or might impinge on the safety of participants (e.g., noise levels, visual stimuli, distractions,
physical activity including performances or activities of any kind that may be challenging) or that for some people could be

Because of the recruitment of participants across different ethnic groups, some may identify as from communities who have been
impacted by the harms and colonisation and racism in the past and present. This is distinct in some ways from the experience of
the researcher who is also embedded in the subject from an academic perspective. Although there may be benefits for
participants to engage in research that opens the dialogue around the science that dispels typological thinking and racist ideology
they may be discomfort and trauma from these discussions that the researcher is unable to address. Therefore psychological
support has been secured to provide additional support to all participants if these issues arise.

17. Data Protection and Data Security
Please note that if any human participants are involved in this research, this information must be provided and relevant
documents (that is, participant information sheet including a data protection notice) must be submitted with this
application. This is a legal requirement under UK GDPR.
What arrangements are there for data security during and after the study? Describe how the data will be used, both during and
after the research. Indicate whether it will be disseminated through publication, presentation or other means, and in what form.
Describe where and how the data will be stored.
If relevant, indicate the types of personal data that will be recorded and confirm its collection is necessary and proportionate to the
aims of the study.
Describe any risks associated with any personal data that is collected, shared, or analysed for the purposes of this research –
including audio or video recordings.
Datasets involving high-risk data should seek advice from the University’s Data Protection Officer. High-risk data includes personal
data that, if there were a breach, could be extremely harmful to an individual’s health, safety, professional or personal life; the
profiling of children or vulnerable individuals; direct collection of data without consent; combining or processing data in a way that
could have significant effects for individuals.
Please confirm that you have, or are in the process of, setting up appropriate data sharing agreements where relevant. Please also
confirm that no sharing of identifiable and/or non-anonymised participant will occur before such an agreement is in place.
Digital data stored on a computer requires compliance with the General Data Protection Regulation. Please indicate if you have
discussed this with a relevant Data Protection Officer and describe any special circumstances that have been identified from that
discussion.
Please confirm that all applicants have received GDPR training. Say who will have access to participants' personal data during the
study and for how long personal data will be stored or accessed after the study has ended, and whether data will be transferred or
stored outside the UK.
Please outline also any perceived risks in relation to data security, including any issues around potential unauthorized access,
corruption, or theft of the digital information generated through the research, especially if the research is to be undertaken in a
digital medium.
Full information regarding University policies on data protection and data security are available here: https://www.information-
compliance.admin.cam.ac.uk/data-protection
Please limit the response for this question to 200 words (unless there are substantial ethical issues to discuss).

Please see study protocol


Coded/de-identified transcripts, fieldnotes, documents and pictures will be stored for analysis in the Atlas.ti software and within the
Atlas.ti web analysis platform. Data communicated to the Atlas.ti web platform is encrypted using TLS authentication and the platform
is GDPR compliant (https://atlastihelp.helpscoutdocs.com/article/168-security-at-atlas-ti-web). Data will be transferred to transcribers
using their secure data transfer systems. All recordings and transcripts will be identified only by a code, and all identifying information
within interviews will be redacted after transcription. Personal information - in the form of the names and contact details of
interviewees - will be stored in a separate, password protected Word .docx file. This file will be deleted 12 months after the end of the
project. Interview data will be stored and backed up through the university OneDrive, and backed up monthly to an external hard disk
drive stored in the office of the lead investigator in the faculty of Education, University of Cambridge. Access to personal information




                                                                  281
                                                                  /12
will only be available to the direct research team (Prof Arday, Prof Middleton and Sasha Henriques) and such personal information
(which will consist of names and contact details) will be stored in a password-protected Word .docx file.

Other collaborators at the Kavli Centre will have access to pseudonymised/coded interview transcripts shared using OneDrive and/or
the Atlas.ti Web platform.

19. High-Risk Research
The University normally insures and indemnifies researchers and departments against risks associated with specific research
studies. The University. Insurance cover for studies not deemed high-risk, that is not involving any procedures, interventions,
treatments, scans, samples, drugs, etc. (e.g. questionnaires, interviews, data analysis only) is provided under the University's Public
Liability and Professional Indemnity Insurance policies. The Faculty of Education will make regular submissions to the University of
lists of research studies that are not deemed high risk. These do not require any further action on behalf of the Primary Investigator.
If you deem the research to be high risk, please note that a separate submission for insurance purposes must be made once ethical
endorsement has been secured. The ‘what happens next’ section below outlines the procedure in this case.
Please consult the University policy at the following link and make a determination around the high-risk nature of the research.
https://www.insurance.admin.cam.ac.uk/insurance-guidance/human-volunteer-studies-and-clinical-trials

What happens next


Once this form has been completed and all relevant documentation (as per the Submission Checklist above) assembled, please
refer to the three stages of the Research Ethics Review Process outlined at the start of this document.

Once the Research Ethics Review Process has completed and ethical endorsement has been received, consult the Risk
Assessment information to ascertain whether you must undertake a risk assessment relating to your research.

If you have deemed your research to be high risk according to University rules in response to Question 19, you will need to make
a separate submission for insurance purposes, only once ethical endorsement has been secured. Information about this process
may be secured from the Research Office (for staff, Research-Office@educ.cam.ac.uk), the Graduate Office (for graduate students,
graduate@educ.cam.ac.uk), or the ethics coordinator for the relevant paper (for undergraduate students) and at the link
included in Question 19. Once this step has been completed (if relevant), the study may commence.

10.5    Appendix B Information and Consent
