10.6    Appendix B1


Participant information Sheet




                  Sanger Scientist Observation and Interview Information sheet

Title: Race, Ethnicity and Genetic Ancestry in Genomic Research.


This information sheet is about a project that you can choose to take part in. It explains how personal
information (data) about you will be processed if you participate.


Before you decide to take part, please read this information sheet carefully. You can have someone
help you to read this information if you need to. You can also discuss the project with others if you
wish.


If you have any questions, you can contact us using the details at the end of this document.


What is the purpose of the project?


There are challenges around the use of human population categories, race, ethnicity, and ancestry in
genomic research. These include how these terms are defined and implemented and how this impacts
our understanding of genes and their function. Importantly, the historical and current misuse that
surrounds this complexity has resulted in unequal outcomes of genomic research for individuals of
different ethnic and geographic populations.


This research aims to investigate what makes genomic research practice around data categorisations
‘socially just’ when issues around race, ethnicity and ancestry are encountered. The project will look at
how genomic researchers and the public who engage with them use these concepts. The understanding
of the systems that underpin ethical choices around population descriptors will be used to inform
science policy.
What does the project involve?



This study will use an ethnographic approach to understand the experiences and perspectives of the
issues surrounding race and ethnicity in genomic research practice. This will include:




    1. Multiple interviews of selected scientists at the Wellcome Sanger Institute over the course of
       six months
    2. Six-month observation of scientists in their professional environment at the Wellcome Sanger
       Institute (e.g., general team meetings and seminars, workshops/meetings around data
       categorisation)
    3. Observation of three public events to observe how the public connects with ideas around race,
       ethnicity, and ancestry in genomics.
    4. Semi-structured interviews with a sample of the public at each event to discuss themes
       emerging from the analysis of the scientists’ data.


Interviews will generally be conducted online over Zoom or in person. Recordings will be made and
transcribed and stored on a password-protected laptop. Recordings will be deleted once the analysis
of this project is complete (January 2025). Group settings such as meetings and public events may also
be recorded when feasible.

The aim is to understand the experience of scientists encountering and dealing with race,
ethnicity and genetic ancestry in their research.

Why have I been invited?

You have been asked to take part because you have been identified as having relevant expertise,
possibly because of your involvement in research.

What will happen if I decide to take part?


If you agree to participate, you will be interviewed over the course of the next six months three
times. Interviews will generally be conducted online over Zoom or in person and will last up to 60
minutes. The time of interviews may vary depending on your availability or your answers.

With your consent, observations of your teams and meetings will also take place. Field notes will be
qualitative notes recorded during or soon after observations. They will be factual information (times,
dates, behaviour observed, comments by participants) and reflections and interpretations.

Interviews and attendance at meetings and events may be recorded via camera or Zoom. These
recordings will be used to support observations from field notes, and the researcher will ask permission
before any recordings are taken.

What happens if I do not want to take part or if I change my mind?




                                                  288
                                                  /12
You can withdraw from the project and thereby withdraw your consent without giving a reason. If you
are unsure about continuing, you can discuss your concerns with the researcher or simply inform
them of your decision to withdraw.


You are free to withdraw at any time and do not have to give a reason. If at any point you decide to
withdraw, all data concerning you (e.g. recordings, notes or transcripts) will be destroyed to prevent
further use. However, data already included in publications or reports can not be withdrawn.


If you decide to withdraw your consent after your opinions have been used, we will inform you where
necessary that you have withdrawn your consent. But we won’t be able to withdraw your data if it has
already been made public (e.g. in a written publication) prior to that point.



Will my involvement be kept confidential?

All information collected will be treated as strictly confidential. No personal data about individual
participants will be included in the notes and transcripts or in any outputs (e.g. article, presentation)
resulting from the study.

Who will carry out the research?

The research will be conducted by Sasha Henriques, a PhD Student based at the Faculty of
Education at the University of Cambridge. Supervised by Prof. Anna Middleton, Cambridge University.

What will happen to the data?

Your personal details will be removed from the data and will not be shared outside of the core
research team.

The data will be analysed and used in the preparation of a PhD thesis, academic papers, policy
recommendations and public-facing outputs. Summary interview content, verbatim quotes from
interviews and excerpts from the notes may be used in publications or other academic outlets such as
conferences or meetings. These will not contain your personal details and will be attributed to you
using broad descriptors (e.g. Young person at the event).

Hand-written notes will be kept on secure premises. Digital files will be kept encrypted on the
Cambridge University OneDrive and downloaded to the researcher's external drive. You will be
identified by a code rather than your name. The anonymised data will be shared with the research
team at Wellcome Connecting Science and the Kavli Centre for Ethics at the University of Cambridge.
Recordings will be deleted once the analysis of this project is complete (January 2025).

Who is funding the research?

This doctoral project is funded by the Sanger Institute Director’s Office (Associate Director: Julia
Wilson) and is part of the Black PhD Fellowship programme delivered by the Head of Equality,
Diversity and Inclusion at the Sanger Institute (Dr Saher Ahmed).

Who is responsible for this project?




The institutional sponsor for this research is the University of Cambridge.



                                                   289
                                                   /12
This research has been developed according to the ethics guidelines of the British Educational
Research Association and reviewed by the research ethics review process of the Faculty of
Education, University of Cambridge. It is the responsibility of Prof. Anna Middleton and Sasha
Henriques at Cambridge University.




If you decide to take part in the project, you will be asked to sign a consent form to confirm your
agreement to participate and for the Project Team to use your personal information as set out below.

What if something goes wrong?

It is unlikely that there are any health or safety risks with your involvement in this project, given the
historical and current inequalities and atrocities associated with the use of race and ethnicity in science.
Therefore, the questions asked and topics covered may be uncomfortable for you. Although we will
attempt to protect your anonymity, the unique nature of your research or your place of work may impair
the extent to which this can be ensured.

If you are unhappy about the way you have been treated or wish to make a complaint, you can
contact the GRL Research Governance team on researchgovernance@sanger.ac.uk or the PhD
supervisor Anna Middleton am25624@cam.ac.uk.



How do I take part?

If you decide to take part you will be asked to sign a consent form.

What if I want to know more about the research before deciding whether to take part?

Contact the research team for more information:

participant in a research study, please see: https://www.information-

10.8    Appendix B3


Public Information (adult) sheet




                             Public information sheet and consent form

                   Title: Race, Ethnicity Ancestry in Genomic (genetic) Research.

This information sheet is about a project that you can choose to take part in. It explains how
personal information (data) about you will be processed if you participate.

Before you decide to take part, please read this information sheet carefully. You can have someone
help you to read this information if you need to. You can also discuss the project with others if you
wish.

If you have any questions, you can contact us using the details at the end of this document.

Background

In genetic studies, humans are sometimes put in groups (populations) to work out their differences
and similarities. Ways to group humans include race, ethnicity, or ancestry.

These groups mean different things and are not always accurate measures of how we are the same
or different. The way these groups have been used in research can cause unequal benefits and some
people being mistreated.

This research aims to study if and how social justice (doing the best for society) is part of grouping
human data in genetic research. The project will look at how genetic researchers and the public
understand this. This understanding will be used to inform policy and guidance recommendations at
the Wellcome Sanger Institute.

What does the study involve?

The researcher aims to understand the issues surrounding race and ethnicity in genomic research
practice and to do this they will:



                                                 293
                                                 /12
    1) Carry out multiple interviews of selected scientists at the Wellcome Sanger Institute over the
       course of six months
    2) Undertake a six-month observation of scientists in their professional environment at the
       Wellcome Sanger Institute (e.g., general team meetings and seminars, workshops/meetings
       around data categorisation)
    3) Observe public events to observe how the public connects with ideas around race, ethnicity,
       and ancestry in genomics.
    4) Carry out semi-structured interviews with a sample of the public at these events to discuss
       themes emerging from the analysis of the scientists’ data.


Interviews will generally be conducted online over Zoom or in person. Recordings will be made of
the interviews and stored on a password-protected laptop. Group settings such as meetings and
public events may also be recorded when feasible.

The aim is to understand the experience of scientists encountering and dealing with race, ethnicity
and genetic ancestry in their research.

Why have I been invited?

You have been asked to take part because you have been identified as having relevant experience or
knowledge of how race, ethnicity and genetic ancestry are used in genetics.

What does participation involve?

You will be asked to sign a consent form. The researcher will take field notes of observations and
conversations with you while you attend an art exhibition around genetics and identity.

Conversations may be recorded via Dictaphone, camera or Zoom. These recordings will be used to
support observations from field notes.

Field notes will be factual information (times, dates, behaviour observed, comments by participants)
and the researcher’s reflections and interpretations.

At any point, you can ask the researcher to leave or stop taking notes. You are welcome to review
the notes and you can decide to take things off the record at any moment up to the point of analysis.

You may be invited to take part in formal and informal interviews about your experiences and
perspectives.

Formal interviews will be audio-recorded, or if conducted online, video-recorded. The interview will
take place at a time that is convenient to you. Ideally, it would be face-to-face but can be conducted
by telephone, Zoom or Teams. The duration of interviews will vary depending on the time available
and the length of your answers, but we expect most interviews to take around 45 minutes to 1 hour.




                                                 294
                                                 /12
At any moment up to the point of analysis, you can request to review the transcripts/notes of your
interview and decide to take things off the record.

Can I withdraw from the study?

Yes, you are free to withdraw at any time and do not have to give a reason. If at any point you
decide to withdraw, all data concerning you (e.g. notes or transcripts) will be destroyed.

Due to the nature of the observations, it might not be possible to destroy all data concerning the
activities that you took part in (e.g. data about your contribution to an event will be removed but
notes about the rest of the event will be retained).

If you have any concern, you can speak with the researcher who will do their utmost to meet your
request.

Will my involvement be kept confidential?

All information collected will be treated as strictly confidential. No personal data about individual
participants will be included in the notes and transcripts or in any outputs (e.g. article, presentation)
resulting from the study.

Who will carry out the research?

The research will be conducted by Sasha Henriques, a PhD Student based at the Faculty of Education
at the University of Cambridge. Supervised by Prof. Anna Middleton, Cambridge University.

What will happen to the data?

Your personal details will be removed from the data and will not be shared outside of the core
research team.

The data will be analysed and used in the preparation of a PhD Thesis, academic papers, policy
recommendations and public-facing outputs. Summary interview content, Verbatim quotes from
interviews and excerpts from the notes may be used in publications or other academic outlets such
as conferences or meetings. These will not contain your personal details and will be attributed to
you using broad descriptors (e.g. Young person at the event).

Hand-written notes will be kept in secure premises. Digital files will be kept encrypted on the
Cambridge University OneDrive and downloaded to the researchers external drive. You will be
identified by a code rather than your name. The anonymised data will be shared with the research
team at Wellcome Connecting Science and the Kavli Centre for Ethics at the University of Cambridge.
Recordings will be deleted once the analysis of this project is complete (January 2026).

Who is funding the research?

The research is funded by Genome Research Limited, Hinxton, UK

Who is responsible for this project?

If you decide to take part in the project, you will be asked to sign a consent form to confirm your

10.11 Appendix B6


Public Information (Under 18 ) sheet




                         Public information sheet and consent form




About this information sheet

This information sheet is about a research project that you can choose to take part in. It
explains what the project is about and how information about you will be used if you
decide to take part.

Please read this information carefully. You can ask an adult you trust, such as a parent,
carer, or teacher, to help you read and understand it. You can also talk about the project
with other people if you want to.

If you have any questions at any time, you can contact the research team using the
details at the end of this document.



Taking part is your choice.

Background

In genetic research, people are sometimes put into groups to help scientists understand
similarities and differences between humans. These groups can be called race, ethnicity,
or ancestry.

These words can mean different things to different people and they are not always
accurate ways of describing who we are. In the past, using these kinds of groups in
research has sometimes led to unfair treatment or unequal benefits for some people.



This research looks at whether ideas about social justice, meaning doing what is fair and
responsible for society, are part of how human genetic data is grouped and used. The
project explores how scientists and members of the public think about race, ethnicity,
and ancestry in genetics.
What is learned from this research will be used to help improve guidance and policies at
the Wellcome Sanger Institute.

What does the study involve?

The researcher wants to understand how race, ethnicity, and ancestry are talked about
and used in genomic research. To do this, they will:

Carry out interviews with scientists working at the Wellcome Sanger Institute over a
period of six months

Observe scientists in their normal working environments, such as meetings, seminars, or
workshops about data and research practices

Observe public events where genetics, race, ethnicity, or identity are discussed

Carry out interviews with some members of the public at these events to talk about ideas
that come up during the research

Interviews will usually take place online using Zoom, or in person. Interviews will be
recorded and stored securely on a password protected device. Some group activities,
such as meetings or public events, may also be recorded when possible.

The aim is to understand people’s experiences and views, not to test or judge them.



Why have I been invited?



You have been invited because you are attending, or have taken part in, an event or
activity linked to genetics, identity, race, ethnicity, or ancestry, and your experiences or
views may help the researcher understand how these topics are understood by young
people.

What does participation involve?

If you decide to take part, you will be asked to give your agreement, called assent. A
parent or carer with parental responsibility will also be asked to give consent.

The researcher may take notes while observing activities at an event, such as an art
exhibition related to genetics and identity. These notes may include factual details, such
as what happens at the event, and the researcher’s reflections.

Some conversations may be recorded using audio, video, or online tools such as Zoom.
These recordings are used only to help the researcher remember what was said.

You can ask the researcher to stop taking notes or recording at any time. You can also
ask to see what has been written about you and ask for parts to be taken off the record
at any point before the data is analysed.
You may also be invited to take part in an interview to talk about your experiences or
views. Interviews usually last between 45 minutes and one hour and will be arranged at
a time that suits you. Interviews can take place in person, online, or by phone.



Can I withdraw from the study?

Yes. Taking part is completely voluntary.

You can change your mind and stop taking part at any time without giving a reason. If
you withdraw, any information that identifies you will be destroyed.

Because some parts of the research involve group activities or events, it may not always
be possible to remove all information about an event as a whole. However, anything that
could identify you personally will be removed wherever possible.

If you are unsure or concerned, you can talk to the researcher, who will do their best to
respect your wishes.



Will my involvement be kept confidential?

Yes. Your information will be treated as confidential.

Your name and other identifying details will not be included in notes, transcripts, or
publications. When quotes or summaries are used, they will be labelled using general
descriptions, such as “young person at the event”.



Who will carry out the research?

The research is being carried out by Sasha Henriques, a PhD student in the Faculty of
Education at the University of Cambridge.

The project is supervised by Professor Anna Middleton, University of Cambridge.

What will happen to the data?

Your personal details will be removed from the data and replaced with a code. Only the
core research team will have access to the data.

The data will be used for a PhD thesis, academic publications, policy recommendations,
and public facing outputs. Short quotes or summaries may be used, but these will not
identify you.

Handwritten notes will be stored securely. Digital files will be encrypted and stored on
secure University of Cambridge systems.

Anonymised data may be shared with research teams at Wellcome Connecting Science
and the Kavli Centre for Ethics at the University of Cambridge.

Recordings will be deleted once the analysis is complete, expected in January 2026.
Who is funding the research?

The research is funded by Genome Research Limited, Hinxton, UK.



Who is responsible for this project?

This project follows the ethics guidelines of the British Educational Research Association
and has been reviewed through the Faculty of Education research ethics process at the
University of Cambridge.

Responsibility for the project lies with Professor Anna Middleton and Sasha Henriques at
the University of Cambridge.

What if something goes wrong?

The research is not expected to cause harm or distress. However, if you are worried
about the study or how it is being carried out, you or your parent or carer can contact:

Graduate Office, University of Cambridge

Email: graduate@educ.cam.ac.uk

If any of the topics discussed raise difficult feelings, a qualified counsellor is available.
Please ask the person organising the event for details.

will also be asked to sign a consent form.

10.15 Appendix D Interview Topics Guide
Overview

Interviews were semi-structured and exploratory. The guide below outlines the core topic
areas that informed interviews across the study. Questions were adapted over time in
response to emerging themes, prior interviews, and ongoing ethnographic observation.

           1. Introduction and record consent at each interaction

           2. Background and Role



   •   Can you describe your current role and responsibilities?
   •   How would you describe your field of research?
   •   What are the primary aims or goals of your work?
   •   In an ideal scenario, what do you think your field might achieve?
   •   How do you describe yourself - use the descriptors you find comfortable do not
       present as a tick box but part of the conversation

           3. Research Practice and Challenges

   •   What are some of the biggest challenges in your work?
   •   Do these challenges tend to be technical, organisational, or collaborative
   •   What challenges do you see for your institution?



24. What challenges do you see for science more broadly?



   1. Diversity, Populations, and Categorisation



   •   Does lack of diversity in data or samples arise in your work?
   •   What does a “diverse population” mean to you in a research context?
   •   How do you understand and use terms such as race, ethnicity, ancestry, and
       population?
   •   How are population categories constructed in your field – question about how they
       work
   •   When are population labels used, avoided, or removed, and why?
   •   What are the scientific implications of excluding populations without appropriate
       controls?
   •   What are the implications for the populations themselves?



4. Ethics in Research Practice



   •   How do you define ethics in the context of your work?
   •   Where did you learn about ethics as a scientist?
   •   What ethical guidance do you rely on in day-to-day research practice?
   •   What ethical principles guide your use of human population data?
   •   How do ethical issues arise unexpectedly in your work?
   •   What does “overly cautious ethics” mean to you, if anything?
   •   How do you assess whether collaborators work to similar ethical standards?
   •   What practices enable ethical behaviour, or allow unethical practices to persist?



5. Responsibility, Harm, and Governance

   •   Do you think genetics or genomic research has responsibilities beyond technical
       accuracy?
   •   In what ways might genomic research cause harm, intended or unintended?
   •   Does your work generate knowledge that could be misused?
   •   What mechanisms exist to prevent or manage such risks?
   •   How are responsibilities distributed across individuals, teams, and institutions?



6. History, Society, and Social Change



   •   Why do you think race remains an evocative category in science?
   •   Do you think historical knowledge is important for ethical scientific practice?
   •   How do changes in society shape what is considered ethical in research?
   •   How do you understand the relationship between science and social justice?
   •   Do you see social justice as relevant to your own work? If so, how?



7. Translation, Benefit, and Impact



   •   Who do you think ultimately benefits from your research?
   •      How do research outcomes help populations that are under-represented in
          datasets?
   •      What methods exist to support smaller or marginalised populations within large-
          scale studies?
   •      What barriers limit the use of these methods in practice?
   •      How close do you feel your work is to clinical or public benefit?



8. Follow-up and Reflection (where applicable)

   •      What has been happening since we last spoke?
   •      Have issues around data, diversity, or ethics arisen recently?
   •      Are there topics we discussed previously that you have since reflected on
          differently?
   •      Are there ethical questions in your field that you think are routinely avoided or
          deferred?
   •      How do you think patients or publics view the kind of research you do, and does
          that matter to you? the research environment.


Closing



   •      Is there anything we have not discussed that you think is important?
10.16 Appendix E Library process for Sanger early publication review


The library process involves systematic searches of PubMed, Europe PMC, Web of Science,
Wellcome Open Research, and bioRxiv/medRxiv. Searches are conducted primarily in the
author address or affiliation fields, in order to capture outputs from the following: the
Wellcome Sanger Institute, Wellcome Connecting Science, the Genomic Surveillance Unit,
Health Data Research UK (Cambridge only), Open Targets, the European Bioinformatics
Institute (EMBL-EBI), and Elixir.

Coverage is deliberately broad. The library aims to capture the entire publication output of
these units, with only minor supplementation from non-staff papers occasionally suggested
by colleagues. Staff suggestions, however, were rare. Published articles were
comprehensively covered, but coverage of preprints was acknowledged to be patchy, and
book chapters or conference abstracts were excluded. Overall, coverage of peer-reviewed
research was considered strong and systematic.

Saved search strategies were maintained across databases to ensure consistent capture. The
table 2. below summarises the main search formulations used by the library in 2023.

Proposal for a new Post-Doctoral Fellowship and Doctoral project exploring
race, equity and research good practice


   Category of paper (Science / Strategic / Oversight / Operations)        Operations

           GRL Meeting Paper Journey                                       Date                 Status
   1       Proposal for Post-doctoral Fellowship (circulated & discussed   20 April 2021        Complete
           with XXXX)

           Doctoral project exploring race, equity and research good
           practice (XXX)
   2       To OB for discussion/approval                                   May 2021             Complete
   3       BoM update and agreement                                                             Pending




Executive Summary
There are two strands to this proposal:
Proposal 1 – Post-doctoral Fellowship aimed at people form a Black
British background
It is proposed that we initiate a new Post-Doctoral Fellowship aimed at people from a Black
British background. This will run in a similar way to the Janet Thornton Fellowship, with at
least one Fellowship awarded every year (see below). This scheme is not intended to
replace work that we need to do to improve our processes to ensure that excellence,
regardless of background, can be supported, brought in and nurtured at Sanger. This is
positive action to acknowledge and recognise the disadvantage that our Black colleagues
face in all walks of life, including academia, due to discrimination and racism. To initiate our
own scheme would demonstrate that we are actively trying to be agents for positive change
and setting a standard for others. We will set-up a steering group with both internal and
external representation to provide advice and help shape the scheme and the Fellows will
have full access to mentorship, support and training to enable them to flourish. We will also
explore complimentary partnerships with external organisations.



Proposal 2 – PhD project exploring race, equity and research good
practice
A new joint collaborative project across GRL has been set-up between the Sanger and
Connecting Science (XXXX’s team) to support a doctoral researcher (PhD candidate), to
explore whether it is possible to create race and ethnicity categories that are helpful for
genomic science, and culturally acceptable for different global populations. This project will
also investigate what type of studies and analyses should take ethnicity into consideration
and how, and if there are projects that should not.

Key to the approach is providing an opportunity for a doctoral researcher from a Black or
other under-represented Black, Asian and Minority, Ethnic (BAME) background to both
develop their career in this field, and to utilise their lived experience as part of a minority
group, to help connect with specific communities so that voices of under-represented
research participants can be heard.
Wellcome Connecting Science Society and Ethics Research Group supervises PhD students
in the social sciences at the University of Cambridge, however, as these candidates are not
doing molecular projects they are currently ineligible to receive support through the
current Sanger PhD programme and so are all personally funded by individuals or
externally funded (e.g. via NIHR). Within the next QQ , the Society and Ethics Research
group have already costed funding for 1 PhD student and within this proposal, are looking
to finance 2 BAME PhD students, to start in October 2022.

Background
Racial inequalities persist in society and we see this mirrored in Higher Education, where
there is a lack of diversity amongst senior leaders and an under-representation of BAME
people progressing within the sector. When we disaggregate the data, we see that Black
British people are particularly disadvantaged and this is propagated along the academic
pipeline - from degree awarding gaps at undergraduate level, through to fewer numbers at
postgraduate and professoriate levels and lower success rates with funding councils and
granting agencies (see Appendix A for a data snapshot).

The significant bias in genetic research datasets towards ‘White European’ samples has
been recognised, with many global calls to address this. However if we acknowledge that
race is predominantly a social construct, then the term ‘White’ has little scientific value as a
descriptor, and European could include individuals from a wide range of backgrounds,
irrespective of race. For example - “I’m White Northern European” or “I have Ashkenazi
Jewish ancestry” or “I’m mixed race”, could all refer to the same person. Within the BAME
doctoral research projects, we are looking to explore whether it is possible to create race
and ethnicity categories that are helpful for genomic science, and culturally acceptable for
different global populations. This project will also investigate what type of studies and
analyses should take ethnicity into consideration and how, and if there are projects that
should not. This will be supported by the Institute’s Research Culture Lead, Hayley Clissold
and Head of Policy, Sarion Bowers.

Processes
It is envisaged that the Post-Doctoral Fellowship scheme will run in a similar way to the
Janet Thornton Fellowship where we build on the candidates’ skills and experiences and co-
create a project. E.g. candidates would apply to a broad project outline set by Sanger Faculty
and if shortlisted, work with them to develop a project proposal. Shortlisted candidates will
be interviewed by a panel, including members of Faculty, the steering group and the Head of
EDI. Unsuccessful candidates will be offered the opportunity to be mentored and supported
to apply again.

The BAME Doctoral scheme is a partnership between the Sanger Institute and Wellcome
Connecting Science Society and Ethics Research Group and will enable candidates to
complete a social sciences in the context of genomics PhD project awarded through the
Faculty of Education, University of Cambridge. The projects will be co-created between the
EDI team, Wellcome Connecting Science and the Policy team at Sanger Institute.

Finances with CFO Note on Funding

Doctoral Fellows

To ensure that financial barriers do not deter promising BAME candidates, with family or
other responsibilities, from applying, we intend to offer a more generous stipend equivalent
to the salary of an early career researcher, in addition to a research budget, and university
fees over three years. The rationale for this stipend has been discussed with the Wellcome
EDI team and they have offered their full endorsement. We have also discussed this with the
Faculty of Education, University of Cambridge, where the PhD candidates will be registered,
and in line with their own positive action programmes, this enhanced stipend is also
supported. We also already have one BAME PhD student who is registered to start their PhD
studies this year, who will be receiving the same enhanced stipend (the funding for this has
already been committed by Sanger DO via Julia Wilson, Sarion Bowers and Connecting
Science DO via Julian Rayner). Thus precedent has been set and we are asking, as a one-off,
one-off for 2 additional PhD students only under this specific scheme.

Although there was no provision for this project in the QQ Submission to Wellcome in

this project along with others “at risk” if necessary without clear sight of funding
source.

Next Steps
With Operations Board approval, we will engage with BoM to sign off the proposals and
work with the Finance team to secure funding and will set-up and mobilise the steering
group to help develop the approaches and processes. We have strong support from Sanger
Faculty and enthusiasm from external senior Black academics who we have connected with
to explore both of these concepts.
10.20 Appendix G2


Board of Management paper on call for data diversity (XXX = redacted)




ARP paper number: x Board Meeting: xxxx

Author: xxxx To be called for paper: xxxx




Title here



   Category of paper (Science / Strategic / Oversight / Operations)     xxx




   Board Action (Approve / Note / Discuss and comment)                  xxx




Purpose

The conditions of our core grant award from Wellcome requires that we develop a strategy
around data diversity by May 2024. This paper will provide a high-level outline of the key
components of such a strategy and an outline plan for development. The board is asked to
note this paper and provide feedback.

Background

The Wellcome Sanger Institute is one of the largest producers and holders of genomic data
in the world. Since the Human Genome Project, the question of whose genome(s) should be
sequenced, to represent humanity, has been a recurring theme. As genomics has scaled,
projects have moved to exponentially larger numbers of genomes. Internationally, countries
have been increasing their genomics capacity with high income countries having
significantly greater genomics capacity than counterparts in low and middle income
countries. As genomics capacity has developed, research in genomics has encountered the
same issues with recruiting and engaging participants from under-represented groups that
research in general faces. This concentration of genomic capacity and bias in participant
recruitment has resulted in the majority of global genomic data being derived from
participants of European-decent. The precise situation within Sanger’s own data is
unknown at this point, but the assumption is that our data reflects this general bias; noting
that there have been projects specifically focussing on different populations and
communities, such as the Born in Bradford study and studies of tribal groups in Papua New
Guinea.

As well as a bias towards European participants, research has historically been biased
towards men, with women often being entirely excluded from participating in research for a
variety of reasons including fluctuations in hormone cycles and possible effects on future
and existing pregnancies.

While it is likely participation is less of an issue within genomics, although barriers to
women participating in research remain, if analyses are not sex-disaggregated important
biological differences are not captured, to the detriment of women’s health.

The lack of “diversity” in research, and genomics in particular, has become an increasingly
pressing issue within the community. As genomic research moves into real world
applications, often partnered with other big data innovations such as AI, it has become an
important issue of social justice that the biases in data are addressed in order to prevent or
avoid exacerbating harms in communities already under-served by research. Wellcome is
seeking to address this issue in the research it funds and as a result one condition of our
award states,

“The Sanger Institute generates large-scale genomic and molecular data for the global scientific
and research community. The Institute is asked to develop and implement an organizational
strategy around data diversity, which encompasses (1) considering the representativeness,
generalizability and diversity of the data it generates based on the populations, cohorts and
questions that are being addressed, and (2) identifying gaps and implementing solutions for
improving data representativeness, generalizability and diversity where necessary. A draft strategy
should be submitted by the Sanger Institute to Wellcome by 24 May 2024, and an approved plan
submitted by 22 November 2024.”

Proposed plan

The question being asked by Wellcome is not straightforward and is open to interpretation
and debate. While the initial framing of the requirement is around “diversity in data”, the
specific focus on the representativeness and generalisability of the data takes us beyond
experimental design and into scientific strategy.

As such, there will be two broad components to the strategy. The first component can
loosely be considered “experimental design”, ie the tools and guidelines for how we design
and conduct our research to create a “diverse” research output with findings that are
applicable to the populations that need to benefit from the research. The second component
is our scientific strategy, ie what research questions are we asking. The first component can
help shape the second component, through the provision of tools and frameworks to
support decision making. Our research can never be considered representative, diverse and
generalizable if we only address diversity in participant recruitment but do not use that
information in our analyses. Similarly diverse research participant recruitment is of limited
use if we are not asking research questions that represent a range of populations and
cohorts.

The Policy Team, within the Director’s Office, will take responsibility for the first
component, and the rest of the paper will largely focus on this aspect. The science strategy is
set every five years and for now is outside the immediate scope of this project.

Developing a Strategy - Experimental Design

In order to develop tools, frameworks and guidelines to direct experimental design and
produce “diverse data” we will need to work through four high level steps:

Definitions – we will need to agree a set of definitions for what is meant by “representative”,
“generalizable” and “diverse”, and how they apply to experimental design. We must ensure that
there is agreement on terminology. Some of these terms may vary in their meaning based on the
context they are being used in, making it important to have agreement on their use and meaning.

Baseline – in order to identify gap and propose solutions we will need to develop a baseline
measurement of what our current position is. This presents a lot of challenges, but we propose
to develop a pragmatic approach which considers broad categories to provide a basic outline.
This should allow us to identify gaps and allow us to loosely measure improvements without
being overly burdensome.

Tools and frameworks – there is already significant activity in this space. XXXXX the GA4GH
Policy Analyst who sits in the Sanger Policy Team co-leads a working group working on
diversity in data. Sasha Henriques is a PhD student in Anna Middleton’s group who is being
funded by the DO to work on representation in data. Genomics England have a partnership
strategy to support organisations working on these questions to join up and work together. We
will explore what tools and resources are already available to the community that we can
utilise, before developing any of our own.

Policies and Guidelines – the end product of this work will be to develop policies and guidelines
for Sanger Institute research and researchers to support experimental design for diverse data
and ensures our research is representative and generalizable.

Considerations and Recommendations

Sex and Gender – the majority of the work being undertaken already in the field looking at
diversity in data is considering the issues of race, ethnicity and ancestry. However, sex and
gender is an extremely important issue and one that is largely neglected. The scientific rationale
for considering biological sex is strong and significantly less complex than for ethnicity and
ancestry.
Recommendation: we use biological sex as a pilot for developing a strategy on experimental design.

Scientific Racism – one of the most challenging aspects of considering race, ethnicity and
ancestry in genomic research is considering how and when categories that may be linked to race
are used in our own research and also how that research could be misused by others. There is a
tension between the social construct of race and the use of categories for race and ethnicity,
which are often self-identified, for genetic analyses.

Recommendation: we work with our Race Equity Network, and other relevant stakeholders
including faculty and BAME staff and students to identify actions we can take to prevent misuse of
our data and counter scientific racism. We should also work with these groups to develop a strong
justification for gathering diverse data.

Representation – in developing a strategy on diversity in data we must ensure that relevant
stakeholders are consulted and engaged. This applies to both questions of biological sex or
gender and ancestry and ethnicity and is critical where we seek to prevent harm through
discrimination or counter historic social injustice.

Recommendation: identify and evaluate different possibilities for stakeholder engagement

e.g. advisory board versus focus groups.

Wellcome – although this work is a condition of our funding, Wellcome have not given much
indication of their expectations in this space. Ongoing discussions will be essential to ensure our
work meets their expectations and we should seek to influence their views.

Discussions at other meetings and boards – within GRL reporting structure

Although this has not been discussed at other boards and meeting at this point, engaging
with Connecting Science, the EDI team and different internal and external groups, including
the EDI Forum will be an integral part of this work.

Risks

Failure to develop a strategy on diversity in data would be a breach of our funding
conditions, resulting in sanctions or reduced funding in future.

Potential Conflicts of Interest

N/A

Conclusions and Next steps Conclusions

The Sanger Institute must develop a strategy on diversity in data by May 2024. We propose
a series of steps to help us develop the tools and guidelines that underpin a strategy, and we
will start by piloting these steps with sex and gender.
